Rickshaw Boy

Preface  

Lao She (1899–1966), originally named Shu Qingchun, courtesy name Sheyu, was a native of Beijing. He lost his father at a young age and grew up in poverty, deeply influenced by the social life, thoughts, and aesthetic tastes of the lower-class commoners in old Beijing. In 1924, he went to England to teach and began his literary career, gaining attention for his humorous style. After returning to China in 1930, he created works such as "Rickshaw Boy" and "Divorce," establishing himself as the foremost writer of urban vernacular literature in modern literary history. During the War of Resistance, he chaired the work of the All-China Resistance Association of Literary and Art Workers and wrote the million-word novel "Four Generations Under One Roof." After the founding of New China, he produced powerful plays such as "Dragon Beard Ditch" and "Teahouse," earning the title "People's Artist."  

The novel tells the tragic story of a rickshaw puller named Xiangzi in old Beijing.  

Xiangzi drifted from the countryside into the city and became a rickshaw puller. Young and robust, with no bad habits, he was full of confidence in the future. His only wish was to buy his own rickshaw and become an "independent" and "free" puller. However, the repeated setbacks of buying and losing a rickshaw shattered his life ideals and plunged him into despair.  

Emotionally, Xiangzi also lost his autonomy. Hu Niu, the daughter of rickshaw yard owner Liu Siye, liked Xiangzi. Although Xiangzi despised her for being old and ugly, he could not resist her seduction and married her. After the marriage, Xiangzi used Hu Niu's private savings to buy Er Qiangzi's old rickshaw. But soon, Hu Niu died from difficult childbirth, and Xiangzi had to sell the rickshaw to bury her. This loss of family and fortune left Xiangzi in deep sorrow. After Hu Niu's death, Xiao Fuzi's attentive care made him sincerely love this unfortunate girl. He seemed to regain the courage to live, but fate continued to torment him. Driven by life's hardships, Xiao Fuzi turned to prostitution and hanged herself before Xiangzi could "make good." This extinguished Xiangzi's last glimmer of hope.  

From then on, Xiangzi completely collapsed and degenerated. He indulged in drinking, gambling, whoring, and deceit, even selling people out for money, until he became a social outcast, a walking corpse, waiting for death.  

"Rickshaw Boy" employs Beijing colloquial speech and dialect, along with vivid depictions of old Beijing's customs and practices, making it a natural, rhythmic, and classic work of modern vernacular fiction.  

I  

We are telling the story of Xiangzi, not the camel; "Camel" is just Xiangzi's nickname. So, let's start with Xiangzi. As for the connection between "Camel" and Xiangzi, we'll touch on it casually here and let it be.  

The rickshaw pullers of Beiping were divided into several factions. The young, strong, and agile ones preferred to rent good-looking rickshaws, pulling "full days," free to decide when to start and stop. They would park their rickshaws at fixed "stands"[1] or outside gates, waiting for customers who wanted a fast ride. If lucky, they might earn a dollar or two at once; if not, they might waste an entire day without even covering the "rental fee," but they didn't care. This faction had roughly two aspirations: either to secure a regular contract or to buy their own rickshaw. Once they owned one, whether they pulled monthly contracts or casual fares didn't matter much—after all, the rickshaw was theirs.  

Slightly older pullers, or those whose physical condition meant they couldn't run as fast, or those with family obligations preventing them from wasting a day, mostly pulled rickshaws that were about eighty percent new. Both the pullers and their rickshaws were decent enough to maintain some dignity when haggling. These pullers might work "full days" or "half days." In the latter case, since they still had energy, they would "pull late shifts"[2] regardless of summer or winter. Nighttime naturally required more caution and skill than daytime, but it also earned more money.  

Those over forty or under twenty likely struggled to find a place in the first two factions. Their rickshaws were old, and they dared not "pull late shifts," so they could only set out early, hoping to work from dawn until three or four in the afternoon to cover the "rental fee" and their own meals. Their rickshaws were worn out and slow, so they had to travel farther and charge less. They hauled goods at melon markets, fruit markets, and vegetable markets—low pay, but no need to rush.  

Among them, those under twenty—some starting the trade at eleven or twelve—rarely transformed into skilled pullers by their twenties, since childhood injuries made it hard to grow strong. They might pull rickshaws all their lives without ever making a name for themselves. Those over forty, who had already pulled for eight or ten years, had muscles and sinews so worn that they willingly lagged behind. They knew that sooner or later, a stumble would leave them dead on the road. Their pulling postures, their bargaining tricks, their shortcuts and detours—all reminded them of past glories, and they would sneer at the newcomers. Yet this pride did nothing to lessen the darkness ahead, and they often sighed while wiping their sweat. Still, compared to other rickshaw pullers in their forties, they seemed less miserable. These others had never imagined they would end up pulling rickshaws, only taking up the shafts when the line between life and death blurred. Dismissed policemen or school servants, small traders who had lost their capital, or unemployed craftsmen—when they had nothing left to sell or pawn, they gritted their teeth and, with tears in their eyes, embarked on this path of death. These people had already sold the prime of their lives, now dripping their cornbread-and-sweat onto the streets. Lacking strength, experience, and friends, they received no sympathy even from their fellow pullers. They pulled the shabbiest rickshaws, whose tires would puncture countless times a day. They had to beg forgiveness from passengers, even though fifteen coppers was already considered a good deal.  

Besides them, differences in environment and knowledge formed another faction of pullers. Those from Xiyuan or Haidian naturally found it easier to travel to the Western Hills, Yenching University, or Tsinghua University. Similarly, those from outside Andingmen went to Qinghe or Beiyuan; those from outside Yongdingmen went to Nanyuan… These were long-haul pullers who disdained short fares, preferring one journey to many, unwilling to scrape together three or five coppers. Yet they were outclassed by the pullers near Dongjiaominxiang, who specialized in foreign clients and could pull all the way from Jiaominxiang to Yuquan Mountain, the Summer Palace, or the Western Hills in one go. Their stamina aside, most pullers couldn't compete for this trade because these foreign-serving pullers had unique knowledge—they could speak foreign languages. They knew where British and French soldiers went: Wanshou Mountain, Yonghe Temple, and the "Eight Great Hutongs." They had their own foreign phrases, which they refused to teach others. Their running style was unique—a steady four-six pace, neither fast nor slow, heads down, eyes straight ahead, hugging the curb, exuding an air of peaceful detachment and specialized skill. When pulling foreigners, they could skip the uniform vest and instead wore long-sleeved white shirts, white or black trousers with extra-wide legs, tied at the ankles with thin straps, and double-sole black cloth shoes with wide toes. Clean, neat, and proud. Seeing such attire, no other puller would compete for fares or race them—these pullers seemed to belong to a different trade.  

With this simple analysis, let's pinpoint Xiangzi's place, as precisely as a nail in a machine—or so we hope. Before he earned the nickname "Camel," Xiangzi was a relatively free rickshaw puller, meaning he belonged to the young, strong faction that owned their own rickshaws: his own rickshaw, his own life, all in his hands—a top-tier puller. This was no easy feat. One year, two years, at least three or four; drop by drop, countless tens of thousands of drops of sweat, before he earned that rickshaw. Gritting his teeth through wind and rain, skimping on food and drink, he saved up for it. That rickshaw was the total result and reward of all his struggles and hardships, like a medal on a battle-hardened warrior. When he rented others' rickshaws, he worked from dawn to dusk, from east to west, from south to north, like a spinning top whipped into motion—he had no self. Yet amid this whirl, his eyes didn't blur, his mind didn't waver; he always dreamed of a distant rickshaw, one that would let him be free and independent, like his own hands and feet. With his own rickshaw, he needn't endure the anger of rickshaw owners or fawn over anyone; with his own strength and rickshaw, he could open his eyes and have a meal.  

He wasn't afraid of hardship, nor did he have the excusable but unwise bad habits common among rickshaw pullers. His intelligence and effort were enough to turn his ambitions into reality. Had his circumstances been better, or had he received more education, he certainly wouldn't have ended up in the "rubber-wheel crowd," and no matter what he did, he wouldn't have wasted his opportunities. Unfortunately, he had to pull a rickshaw; well, even in this trade, he proved his ability and cleverness. He seemed like someone who could make a good ghost even in hell. Growing up in the countryside, having lost his parents and a few acres of poor land, he ran to the city at eighteen. With the sturdiness and honesty of a country lad, he had tried almost every job where selling his strength could earn a meal. But soon, he realized pulling a rickshaw was an easier way to make money; other manual labor had limited income, while rickshaw pulling offered more variety and opportunity—you never knew when or where you'd encounter a reward beyond expectations. Of course, he knew such chances weren't purely accidental; both the man and the rickshaw had to be presentable, with something to offer that a discerning customer would appreciate. After some thought, he believed he had the qualifications: he was strong and young. What he lacked was experience—he hadn't run yet and dared not start with a fancy rickshaw. But this was a surmountable difficulty. With his body and strength as a foundation, he only needed to practice for ten days or half a month to get the hang of it, then rent a new rickshaw. Before long, he might secure a contract job, then scrimp and save for a year or two—even three or four—and surely buy his own rickshaw, the finest one! Looking at his powerful muscles, he thought it was just a matter of time—a goal and aim he would definitely achieve, not a dream!  

His physique and muscles developed ahead of his years; barely twenty, he was already tall and big, though his limbs hadn't yet been shaped by time into a fixed form, he seemed like an adult—one with a face and body still showing a childlike naivety and mischief. Watching the top-tier pullers, he planned how to cinch his waist to better show off his iron-fan-shaped chest and straight, solid back; turning his head, he looked at his shoulders—how broad, how imposing! With his waist cinched, he'd put on wide white trousers, the cuffs tied with chicken-gut straps, revealing his oversized feet! Yes, he could undoubtedly become the most outstanding rickshaw puller; he smiled foolishly to himself. His appearance wasn't remarkable, but what made him endearing was the spirit on his face. His head wasn't large, with round eyes, a fleshy nose, short thick brows, and a shaved head that was always shiny. His cheeks had no excess flesh, but his neck was almost as thick as his head; his face was perpetually ruddy, with a particularly shiny, not-small scar between his cheekbone and right ear—left from when he was a child sleeping under a tree and got bitten by a donkey. He didn't pay much attention to his looks; he loved his face as much as his body, both so sturdy and tough; he considered his face part of his limbs, as long as it was tough enough. Yes, after arriving in the city, he could still stand on his head for half a day. Standing like that, he felt he was like a tree, every part of him straight and strong.  

He truly resembled a tree—firm, silent, yet full of vitality. He had his own plans and some cunning, but he didn't like discussing them with others. Among rickshaw pullers, personal grievances and difficulties were public topics of conversation; at the "stands," in small teahouses, in crowded compounds, everyone reported, described, or shouted about their own affairs, and these became communal property, spreading like folk songs from place to place. Xiangzi was a country boy.His speech was not as glib as that of city dwellers. If glibness were a talent from birth, he was naturally unwilling to talk much, and therefore not inclined to learn their slick and malicious chatter. He knew his own business and disliked discussing it with others. With his mouth often idle, he had time to think, and his eyes seemed always to be gazing into his own heart. Once his mind was made up, he would follow the path it opened. If the way was blocked, he could remain silent for a day or two, gritting his teeth as if biting his own heart. He decided to pull a rickshaw, and so he went to pull one. He rented a broken-down rickshaw and first trained his legs. The first day he earned almost nothing. The second day brought decent business, but then he had to lie low for two days; his ankles swelled up like two bottle gourds and he could no longer lift them. He endured it, no matter how much pain. He knew this was inevitable—it was a hurdle that must be crossed in rickshaw pulling. Unless he got past this, he couldn't dare to run freely.

Once his feet were better, he dared to run. This gave him great relief, because there was nothing else to fear: he was very familiar with the place names, and even if he sometimes took a detour, it didn't matter much—after all, he had plenty of strength. The methods of pulling a rickshaw, drawing on his experience with pushing, pulling, carrying, and shouldering, weren't too hard to grasp. Besides, he had his own principle: be more cautious, less competitive, and generally, nothing would go wrong. As for haggling over fares and vying for passengers, he was slow of tongue and quick-tempered, no match for those old hands. Knowing this weakness, he simply didn't go much to the "rickshaw stand" [1]; wherever there were no rickshaws, he would park his. In these quiet spots, he could negotiate the fare at ease, and sometimes he wouldn't even set a price, just saying, "Get in, pay as you see fit!" His appearance was so honest, his face so simple and endearing, that people seemed to have to trust him, unable to believe this big, simple fellow could cheat them. Even if they had doubts, they could only suspect he was a country bumpkin new to the city, probably not knowing the way, and thus unable to quote a fare. When people asked, "Do you know the way?" he would give a smile, half pretending to be dense and half being sly, leaving them at a loss.

In two or three weeks, he had gotten his legs in shape. He knew his running style was good to look at. Running style was proof of a rickshaw puller's skill and qualifications. Those who splayed their feet, flapping them on the ground like two palm-leaf fans, were undoubtedly newcomers fresh from the countryside. Those who bent their heads low, scraped their feet along, and ran at about the same speed as walking—while showing great signs of running—were men over fifty. The experienced but weak ones had another method: they hunched their chests deeply, lifted their legs high, and bobbed their heads with each step; in this way, they looked like they were running hard, but in fact were no faster than anyone else. They relied on their "airs" to maintain their dignity. Xiangzi certainly never adopted any of these postures. His legs were long and his strides large, his waist very steady; he ran with little noise, each step having some give, and the shafts didn't jiggle, making the passenger feel safe and comfortable. When he wanted to stop, no matter how fast he was running, he'd just lightly brush his big feet against the ground a couple of times and he'd stop. His strength seemed to reach every part of the rickshaw. With his back slightly bent, his hands loosely holding the shafts, he was agile, neat, and precise. He didn't look hurried but ran fast, and though fast, he wasn't dangerous. Even among those who pulled private rickshaws, this was considered exceptional.

He swapped for a new rickshaw. From the day he swapped, he found out that a rickshaw like the one he rented—with soft springs, solid brass fittings, a large rain curtain, double lamps, and a slender-necked, large brass horn—was worth over a hundred yuan. If the paintwork and brass were a bit shoddier, a hundred yuan would cover it. Roughly speaking, with just a hundred yuan, he could get himself a rickshaw. Suddenly he thought: if he could save a dime a day, a hundred yuan would take a thousand days. A thousand days! Stacking a thousand days together, he could hardly figure out how far away that was. But he made up his mind—a thousand days, or even ten thousand days, he had to buy a rickshaw! The first step, he figured, was to pull a private rickshaw. If he got a client with many social engagements and dinners, averaging a dozen or so banquets a month, he could pocket two or three yuan in meal money. On top of that, if he could save a few dimes or even a few yuan each month, in a year he could save fifty or sixty yuan! That way, his hope seemed much closer. He didn't smoke, drink, gamble, or have any vices; he had no family burdens. As long as he gritted his teeth, everything would work out. He swore to himself: in a year and a half, he—Xiangzi—would have a rickshaw of his own, brand new, not a second-hand or a refurbished one.

He really did get a private rickshaw job. But reality didn't quite match his hopes. True, he had gritted his teeth, but after a year and a half, he hadn't fulfilled his vow. He had indeed taken on private rickshaw work, and he was cautious in handling things. Unfortunately, things in the world aren't always one-sided. He could be as careful as he liked, but that didn't stop the master from firing him. Whether after two or three months, or after ten days or so, it would be over. He'd have to find another job. Naturally, while looking for a new job, he also had to pick up odd fares—like riding a horse while looking for another mount, he couldn't afford to be idle. During such times, he often made mistakes. He forced himself to keep up his spirits, not just to scrape together a day's meal [3], but also to continue saving for the rickshaw. But forcing oneself is never a good thing: when he pulled the rickshaw, he couldn't concentrate on running. It seemed he was always thinking about something, and the more he thought, the more he feared, and the more resentful he became. If this kept up, when would he ever buy a rickshaw? Why was it like this? Was he still lacking in ambition? When lost in such thoughts, he forgot his usual caution. Once, a leather tire ran over some broken brass and pottery shards and went flat, forcing him to stop. Worse still, sometimes he bumped into pedestrians. Once, in his hurry to squeeze through, he even lost the axle cap. If he had been pulling a private rickshaw, these mistakes would never have happened. But once he was out of a job, he felt unhappy and became somewhat reckless. Damaging the rickshaw naturally meant paying for the repairs, which only made him more anxious, adding fuel to the fire. For fear of causing even bigger trouble, he sometimes slept the whole day away lazily. When he opened his eyes, the whole day had already been wasted, and he would regret and hate himself. Moreover, during such periods, the more anxious he became, the harder he was on himself, and his eating and drinking became irregular. He thought he was made of iron, but he could still fall ill. When sick, he grudged spending money on medicine and forced himself to tough it out. In the end, his illness worsened, and not only did he have to buy medicine, he also had to rest for several days straight. These difficulties made him grit his teeth even harder, but the money for the rickshaw didn't accumulate any faster for it. It took a full three years before he had scraped together a hundred yuan!

He couldn't wait any longer. His original plan was to buy the most complete, the newest, the most satisfactory rickshaw, but now he had to settle for what a hundred yuan could buy. He couldn't wait any longer—what if something happened and he lost a few more yuan? Just then, there was a rickshaw that had just been made (custom-ordered but not picked up due to lack of funds) that was pretty close to what he wanted. It was worth over a hundred, but because the deposit had been forfeited, the shop was willing to take a little less. Xiangzi's face was bright red, his hands trembling as he slapped down ninety-six yuan. "I want this rickshaw!" The shop owner wanted to round it up to an even hundred. He talked for ages, pulling the rickshaw out and then back in, opening the canopy and then closing it, honking the horn. Every action was accompanied by a string of the best adjectives. Finally, he kicked the steel spokes a couple of times. "Listen to that sound, like a bell! Take it away! Even if you smash it to pieces, if a single steel spoke goes soft, bring it back and throw it in my face! One hundred yuan, not a cent less, or we're done!" Xiangzi counted the money again. "I want this rickshaw, ninety-six!" The shopkeeper knew he was dealing with someone who had a one-track mind. He looked at the money, looked at Xiangzi, and sighed. "Let's be friends. The rickshaw is yours. Six-month warranty: unless you smash the body, I'll fix anything for free. Here's the warranty, take it!"

Xiangzi's hands trembled even more. He tucked the warranty away, pulled the rickshaw, and almost cried. He pulled it to a quiet spot and carefully examined his rickshaw, trying to see his face reflected in the paint! The more he looked, the more he loved it. Even the parts that weren't quite to his ideal could be forgiven, because it was already his rickshaw. After gazing at it until he could rest for a while, he sat down on the new rubber mat of the footrest and looked at the shiny brass horn on the shafts. It suddenly occurred to him that he was twenty-two years old. Since his parents had died young, he had forgotten which day his birthday was. Since coming to the city, he had never celebrated a birthday. Well, buying a new rickshaw today—let this be his birthday! Both a day for him and for the rickshaw—easy to remember. And since the rickshaw was the fruit of his own labor, there was no reason not to consider man and rickshaw as one.

How to celebrate this "double birthday"? Xiangzi had an idea: his first fare had to be someone well-dressed; absolutely not a woman. Preferably to Qianmen, or else Dong'an Market. After dropping them off, he would treat himself to a meal at the best food stall—something like hot sesame cakes stuffed with quick-fried mutton. After eating, if there was a good fare, he'd take one or two more; if not, he'd quit for the day. That was his birthday!

Ever since he got this rickshaw, his life became more and more vigorous. Whether pulling a private rickshaw or odd fares, he no longer had to worry about the "rickshaw rent" every day—whatever he earned was all his own. Feeling at ease in his heart, he was friendlier to others, and business went more smoothly. After half a year, his hopes grew even bigger: if things kept going like this, in two years, at most two years, he could buy another rickshaw, then another, and another... He could even start his own rickshaw yard!

But hope is often dashed, and Xiangzi was no exception.

[1] Rickshaw stand, i.e., a parking spot.  

[2] "Late shift" means going out after 4 p.m. and pulling until just before dawn.  

[3] "Jiaogu," i.e., food and living expenses.

Two

Since buying the rickshaw, Xiangzi ran faster than ever. His financial situation was a bit easier, and his courage had grown accordingly. But he was always extra careful with his own rickshaw. Yet, when he looked at himself and then at his rickshaw, he felt something was off if he didn't run fast.

Since coming to the city, Xiangzi had grown over an inch taller. He himself could feel it, as if he still had more growing to do. True, his skin and features had become tougher and more set, and a small mustache had appeared on his upper lip; but he thought he should still grow a bit taller. When he had to duck his head low to get through a small door or a gate, he wouldn't say anything, but secretly he was happy, because he was already so tall, and yet he felt he was still growing. It seemed he was both an adult and a child, quite interesting.

A man this big, pulling such a beautiful rickshaw—his own rickshaw—with springs so soft they swayed gently, even the shafts trembling slightly; the body so shiny, the seat so white, the horn so loud—how could running slowly do justice to himself, let alone to the rickshaw? This wasn't vanity; it seemed more like a responsibility. Only by running fast, flying fast, could he fully demonstrate his own strength and the rickshaw's elegance. That rickshaw was truly lovable. After half a year of pulling it, it seemed to have developed a kind of awareness and feeling. Whenever Xiangzi twisted his waist, crouched down, or straightened his back, it responded immediately, giving him the most agreeable support. There was no friction or awkwardness between him and it. When he reached flat, sparsely populated ground, Xiangzi could hold the shafts with one hand, and the softly humming leather tires seemed to spur him on like a sharp, swift wind—fast and smooth. When he reached his destination, his clothes and trousers could be wrung out with sweat, drenching like he'd just been pulled out of a basin. He felt tired, but it was a pleasant, proud exhaustion—like riding a fine horse for several dozen li. If boldness didn't mean carelessness, Xiangzi was never careless when he ran boldly. If running slow was letting others down, running fast and damaging the rickshaw was letting himself down. The rickshaw was his life, and he knew how to be careful. With caution and boldness together, he became more and more confident, deeply believing that both he and the rickshaw were made of iron.

Therefore, he not only dared to run boldly, but he also barely considered when to go out. He felt that using his strength to pull a rickshaw to earn a living was the most dignified thing in the world. If he wanted to go out, no one could stop him. He paid little attention to outside rumors—whether troops had arrived at Xiyuan again, fighting had broken out at Changxindian, arrests were being made outside Xizhimen, or Qihuamen had been closed for half a day—he didn't really care. Naturally, when the shops along the street had all closed and the roads were full of armed police and security forces, he wouldn't deliberately look for trouble. Like everyone else, he would quickly stop his rickshaw. But rumors, he didn't believe. He knew how to be cautious, especially because the rickshaw was his own. But after all, he was a country man, not like city folk who would take a gust of wind for a coming storm. Besides, his physique made him believe that even if he unfortunately hit a "tight spot," he would find a way out and not suffer too much. He was not easy to bully—so tall, with such broad shoulders!

Nearly every year, news of war and rumors grew along with the spring wheat. Ears of grain and bayonets could be considered symbols of northerners' hopes and fears. Xiangzi's new rickshaw had just turned six months old when it was the season when wheat needed spring rain.Spring rain does not necessarily fall according to the people's hopes, but war always comes whether anyone hopes for it or not. Rumors or facts, Xiangzi seemed to have forgotten he had once worked the fields; he cared little about how war destroyed the land, nor did he pay much attention to the presence or absence of spring rain. He only cared about his rickshaw—it could produce pancakes and all kinds of food, like a万能 field, obediently following him, a living, precious piece of land. Because of the drought and the news of war, grain prices had risen; Xiangzi knew this. But like the city folk, he could only complain about the high cost of grain without a clue what to do. Grain was expensive; let it be expensive—who had a way to make it cheap? This attitude made him focus only on his own life, pushing all disasters and calamities to the back of his mind.

If the city people had no solutions for anything, they knew how to spread rumors—sometimes completely fabricated, sometimes exaggerating a shred of truth tenfold—to show they weren't foolish or idle. They were like little fish, sticking their mouths above the water when idle, proudly spitting out completely useless bubbles. Among the rumors, the most interesting were those about war. Other kinds of rumors often remained just rumors, like talk of ghosts and spirits, never turning into real sightings. But rumors about war, precisely because there was no accurate information, could have immediate effects. On minor details, they might deviate greatly from the truth, but on whether war itself would happen, they were ninety percent correct. "War is coming!" Once those words were spoken, sooner or later war would indeed break out; as for who was fighting whom and how, everyone had their own version. Xiangzi wasn't unaware of this. However, laborers—including rickshaw pullers—though not welcoming war, wouldn't necessarily suffer if it came. Every time war broke out, it was the rich who panicked most. As soon as they caught wind of trouble, they hurried to flee; money made them come quickly and run quickly too. But they couldn't run themselves, their legs too weighed down by money. They had to hire many people to be their legs, someone to carry their trunks, someone to pull carts for the old and young, men and women. At such times, the brothers who lived by their hands and feet saw their prices soar: "Qianmen, East Station!" "Where?" "East—Station!" "Oh, just give one yuan four mao! No bargaining, with soldiers and chaos all around!"

It was under these circumstances that Xiangzi pulled his rickshaw out of the city. The rumors had been around for over ten days, and prices had already risen, but war still seemed far off, unlikely to reach Beiping anytime soon. Xiangzi kept pulling his rickshaw as usual, not slacking off because of the rumors. One day, after pulling to the western district, he noticed something amiss. At the west end of Huguo Temple Street and Xinjiekou, no one was shouting, "Going to Xiyuan? Tsinghua?" He loitered around Xinjiekou for a while. He heard that rickshaws no longer dared to go out of the city; outside Xizhimen, they were seizing carts—big carts, small carts, mule carts, rickshaws—all being taken. He thought of having a bowl of tea and then turning south with his rickshaw; the stillness at the rickshaw stand revealed the real danger. He had considerable courage, but he wasn't about to walk into certain death. Just at this critical moment, two rickshaws came from the south, carrying what looked like students. The pullers were shouting as they went, "Anyone for Tsinghua? Hey, Tsinghua!"

No one at the stand responded. Some looked at the two rickshaws with a faint, indifferent smile, others sat with small pipes in their mouths, not even lifting their heads. The two pullers kept shouting, "Deaf? Tsinghua!"

"Two yuan, I'll go!" a young, bald, short man spoke up when no one else answered, half-jokingly. "Pull over! Find another one!" The two rickshaws stopped.

The young bald man hesitated for a moment, seeming unsure what to do. The others still didn't move. Xiangzi realized that going out of the city must be dangerous; otherwise, why would no one grab two yuan for Tsinghua—usually just two or three mao? He didn't want to go either. But the bald young man seemed determined: if someone would go with him, he'd risk it. He fixed his eyes on Xiangzi: "Big guy, what about you?"

The words "Big guy" made Xiangzi smile; it was a compliment. His mind started turning: with such a compliment, it seemed he ought to support that short, bold bald fellow. Besides, two yuan was two yuan—this wasn't something you ran into every day. Danger? Would it really be that coincidental? What's more, a couple of days ago someone said the Temple of Heaven was full of soldiers; he'd seen it himself, and there wasn't a single soldier there. Thinking this over, he pulled his rickshaw over.

When they reached Xizhimen, the gate tunnel was almost empty of pedestrians. Xiangzi's heart sank a little. The bald man also sensed something was wrong, but still smiled and said, "Let's go for it, buddy! Fortune or misfortune, today is today!" Xiangzi knew things were going badly, but having mixed in the streets for these few years, he couldn't back out now, couldn't act like a woman!

Once outside Xizhimen, there truly wasn't a single rickshaw in sight; Xiangzi lowered his head, not daring to look left or right. His heart seemed to press straight against his ribs. When they reached Gaoliang Bridge, he glanced around—no soldiers—and relaxed a bit. Two yuan was still two yuan, he figured; without some guts, how could he find such a sweet deal? He usually didn't like to talk much, but now he felt like saying a few words to the short bald man; the street was terrifyingly quiet. "Take the dirt path? On the road—" "Of course," the short man guessed his meaning, "once we get on the sidewalk, we'll have some security!"

Before they even reached the sidewalk, Xiangzi and the short bald man, along with their rickshaw and themselves, were captured by a dozen or so soldiers!

Even though it was the season for the Miaofeng Mountain temple fair, the night chill was still more than a single shirt could withstand. Xiangzi had nothing extra on him except a gray single-layer army tunic and a pair of blue army trousers, both stinking with sweat—already foul before they'd even reached him. This ragged uniform reminded him of the white cotton shirt and the indanthrene blue lined jacket and pants he'd been wearing; how clean and decent they had been! Yes, there were things in the world more decent than indanthrene blue, but Xiangzi knew how hard it had been for him to get that clean and neat. Smelling the stench of his own sweat now, he saw his past struggles and successes as all the more glorious, magnified tenfold. The more he thought about the past, the more he hated those soldiers. His clothes, shoes, hat, his rickshaw, even the cloth belt around his waist—all had been taken from him; all they left were his body, black and blue with bruises, and his feet covered in blisters! But the clothes didn't matter; the wounds on his body would heal soon. His rickshaw—the one he'd earned with years of blood and sweat—was gone! Disappeared as soon as they'd pulled into camp! All his past hardships and difficulties could be blinked away, but he couldn't forget that rickshaw!

He wasn't afraid of hardship, but getting another rickshaw wasn't something you could just talk about; it would take at least several more years! All his past success had been for nothing; he'd have to start over from scratch! Xiangzi wept! He hated not only those soldiers but everything in the world. Why did they have to bully a person like this? Why? "Why?" he shouted. This shout—though it relieved him a bit—immediately reminded him of danger. Forget everything else; escape was the priority!

Where was he? He couldn't even answer that correctly. For days now, he'd been running with the soldiers, sweat streaming from his head down to his heels. When moving, he had to carry, pull, or push the soldiers' gear; when stopping, he had to fetch water, build fires, and feed the livestock. From morning till night, he only knew how to put his last bit of strength into his hands and feet; his mind had become a blank. At night, as soon as his head hit the ground, he'd fall asleep like dead, and never opening his eyes again might not be such a bad thing.

At first, he vaguely remembered the soldiers retreating toward the Miaofeng Mountain area. Once they reached the back mountain, he was too busy climbing, constantly thinking that at any moment he might fall into a ravine, his bones picked clean by wild eagles, with no thought for anything else. After wandering in the mountains for many days, suddenly one day the mountain paths grew fewer, and when the sun was behind him, he saw flat land in the distance. The dinner bugle called the soldiers back to camp, and several carrying rifles led a few camels.

Camels! Xiangzi's heart stirred; suddenly he could think again, like a lost person finding a familiar landmark, recalling everything quickly. Camels couldn't cross mountains; he must be on flat land. From what he knew, west of Beijing—places like Balizhuang, Huangcun, Beixin'an, Moshikou, Wulitun, Sanjiadian—all had camel keepers. Could it be that after all this wandering, he'd ended up at Moshikou? What kind of strategy—if these soldiers, who only knew how to run and loot, even had strategy—he didn't know. But he was sure that if this really was Moshikou, the soldiers must have been unable to find a way out of the mountains and were thinking of coming down to find a path to survival. Moshikou was a good place: northeast could lead back to the Western Hills; south could lead to Changxindian or Fengtai; straight out the pass to the west was also an escape route. He thought this over for the soldiers, and in his mind, he mapped out a path for himself: this was his chance to escape. If the soldiers retreated back into the chaotic mountains, even if he escaped their hands, he'd still risk starving to death. To escape, he had to seize this opportunity. From here, he believed, one step could take him straight back to Haidian! Though so many places lay in between, he knew them all; closing his eyes, he had a map: here was Moshikou—heaven, this had to be Moshikou!—then northeast, past Jinding Mountain and Liwang Tomb, to the Badachu; from Sipingtai east to Xingzikou, then to Nanxinzhuang. For some cover, he'd better stick to the mountain, from Beixinzhuang north, past Weijiacun; north, past Nanhetan; further north, to Hongshantou and Jiewangfu; then Jingyi Park! Once at Jingyi Park, he could find his way to Haidian with his eyes closed! His heart felt like it was about to leap out! These days, all his blood seemed to have flowed to his limbs; now, it all seemed to rush back to his heart; his heart burned, and his limbs turned cold; hot hope made him tremble all over!

Until midnight, he couldn't close his eyes. Hope made him happy, fear made him terrified; he wanted to sleep but couldn't, his limbs spread like scattered parts on some dry grass. Not a sound, only the stars keeping time with his heartbeat. The camels suddenly let out two mournful cries, not far from him. He liked this sound, like hearing a rooster crow in the night—both sorrowful and somewhat comforting.

In the distance, artillery sounded, far off but clearly the roar of cannons. He didn't dare move, but instantly the camp was in chaos. He held his breath; this was his chance! He knew for sure the soldiers would retreat again, and definitely into the mountains. The experience of these days taught him that these soldiers fought like bees trapped in a room, just blindly bumping around. With the sound of artillery, the soldiers would certainly run; so, he, too, needed to be alert. Slowly, holding his breath, he crawled along the ground, aiming to find those camels. He knew full well the camels wouldn't help him, but since they were all prisoners together, he felt some sympathy. The camp grew more chaotic; he found the camels—lumps like small hills crouching in the dark, with only heavy breathing, no movement, as if the world were at peace. This emboldened him. He lay down beside the camels, like a soldier hiding behind sandbags. Quickly, he figured it out: the artillery came from the south; even if it wasn't a real fight, it was at least a warning that "this road is blocked." So these soldiers would have to flee back into the mountains. If they really went up the mountain, they couldn't take the camels. Thus, the camels' fate was his fate. If they didn't abandon these animals, he'd be done for too; if they forgot the camels, he could escape. Pressing his ear to the ground, he listened for footsteps, his heart pounding rapidly.

He waited, he didn't know how long, but no one came to lead the camels away. He dared to sit up, looking between the camels' humps; he couldn't see anything, pitch black all around. Escape! Good fortune or bad, escape!

III

Xiangzi had only run a few dozen paces when he stopped. The truth was, he couldn't bear to leave those camels behind, because now all he had was his life. Even a piece of rope on the ground, he'd gladly pick up; even if it was useless, it would comfort him a little—at least he'd have something in his hands, not completely empty. Escaping with his life was urgent, but what use was a bare, naked life? He had to take these animals with him, even though he hadn't yet thought of what use camels could be, but they were still something, and something of considerable size.

He pulled the camels to their feet. He didn't know much about handling camels, but he wasn't afraid of them, because coming from the countryside, he dared to get close to livestock.The camels rose very, very slowly. He didn't bother to carefully check if they were all tied together; feeling that he could pull them along, he took a step forward, whether he was pulling one or the whole "batch."

As soon as he stepped, he regretted it. Camels — used to heavy loads back in the passes — couldn't walk fast. Not only did they have to walk slowly, but they had to walk with extreme caution; camels were afraid of slipping. A puddle of water, a patch of mud — any of these could make them splay their legs or twist their knees. A camel's value lay entirely in its four legs; if the legs went, everything went! And Xiangzi was trying to escape with his life!

But he couldn't bear to let them go. He left everything to fate; a camel gained for free couldn't be abandoned!

Having been used to pulling a rickshaw, Xiangzi had a good sense of direction. Even so, his mind was a bit confused now. When he first found the camels, it seemed his whole heart was focused on them; but once he got them moving, he couldn't tell where he was. The sky was so dark, and his heart so anxious, that even if he could read the stars and adjust his direction, he didn't dare do it leisurely. The stars — in his eyes — seemed even more anxious than he was, jostling each other in the black sky. Xiangzi didn't dare look up anymore. He kept his head down, walking forward with a racing heart but not daring to quicken his pace. He remembered this: since he was pulling camels, he had to follow the main road, not stay along the hillside. From Moshi Pass — if this was Moshi Pass — to Huangcun was a straight road. This was the main road for camels, and it didn't go out of the way at all. "Not going out of the way" was highly valued in the mind of a rickshaw puller. But this road had no cover! What if he ran into soldiers again? Even if he didn't run into soldiers, his ragged military uniform, the mud on his face, and his long hair — could they convince anyone he was a camel puller? No, he didn't look like a camel puller at all! He looked much more like a deserter! A deserter — being caught by the authorities was the least of it; if the villagers caught him, he'd be buried alive at best! At this thought, he shuddered, and the soft thud of camel hooves behind him suddenly startled him. If he wanted to escape with his life, he still had to give up these burdens. But in the end, he couldn't let go of the rope on the camels' noses. Keep walking, walk, go wherever the road leads, deal with whatever comes; if he lived, he'd gain a few beasts; if he died, he'd accept his fate!

However, he took off his military coat and ripped off the collar in one go; those responsible brass buttons were also torn off and tossed into the darkness, without even a sound. Then, he draped this collarless, buttonless thin coat over himself diagonally, tying the two sleeves together in a knot across his chest, like carrying a bundle. This, he thought, might reduce his resemblance to a defeated soldier; he also rolled up his trouser legs a bit. He knew this still didn't look much like a camel puller, but at least it wasn't entirely like a deserter anymore. With the mud on his face and the sweat on his body, it was probably enough to fit the profile of a "coal black" [1]. His thoughts were slow, but thorough, and once he thought of something, he immediately acted on it. In the dark of night, no one could see him; he didn't really need to do this right away, but he couldn't wait. He didn't know the time; day might suddenly break. Since he wasn't following the mountain path, he had no chance to hide during the day; if he wanted to keep traveling by daylight, he had to make people believe he was a "coal black." Having thought of this and acted on it right away, he felt a bit relieved, as if the danger was past and Beijing was right ahead. He had to get to the city steadily and quickly, because he had no money, no dry rations, and couldn't afford to waste any more time. At this thought, he considered riding a camel to save strength and stave off hunger a little longer. But he didn't dare mount; even if it were steady, he'd have to make the camel kneel first to get on; time was precious, and he couldn't afford the trouble. Besides, if he got up that high, it would be harder to see the ground beneath him; if the camel fell, he'd fall too. No, he'd just keep walking like this.

He roughly felt he was following the main road; direction and location were both vague. The night was deep, and days of fatigue, along with the terror of escape, left him physically and mentally uncomfortable. After walking a bit further, his steps so even and slow, he gradually felt drowsy. The night was still very dark, with a damp, cold mist in the air, making his mind feel even more lost. He strained to look at the ground, which always seemed to have rises and dips, but when he stepped down, it was flat. This caution and deception made him even more uneasy, almost irritable. He gave up watching the ground altogether, looking straight ahead and shuffling his feet. He couldn't see anything around him; it was as if all the world's darkness was waiting for him, stepping out of the darkness and into more darkness, followed by the silent camels.

He gradually got used to the outer darkness, his mind seemed to stop working, and his eyes involuntarily closed. He didn't know if he was still walking forward or had already stopped; he just felt waves of fluctuation in his heart, like a surging black sea, darkness merging with his mind, both vague, both rising and falling, both hazy. Suddenly, his heart stirred, as if remembering something, or perhaps hearing some sound — he couldn't tell — but he opened his eyes again. He was indeed still walking forward; he had forgotten what he was just thinking about, and there was no movement around him. His heart pounded for a while, then gradually calmed. He told himself not to close his eyes again, nor to let his mind wander; getting to the city quickly was the most important thing. But if his mind wasn't occupied, his eyes would easily close again; he had to think of something, had to stay awake. He knew that once he fell down, he could sleep for three days straight. What to think about? His head was a bit dizzy, his body damp and uncomfortable, his hair itchy, his feet sore, his mouth dry and astringent. He couldn't think of anything else except to pity himself. But he couldn't even think clearly about his own affairs; his head was so empty and swollen that as soon as he thought of himself, he forgot himself again, like a candle about to go out, unable even to illuminate itself. Add to that the darkness all around, and he felt as if he were floating in a mass of black air; although he knew he still existed and was still moving forward, there was nothing else to prove he was going anywhere, making him feel like he was floating alone on a desolate sea, unable to trust himself. He had never experienced such unsettling distress and absolute loneliness before. Normally, although he didn't much like making friends, when alone in the sunlight, with the sun warming his limbs and various things before his eyes, he wasn't afraid. Now, he wasn't afraid, but the uncertainty of everything was unbearable. If the camels had been as unruly as mules or horses, maybe it would have kept him alert, but the camels were so docile, so docile it annoyed him; in his most dazed state, he suddenly doubted whether the camels were still behind him, startling him; he almost believed these big beasts might quietly slip into a dark side path without him noticing, like pulling a piece of ice that gradually melts away.

At some point, he sat down. If he were to die just like that, even if he retained awareness after death, he wouldn't remember how he sat down or why. He sat for five minutes, maybe an hour — he didn't know. He also didn't know if he sat down first and then fell asleep, or fell asleep first and then sat down. Probably he fell asleep first and then sat down, because his fatigue could have made him fall asleep standing up.

He suddenly woke up. It wasn't the natural waking from sleep, but a sudden start, as if jumping from one world to another, all in the blink of an eye. He still saw darkness, but he clearly heard a rooster's crow, so clear it felt like a hard thing scratched inside his brain. He was fully awake. The camels? He didn't have time to think about anything else. The rope was still in his hand, and the camels were still beside him. His heart calmed. He was too lazy to get up. His body was sore and tired; he didn't want to get up, but he didn't dare sleep anymore. He had to think, think carefully, for a good plan. It was at this moment that he thought of his rickshaw and cried out, "On what grounds?"

"On what grounds?" But shouting into the void was useless. He reached out to touch the camels; he still didn't know how many he had pulled. He counted them carefully — three in total. He didn't feel it was too many or too few; he focused his thoughts on these three, and though he hadn't yet figured out exactly what to do, he vaguely thought that his future depended entirely on these three beasts.

"Why not sell them and buy another rickshaw?" He almost jumped up! But he didn't move, as if feeling ashamed for not having thought of this most natural and efficient solution earlier. Joy overcame shame; he made up his mind: hadn't he just heard a rooster crow? Even if roosters sometimes crow at one or two in the morning, dawn couldn't be far off. Where there's a rooster, there's a village; maybe it was Beixinan? There were camel breeders there; he had to hurry, arrive by dawn, get rid of the camels, and buy a rickshaw as soon as he entered the city. In times of war and chaos, rickshaws were bound to be cheaper; he was so focused on buying a rickshaw that selling the camels seemed like no trouble at all.

Thinking of the connection between camels and rickshaws, his spirits lifted, and it seemed his body had no discomforts at all. If he had thought that these three camels could buy him a hundred acres of land or be traded for a few pearls, he wouldn't have been this happy. He stood up quickly, pulled the camels, and started walking. He didn't know the current market price for camels; he had only heard that in the old days, before trains, a single camel could be worth a large silver ingot [2], because camels were strong and ate less than mules or horses. He didn't hope for three ingots; he just hoped to get a hundred or so, exactly enough to buy a rickshaw. The more he walked, the lighter it got; yes, the brightness was ahead — he was indeed heading east. Even if he had taken the wrong path, his direction was right: the mountains were to the west, the city to the east; he knew that. The uniform pitch-blackness around him gradually differentiated into shades, though he couldn't yet distinguish colors; fields and distant trees had taken shape in the general grayness. The stars grew sparse, and the sky was covered with a gray haze, like clouds or fog, dim, but much higher than before. Xiangzi almost dared to look up. He also began to smell the grass by the roadside and hear a few bird calls; having seen vague shapes, his ears, eyes, nose, and mouth seemed to regain their proper functions. He could also see everything on his own body, ragged and miserable as it was, but enough to convince himself he was still alive, like the feeling of waking from a nightmare, realizing how precious life was. After looking at himself, he turned to look at the camels — as ugly as he was, and as lovable. It was shedding season for the beasts; the camels' gray-red skin was already showing, with only scattered patches of loose, weak, long hair hanging here and there, ready to fall off at any moment, like giant beggars among animals. The most pitiful was their long, hairless necks — so long, so bald, curved and stupid, stretching far out like some dejected, skinny dragon. But Xiangzi didn't despise them; no matter how unsightly they were, they were still living things. He admitted to himself that he was the luckiest man in the world; heaven had sent him three living treasures worth a rickshaw — this wasn't something that happened every day. He couldn't help but laugh out loud.

A hint of red seeped through the gray sky; the ground and distant trees appeared even darker. The red gradually blended with the gray, turning gray-purple in some places, unusually red in others, while most of the sky was the color of grape gray. After a little while, a bright golden yellow shone through the red; all sorts of colors began to glow. Suddenly, everything became very clear. Then, the eastern dawn turned into a deep red, and the sky above showed blue. The red clouds scattered, golden rays shot out one by one; horizontal were the clouds, vertical were the light, weaving a magnificent, radiant spider web in the southeast corner of the sky: green fields, trees, and wild grass shifted from dark green to glowing jade. The trunks of old pines were tinged with gold and red; the wings of flying birds flashed with golden light; everything seemed to smile. Xiangzi wanted to shout a few times at that expanse of red light. Ever since he was taken by the soldiers, he seemed not to have seen the sun; his heart was always cursing, his head always down, forgetting the sun and moon, forgetting heaven. Now, he walked freely, the brighter it got, the sun giving a touch of gold to the dew on the grass leaves, also lighting up Xiangzi's brows and hair, warming his heart. He forgot all hardships, all dangers, all pain; no matter how ragged and dirty his clothes, the sun's light and heat did not exclude him — he was living in a universe of light and warmth; he was happy, he wanted to cheer!

Looking at his tattered clothes, then at the three shedding camels behind him, he smiled. With such a shabby man and beasts, he thought, to have escaped danger and be walking toward the sun again — truly strange! No need to think about who was right or wrong; everything was fate, he believed. He relaxed, walking slowly; as long as heaven protected him, he had nothing to fear. Where had he gotten to? He didn't want to ask, even though there were already men and women working in the fields. Keep walking.It didn't seem to matter much if he couldn't sell the camels for a while; first, he'd get to the city. He longed to see the city again, even though he had no parents or relatives there, no property at all. Still, it was his home, the entire city was his home, and once he got there, he would find a way. In the distance, there was a village, a sizable one. The willows at the edge of the village stood like a row of tall green guards, bowing their heads to look at the low houses, with cooking smoke floating above them. From afar, he heard a village dog barking, a very pleasant sound. He headed straight for the village, not expecting any stroke of luck, as if just to show that he was afraid of nothing. He was a good man, so of course he had nothing to fear from the village's honest folk; now everyone was under the bright, peaceful sunlight. If possible, he wanted a drink of water; even if he couldn't get any, it didn't matter. Since he hadn't died in the mountains, what was a little more thirst?

The village dogs barked at him, but he paid little attention. The stares of the women and children made him uncomfortable. He must be a very strange camel driver, he thought; why else would everyone stare at him so blankly? He felt deeply humiliated: the soldiers hadn't treated him like a person, and now in this village, everyone looked at him as if he were a freak! He didn't know what to do. His stature and strength had always made him proud and self-respecting, but in these past days, he had suffered humiliation and hardship for no reason. Looking across the rooftops of a house, he saw the bright sun again, but it didn't seem as lovely as before!

On the only main road in the village, pig urine, horse urine, and sewage had collected into several foul-smelling puddles. On the north side of the road was a relatively wealthy household, with tiled houses in the back, but the front gate was only blocked by a wooden fence, with no wooden door or gatehouse. Xiangzi's heart stirred: tiled houses—a rich man; a wooden fence without a gatehouse—someone who keeps camels! Good, he would rest here for a while; maybe a good opportunity would come up to get rid of the camels!

"Se! Se! Se!" Xiangzi called for the camels to kneel. For commands to move camels, he only knew that "Se..." meant to kneel. He used it with great satisfaction, deliberately letting the villagers see he wasn't an outsider. The camels really did kneel, and he himself sat down generously under a small willow tree. They looked at him, and he looked at them; he knew this was the only way to lessen the villagers' suspicion.

After sitting for a while, an old man came out of the courtyard, wearing an unbuttoned blue cloth jacket, his face shiny. One could tell at a glance that he was a country rich man. Xiangzi made up his mind: "Old man, is there some water handy? Let me have a bowl!"

"Ah!" The old man rubbed a bit of mud between his fingers on his chest, sized up Xiangzi with a glance, and carefully looked at the three camels. "There's water! Where are you from?"

"From the west!" Xiangzi dared not give a place name, because he wasn't sure.

"There are soldiers in the west?" The old man's eyes fixed on Xiangzi's army trousers.

"I was rounded up by the soldiers, just escaped."

"Ah! No danger on the western route with the camels?"

"The soldiers have all gone into the mountains, the road is very safe."

"Hmm!" The old man nodded slowly. "Wait here, I'll get you some water."

Xiangzi followed him in. In the courtyard, he saw four camels. "Old man, keep my three, and make up a full set?"

"Hmph! A full set? Thirty years ago, I had three sets! Times have changed, who can afford to feed camels now!" The old man stopped, staring blankly at the four beasts. After a long pause: "A few days ago, I was thinking of joining with the neighbors to send them outside the pass to graze[3]. But there's trouble with soldiers everywhere, east and west, who dares to travel? Keeping them here for the summer is heartbreaking, just heartbreaking to watch. Look at these flies! When it gets really hot, add in the mosquitoes, and watch these fine animals suffer alive, sigh!" The old man nodded repeatedly, seeming full of endless regret and complaint.

"Old man, keep my three, make up a full set and take them outside the pass to graze. These lively animals, spending a summer here, will be half killed by the flies and mosquitoes!" Xiangzi was almost pleading.

"But who has the money to buy them? This isn't the year for raising camels anymore!"

"Just keep them, give me whatever you can. I want to get rid of them so I can go to the city to make a living!"

The old man looked Xiangzi over carefully again and felt he was definitely not a bandit. Then he turned to look at the animals outside the gate, seeming to truly like those three camels—knowing full well there was no benefit in buying them, but a book lover wants to buy books when he sees them, a horse breeder can't bear to part with horses, and the man who once had three sets of camels felt the same. Besides, Xiangzi said he could sell them cheap. A knowledgeable person getting a bargain easily forgets whether there's any benefit in owning the thing.

"Young man, if I were well off, I'd really want to keep them!" The old man spoke the truth.

"Just keep them, do whatever you think is right!" Xiangzi was so sincere that the old man felt a bit embarrassed.

"To tell the truth, young man, thirty years ago, these would be worth three large silver ingots[2]. But these days, with wars and chaos, I—you'd better go hawk them somewhere else!"

"Whatever you can give is fine!" Xiangzi couldn't think of anything else to say. He understood the old man's words were very honest, but he didn't want to go all over the world selling camels—he might not sell them, and maybe something else would go wrong.

"Look, look, it's really hard to say twenty or thirty dollars, but it's also hard to part with the money. These times, no way around it!"

Xiangzi's heart grew a bit colder. Twenty or thirty dollars? That was still far from buying a rickshaw! But first, he wanted to get it done quickly, and second, he didn't believe he'd be so lucky as to run into another buyer. "Old man, whatever you can give is fine!"

"What do you do, young man? I can tell, this isn't your trade!"

Xiangzi told the truth.

"Oh, you risked your life to get these animals!" The old man felt very sympathetic towards Xiangzi, and was relieved too—these weren't stolen. Though it wasn't far off from stealing, still, there were the soldiers in between. After a disaster caused by soldiers, nothing could be judged by normal standards.

"How about this, friend, I'll give you thirty-five dollars. If I say that's not a bargain, I'm a son of a bitch. If I could give one more dollar, I'd be a son of a bitch too! I'm over sixty. Hmph, what else can I say!"

Xiangzi was at a loss. When it came to money, he had never been one to let a single cent go. But during these days in the army, suddenly hearing the old man's sincere and emotional words, he felt embarrassed to argue. Besides, the thirty-five silver dollars he could get in hand seemed more reliable than the ten thousand he hoped for, even if a life traded for only thirty-five dollars was indeed a little low! Just the three big live camels alone couldn't, absolutely couldn't, be worth only thirty-five silver dollars! But what could he do? "The camels are yours, old man! I'll just ask one more thing—find me a jacket and a little something to eat!"

"That's fine!"

Xiangzi drank some cool water, then, with thirty-five bright silver dollars, two cornmeal cakes, and wearing a ragged white jacket that only reached his chest, he wanted to stride into the city in one go!

[1] Pu'er means style or pattern; it is approximate.

[2] Dabao, silver ingots weighing fifty taels each.

[3] Fangqing, to let livestock graze on green grass.

Four

Xiangzi lay in a small inn outside Haidian for three days, his body alternating between chills and fever, his mind hazy and confused, a row of purple blisters sprouting on his gums. He only wanted to drink water and had no desire to eat. After three days of hunger, the fever subsided, and his body went as limp as taffy. It was probably during these three days that his connection with the three camels was overheard in his delirious mutterings or wild talk. By the time he woke up clear-headed, he was already "Camel Xiangzi."

Ever since he first came to the city, he had been simply "Xiangzi," as if he had no surname at all; now, with "Camel" placed before "Xiangzi," even fewer people cared what his real surname was. Whether he had a surname or not, he himself didn’t care. Still, swapping three animals for just a few bucks and ending up with a nickname to boot struck him as a bit of a bad deal.

As soon as he could struggle to his feet, he wanted to go out and have a look. He hadn’t expected his legs to be so weak; when he reached the inn’s entrance, his knees gave way and he sat down on the ground. He sat there dazed for a long while, cold sweat beading on his forehead. After bearing it a bit longer, he opened his eyes, his stomach rumbling, and felt a pang of hunger. Standing up very slowly, he found a wonton vendor. He ordered a bowl of wontons but stayed sitting on the ground. He took a sip of the broth, felt nauseated, held it in his mouth for ages, and forced it down reluctantly; he didn’t want to drink any more. But after a while, the hot soup threaded its way down to his belly like a strand, and he let out two loud belches. He knew then that he was alive again.

With a little food in his stomach, he had the mind to take stock of himself. He had lost a lot of weight, and his tattered trousers were too filthy to get any filthier. He didn’t feel like moving, but he was eager to restore his usual clean and neat appearance; he refused to enter the city looking so disheveled and bedraggled. However, looking clean and neat cost money—getting a haircut, changing clothes, buying shoes and socks all required cash. The thirty-five yuan in his hand should stay untouched; even without spending a cent, he was still far from the amount needed to buy a rickshaw! Yet, he felt sorry for himself. Though he had been taken by the soldiers only a short time, now when he thought about it, everything seemed like a nightmare. That nightmare had aged him considerably, as if he had suddenly gained several years. Looking at his own big hands and big feet, they were clearly his, yet they felt as if they had appeared from somewhere else. He was deeply distressed. He dared not think about the past grievances and dangers; even without thinking, they still existed, like on an overcast day, not looking at the sky but knowing it was dark. He felt his body was especially precious and shouldn’t be treated too harshly anymore. He stood up, knowing full well he was still weak, but felt an urgent need to spruce himself up, as if just getting a haircut and changing clothes would instantly make him strong again.

Once he was all dressed up, it cost him only two yuan and twenty cents. A set of coarse blue cotton pants and jacket, roughly like canvas, cost one yuan; black cloth shoes, eighty cents; thread socks, fifteen cents; and a straw hat, twenty-five cents. The ragged things he took off were exchanged for two packs of matches. Holding the two packs of matches, he headed west along the main road toward Xizhimen Gate. He hadn’t gone far before he felt weak and exhausted. But he gritted his teeth. He couldn’t take a rickshaw—from any angle, he couldn’t take one: how could a country fellow treat a mere eight or ten li as a real distance, especially when he himself was a rickshaw puller? That aside, being laid low by such a minor illness given his stature and strength was ridiculous; unless he fell flat and couldn’t get up again, he would roll all the way into the city if he had to—he would never give in! If he couldn’t make it into the city today, he thought, then Xiangzi was finished. He trusted only his own body, no matter what illness struck!

He set off unsteadily. Not far from Haidian, stars danced before his eyes. Leaning against a willow tree, he steadied himself for a long time; the world spun dizzily for a while, but he never sat down. Gradually, the spinning of heaven and earth calmed, and his heart seemed to fall back into place from far away. Wiping the sweat from his forehead, he took another step. Having already gotten a haircut and changed into new clothes and shoes, he felt this was treating himself well enough; so, his legs had to do their duty—walk! In one go, he reached the suburbs outside the gate. Seeing the hustle and bustle of people and horses, hearing the chaotic, grating noises, smelling the dry, foul odors, and stepping onto the fine, soft, grimy dust, Xiangzi felt like crawling down to kiss that gray, stinking ground—that beloved ground, the ground that sprouted silver coins! With no parents or brothers, no clan or relatives, his only friend was this ancient city. This city had given him everything; even starving here was better than in the countryside. Here, there were things to see and hear, light and color everywhere, sounds all around; as long as he was willing to work, there were countless coins and endless fine things to eat and wear. Here, even a beggar could get oily broth and leftovers, while in the countryside there was only cornmeal. Just west of Gaoliang Bridge, he sat down on the riverbank and shed a few hot tears!

The sun was slanting west. The old willows by the river leaned askew, their tips touched with golden light. The river didn’t have much water, but it was covered in green algae, like a long, greasy green ribbon—narrow, long, deep green, exuding a slightly fishy, damp odor. On the north bank, the wheat had already shot out its awns, short and withered, its leaves dusted with a layer of dirt. On the south bank, the lotus leaves, small and delicate, floated weakly on the water, tiny bubbles occasionally rising around them. On the eastern bridge, people and carts passed back and forth, looking particularly hurried in the slanting sunlight, as if all felt the unease of approaching dusk. To Xiangzi’s eyes and ears, all this was fascinating and endearing. Only such a stream could truly be called a river; only such trees, wheat, lotus leaves, and bridges could be called trees, wheat, lotus leaves, and bridges. Because they all belonged to Beiping.

Sitting there, he was in no hurry. Everything before him was familiar and dear; even if he were to sit there and die, he felt he wouldn’t mind. After resting for a good long while, he went to the bridgehead and had a bowl of plain tofu pudding: vinegar, soy sauce, Sichuan pepper oil, and chopped chives, scalded by the hot white tofu, released an utterly aromatic smell, so fragrant that Xiangzi had to hold his breath; holding the bowl, looking at the deep green chive bits, his hands trembled uncontrollably. He took a bite, and the tofu blazed a path through his insides; he added two more small spoonfuls of chili oil himself. After finishing one bowl, his sweat had soaked through the waistband of his trousers. Half-closing his eyes, he handed the bowl out: "Another bowl!"

Standing up, he felt like a man again. The sun was still at its lowest point in the west; the river was tinged slightly red by the evening glow. He felt so exhilarated he wanted to shout out loud. Touching the smooth scar on his face, feeling the money in his pocket, and glancing once more at the sunlight on the turret, he forcibly forgot his illness, forgot everything, as if driven by some unspoken wish, and decided to walk into the city.

Inside the city gate, all kinds of rickshaws and all kinds of people were crowded together. No one dared go fast, but everyone wanted to get through quickly. The sounds of whips, shouts, curses, horns, bells, and laughter all buzzed together in the gate tunnel—like a loudspeaker—forming a continuous hum, as if everyone was making some noise, all buzzing. Xiangzi’s big feet stepped left and right, his hands pushing aside on both sides, like a long, skinny fish frolicking with the waves, and he squeezed into the city. In one glance, he saw Xinjiekou; the road was so wide, so straight, his eyes lit up, as bright as the reflection on the rooftops to the east. He nodded.

His bedding was still at the Renhe Rickshaw Shed on Xi'anmen Street, so naturally he planned to go there. Because he had no family, he had always lived at the rickshaw shed, even though he didn’t always pull the shed’s rickshaws. The owner of Renhe, Fourth Master Liu, was nearly seventy; old in years, but not honest in heart. In his youth, he had been a warehouse guard, run a gambling den, trafficked in people, and lent out usurious loans. For all these trades, he had the necessary qualifications and skills—strength, cunning, tactics, connections, and a "reputation." During the Qing dynasty, he had been in gang fights, kidnapped respectable women, and undergone the ordeal of kneeling on iron chains. Kneeling on those iron chains, Fourth Master Liu didn't frown or beg for mercy. He tough it out through his court case; that was what they called having a "reputation." After getting out of prison, the Republic coincidentally arrived, and the power of the police grew greater and greater. Fourth Master Liu saw that local heroes were a thing of the past; even if Huang Tianba were reborn, he wouldn't have many opportunities. So he opened a rickshaw shed. Coming from a thuggish background, he knew how to handle the poor—when to tighten the screws and when to loosen up; he had a natural talent for manipulation. None of the rickshaw pullers dared try any tricks with him. With a glare or a hearty laugh, he could leave people utterly confused, as if one foot were in heaven and the other in hell, completely at his mercy. By now, he owned over sixty rickshaws, and the worst of them were still seventy to eighty percent new; he didn't keep broken-down ones. His rental fees were higher than others, but at the three main festivals, he gave two extra days off. Renhe Shed had living quarters; bachelors who pulled his rickshaws could stay for free—but they had to pay their rental dues. If someone couldn’t pay and kept pestering him, he’d confiscate their bedding and throw them out the door like a broken teapot. If anyone had an emergency or fell ill, they just had to tell him, and he wouldn’t hesitate; he’d help through water and fire with enthusiasm. That was what having a "reputation" meant.

Fourth Master Liu had the face of a tiger. Nearly seventy, his back was still straight, and he could still walk ten or twenty li without trouble. He had big round eyes, a large nose, a square mouth, and a pair of big tiger-like fangs; when he opened his mouth, he looked just like a tiger. He was almost as tall as Xiangzi, his head shaved clean, and he wore no mustache. He considered himself a tiger, but unfortunately had no son—only a tiger-like daughter of thirty-seven or eight. Anyone who knew Fourth Master Liu would also know Tiger Girl. She, too, had a tiger’s head and tiger’s brain, which scared off men; she was a capable hand at helping her father run the business, but no one dared marry her. She was just like a man in everything, even cursing with a man’s straightforwardness, sometimes with even more variety. Fourth Master Liu handled the outside, Tiger Girl the inside, and together the father and daughter managed Renhe Shed like a fortress. Renhe Shed became an authority in the rickshaw world, and the methods of the Liu father and daughter were often on the lips of rickshaw pullers and owners, like scholars quoting the classics.

Before he bought his own rickshaw, Xiangzi had pulled rickshaws for Renhe Shed. He entrusted his savings to Fourth Master Liu for safekeeping. When he had scraped together enough money, he took it back and bought that new rickshaw.

"Fourth Master Liu, take a look at my rickshaw!" Xiangzi pulled the new rickshaw into Renhe Shed. The old man glanced at the rickshaw and nodded: "Not bad!"

"But I’ll still stay here; when I get a monthly job, I’ll move to the residence!" Xiangzi said with considerable pride.

"Fine!" Fourth Master Liu nodded again.

So, whenever Xiangzi found a monthly job, he moved to the residence; and when he lost it and went back to pulling individual fares, he lived at Renhe Shed.

Being able to live at Renhe Shed without pulling Fourth Master Liu’s rickshaw was, in the eyes of other pullers, a rare thing. For this reason, some even guessed that Xiangzi must be related to Old Man Liu; others said Old Liu had probably taken a liking to Xiangzi and wanted to make him a live-in son-in-law for Tiger Girl. Though there was a bit of envy and jealousy in these guesses, if it really were true, then once Fourth Master Liu died, Renhe Shed would surely belong to Xiangzi. This kept them only daring to guess wildly, not daring to say anything unpleasant in front of Xiangzi. In reality, Old Man Liu’s preferential treatment of Xiangzi had another reason. Xiangzi was the kind of person who, even in a new environment, could still hold onto old habits. If he had become a soldier, he would never have put on that tiger’s uniform and immediately pretended to be stupid while bullying others. In the rickshaw shed, he never stayed idle; as soon as his sweat dried, he found something to do. He would wash rickshaws, pump up tires, dry the rain covers, oil the parts... no one had to tell him; he did it willingly and happily, as if it were some excellent entertainment. The shed usually had about twenty pullers living there permanently; when they weren’t pulling, they either sat around chatting or pulled the covers over their heads to sleep. Xiangzi—only Xiangzi—kept his hands busy. At first, everyone thought he was buttering up Fourth Master Liu, sucking up to him; after a few days, they saw there was no sycophantic intention at all—he was so sincere and natural—so they had nothing more to say. Old Liu never praised him once, never gave him an extra glance; the old man knew what was what. He knew Xiangzi was a good hand, and even if he didn’t pull his rickshaw, he still wanted Xiangzi in the shed. With Xiangzi there, the courtyard and the entrance were always spotlessly clean, to say nothing of everything else. Tiger Girl liked this big, simple fellow even more. Whatever she said, Xiangzi always listened attentively and never argued with her; the other pullers, hardened by their hardships, always spoke roughly. She wasn’t afraid of them at all, but she didn’t want to bother with them either. So her words were all reserved for Xiangzi. When Xiangzi went to pull a monthly job, both Liu father and daughter felt as if they had lost a friend. When he came back, even the old man’s cursing seemed more spirited and kinder.

Xiangzi, carrying his two packs of matches, entered Renhe Shed. It wasn’t dark yet; the Liu father and daughter were having dinner. Seeing him come in, Tiger Girl put down her chopsticks: "Xiangzi! Did a wolf drag you off, or were you digging for gold in Africa?" "Hmph!" Xiangzi didn’t say anything.
