Fourth Master Liu’s big round eyes swept over Xiangzi, but he said nothing. Xiangzi, wearing his new straw hat, sat down across from them.

"If you haven’t eaten yet, join us!" Tiger Girl said, as if greeting a close friend.

Xiangzi didn’t move, but felt an indescribable warmth inside. He had always treated Renhe Shed as home: when he pulled monthly jobs, the masters often changed; when he pulled individual fares...The seat changed every now and then; only here he was allowed to stay permanently, with people always chatting with him. Just now escaped with his life, back among familiar faces, and still being fed, he almost wondered if they intended to trick him, yet he nearly shed tears.

"I've just had two bowls of bean curd!" he said, showing a bit of courtesy.

"Where have you been?" Liu Siye's big round eyes were still fixed on Xiangzi. "Where's the cart?"

"The cart?" Xiangzi spat.

"Come over and have a bowl of rice first! It won't poison you! What good are two bowls of bean curd?" Tigress pulled him over, like an older sister-in-law doting on her younger brother-in-law. Xiangzi didn't reach for the bowl but first pulled out his money: "Fourth Master, hold this for me, thirty dollars." He put the small change back in his pocket.

Liu Siye raised an eyebrow and asked, "Where'd you get it?"

While eating, Xiangzi recounted how he'd been taken by the soldiers.

"Hmph, you fool!" Liu Siye shook his head after hearing the story. "If you'd brought them into town and sold them to the stew pot, each one would fetch over ten bucks; if it were winter with full camel fur, three of them would sell for sixty bucks!"

Xiangzi had already been feeling regret, and hearing this made him even more upset. But then, thinking it over, selling three live animals to the stew pot for slaughter was a bit cruel; he and the camels had both escaped, so they all deserved to live. He said nothing, and his heart calmed down.

Miss Hu cleared away the dishes, and Liu Siye, tilting his head back, seemed to recall something. Suddenly he smiled, revealing two tiger-like teeth that grew firmer with age: "Fool, you said you fell ill in Haidian? Why didn't you come straight back via Huangcun Avenue?"

"I took the long way around the Western Hills, afraid that if I took the main road I'd be caught, in case the villagers came to their senses and took me for a deserter!"

Liu Siye smirked, his eyes darting inward a couple of times. He worried Xiangzi's story might be false—what if the thirty dollars were stolen? He wouldn't want to hold stolen goods for someone. In his youth, he'd done plenty of lawless things; now, claiming to have reformed, he had to be careful and knew how to be so. Xiangzi's story had only this one flaw, but he explained it without a trace of panic, and the old man relaxed.

"What to do now?" The old man pointed at the money.

"Up to you!"

"Buy another cart?" The old man showed his tiger teeth again, as if to say: "Buy your own cart, and still live in my place for free?"

"Not enough! If I buy, it has to be new!" Xiangzi didn't look at Liu Siye's teeth, just focused on his own heart.

"Lend it to you? One percent interest—others pay two and a half!"

Xiangzi shook his head.

"Better to pay me one percent than to sign a note with a cart shop!"

"I won't sign any notes either," Xiangzi said thoughtfully. "I'll save slowly, and when I have enough, I'll pay cash for it!"

The old man looked at Xiangzi as if he were some strange character—annoying, but not worth getting angry over. After a moment, he picked up the money: "Thirty? Don't play tricks!"

"That's right!" Xiangzi stood up. "Going to bed. Here's a pack of matches for you, old man!" He set a pack of matches on the table, then hesitated: "Don't tell anyone about the camels!"

Five

Old Liu indeed didn't spread the word for Xiangzi, but the story of the camels quickly spread from Haidian into the city. Before, though no one could find fault with Xiangzi, his stubbornness made them think he was somewhat unsociable and difficult. Since "Camel Xiangzi" became known, though he still kept his head down and worked, not being very friendly, people began to see him differently. Some said he'd found a gold watch, others said he'd come into three hundred silver dollars; those who claimed to know the most details nodded and said he'd pulled thirty camels back from the Western Hills! Though the versions differed, the conclusion was the same—Xiangzi had struck ill-gotten wealth! For someone who came into such wealth, no matter how much of a "social outcast" he was, people would still show respect as a rule. Since earning money through hard work was so tough, everyone hoped for some unexpected fortune. And since such fortune was so rare, anyone with a bit of luck was bound to be extraordinary, blessed with great fate. Thus, Xiangzi's silence and unsociability transformed into the "reserve of a man of worth." He deserved it, and they ought to flock to curry favor with him. "Come on, Xiangzi! Tell us, tell us how you made your fortune!" Xiangzi heard this every day. He said nothing. Only when pushed to the point where the scar on his face reddened did he say, "Fortune? Damn it, where's my cart?"

Yes, that was true—where had his cart gone? People began to think. But worrying for others is never as good as celebrating for them, so they forgot about Xiangzi's cart and dwelled on his good luck. After a few days, seeing that Xiangzi was still pulling a cart and hadn't changed trades or bought property, they cooled off a bit, and when mentioning "Camel Xiangzi," no longer pressed why he was specifically "the camel," as if that was just what he should be called.

But Xiangzi himself didn't let the matter slip away lightly. He longed to buy a new cart immediately, and the more anxious he became, the more he thought about the old one. He worked tirelessly from dawn to dusk, but as he worked, he'd remember that incident. Recalling it made him feel stifled, and he couldn't help but think: what was the use of being strong-willed? The world wasn't any fairer because of his resolve; by what right had his cart been snatched away? Even if he got another one right away, who's to say it wouldn't happen again? The past felt like a nightmare, making him barely dare to hope for the future. Sometimes, seeing others drink, smoke, or visit brothels, he almost felt envious. If being strong-willed was useless, why not enjoy the present? They were right. Even if he didn't go to brothels, he should at least have a couple of drinks and take it easy. Smoking and drinking now seemed to have a special allure; he felt these things cost little and would surely comfort him, letting him keep slogging forward while forgetting past pain.

Yet, he still dared not touch them. He had to save every extra cent he could; only this way could he buy his own cart early. Even if he bought it today and lost it tomorrow, he had to buy it. This was his ambition, his hope, even his religion. Without pulling his own cart, he felt he'd lived in vain. He never dreamed of becoming an official, getting rich, or buying property. His only ability was pulling a cart; his most reliable hope was owning one; not buying a cart would mean failing himself. He pondered this day and night, counting his money; if he ever forgot about it, he forgot himself and felt like nothing but a running beast, devoid of improvement or humanity. No matter how good the rented cart, pulling it always felt lackluster, as unnatural as carrying a stone. Even with a rented cart, he didn't slack off, always keeping it spotless and never crashing into things; but this was just caution, not joy. Yes, cleaning his own cart, like counting his own money, was true happiness. He still abstained from smoking and drinking, and even refused good tea. In the teahouse, a respectable rickshaw puller like him, after a fast run, would treat himself to ten-cent tea with two packs of sugar to restore energy and cool down. When he ran until sweat dripped from his ears and his chest felt raw, he really wanted to do this; it wasn't habit or show—he genuinely needed two bowls of tea to settle himself. But he only thought about it; he still drank the cheap, broken leaves. Sometimes he wanted to scold himself for suffering so much; but for a puller to save a bit each month, how else could it be done? He steeled himself. Get the cart first, get the cart first! Having the cart would make up for everything!

He was just as tight-fisted with spending as he was relentless with earning. Without monthly contracts, he pulled all day, leaving early and returning late; he wouldn't stop until he'd earned a set amount, regardless of time or his legs. Sometimes he pushed on for a whole day and night. In the past, he wouldn't snatch fares from others, especially the old and weak; with his physique and his cart, if he competed with them for seats, what chance would they have? Now, he didn't care much about that. He only saw money—every extra cent counted—no matter the hardship of the job or who he was competing with; he just took the fare and ran, like a starving beast. Running gave him some comfort, feeling that only by never stopping could he hope to buy a cart. Gradually, the reputation of "Camel Xiangzi" far outshone that of plain Xiangzi. Many times, he'd snatch a fare and run off, followed by a chorus of curses. He didn't retort, just ran with his head down, thinking: "If it weren't for buying a cart, I'd never be this shameless!" It was as if he were silently asking for forgiveness, unwilling to say it outright. At the cart stands or in teahouses, seeing others glare at him, he wanted to explain; but seeing their coldness, and given he never drank, gambled, played chess, or chatted with them, his words stayed bottled up. Embarrassment turned to resentment, and his temper flared; they glared, and he glared back. Remembering how they'd respected him when he first escaped from the mountains, now being so looked down upon made him feel worse. Alone, holding a pot of tea in a teahouse or counting his newly earned coppers at a cart stand, he forced his anger down. He didn't want to fight, though he wasn't afraid to. The others weren't afraid either, but picking a fight with Xiangzi was worth thinking about—none could match him, and ganging up on one was hardly honorable. Struggling to suppress his temper, he could think of no other way but to endure until he bought his cart. With his own cart, he wouldn't have to worry daily about rent, and he could be gracious, no longer offending people by snatching fares. Thinking this, he glanced at them, as if to say: "Just wait and see!"

For himself, he shouldn't have pushed so hard. After escaping back to town, he hadn't waited to fully recover before pulling the cart again; though he showed no weakness, he often felt tired. But he dared not rest, thinking that more sweat would ease his aches. As for food, he didn't dare skimp, but neither could he afford much good stuff. He noticed he'd lost a lot of weight, but his frame was still tall and his bones still strong, so he relaxed. He always thought that being taller and bigger meant he could endure more hardship, never considering that a larger body, bearing more strain, needed more nourishment. Tigress had warned him several times: "If you keep this up, coughing up blood will be your own problem!"

He knew it was good advice, but with things going badly and poor health, he was irritable. Glaring slightly, he said: "If I don't push, when will I ever buy a cart?"

If anyone else had glared like that, Tigress would have cursed for half a day; but with Xiangzi, she was exceptionally polite and caring. She just pursed her lips: "You've got to pace yourself when buying a cart—think you're made of iron? You should rest a full three days!" Seeing Xiangzi wouldn't listen: "Fine, have it your way, but don't blame me if you die!"

Liu Siye also disapproved of Xiangzi: his relentless pace, leaving early and returning late, was bad for the cart. Even though renting a cart for a full day had no time limits—you could go out and return anytime you liked—if everyone worked like Xiangzi, a cart would wear out at least half a year early; nothing, no matter how sturdy, could withstand constant heavy use! Besides, Xiangzi was so focused on pushing that he barely had time to help clean the cart and such, another loss. The old man was a bit displeased. But he said nothing; pulling all day with no set time was the norm; helping clean the cart was a favor, not an obligation. Given his standing, he couldn't make a fuss with Xiangzi. He could only show a hint of displeasure from the corner of his eye, keeping his mouth tightly shut. Sometimes he really wanted to kick Xiangzi out; but looking at his daughter, he didn't dare. He had no intention of making Xiangzi a son-in-law, but since his daughter liked this brash young man, he didn't want to meddle. He had only this one daughter; with little hope of her marrying, he couldn't drive away her friend. Truth be told, Tigress was so useful that he really didn't want her to marry; this selfishness made him feel a bit guilty toward her, and thus somewhat afraid of her. The old man, who had feared nothing all his life, grew afraid of his own daughter in old age; feeling a bit embarrassed, he reasoned: if he feared someone, it proved he wasn't entirely lawless. With this fact, perhaps he wouldn't face bad karma at death. So, he admitted he should fear his daughter and didn't drive Xiangzi out. This didn't mean he'd let his daughter do as she pleased, even marry Xiangzi. No. He saw that she might have that intention, but not necessarily.But Xiangzi didn't dare to curry favor with her.  

He figured he'd just be more careful—there was no point in provoking his daughter first. Xiangzi didn't pay any attention to the old man's expression; he had no time to bother with such idle matters. Even if he had thoughts of leaving Harmony Yard, it wasn't out of spite, but because he was hoping to get a monthly contract. He was already starting to tire of picking up individual fares. For one thing, it made him look bad competing for customers, and for another, his daily income was never steady—more one day, less the next, so he couldn't predict when he'd scrape together enough to buy a rickshaw. He wanted some certainty in his mind. Even if the leftover amount was small, as long as he could count on saving a fixed sum each month, he'd feel hope and be able to relax. He was the type who liked things to fit neatly into place, one carrot to one hole.  

He took on a monthly contract. Hmph, it was just as frustrating as picking up fares! This time it was at the Yang residence. Mr. Yang was from Shanghai, Mrs. Yang from Tianjin, and the second Mrs. Yang from Suzhou. One master and two mistresses, with all sorts of accents, had produced who knows how many children. On his very first day, Xiangzi nearly lost his mind. Early in the morning, the first wife took the rickshaw to the market to buy groceries. When she came back, they split up to send the young masters and misses to school—some to middle school, some to elementary, some to kindergarten. Different schools, different ages, different looks, but all equally annoying, especially when they were in the rickshaw; the most well-behaved of them still had two more hands than a monkey. Once the children were all sent off, Mr. Yang went to his government office. Xiangzi rushed him there, then hurried back to take the second wife to Dong'an Market or to visit relatives. After that, he picked up the children for lunch. When they finished eating, he sent them off again. After dropping off the students, Xiangzi thought he could finally eat, but the first wife, in her Tianjin drawl, told him to go carry water. Sweet water for the Yang household was delivered, but the bitter water for laundry had to be fetched by the rickshaw puller. This task wasn't part of the deal, but Xiangzi, to keep things going, didn't argue. Without a word, he filled the vat. Setting down the buckets, he was about to grab his bowl when the second wife told him to go buy something. The first and second wives were never on good terms, but when it came to household management, they agreed on one thing: never let a servant rest for a moment. Another thing: they couldn't stand watching a servant eat. Xiangzi didn't know this. He thought it was just his first day and things happened to be busy, so he said nothing and bought himself a few sesame cakes with his own money. He loved money like his own life, but to keep the job, he had to steel himself.  

When he came back from shopping, the first wife told him to sweep the yard. Mr. Yang, the first wife, and the second wife all dressed up beautifully when they went out, but the house and yard were like a giant garbage dump. Xiangzi felt sick just looking at the yard, so he focused on sweeping it, forgetting that a rickshaw puller wasn't supposed to do odd jobs. Once the yard was clean, the second wife told him to sweep the house too while he was at it. Xiangzi didn't refuse. What amazed him was how two such elegant, well-dressed ladies could let their house get so filthy you couldn't even step inside! After he tidied up the rooms, the second wife handed him a grubby little one-year-old. He was at a loss. He knew all about hard labor, but he'd never held a baby before. He cradled this little master in both hands—if he didn't hold tight, the child might slip; if he held too hard, he might hurt its delicate bones. He broke out in a sweat. He thought about handing the precious bundle to Mama Zhang, a big-footed woman from north of the river. But as soon as he found her, she cursed him out thoroughly. The Yang household changed servants every three to five days. The master and mistresses always treated servants like family slaves, thinking they had to work the poor to death to justify their wages. Only Mama Zhang had stayed for five or six years, because she dared to curse anyone out—whether it was the master or the mistress, if they angered her, she'd let them have it. With Mr. Yang's venomous Shanghai-style cursing, Mrs. Yang's robust Tianjin dialect, and the second wife's fluent Suzhou tone, they were usually invincible. But when they met Mama Zhang's brute force, they started to feel a sense of mutual respect, like heroes facing off against champions. So they quite appreciated her and took her into their inner circle.  

Xiangzi grew up in the northern countryside, where casual cursing was the worst taboo. But he didn't dare hit Mama Zhang, because a good man doesn't fight a woman, and he didn't want to trade insults either. He just glared at her. Mama Zhang fell silent, as if she sensed some danger. Just then, the first wife called Xiangzi to pick up the children. He quickly handed the muddy little brat back to the second wife, who thought he was deliberately disrespecting her and blurted out a string of curses. The first wife was also unhappy that Xiangzi had held the child for the second wife, so when she heard the second wife cursing him, she joined in with her shrill voice, also cursing him. Xiangzi became the target of all their abuse. He hastily pulled the rickshaw out and left, almost forgetting to get angry because he'd never seen anything like this before. Suddenly caught in such a mess, he was completely bewildered.  

He brought the children back in batches. The yard was noisier than a market. Three women's scolding and a crowd of children's crying made it as chaotic as Dazhalan during a show's intermission, and just as senselessly confusing. Luckily, he still had to pick up Mr. Yang, so he rushed out again. The street, with its people shouting and horses neighing, was somehow more bearable than the chaos in the house.  

It wasn't until twelve o'clock that Xiangzi finally had a moment to catch his breath. He wasn't just physically exhausted; his head kept buzzing. The Yang family, old and young, had all gone to sleep, but in his ears he could still hear the master and mistresses yelling, like three different phonographs spinning wildly in his mind, making him restless. He couldn't think anymore; he just wanted to sleep. But as soon as he entered his little room, his heart sank, and he wasn't sleepy anymore. It was a gatehouse with two doors, separated by a wooden partition. Mama Zhang lived on one side, he on the other. The room had no lamp, only a small window about two feet wide on the street wall, just under a streetlight, giving a little light. The room was damp and foul, with dust an inch thick. Against the wall was a plank bed, nothing else. He felt the bed board and realized that if he put his head down, he'd have to brace his feet against the wall; if he stretched his legs out flat, he'd have to sit up halfway. He couldn't sleep in a curled-up position. After thinking it over, he pulled the plank bed diagonally, so that the two ends were angled like rhinoceros horns. That way, he could lay his head flat and let his legs dangle, just to get through one night.  

He brought his bedding in from the hallway, spread it out carelessly, and lay down. His legs were dangling, which wasn't comfortable, and he couldn't sleep. He forced his eyes shut and comforted himself: Sleep, you have to get up early tomorrow! I've endured all kinds of hardship, why can't I bear this one? Never mind that the food is bad and the work is heavy—maybe there'll be mahjong games, banquets, dinner parties. Why did we come out here, Xiangzi? Wasn't it for the money? As long as you bring in more money, you've got to put up with anything! Thinking like this, he felt much better. He sniffed the room; it didn't smell as bad as before. Slowly, he drifted off to sleep. He hazily felt bedbugs but didn't bother to go after them.  

After two days, Xiangzi's heart had completely cooled. But on the fourth day, a female guest arrived, and Mama Zhang was busy setting up a mahjong table. His heart, frozen solid like a small lake, suddenly felt a spring breeze. Once the ladies started playing mahjong, they handed all the children over to the servants. Since Mama Zhang had to attend to the cigarettes, tea, and towels, the little monkeys fell entirely under Xiangzi's charge. He hated those little rascals, but he sneaked a look into the room. The first wife was handling the kitty, looking very serious. He thought to himself: This old lady might be tough, but maybe she's not stupid. She knows to use this chance to let the servants pocket a few extra coins. So he treated the little monkeys with special patience. For the sake of the kitty, he had to treat these little brats like young masters and misses.  

When the mahjong game ended, the first wife told him to take the guests home. The two female guests wanted to leave at the same time, so another rickshaw had to be hired. Xiangzi hailed one. The first wife fumbled all over herself for money, ready to pay the guests' fare. The guests made a polite show of refusal, but the first wife shouted as if fighting for her life: "What's this, dear sister! You're at my place, and you can't let me cover a rickshaw fare? Dear sister! Get in!" Only then did she manage to pull out a dime.  

Xiangzi saw it clearly—when she handed over the dime, the wife's hand trembled a little.  

After seeing off the guests and helping Mama Zhang clean up the mahjong table and such, Xiangzi glanced at the first wife. She told Mama Zhang to go get some hot water. Once Mama Zhang left the room, she pulled out a dime. "Take this. Stop staring at me!"  

Xiangzi's face suddenly turned purple. He straightened his back, as if his head might hit the ceiling beam, grabbed the bill, and threw it at the wife's fat face. "Give me four days' wages!" "What's gotten into you?" After saying this, she looked at Xiangzi again, said nothing, and handed over his four days' pay. As he carried his bedding out the gate, he heard her unleash a torrent of curses from the courtyard.  

[1] Dinner party, i.e., a banquet.  

VI  

On an early autumn night, with starlight and leaf shadows shifting in the gentle breeze, Xiangzi looked up at the distant Milky Way and sighed. The weather was so cool, and his chest was so broad, but he felt the air wasn't enough; his chest was very tight. He wanted to sit down and have a good cry. With his build, his endurance, and his ambition, to be treated like a dog or a pig, to be unable to hold down a job—he didn't just resent the Yang family. He vaguely felt a sense of hopelessness, afraid that he'd never amount to anything in this life. Carrying his bedroll, he walked slower and slower, as if he were no longer the Xiangzi who could just lift his legs and run ten miles.  

When he reached the main street, there were few pedestrians, but the streetlights were very bright. He felt even more empty and adrift, not knowing where to go. Where to? Naturally, back to Harmony Yard. But that made his heart ache a little. Merchants and laborers didn't fear having no business; they feared getting a customer and then losing the deal, like when a customer walks into a restaurant or barbershop, takes one look, and walks out. Xiangzi knew that quitting and being fired were common. If this place didn't want him, somewhere else would. But he had humbled himself to keep the job, swallowed his pride to buy a rickshaw, and ended up lasting only three and a half days—just like those old hands who'd been through every household. It broke his heart. He almost felt too ashamed to go back to Harmony Yard, where everyone would make fun of him: "Look, Camel Xiangzi couldn't even last three and a half days, huh!"  

If not Harmony Yard, then where? To avoid thinking about it, he headed straight for Xianmen Street. Harmony Yard's front consisted of three shop bays. The middle one served as the office, where rickshaw pullers could come in to settle accounts or discuss matters, but they weren't allowed to wander through freely, because the east and west bays were the bedrooms of Father and Daughter Liu. Next to the west bay was a cart gate with two green painted doors, above which curved a thick iron bar holding an extremely bright, uncovered electric light. Beneath the light hung a horizontal iron plate with gold characters: "Harmony Rickshaw Yard." Pullers coming in and out with their carts, or passing through at any time, used this gate. The dark green paint on the doors, against the gold characters, shone under the white electric light. The carts going in and out were all handsome—black or yellow lacquer, equally glossy and gleaming, with snow-white cushions. Even the pullers felt a touch of pride, as if they were nobles among rickshaw men. Entering the gate and turning past the west bay of the front building, you reached a large square courtyard with an old locust tree in the middle. The east and west rooms were open-faced and used for storing carts. The south rooms and a few small rooms in the back courtyard behind the south building were all dormitories for the pullers.  

It was probably past eleven. Xiangzi saw the overly bright and lonely light of Harmony Yard. The office and the east room had no light, but the west room was still lit. He knew that Tiger Girl hadn't gone to sleep yet. He thought he'd slip in quietly so she wouldn't see him. Precisely because she usually thought highly of him, he didn't want her to be the first to witness his failure.  

Just as he pulled the rickshaw under her window, Tiger Girl came out of the cart gate:  

"Oh, Xiangzi? What—" She was about to ask more, but seeing his dejected look and the bedroll on the cart, she swallowed her words.  

What he feared came to pass. Xiangzi's shame and frustration condensed into a lump. He stopped in his tracks and stood there dumbfounded. He couldn't speak, just stared foolishly at Tiger Girl. She looked different tonight. Whether it was the electric light or because she'd put on powder, her face was much paler than usual. The paleness softened some of her fierceness. She'd clearly put on some rouge on her lips, which gave her a touch of charm. Xiangzi found this very strange and felt even more flustered. Because he'd never thought of her as a woman before, suddenly seeing those red lips made him feel a bit embarrassed. She was wearing a short, light-green silk padded jacket and loose, dark-blue cotton trousers. The green jacket, under the electric light, gave off a soft, slightly melancholic silk sheen. Because it was short, it revealed a little of her white trouser waist, making the green more striking and plain. The loose black trousers fluttered slightly in the breeze, like some sinister air trying to escape the glaring light and merge with the darkness. Xiangzi dared not look anymore. He blankly lowered his head, but the image of that small, glowing green jacket lingered in his mind. Given the Liu family's wealth, she could have worn silks and satins every day. But spending her days dealing with rickshaw pullers, she always wore plain cotton clothes. Even if there were some patterns, they were inconspicuous on the cotton fabric.Xiangzi felt as if he were seeing something utterly new and strange, both familiar yet novel, leaving him a bit unsettled.

Already troubled at heart, encountering this fresh, living thing under the harsh light left him at a loss. Unwilling to move himself, he almost wished Miss Tigress would hurry inside or order him to do something—anything but this torment, a nameless, unbearable agony.

"Hey!" she stepped closer, her voice low. "Don't just stand there! Go put the cart away and come right back. I have something to tell you. See you inside."

Having long been used to helping her with errands, he had no choice but to obey. But today she was different from usual, and he felt like thinking it over. Standing there frozen in thought felt awkward too. At a loss, he pulled the cart inside. Glancing at the southern room, he saw no light—probably everyone was asleep, or maybe there were still carts not put away. After stowing the cart, he turned back to her door. Suddenly, his heart began to race.

"Come in! I have something to tell you!" She poked her head out, half-smiling, half-scolding. He walked in slowly.

On the table were a few not-quite-ripe pears, their skins still greenish. A wine pot, three white porcelain cups. A large platter with half a soy-sauce chicken, along with some smoked liver and seasoned tripe.

"Look," Miss Tigress pointed him to a chair and waited until he sat down before saying, "Look, I'm treating myself today. You have some too!" As she spoke, she poured him a cup of wine. The pungent scent of strong liquor mixed with the smoky meat, heavy and thick. "Drink up, have some of this chicken. I've already eaten, no need to fuss! I just did a fortune-telling with dominoes and knew you'd be coming back—was I right or what?"

"I don't drink!" Xiangzi stared blankly at the cup.

"Don't drink? Then get out. I'm being nice, and you don't appreciate it? You stupid camel! It won't burn you to death! Even I can down four ounces. Don't believe me? Watch!" She raised the cup, gulped down most of it, shut her eyes, and let out a hah. Holding the cup out: "Drink! Or I'll grab you by the ear and pour it down your throat!"

Xiangzi was filled with resentment, nowhere to vent it. Faced with this taunting, he really wanted to glare back at her. But he knew Miss Tigress had always treated him well, and she was straightforward with everyone—he shouldn't offend her. Since he didn't want to offend her, he thought he might as well pour out his grievances. He usually wasn't much of a talker, but today it seemed a thousand words were bottled up inside him, and he had to speak them to feel better. With that thought, he felt she wasn't mocking him but rather showing honest concern. He took the cup and drained it. A burning heat slowly, precisely, and forcefully went down his throat. He stretched his neck, straightened his chest, and let out two awkward burps.

Tigress laughed. He barely managed to swallow the liquor, and hearing her laugh, he quickly glanced toward the eastern room.

"No one's there," she said, stifling her laughter, though the smile lingered on her face. "The old man went to celebrate Auntie's birthday—he'll be gone two or three days. Auntie lives in Nanyuan." As she spoke, she refilled his cup.

Hearing this, his mind took a turn, sensing something off somewhere. At the same time, he couldn't bring himself to leave. Her face was so close to his, her clothes so clean and smooth, her lips so red—all of it gave him a fresh jolt. She was still old and ugly, but she had more energy than usual, as if she had suddenly become someone else—still her, but with something extra added. He didn't dare dwell on this new thing, couldn't readily accept it, yet couldn't bear to refuse it. His face flushed. As if to bolster his courage, he took another drink. He had meant to pour out his grievances earlier, but now he'd forgotten. Red-faced, he couldn't help stealing a few more glances at her. The more he looked, the more confused he became; she increasingly revealed something he couldn't grasp, a hot, forceful something transmitted to him, gradually turning her into an abstract thing. He warned himself to be careful, but he also wanted to be bold. After downing three cups of wine, he forgot what caution meant. Gazing at her in a daze, he inexplicably felt exhilarated and daring, fiercely eager to seize some new experience and pleasure. Ordinarily, he was a bit afraid of her; now, there was nothing fearsome about her at all. Instead, he felt authoritative and strong, as if he could take her like a cat and hold her in his hands.

The light in the room was extinguished. The sky was very dark. Now and then, one or two stars pierced the Milky Way or streaked into the darkness, trailing red or white tails, light or stiff, falling straight or sweeping sideways, sometimes flickering and trembling, stirring some heat and light in the sky, flaring brief bursts into the dark. Sometimes one or two stars, sometimes several, fell together, making the quiet autumn sky quiver, throwing the myriad stars into momentary confusion. Sometimes a single giant star shot across the corner of the sky, its tail very long, radiating sparks; red, then yellow; in its final thrust, it suddenly brightened the sky's edge with a streak of white, as if tearing through layers of darkness, letting in a lingering milky light. When the afterglow faded, the darkness seemed to waver a few times before closing in again; the lazy, quiet stars resumed their places, smiling in the autumn wind. On the ground, autumn fireflies seeking mates flitted about, playing the same game as the stars.

The next day, Xiangzi got up very early, harnessed his cart, and went out. His head and throat ached—his first time drinking, but he paid it no mind. Sitting at the mouth of a small alley, the morning breeze brushed his head, and he knew this headache would soon pass. But there were other things on his mind, weighing him down, and no easy way to shake them off. The events of last night made him suspicious, ashamed, uneasy, and vaguely dangerous.

He didn't understand what was going on with Miss Tigress. She was no longer a virgin—Xiangzi had only learned that a few hours ago. He had always respected her and never heard of any impropriety; though she was casual and straightforward with everyone, no one gossiped about her behind her back. Even if some rickshaw men spoke ill of her, it was only about her being tough, nothing else. So why had last night happened?

This left him confused, and Xiangzi began to doubt the whole affair. She knew he wasn't at the rickshaw yard—how could she have been waiting for him so single-mindedly? If it could be anyone... Xiangzi lowered his head. Coming from the countryside, he had never really thought about marriage, but he had his plans: if he owned his own cart, lived more comfortably, and wanted to marry, he would go back to the countryside and find a young, strong, hardworking girl who could wash and cook. Guys his age, even if supervised, all sneaked off to "white houses" [3]—Xiangzi never went along. One, he prided himself on being ambitious and wouldn't waste money on women; two, he had seen with his own eyes those fools throwing away money—some only eighteen or nineteen—standing in the latrine, heads against the wall, unable to pee. Finally, he had to behave properly to be worthy of a future wife, because if he married, it had to be a pure, spotless girl, so he had to be aboveboard himself. But now, now... thinking of Tigress, if looked at as a friend, she was decent enough; as a woman, she was ugly, old, tough, shameless! Even remembering the soldiers who stole his cart and nearly killed him didn't make him hate anyone as much as he hated her! She had destroyed the fresh, cool edge he brought from the countryside, and now he was a man who'd fooled around with a woman!

And if this got out, what if Liu the Fourth found out? Did Liu the Fourth know his daughter was damaged goods? If he didn't, wouldn't Xiangzi be stuck carrying the blame alone? If he knew all along and didn't bother to control her, what kind of people were father and daughter? And mixing with such people, what was he? Even if both of them were willing, he couldn't take her—whether Old Man Liu had sixty carts or six hundred or six thousand! He had to leave Harmony Yard immediately, cut ties with them once and for all. Xiangzi had his own skills—by his own ability, he would buy a cart and get a wife, and that would be honorable! Thinking this, he lifted his head, feeling like a real man, with nothing to fear, nothing to worry about—as long as he worked hard, he would surely succeed.

Twice he was hailed for a ride, but neither panned out. That awkward feeling suddenly returned. He didn't want to think anymore, but his heart was stifled. This affair seemed different from everything else; even if there was a solution, it wouldn't be easy to forget. Something seemed to stick to him, and a black spot had lodged in his heart that could never be washed away. No matter how much he resented her, how much he loathed her, she seemed to grip his heart; the more he tried not to think of her, the more she suddenly leapt out of his mind—a naked her, offering all her ugliness and beauty at once, like buying a pile of junk where among the scrap metal there might be a few shiny trinkets you couldn't bear to refuse. He had never been this close to anyone before; though it came suddenly, though it was a trick or temptation, such a bond couldn't be easily forgotten. Even if he tried to set it aside, it naturally coiled in his heart, as if rooted. For him, this was not just an experience but an indescribable turmoil, leaving him at a loss. He had no solution for her, for himself, for the present or the future—like a little insect caught in a spider's web, too late to struggle.

He made a few trips in a daze. Even while running, he couldn't shake the thought. Not in a clear, orderly way, but a meaning, a taste, a feeling would pop up, vague yet familiar. He wanted to go drink alone, get completely blotto, find some relief—he couldn't take this torment anymore! But he dared not. He couldn't ruin himself over this. He thought again about buying a cart. But he couldn't focus; something always blocked his mind. Before he got to the cart, that thing would sneak out and occupy his heart, like a black cloud covering the sun, cutting off the light. By evening, when he planned to stow his cart, he felt even worse. He had to go back to the yard, but he was terrified of returning. What if he ran into her? He pulled the empty cart around the streets, twice coming near the yard, only to turn away again, like a kid playing hooky for the first time, afraid to go home. Strangely, the more he tried to avoid her, the more he wanted to run into her. The darker it got, the stronger the urge. A reckless, tempting thought—knowing it was wrong but wanting to try—tightly gripped his heart. As a kid, poking a hornet's nest with a pole was like this: scared, but heart thumping to try, as if some evil force drove him. Vaguely, he felt a force stronger than himself, kneading him into a ball and hurling him into a blazing fire; he couldn't stop his own advance.

He circled back to Xianmen Gate. This time, he decided not to hesitate, to go straight to find her. She was no longer anyone in particular; she was just a woman. His whole body grew hot. Just as he reached the gate, a man in his forties came walking under the lamplight. Xiangzi seemed to recognize his face and manner but didn't dare greet him. Almost instinctively, he said, "Need a ride?" The man paused, startled: "Xiangzi?" "Yes," Xiangzi smiled. "Mr. Cao?"

Mr. Cao smiled and nodded. "I say, Xiangzi, if you're not tied up at any mansion, why not come work for me again? The man I have now is too lazy—he never cleans the cart, even though he's fast [4]. Are you interested?"

"Of course I am, sir!" Xiangzi seemed to have forgotten how to smile, wiping his face constantly with a small towel. "Sir, when do I start?"

"Well," Mr. Cao thought for a moment, "the day after tomorrow."

"Yes, sir!" Xiangzi thought too: "Sir, shall I take you back?" "No need. I went to Shanghai for a while. When I came back, I moved. Now I live on North Long Street. I'm out for a walk tonight. See you the day after tomorrow." Mr. Cao told Xiangzi the house number and added, "Still using my own cart."

Xiangzi felt so happy he could fly; the troubles of the past few days were wiped away, like a white stone road washed by heavy rain. Mr. Cao was his old master—though they hadn't been together long, they got along famously. Mr. Cao was a very gentle man, with a small family: just a wife and a little boy. Xiangzi pulled his cart straight to Harmony Yard. The light in Miss Tigress's room was still on. Seeing this light, Xiangzi suddenly froze.

After standing a long time, he decided to go in, tell her he found a monthly job, pay back the two days' fee, withdraw his savings, and cut ties completely—naturally, he wouldn't say it directly, but she'd understand.

He went in, stowed the cart first, then came back and boldly called out, "Miss Liu."

"Come in!"He pushed open the door, and found her lying diagonally on the bed, dressed in her usual clothes, barefoot. Still reclining, she said, "Well? Got a taste of it, have you?"

Xiangzi's face turned as red as an egg given as a gift when a child is born. After a long pause, he stammered, "I've found another job, starting the day after tomorrow. The man has his own rickshaw..."

She cut him off: "You kid just don't know what's good for you!" She sat up, half laughing, half annoyed, pointing at him: "You've got food here, you've got clothes here; is it that you can't be happy unless you go out and sweat like a dog? The old man can't control me, and I'm not about to spend my whole life as a widow! Even if the old man really digs in his heels, I've got some savings of my own, and between us we could get two or three rickshaws, pocket a dollar or eighty cents a day — isn't that better than you running your legs off on the streets all day? What's wrong with me? Except that I'm a bit older than you, and not by much! But I can protect you, care for you!"

"I want to pull a rickshaw!" Xiangzi could find no other retort.

"Stubborn blockhead! Sit down first, I won't bite you!" After she finished speaking, she laughed, revealing a pair of fangs.

Xiangzi sat down, his veins bulging. "What about my money?"

"It's in the old man's hands; it won't get lost, don't be scared; and don't you go asking him for it, you know what he's like? When there's enough to buy a rickshaw, you can ask, and not a single cent will be short; ask for it now, and he'll curse your soul right out of you! He's been good to you! It won't get lost; if it's short a cent, I'll pay you back two! You country bumpkin! Don't make me tear into you!"

Xiangzi had nothing more to say. He hung his head for a long time, fumbled out two days' worth of rickshaw rent, and placed it on the table: "Two days." Then, as an afterthought: "Today counts as turning in the rickshaw; tomorrow I'll take a day off." He didn't really want to take a day off at all; but this way, things seemed more clean-cut; once the rickshaw was returned, he would never stay at Harmony Yard again.

Miss Hu came over, grabbed the money, and stuffed it into his coat pocket: "These past two days, rickshaw and man have been a freebie! You lucky kid! Just don't be ungrateful!" After saying this, she turned around and locked the door from the inside.

[1]

 主儿, meaning person. Here it refers to the owner of a private rickshaw.

[2]

 吹, meaning to break up, to be finished.

[3]

 白房子, the lowest class of brothel.

[4]

 麻利, meaning quick, fast.

Seven  

Xiangzi arrived at Mr. Cao's house.  

He felt a bit ashamed when it came to Tiger Girl. But since the affair had been initiated by her seduction, and besides, he had no designs on her money, he figured that cutting ties with her once and for all wouldn't be too unjust. What really worried him was the money Liu Fourth still held. If he went to ask for it right away, the old man might get suspicious. If he never went to see the father and daughter again, Tiger Girl might get angry, badmouth him to her father, and "gobble up" that money. If he kept letting the old man hold onto it, he'd run into her whenever he went to Harmony Yard, which would be awkward. He couldn't think of a good solution, and the more clueless he felt, the more uneasy he grew.  

He was tempted to ask Mr. Cao for advice, but how could he explain it? That business with Tiger Girl was something he couldn't tell anyone. Thinking it over, he really regretted it. He was starting to realize that this wasn't something he could just cut off cleanly. This kind of thing could never be washed away, like a dark stain on flesh. Losing his rickshaw for no reason, and then this entanglement coming out of nowhere—he felt his whole life was probably finished. No matter how hard he tried, it was all for nothing. After mulling it over, he saw one thing: in the end, he might have to swallow his pride and take Tiger Girl. Not for her sake, but for those rickshaws, right? "The cuckold gets to eat a couple of fried meat dishes!" He couldn't stand it, but when the time came, he might have no choice! He'd just have to keep forging ahead, doing the best he could while bracing for the worst. He no longer dared to be as confident as before. His height, his strength, his spirit—none of it mattered anymore. His life was his own, but others controlled it—controlled by the most despicable things.  

Logically, he should have been pleased, because Mr. Cao's house was the most likable of all the mansions he'd worked in. The pay at Mr. Cao's wasn't any higher than elsewhere, and aside from holiday bonuses, there wasn't much extra cash. But Mr. and Mrs. Cao were both very kind and treated everyone like a human being. Xiangzi wanted to earn more money, to work like mad for it, but he also wanted a decent place to live and enough food to fill his belly. Mr. Cao's place was clean everywhere, even the servants' quarters; the food wasn't harsh, and they never gave the help spoiled things to eat. He had a roomy space of his own and could enjoy three meals in peace, and on top of that, the masters were courteous. Even Xiangzi couldn't focus solely on money. Besides, with good food and lodging and work that wasn't tiring, building up his health wasn't a loss. If he had to pay for his own meals, he'd never eat this well. Now that there was ready-made food that actually stayed down, why wouldn't he eat his fill? Food cost money too, and he figured that clearly. Besides, although the Cao family didn't gamble or entertain often, leaving little spare change, any odd jobs he did could still net him a dime or two. For instance, when the mistress sent him to buy medicine pills for the child, she'd always give him an extra dime for rickshaw fare, even though she knew he could run faster than anyone. That little bit of money didn't amount to much, but it made him feel a kind of human warmth, a consideration, that put his heart at ease. Xiangzi had met plenty of masters in his time—nine out of ten would delay paying him a day's wages as long as possible, as if they'd prefer free labor, treating servants like cats or dogs, or worse. The Cao household was an exception, which was why he liked it here. He'd tidy the yard and water the flowers without waiting to be told, and whenever they saw him at it, they'd say something nice. At such times, they'd also dig out some old things for him to trade for matches, even though those things were still usable, and he'd end up keeping them. Here, he felt a bit of human dignity.  

In Xiangzi's eyes, Liu Fourth could be considered a Huang Tianba. Fierce as he was, he cared about face and reputation, and never played both sides. The only other figure of stature in his mind, besides Huang Tianba, was Confucius. He had no idea what Confucius was really like, except that he supposedly knew a lot of characters and was quite reasonable. Among the mansions he'd worked in, there were both scholarly and martial types. Among the martial ones, not a single one could match Liu Fourth; among the scholarly, though there were teachers at universities and officials with good posts in the yamen who certainly knew plenty of characters, he hadn't met a single reasonable one. Even if the master was reasonable, the mistress and young ladies were hard to please. Only Mr. Cao knew his characters, was reasonable, and Mrs. Cao was proper and winning. So Mr. Cao must be Confucius—if Xiangzi couldn't picture what Confucius looked like, then he must surely resemble Mr. Cao, whether Confucius liked it or not.  

In truth, Mr. Cao wasn't anything special. He was just a middle-class person who sometimes taught and sometimes did other things. He considered himself a "socialist" and also an aesthete, somewhat influenced by William Morris. He had no deep insights in politics or art, but he had one virtue: whatever little he believed in, he could put into practice in the small events of daily life. He seemed to realize he had no extraordinary talent to accomplish earth-shattering deeds, so he arranged his work and family according to his own ideals. Though it did nothing for society, at least he wanted to match his words with deeds and not come off as a hypocrite. Thus, he paid great attention to small matters, as if to say that as long as his little family was well-ordered, society could go its own way. This sometimes made him ashamed, sometimes pleased him, as if he clearly saw his family as a small oasis in the desert, offering a bit of water and food to those who came, with no greater meaning.  

Xiangzi had stumbled upon this little oasis. After wandering the desert for so many days, he thought it a miracle. He had never met anyone like Mr. Cao before, so he regarded the man as a sage. Perhaps it was due to his lack of experience, or perhaps there weren't many such people in the world. When he pulled Mr. Cao out on the rickshaw, Mr. Cao's clothes were so elegantly plain, his manner so lively and gracious, and Xiangzi himself so clean and tidy, tall and sturdy, that he ran with extra pleasure, as if only he was fit to pull Mr. Cao. At home, everything was so clean, so quiet, that he felt comfortable and settled. When he was in the countryside, he often saw old men sitting silently under the autumn moon or winter sun, puffing on bamboo pipes. Though he was too young to imitate them, he loved watching them sit so still—there must be, he guessed, some kind of flavor to it. Now, though he was in the city, the tranquility of Mr. Cao's place reminded him of the countryside. He truly wished he could take up a pipe and ponder some kind of flavor.  

Unfortunately, that woman and that money kept him from peace of mind. His heart was like a green leaf, wrapped by an insect in silk, ready to form a cocoon. Over this matter, he couldn't relax. With others, even Mr. Cao, he'd often stare blankly and give irrelevant answers. This pained him greatly. The Cao household went to bed early; by nine or ten in the evening, there was nothing to do. He'd sit alone in his room or the yard, turning things over and over in his mind, fixated on those two matters. He even thought of getting married right away, which would surely put an end to Tiger Girl's intentions. But how could pulling a rickshaw support a family? He knew the bitter lot of the guys in the tenement courtyards—men pulling rickshaws, women sewing for pennies, kids picking coal cinders, gnawing on watermelon rinds from trash piles in summer, and lining up at the gruel kitchens in winter. Xiangzi couldn't bear that. Besides, if he got married, the money in Old Liu's hands would surely be lost. Would Tiger Girl let him off easy? He couldn't give up that money—it was earned with his life!  

His own rickshaw had been bought in early autumn last year. Over a year later, he had nothing now but over thirty dollars he couldn't get back, and a mess of entanglements. The more he thought about it, the more miserable he felt.  

It was more than ten days after the Mid-Autumn Festival, and the weather was slowly turning cool. He calculated that he needed to buy a couple of pieces of clothing. More money! If he bought clothes, he couldn't save anything at the same time. The hope of buying a rickshaw—he hardly dared to hope anymore! Even if he kept working as a monthly hire, what kind of life was that for a lifetime?  

One evening, Mr. Cao came back a bit late from the eastern part of the city. To be careful, Xiangzi took the main road past Tiananmen. The road was flat and open, with few people, a light cool breeze, and quiet streetlights. He picked up his pace. The gloom that had been weighing on him for days was momentarily forgotten. Listening to his own footsteps and the soft creak of the rickshaw shafts, he forgot everything. Unbuttoning his jacket, the cool wind whistled against his chest, and he felt exhilarated, as if he could just keep running like this, all the way to some unknown place, dying from the run would be simpler. Faster and faster he went. Whenever there was a rickshaw ahead, he'd overtake it. In no time, he was past Tiananmen. His feet seemed like springs, barely touching the ground before bouncing up. The rear wheel spun so fast the spokes were invisible, the rubber tires seeming to leave the ground, carrying man and rickshaw along like they were swept up by a sudden gust. Mr. Cao, hit by the cool breeze, was probably half-asleep; otherwise, he would have stopped Xiangzi from flying like this. Xiangzi was in full stride, vaguely thinking that a good sweat would let him sleep soundly tonight, without worrying about anything.  

Not far from North Long Street, the northern half of the road was pitch black under the locust trees outside the red wall. Xiangzi was just about to slow down when his foot hit something raised. His foot struck, and the wheel followed. Xiangzi pitched forward. Crack—the rickshaw shaft broke. "What happened?" Mr. Cao tumbled out with his words. Xiangzi didn't make a sound; he got up from the ground. Mr. Cao sat up lightly too. "What happened?"  

A fresh pile of road-repair stones—but no red warning light had been placed.  

"Are you hurt?" Xiangzi asked.  

"No. I'll walk back. You pull the rickshaw." Mr. Cao was still calm, feeling around on the stones to see if anything had fallen.  

Xiangzi felt the broken end of the shaft. "It's not broken off much, sir. Sit back down—I can still pull it!" With that, he yanked the rickshaw free from the stones. "Sit down, sir!"  

Mr. Cao didn't want to ride, but he heard the sob in Xiangzi's voice, so he got back on.  

Under the electric light at the entrance to North Long Street, Mr. Cao saw that the skin on his right hand was scraped off. "Xiangzi, stop!"  

Xiangzi turned his head, his face covered in blood.  

Mr. Cao was frightened and couldn't think of what to say. "You—quick, quick—"  

Xiangzi, confused, thought he was being told to run faster. He tightened his back and sprinted straight home.  

Setting down the rickshaw, he saw blood on Mr. Cao's hand and hurried into the yard to get medicine from the mistress.  

"Never mind me—look at yourself first!" Mr. Cao ran inside. Xiangzi checked himself and began to feel the pain. Both knees and his right elbow were scraped raw. On his cheek, he thought it was sweat, but it was blood. Ignoring everything else, he sat on the stone steps of the gate, staring blankly at the rickshaw with its broken shaft. The brand-new black-lacquered rickshaw had a snapped shaft, leaving two bare white wood splinters sticking out awkwardly. It looked jarring and ugly—like a well-pasted paper doll missing its feet, with two bare sorghum stalks poking out. Xiangzi stared at those two white wood splinters. "Xiangzi!" Gao Ma, the Cao family's maidservant, called out loudly. "Xiangzi! Where are you?"  

He stayed seated, not moving, his eyes fixed on the broken shaft. Those two white wood splinters seemed to pierce straight into his heart.  

"What's the matter with you! Not a word, hiding here—you scared me half to death! The master's calling you!" Gao Ma's speech always mixed events with emotions, making it seem both complicated and touching. She was a widow of thirty-two or thirty-three, clean, quick, with a deft and meticulous hand. Elsewhere, some found her too meddlesome, full of ideas and often a bit crafty. The Cao family liked clean, bright people and didn't pay much attention to minor quibbles  

, so she had been with them for two or three years. Even when the entire Cao family went elsewhere, they always took her along. "The master's calling you!" she repeated. When Xiangzi stood up, she saw the blood on his face. "You scared me to death! Oh my god! What happened? Why aren't you moving? If you get tetanus, that'll be the end! Hurry! The master has medicine!"  

Xiangzi walked ahead, with Gao Ma chattering behind him as they entered the study. Mrs. Cao was there too, bandaging Mr. Cao's hand with medicine. When she saw Xiangzi coming in, she also let out a "Whoa!"  

"Ma'am, he's really taken a bad fall this time," Gao Ma said, afraid the madam wouldn't notice, as she hurried to pour cold water into the basin and kept talking: "I knew it! Whenever he starts running, he doesn't care about his life—sooner or later, something would happen. And sure enough! Why don't you wash up quick? Wash up and put some medicine on, eh!"  

Xiangzi stood still, holding his right elbow. The study was so clean and tasteful, and there he was, a big man covered in blood—so out of place. Everyone seemed to sense something was wrong, even Gao Ma fell silent.  

"Sir," Xiangzi said, head bowed, voice low but firm. "Find someone else, sir! Keep this month's wages for the rickshaw repairs. The shaft is broken, and the left lantern's glass is shattered. The rest is still fine."  

"Wash up first, put on some medicine, and then we'll talk about the rest." Mr. Cao was looking at his hand while the madam slowly wound gauze around it."Wash up first!" Gao Ma had more to say. "The master didn't say anything, so don't you go blaming him first!"

Xiangzi still didn't move. "No need to wash, it'll be fine in a bit! A monthly contract puller, throwing someone off, damaging the cart, no face left to..." His words weren't enough to fully express his meaning, but his emotions had already been vented, leaving him only short of bursting into tears. Quitting his job and forfeiting his wages, in Xiangzi's view, was almost like suicide. But responsibility and face, at this moment, seemed more important than life itself, because the one he'd thrown wasn't just anyone, but Mr. Cao. If he'd thrown that Mrs. Yang, so what? Served her right! With Mrs. Yang, he could put on the streetwise toughness, because she didn't treat him like a person, so he didn't need to be polite. Money was everything; face and rules meant nothing. Mr. Cao wasn't that kind of person at all. He'd have to sacrifice the money to save face. He didn't have time to hate anyone but his own fate. He almost thought: after leaving the Cao household, he'd never pull a rickshaw again. Even if his own life wasn't worth much, he could gamble it away. But what about others' lives? What if he'd actually killed someone? He'd never thought about this before, but because this time he'd injured Mr. Cao, he'd come to realize this truth. Fine, he could forgo the wages, change his line of work from now on, and stop engaging in this life-endangering business. Pulling a rickshaw was his ideal job; giving it up meant abandoning hope. He felt his whole life would just muddle along in mediocrity, without even the hope of becoming a good rickshaw puller. What a waste of his big build! When he was out picking up random fares, he'd unceremoniously "snatch"[2] business, earning him the scolding and mockery of others. But he could forgive himself this shamelessness because it came from his ambition to buy his own rickshaw. But getting into trouble while on a monthly contract? What could he say for himself? If this got out, Xiangzi throwing someone off and damaging a cart—what kind of monthly contract puller was that! Xiangzi had no way out! He couldn't wait for Mr. Cao to fire him; he had to scurry off on his own!

"Xiangzi," Mr. Cao's hand was already bandaged, "go wash up! Don't talk about quitting for now. It wasn't your fault. If someone puts a stone there, they should put a red light. Forget it, go wash up and put some medicine on."

"That's right, sir," Gao Ma chimed in again. "Xiangzi's just embarrassed. Of course, throwing the master like this! But since the master says it's not your fault, don't keep brooding! Look at him, big and strong, acting like a child, truly worried! Say something, ma'am, to put his mind at ease!" Gao Ma's words were like a gramophone record, going around in circles, covering everyone without any clear beginning or end.

"Go wash up quickly, I'm scared!" Mrs. Cao only said this much.

Xiangzi's mind was in a turmoil. Finally hearing the mistress say she was afraid of blood, he felt he'd found something to comfort her. He brought out the washbasin and washed his face a few times at the study door. Gao Ma waited for him inside the door with the medicine bottle.

"What about your arms and legs?" Gao Ma daubed his face with medicine. Xiangzi shook his head, "Never mind!"

The Cao couple went to rest. Gao Ma, holding the medicine bottle, followed Xiangzi out. When they reached his room, she put the bottle down and stood at the doorway: "Put some on yourself later. I say, don't be so stubborn over such a small thing. Back in the day, when my old man was alive, I used to quit jobs often too. First, because I was worn out outside and he was no good, making me angry. Second, because I was young and hot-tempered; if a word didn't sit right, I'd quit! Selling my strength for money doesn't make me a slave; you may have your stinking money, but even a clay figurine has its temper; an old lady can't be expected to tend to everyone! I'm much better now. With the old man dead, I've got nothing to worry about, and my temper's improved a bit. Here—I've been here nearly three years. It's true, I started on the ninth of September—the tips are small, but the people are decent to us. We sell our strength for money; just nice words don't amount to anything. But then again, you have to look at the long run: quitting jobs every few days, taking six months off in a year, that doesn't pay off either. Better to find an easygoing boss, stick with it for a long time; even if the tips are small, you can still save a bit by hanging in there regularly. As for today's incident, since the master didn't say anything, let it go. Why bother? I'm not trying to pull rank, but you're still a young brother, easy to get riled up. No need at all; losing your temper won't fill your belly. Being as honest as you are, settling down here for a while is better than flying all over the place. I'm not taking their side at all; I'm doing this for you, we get along so well!" She paused for breath: "Alright, see you tomorrow. Don't be stubborn; I'm a straightforward person, I say what I mean!"

Xiangzi's right elbow was very painful, and he couldn't sleep all night. After mulling it over back and forth, he felt Gao Ma's words made sense. Everything else was fake; only money was real. Save money for a cart; getting angry won't fill your belly! With this thought, a bit of peaceful sleepiness came over him.

[1] Over the mark, details, small protocols.

[2] Snatching someone else's ongoing business is called "snatching."

When you get tired of reading, remember to take a break~

Public Account: Good Morning Li

E-book search and download

Book list sharing

Book friends learning and exchange

Website: Chenjin Books https://www.chenjin5.com

E-book search and download

E-book package resource sharing

Learning resource sharing eight

Mr. Cao fixed the cart and didn't deduct Xiangzi's wages. Mrs. Cao gave him two pills of "Sanhuang Baola," but he didn't take them. He didn't bring up quitting again. Although he felt quite embarrassed for several days, Gao Ma's words ultimately won out. After some time, life returned to its routine, and he gradually forgot the matter. All his hopes began to sprout anew. Sitting alone in his room, his eyes would gleam as he figured out how to save money and how to buy a cart; he would mutter nonstop under his breath, as if afflicted by some obsession. His calculations were very clumsy, but he'd often chant "six sixes are thirty-six" in his mind and out loud; it had little to do with his actual money, but saying it made him feel more substantial, as if he really had a ledger.

He had considerable admiration for Gao Ma, feeling that this woman had more cunning and capability than most men. Her words struck at the root. He didn't dare seek her out for idle chat, but if he met her in the yard or at the gate and she had time for a few words, he was very willing to listen. Every set of words she spoke gave him food for thought for half a day. So whenever he met her, he'd give a foolish smile, letting her know he admired her words, and she'd feel a bit of pride. Even if she was busy, she'd exchange a few sentences.

However, when it came to handling money, he dared not recklessly follow her advice. Her ideas, he thought, weren't really bad, but they were a bit risky. He was very willing to listen to her, to learn more tricks and broaden his mind. But in practice, he stuck to his old principle—never let go of money easily.

Indeed, Gao Ma had her ways: ever since she became a widow, she'd lend out whatever little money she could save each month. One yuan was a sum, two yuan was a sum, lending to servants, third- or fourth-class police officers, and small-time vendors, charging at least thirty percent interest. These people would often get red-eyed and frantic over a single yuan. Even if someone offered them one yuan but counted it as two, they'd still take it. Otherwise, they'd never see that money; the money they saw was poisoned—taking it would drain their blood—but they had to take it anyway. Anything that could give them a moment's relief, they'd dare to grab; life was just about catching that breath, and tomorrow could take care of itself. Gao Ma, when her husband was alive, had suffered from this poison herself. When her husband was drunk and came looking for her, he would not leave without a yuan. If she didn't have it, he'd make a drunken scene outside the gate. With no other option, she'd have to borrow that yuan immediately, no matter the interest. From this experience, she learned this method, not out of revenge, but regarding it as reasonable, almost as emergency charity. There were those who urgently needed money and those willing to lend—like Zhou Yu striking Huang Gai, both willing and consenting!

In principle, she didn't see anything wrong with it. So in practice, she had to be tough, not throwing money away like water. Each profession has its own rules. This required insight, finesse, caution, and fierceness, so that you wouldn't lose everything[1]. She put in no less effort than a bank manager, for she needed much more carefulness. Capital may vary in size, but the principle is the same, for this is a capitalist society, like a very fine and very large sieve, slowly sifting money down from the top, with less and less as you go down. At the same time, it also sifts down principles, but the amount is the same at top and bottom, because principles, unlike money, aren't afraid of small sieve holes; they are formless and can slip through the tiniest gaps. Everyone said Gao Ma was tough, and she admitted it herself. Her toughness was forged through hardship and torment. Thinking of her past suffering, even her own husband's heartless unreasonableness, she would grit her teeth. She could be very gentle or very venomous; she knew that without this, she couldn't survive in this world.

She also advised Xiangzi to lend out his money, completely out of goodwill. If he was willing, she could help him: "Let me tell you, Xiangzi, keeping it in your pocket, a single coin stays a single coin! But if you lend it out, money can make more money! No mistake, what are our eyes for? Look carefully before letting money go, don't let it fly away like a bald-tailed eagle. If a policeman doesn't pay the interest on time or return the principal, go to his patrol chief! In a word, he'll lose his job, I dare you! Find out their payday and collect right at the source. If they don't pay back, well, that's a new one! One against ten, whoever you lend to, you need to know their background. Otherwise, lending out like fishing for a pot in the sea—that won't do! Listen to me, it's sure to be right!"

Xiangzi didn't need to say much; his expression already showed how much he admired Gao Ma's words. But when he was alone and calculated, he felt that keeping the money in his own hands was more secure than anything. Sure, it was dead money, it wouldn't make more money, but it was true that it couldn't be lost. He would carefully take out the few yuan he'd saved in the past couple of months—all silver dollars—and flip them one by one, afraid of making noise. The silver dollars were so bright, thick, and eye-catching; he felt even more strongly that he must not let go, unless it was to buy a cart. Everyone has their own methods; he didn't have to follow everything Gao Ma said.

At a former place, a household named Fang, the entire family, including servants, all had savings account books at the post office. Mrs. Fang had also advised Xiangzi: "You can open an account with just one yuan. Why don't you open one? As the saying goes, always think of poverty during times of plenty, so you don't have to hope for plenty when you're poor. You're young, while you have the strength, save a bit. Three hundred and sixty days a year can't all be sunny. It's not trouble, it's safe, and it earns interest. When you're in a pinch, you can withdraw some. What's more convenient? Go, get a form. If you can't write, I'll fill it out for you. All out of good will!"

Xiangzi knew she meant well, and knew the cook Wang Liu and the nanny Qin Ma both had accounts. He really wanted to try it. But one day, Miss Fang told him to deposit ten yuan for her. He looked closely at the little book—it had writing, small red seals. All together, huh, it weighed about as much as a small stack of toilet paper. You put your money in, and they scribble a few characters and stamp a small seal. He felt that even if it weren't a scam, it had to be a scam. You hand over shiny silver dollars, and they just draw a few lines and call it done. Xiangzi wasn't falling for it. He suspected the Fang family was in cahoots with the post office—he always thought the post office was a business with branches everywhere, probably an old establishment, at least as old as Ruifuxiang or Hongji—and that's why they were so eager to drum up business. Even if that wasn't the case, cash in hand was better than a little booklet, much better! The money in the booklet was just a few words!

As for banks and remittance offices, he only knew them as places to pick up fares. If the police didn't stop you from parking your cart there, you were sure to get a "customer." As for what happened inside, he couldn't fathom. Sure, there must be a lot of money in there. But why people went there just to fiddle with money[2], he didn't understand. It was hard for him to get involved with them, so he didn't bother to think about it. There were many things in the city he didn't understand. Hearing his friends discuss them in the teahouse only confused him more, because everyone had a different opinion, and none got to the heart of it. He didn't want to listen anymore, nor think about it. He knew that if he were going to rob somewhere, he'd best rob a bank. Since he didn't plan on becoming a bandit, it was best to just hold onto his own money and not worry about the rest. He thought this was the most sensible approach.

Gao Ma knew he was dead set on buying a cart and gave him another idea: "Xiangzi, I know you don't want to lend out money, so you can buy your own cart sooner. That's one way! If I were a man, and I pulled a rickshaw, I'd want to pull my own rickshaw. Pulling it and singing my own tune, not asking for favors from anyone! If I could do that, I wouldn't trade it for a county magistrate's job! Pulling a rickshaw is hard work, but if I were a man with some strength, I'd rather pull a rickshaw than be a policeman; standing on the street all year round,"""You're only making a couple of bucks a month, with no side income, no freedom; if you grow a beard, they'll make you shave it off, it's just hopeless. I mean, look, if you really want to buy a rickshaw quickly, here's a good idea: start a credit club, ten or so people, at most twenty, each paying two dollars a month, and you take the first pot; isn't that an instant forty or so bucks? You must have some savings too, pool it together and get a rickshaw to pull, just do it! Once you've got the rickshaw, you can run a black-ticket club, no interest, and it's a respectable thing, it'll suit your plans just fine! If you really want to start a club, I'll join one, no hesitation! What do you say?" 

This really made Xiangzi's heart beat a little faster! If he could really scrape together thirty or forty bucks, plus the thirty-plus that Fourth Master Liu was holding, and the few he had himself, wouldn't that be around eighty? Though not enough for a brand-new rickshaw, an eighty-percent-new one could be managed! Besides, this way he could go to Fourth Master Liu and ask for his money back, instead of just leaving it there, which didn't seem right. Eighty-percent-new would be fine; he could pull it for now, and later when he had surplus, he could trade up. 

But where would he find twenty such people? Even if he could gather them, it was a matter of face; if he started a club because he needed money, wouldn't others ask him to join theirs later? In these lean times, clubs often collapsed! A real man doesn't rely on others; simply, if he had the luck to buy a rickshaw, he'd buy it; he wouldn't ask for help! 

Seeing Xiangzi unmoved, Gao Ma felt like teasing him, but then thinking of his earnestness, she felt a bit embarrassed: "You're something else! 'Driving a pig down a small alley—straight in, straight out'; that's fine too!" 

Xiangzi said nothing. After Gao Ma left, he nodded to himself, as if acknowledging that his stubborn grip was worthy of admiration, and felt rather pleased inside. 

It was already early winter. In the evenings, besides the cries of chestnuts and peanuts being sold in the alley, there was the mournful call of "chamber pots." The chamber pot peddler's shoulder pole carried earthenware piggy banks, and Xiangzi bought a large one. For the first sale, the peddler couldn't make change. Xiangzi had a bright idea; he saw the smallest green chamber pot, very amusing, glistening green, with its little spout sticking out. "Keep the change, I'll take this one!" Putting down the piggy bank, he took the little green pot inside: "Young master not asleep yet? Here's a fun little toy!" 

Everyone was watching little Wen—the Cao family's little boy—taking a bath, and when they saw this toy, they all couldn't help laughing. Mr. and Mrs. Cao didn't say much, probably thinking that though it was a bit silly, Xiangzi's good intentions should be accepted, so they smiled at him in thanks. But Gao Ma couldn't keep her mouth shut: "Look at that, really, Xiangzi! A big guy like you, coming up with such a clever idea; what an eyesore!" 

Little Wen loved the toy and immediately started scooping bathwater into the small pot with his hands: "This little teapot, its spout is big!" 

Everyone laughed even harder. Xiangzi straightened up—because when he felt proud, he didn't know what to do with himself—and walked out. He was very happy; this was something he'd never experienced before, everyone's smiling faces all turned toward him, as if he were a very important person. Smiling, he took out the silver dollars again, gently placing them one by one into the piggy bank, thinking to himself: This is safer than anything! When it's full, I'll just smash it against the wall; crack, silver dollars will be more than roof tiles! 

He decided not to ask anyone for anything again. Even Fourth Master Liu, reliable as he was, sometimes seemed awkward; the money wouldn't be lost in his hands, but still, he felt a bit uneasy. Money was like a ring; it was always better in one's own hand. This decision made him feel relieved, as if his belt had been tightened another notch, letting him stand taller and firmer. 

The weather was getting colder, but Xiangzi didn't seem to notice. With a clear plan in mind, the path ahead brightened; in the light, he didn't feel the cold. Ice was beginning to form on the ground, even the dirt on the sidewalks was solidifying, everything showing dryness and firmness, the black soil taking on a faint yellow, as if all moisture had been drained. Especially in the early morning, the ridges of earth churned up by carts were rimmed with frost, and a sharp little wind scattered the morning clouds, revealing a sky that was extremely high, blue, and crisp. Xiangzi was eager to go out early with his rickshaw; the chilly wind cutting through his sleeves made him shiver all over, as if taking a cold bath, a mix of pain and pleasure. Sometimes a gale would hit, making it hard for him to breathe, but he kept his head down, gritted his teeth, and plowed forward like a big fish swimming upstream; the stronger the wind, the more he resisted, as if fighting a decisive battle with it. A sudden blast would leave him breathless; he'd shut his mouth tight, and after a long while, let out a burp, like coming up from a deep dive. After that burp, he'd keep running, charging ahead, nothing able to stop this giant; not a single muscle relaxed, like a green insect besieged by ants, shaking all over in defense. What a sweat!
