When he set down the rickshaw, straightened his back, let out a long breath, and wiped the yellow sand from his mouth, he felt invincible; watching the dust-laden wind sweep past him, he nodded. The wind bent the roadside trees, tore shop banners, stripped wall posters, dimmed the sun, singing, screaming, roaring, echoing! Suddenly it dashed straight, like a maddened great spirit, dragging heaven and earth as it fled; then it grew chaotic, swirling in all directions, like a demon that didn't know what to do and decided to rampage; then it swept sideways, striking everything on the ground off guard, snapping branches, tearing off roof tiles, breaking power lines. But Xiangzi stood there watching; he'd just come out of the wind, and it hadn't done a thing to him! Victory was Xiangzi's! And when he ran with a tailwind, he just had to hold the handles steady; no need to run himself, the wind would push the wheels for him, like a good friend. 

Of course, he wasn't blind; he must have seen those old and weak rickshaw pullers. They wore tattered clothes that a light wind could pierce or a strong wind shred; their feet were bound in who-knows-what. At the stands, they shivered, their eyes darting like thieves; no matter where someone emerged, they'd all rush to ask: "Rickshaw?!" When they got a fare, they'd warm up, their sweat soaking through their thin, ragged clothes. When they stopped, the sweat on their backs would freeze into ice. In the wind, they couldn't lift a step and had to drag the rickshaw by sheer force; when the wind hammered from above, they had to bow their heads to their chests; when it came from below, their feet lost the ground; from the front, raising a hand would turn them into kites; from behind, they couldn't control the rickshaw or themselves. But they tried everything, used all their strength, pulling with all their might to get the rickshaw to its destination, risking their lives for a few coppers. One trip finished, dust and sweat turned into mud, caked on their faces, leaving only three frozen red rings: their eyes and mouth. The days were so short and cold, with few people on the streets; after a whole day of this bitter grind, they might not earn enough for a full meal. Yet the old ones had wives and kids at home; the young ones had parents and siblings! In winter, they were entirely in hell, with just one more breath of life than ghosts, but without the ghosts' leisure; ghosts didn't have all their burdens! Dying like a dog in the street was their greatest peace and comfort; they say frozen ghosts have a smile on their faces! 

How could Xiangzi not see all this? But he had no time to worry or think about them. Their suffering was also his, but he was young and strong, able to bear hardship, unafraid of cold or wind; at night he had a clean place to stay, and by day he wore decent clothes, so he felt he wasn't on the same level as them. Though he was suffering alongside them now, the degree of suffering wasn't exactly the same; for now, he endured less pain, and in the future, he could escape from it. He thought that when he got old, he definitely wouldn't be pulling a broken-down rickshaw, starving and freezing. He believed that his current advantages could guarantee future victory. Just like when he ran into car drivers outside restaurants or mansions; they wouldn't chat with him. Car drivers felt it beneath their dignity to associate with rickshaw pullers. The attitude of car drivers toward rickshaw pullers was a bit like Xiangzi's toward those old and weak disabled men; all in the same hell, but on different levels. They never thought of standing together; instead, each went his own way, personal hopes and efforts blinding everyone, each believing they could build a life with bare hands, groping in the darkness for their own path. Xiangzi didn't think about others, didn't care about others; he only thought about his own money and future success. 

The streets gradually took on a New Year's atmosphere. On clear, windless days, though it was dry and cold, the roadside gained more color: New Year pictures, gauze lanterns, red and white candles, silk head flowers, large and small honey offerings, all displayed, making people feel happy yet a bit uneasy; because no matter who, they wanted to enjoy a few happy days at the New Year, but everyone had some difficulties. Xiangzi's eyes brightened; seeing the New Year goods on the roadside, he thought of the Cao family, who must be preparing gifts. A gift would get him a few dimes for drinks. The holiday bonus was a fixed two dollars, not much; but when guests came for New Year's visits, acting as a doorman would earn him two or three dimes per trip. All together, it would add up; no matter how small, as long as the small change came in regularly, his piggy bank wouldn't cheat him! When idle in the evening, he would stare at this clay friend that only ate money and wouldn't spit it out, softly urging it: "Eat more, eat more, buddy! When you've had your fill, I'll be set!" 

The New Year was getting closer, and in a blink, it was Laba. Joy or fear forced people to plan and prepare; it was still twenty-four hours a day, but these days were different; they wouldn't let anyone pass them casually. Something had to be done, all directed toward the New Year, as if time had suddenly gained awareness and feeling, making people think along with it, and busy themselves accordingly. Xiangzi was on the happy side; the bustle on the streets, the cries of vendors, the hope of holiday bonuses and small change, the New Year's rest, the thought of good food... all made him happy and hopeful like a child. He thought it over: he'd spend a dollar or eighty cents to buy Fourth Master Liu a gift. The gift might be light, but it was the thought that counted; he had to take something. First, to apologize for not visiting the old man lately because he'd been busy at the mansion; second, he could take the opportunity to get back that thirty-plus dollars. Spending a dollar or so to get that money back was a good deal. Having thought this through, he gently shook the piggy bank, imagining how wonderfully heavy and pleasant the sound would be when that thirty-plus dollars was added. Yes, as soon as he got that money back, he'd have nothing to worry about! 

One evening, just as he was about to shake the treasure pot again, Gao Ma called out to him: "Xiangzi! There's a young lady at the door looking for you; I just came from the street, and she kept asking about me." When Xiangzi came out, she added in a low voice: "She's like a big black tower! Scary!" 

Xiangzi's face suddenly flushed as if on fire; he knew things were about to go wrong! 

[1]  

"Let the hawk go" means total loss.  

[2]  

"Gudou" means repeated fiddling or toying. 

Nine 

Xiangzi barely had the strength to step out the door. Dazed and confused, with one foot still inside the threshold, he saw Miss Liu by the streetlight. Her face was probably powdered again, which under the light looked a bit gray-green, like black withered leaves covered with frost. Xiangzi dared not look her straight in the eye. 

Tigress's expression was complex: in her eyes was a glimmer of longing to see him; her mouth was slightly open, showing a hint of a cold smile; her nose was wrinkled a bit, folding some disdain and eagerness; her brows were set, giving her a coquettish and domineering look under all that strange makeup. Seeing Xiangzi come out, she pouted a few times, all the various expressions on her face momentarily unable to settle into one, staring blankly at the ground. She swallowed, seeming to suppress the complex airs and emotions, and put on the worldly manners she'd learned from Fourth Master Liu, half-annoyed and half-laughing, pretending to be careless as she cracked a joke: "Well, you're something! A meat bun thrown at a dog—once gone, never back!" Her voice was loud, just like when she quarreled with the pullers at the rickshaw yard. After these words, all trace of a smile left her face; suddenly she seemed to feel a sense of shame and degradation, and she bit her lip. 

"Don't yell!" Xiangzi seemed to put all his strength into his lips, bursting out these two words, which were soft but very forceful. 

"Hmph! I'm not afraid!" she laughed maliciously, but couldn't help lowering her voice a bit. "No wonder you've been avoiding me; so there's a little vixen of a maid here! I've long known you were no good, pretending to be dumb, though you're big and dark and stupid!" Her voice went up again. 

"Don't yell!" Xiangzi was afraid Gao Ma might be eavesdropping at the door. "Don't yell! Come over here!" He said as he walked toward the street. 

"Wherever I go, I'm not afraid; I've got a loud voice!" she retorted, but she followed him. 

Crossing the street to the east sidewalk, Xiangzi—still remembering his village habits—squatted down by the red wall of the park. "Why are you here?" 

"Me? Hmph, a lot of things!" she said, one hand on her hip.His belly bulged out a little. He looked down at her, thought for a moment, and seemed to take pity on her: "Xiangzi! I need to talk to you, something important!"

This soft, low "Xiangzi" scattered much of his anger. He looked up at her. She still didn't have anything endearing about her, but that "Xiangzi" echoed faintly in his heart, gentle and intimate, as if heard somewhere before, evoking an undeniable, hard-to-break bond. His voice was still low, but gentler: "What is it?"

"Xiangzi!" She moved closer. "I'm pregnant!"

"Pregnant with what?" He was momentarily stunned.

"This!" She pointed to her belly. "You figure it out!"

Dazed and confused, he let out an "Ah," and suddenly understood everything. A thousand things he had never imagined rushed into his mind, so many, so urgent, so chaotic, that his mind suddenly went blank, like a film reel snapping off. The street was very quiet. Grey clouds in the sky hid the moon. A light wind occasionally blew on the ground, stirring withered branches and leaves. A few sharp cat cries sounded in the distance. Xiangzi's mind went from chaos to blankness; he didn't even hear those sounds. Propping his cheek on his hand, he stared blankly at the ground until it seemed to move. He couldn't think of anything, and didn't want to think. He only felt himself growing smaller, yet unable to shrink completely into the earth. His entire life seemed to rest on this single point of distress; nothing else existed! Only then did he feel the cold, his lips trembling slightly.

"Don't just squat there. Say something! Get up!" She seemed to feel the cold too, wanting to move around a bit.

He stood up stiffly and followed her northward, still at a loss for words. His whole body felt numb, as if just waking from a frozen sleep.

"Don't have a plan?" She shot Xiangzi a glance, with a hint of pity in her eyes.

He had nothing to say.

"By the twenty-seventh, the old man's birthday, you've got to come." "Busy! End of the year!" Xiangzi hadn't forgotten his own affairs, even in his turmoil. "I know you, you tough guy, you're harder on the soft and softer on the hard. Talking nicely to you is a waste!" Her voice rose again. The stillness of the street made her voice especially clear, making Xiangzi particularly uncomfortable. "Who do you think I'm afraid of? What are you planning? If you don't want to listen to me, I've got no time to waste spit on you! If we fall out, I'll block your doorstep and curse for three days and nights! I'll find you wherever you go! I don't care who you are!"

"Can you stop shouting?" Xiangzi stepped back from her.

"Afraid of shouting? Then don't take advantage in the first place! You've had your fun, and now you leave me to take the blame. Open your dead eyes and see who I am!"

"Speak slowly, I'm listening!" Xiangzi had felt very cold, but this scolding suddenly made him hot. The heat seemed to push through his frozen skin, making him itch all over, especially on his scalp.

"Now that's settled! Don't look for trouble!" She grinned, showing two canine teeth. "I'm not lying, I really care for you. Don't be ungrateful! Cross me, and you'll get nothing good, I'm telling you!"

"No..." Xiangzi wanted to say "Don't hit me and then rub it better," but couldn't get it all out. He knew plenty of Beijing wisecracks but couldn't say them smoothly. He understood when others said them, but couldn't produce them himself.

"No what?"

"Say your piece!"

"I'll give you a good idea," Tigress stopped, facing him. "Look, if you send a matchmaker, the old man will never agree. He owns rickshaws; you pull one. He won't lower himself to family ties. I don't care. I like you. If I like you, that's enough. Who cares about anything else! No matchmaker can arrange it—as soon as you propose, the old man will think you're after his dozens of rickshaws. Even someone a cut above you wouldn't work. I have to handle this myself. I've chosen you. We'll act first and report later. Since I'm already pregnant, neither of us can escape! But going straight to him like this still won't work. The old man gets more muddled with age. If we let it slip, he'll marry some young girl and kick me out. He's tough, even though he's nearly seventy. If he really marries a young woman, I dare say he could still father two or three kids, believe it or not!" "Let's walk and talk," Xiangzi said, noticing the patrolman on duty had already passed by twice, finding it awkward.

"Let's talk right here! Who can stop us!" She followed his gaze and saw the patrolman. "You're not pulling a rickshaw, so why be afraid of him? Can he just bite someone's thing off for no reason? That would be ridiculous! Let's talk business! Look, this is what I think: On the twenty-seventh, the old man's birthday, you go and kowtow to him three times. After New Year's, you go pay him a visit and win his favor. Once he's happy, get some wine or something and let him drink his fill. When he's seven or eight parts drunk, strike while the iron's hot. Just claim him as your godfather. Later, I'll slowly let him know I'm not well. He'll interrogate me, and I'll give him the silent treatment like Xu Shu in Cao's camp. When he's really pressed, I'll name someone—like that Qiao Er who just died, the second manager of the funeral parlor east of us. He had no family, and he's already buried in the charity graveyard outside Dongzhimen. How can the old man trace it? Once he's at a loss, we'll slowly drop hints. Best to give me to you. You're already his godson, then become his son-in-law—it's not far off. Go with the flow, save us all embarrassment. Don't you think that's a good plan?"

Xiangzi said nothing.

Feeling she'd finished a point, Tigress started walking north, head slightly lowered, as if savoring her own words, or giving Xiangzi a chance to think. At that moment, the wind tore a gap in the grey clouds, revealing moonlight. The two had reached the north end of the street. The imperial moat had long been frozen, still, grey-bright, flat, and solid, supporting the Forbidden City walls. Not a sound came from the Forbidden City. The delicate corner towers, the golden colorful archways, the vermilion gates, the pavilions on Coal Hill—all stood silent, as if listening to sounds hard to hear again. A light breeze passed through, like a sigh, threading softly among the towers and halls, as if trying to reveal some historical tidbits. Tigress headed west, and Xiangzi followed to the Golden Turtle and Jade Rainbow Bridge. There were almost no pedestrians on the bridge. The faint moonlight shone coldly on the two large ice fields on either side of the bridge. The distant pavilions were dim with shadows, seemingly frozen still on the lake, only bits of light glinting from the yellow tiles on their roofs. The trees stirred slightly; the moonlight seemed even more hazy. But the White Pagoda soared into the clouds, stark white, bringing everything into cold, desolate stillness. The entire Three Seas, amid artificial shaping, revealed the northern wild chill. At the bridgehead, the cold air from the ice on both sides made Xiangzi shiver. He didn’t want to go on. Usually, when he pulled a rickshaw across the bridge, he focused entirely on his feet, afraid of mistakes, never looking left or right. Now, he could freely look around, but he found the scenery frightening: the cold grey ice, the moving tree shadows, the pale high pagoda, all seemed so lonely, as if about to suddenly scream or run wild! Even the great white stone bridge beneath his feet felt eerily empty, particularly clean, with even the lights looking desolate. He didn’t want to walk further, didn’t want to look, and certainly didn’t want to stay with her. He truly wanted to jump down, headfirst, smash through the ice, sink, and freeze in the ice like a dead fish.

"See you tomorrow!" He suddenly turned and walked back.

"Xiangzi! That's it, then. See you on the twenty-seventh!" She called to his broad, straight back. After saying this, she glanced at the White Pagoda, sighed, and walked west. Without even turning his head, Xiangzi, as if a ghost were chasing him, scurried in a few steps to the Round City. He was in such a hurry he nearly bumped into the city wall. Supporting himself with one hand, he couldn't help wanting to cry. After a daze, a call came from the bridge: "Xiangzi! Xiangzi! Over here! Xiangzi!" Tigress's voice!

He moved two steps toward the bridge very slowly. Tigress was leaning back, walking down, her mouth slightly open: "I said, Xiangzi, come here; here!" Before he had moved a few steps, she was in front of him. "Here, the thirty-odd dollars you saved; there were a few cents change, which I made up to a full dollar. Here! For no other reason than to show my heart. I care about you, I cherish you, I protect you! Say nothing else, just don't be ungrateful! Here! Take good care of it. Don't blame me if you lose it!"

Xiangzi took the money—a stack of bills—stared blankly for a moment, and found nothing to say.

"So, see you on the twenty-seventh! We won't leave without seeing each other!" She smiled. "The profit is yours. Count it yourself!" She turned and walked back.

He clutched the stack of bills, staring blankly at her, until the bridge's back hid her head. The grey clouds covered the moonlight again. The lights grew brighter; the bridge seemed exceptionally white, empty, and cold. He turned, took big strides, and walked back frantically. When he reached the street gate, the image of that pale, desolate bridge still lingered in his mind, as if only a blink had passed.

Back in his room, he first counted the bills. He counted them two or three times, the sweat on his palms making the bills sticky, so he could never get the total right. After counting, he put them in the little clay piggy bank. Sitting on the edge of the bed, staring blankly at this earthenware vessel, he planned to think of nothing. Money meant a way out. He firmly believed this money box would solve everything; no need to think further. The Imperial Moat, Coal Hill, the White Pagoda, the big bridge, Tigress, the pregnancy... all a dream. When the dream ended, there were an extra thirty-odd dollars in the piggy bank—real!

Having stared enough, he hid the piggy bank and planned to sleep. Even the greatest difficulties could be slept off. Tomorrow he'd deal with it!

He lay down but couldn't close his eyes! Those affairs were like a swarm of bees, one coming out, another going in, each with a stinger at its tip!

He didn't want to think, and really couldn't figure a way out. Tigress had blocked every path; he couldn't escape.

The best thing would be to run away. Xiangzi couldn't go. Even if they sent him to guard the White Pagoda at Beihai, he'd be happy, just not the countryside! Move to another city? He couldn't think of a better place than Beiping. He couldn't leave. He'd rather die here.

Since he didn't want to leave, there was no need to waste energy on other thoughts. Whatever Tigress said, she could do. If he didn't follow her path, she would keep making trouble. As long as he was in Beiping, she would find him! With her, he had to be serious, not try to be clever. If she got anxious, she would drag in Fourth Master Liu. If Fourth Master Liu sold one or two people—and that's not even the worst—he could take Xiangzi's life in some secluded place!

Going over Tigress's words from beginning to end, he felt like he had fallen into a trap, hands and feet caught in clamps with no way out. He couldn't criticize her ideas one by one, so he couldn't find any loopholes. He only felt she had cast a dragnet that wouldn't let even a tiny fish escape! Unable to think through each detail, he lumped it all together, pressing down on his head like a thousand-jin gate. Under this unstoppable pressure, he realized a rickshaw puller's entire fate could be summed up in two words: lousy luck! A rickshaw puller, being a rickshaw puller, shouldn't do anything, not even touch women. Touch them, and a huge mistake would happen. Fourth Master Liu relied on his dozens of rickshaws, and Tigress on her stinking thing, to bully him! He didn't need to think deeply. If he was going to accept fate, fine, go kowtow, claim the old man as godfather, and wait to marry that stinking monster. If he didn't accept fate, he'd have to risk his life!

At this thought, he pushed Tigress and her words aside. No, it wasn't her fierceness; it was the fate of a rickshaw puller, just like a dog that must be beaten and abused, even children hitting it with sticks for no reason. What was the point of such a life? Let it go!

He didn't sleep. Kicking off the blanket, he sat up. He decided to get some wine and get dead drunk. What business? What rules? Screw them all! Get drunk, sleep! Twenty-seventh? He wouldn't even go on the twenty-eighth. Let's see who could do anything to Xiangzi!

Throwing on his big padded coat and picking up the small bowl he used as a teacup, he ran out. The wind had picked up. The grey clouds in the sky had scattered. The moon was small, shedding cold light. Just out of his warm bed, Xiangzi kept sucking in air. There were almost no pedestrians on the street. Only one or two rickshaws stood by the roadside, their pullers' hands over their ears, stamping their feet by the carts to keep warm. Xiangzi ran straight to the little shop in the south. To keep the warmth in, the shop had already closed its door, taking money and handing out goods through a small window. Xiangzi ordered four ounces of cheap liquor and three cents' worth of peanuts. Holding the wine bowl flat, he didn't dare run but walked quickly like a sedan chair bearer, returning to his room. He quickly burrowed into the bedding, his teeth chattering for a while. He didn't want to sit up again. The wine on the table gave off a pungent smell. He didn't like it much, and didn't even feel like touching the peanuts. This sudden cold seemed to wake him like a bucket of cold water. His hand was too lazy to reach out, and his heart was no longer so hot. After lying there for a long time,His eyes, over the edge of the blanket, glanced again at the wine bowl on the table. No, he couldn’t ruin himself over that entanglement, couldn’t break his vow of sobriety now. The situation was indeed difficult, but there was always some crack for him to slip through. Even if there was absolutely no escape, he shouldn’t be the first to roll into the mud; he had to keep his eyes open, see clearly, exactly how others would push him down.

He put out the light, pulled the blanket completely over his head, and tried to sleep that way. Still, he couldn’t fall asleep. Throwing off the blanket and looking out, the window paper was tinged blue by the moonlight in the courtyard, as if dawn was near. The tip of his nose felt the cold of the room, and in the chill there was a hint of wine. He sat up abruptly, reached for the wine bowl, and took a big gulp!

Ten  

Individual solutions were beyond Xiangzi's intelligence. A complete reckoning was beyond his courage. So he had no plan at all, carrying around a bellyful of grievances all day long. Like all living things, after suffering harm, he could only helplessly think of cleaning up the mess himself. A cricket that has lost its thigh still tries to crawl with its little legs. Xiangzi had no definite plan; he just wanted to muddle through day by day, one thing at a time, crawling wherever he ended up, without any thought of leaping upwards.

There were still over ten days before the twenty-seventh. He was completely focused on that one day. Everything he thought, talked about, and dreamed of was only the twenty-seventh. As if, once the twenty-seventh passed, he would have a way to solve everything, even though he knew clearly he was deceiving himself. Sometimes he would think farther ahead, like taking the few dozen dollars he had and going to Tianjin. Once there, if he got lucky, maybe he could switch professions and stop pulling rickshaws. Would Tigress be able to follow him to Tianjin? In his mind, any place you could reach by train must be very far, too far for her to track him down. It sounded good, but he knew in his conscience it was only a last resort. If he could still manage in Beiping, he would stay in Beiping! Thinking this way, he would come back to the twenty-seventh. Focusing on the near and simple was easier. If he could just get through this obstacle, maybe everything could stay the same and he'd weather the storm. Even if he couldn't completely disentangle himself, at least getting past one hurdle was one hurdle.

How to get through this hurdle? He had two ideas: one was to ignore her, simply not go to the birthday celebration. The other was to follow her instructions exactly. Although these two ideas were different, the result was the same: if he didn't go, she definitely wouldn't let it go; if he did go, she still wouldn't let him off. He remembered when he first started pulling rickshaws, he copied others by darting into alleys to take shortcuts, only to end up in a dead-end lane. He went in a circle and ended up back on the same street. Now he was in another such alley, it seemed: no matter which direction he took, the result was the same.

Having no other choice, he tried to think positively. Why not just take her? What was so wrong with that? But no matter how he thought about it, he felt stifled. Thinking of her appearance, he could only shake his head. Never mind her looks, think of her behavior. Hmph! For someone as ambitious and upright as him to marry such a piece of trash, he wouldn't be able to face anyone, and after death, he wouldn't have the face to see his parents! Who could be sure the child in her belly was his? Sure, she might bring a few rickshaws with her, but could that be guaranteed? Fourth Master Liu was no pushover! Even if everything went smoothly, he couldn't stand it. Could he outdo Tigress? She only needed to lift her little finger to make him dizzy, unable to tell north from south. He knew how formidable she was! If he were to start a family, he simply couldn't take her, no other way to put it! If he took her, he would lose himself, and he didn't think so little of himself! There was no way!

Unable to handle her, he turned his anger on himself and felt like giving himself a few sharp slaps. But to be honest, he hadn't really done anything wrong. Everything was her setup, just waiting for him to fall into the trap. The problem seemed to be that he was too honest. Being honest meant you inevitably suffer, with no logic to fall back on!

What made him even more miserable was having nowhere to pour out his grievances. He had no parents, no brothers, no friends. On ordinary days, he thought of himself as a true man, head touching the sky, feet on the ground, with no cares or ties. Now he understood, he regretted. A person couldn't live alone. Especially those fellow rickshaw pullers, they now seemed a bit endearing. If he had made some friends in the past, he thought, strong men like himself, then even if there were a dozen Tigresses, he wouldn't be afraid. They would give him advice and fight for him. But he had always been alone. Finding friends at the last minute wasn't easy! He felt a fear he had never known before. If things went on like this, anyone could bully him. One person alone couldn't prop up the sky!

This fear began to make him doubt himself. In winter, when his master had a dinner party or went to the theater, he would habitually tuck the water tank of the acetylene lamp into his chest, because if left on the rickshaw, it would freeze. Just after running, drenched in hot sweat, pressing that icy little tank against his chest made him shiver instantly. It would take a long time before the tank got a little warm. But on ordinary days, he didn't think this was unreasonable. Sometimes tucking it in, he even felt it was an advantage, since those pulling broken-down rickshaws couldn't even use acetylene lamps. Now, he seemed to realize that earning only that much money a month, he had to endure all the suffering, not even letting a little water tank freeze, but having to carry it against his chest. His own chest, no matter how broad, didn't seem as valuable as a little tank. Before, he thought pulling a rickshaw was his ideal life, the way to start a family and build a career. Now he secretly shook his head. No wonder Tigress bullied him; he was nothing but a man worth less than a little water tank!

On the third night after Tigress came to find him, Mr. Cao went with a friend to a late movie. Xiangzi waited in a small tea house, pressing that ice-like tank against his chest. The weather was bitterly cold. The doors and windows of the small tea house were tightly shut, filled with coal gas, sweat, and the dry, cheap stench of foul cigarettes. Despite this, the windows were covered with a layer of frost. Most of the people drinking tea were rickshaw pullers with monthly contracts. Some leaned their heads against the wall, taking advantage of the warmth inside to close their eyes and doze off. Some held bowls of white liquor, passing it around, then slowly, deliberately, they would drink, smacking their lips on the way down and loudly letting out cold air. Some gripped rolls of flatbread, biting off half in one go, their necks bulging thick and red. Some wore grim faces, complaining loudly to everyone about how they hadn't stopped all day, how their bodies had been wet and dry, dry and wet, who knows how many times! The rest mostly chatted idly among themselves, but when they heard these complaints, they would fall silent for a moment, then, like birds startled from their nests, they all remembered the day's grievances and wanted to share them. Even the man eating flatbread found a gap in his chewing to move his tongue, swallowing and talking at the same time, the veins on his head jumping: "Don't you think us monthly pullers have it easy?! I've been at it since fucking two o'clock and haven't had a drop of water or a grain of rice! Just from Qianmen to Pingzemen—hic!—I've done three fucking rounds! This weather, my asshole is frozen fucking solid, and it keeps letting out gas!" He looked around at everyone, nodded, and took another bite of flatbread.

This shifted everyone's conversation back to the weather, centering on it as they each recounted their hardships. Xiangzi remained silent the whole time, but he listened carefully to what they said. Although their tones, accents, and stories differed, they were all curses and complaints. These words, meeting the grievances in his own heart, were like raindrops falling on dry earth, completely absorbed. He couldn't, and didn't know how to, tell his own story from beginning to end. He could only absorb the bitterness of life from others' words. Everyone was suffering, and he was no exception. Recognizing himself, he also wanted to sympathize with everyone else. When they spoke of sad things, he frowned. When they said something funny, he curled his lip. This way, he felt he was one of them, all bitter friends. Even though he didn't say a word, it didn't matter much. Before, he thought they were just jabbering, that all their endless talking wouldn't make them rich. Today, it seemed like the first time he felt they weren't just talking nonsense, but speaking for him, speaking of the hardships of him and all rickshaw pullers.

Just as the crowd was getting lively, the door suddenly flew open, letting in a blast of cold air. Almost everyone glared outside, wondering who was so inconsiderate as to open the door. The more anxious everyone got, the slower the person outside seemed to move, as if deliberately dawdling.  

The tea house attendant called out, half annoyed, half laughing: "Hurry up, uncle, please! Don't let all the warm air out!"  

Before he finished speaking, the person outside entered, also a rickshaw puller. He looked over fifty, wearing a cotton-padded jacket that was neither short nor long, like a tattered lotus pod, with cotton spilling out at the lapels and elbows. His face looked like it hadn't been washed in days, the skin color indiscernible, only his ears bright red, red like fruit about to fall. Bristly white hair stuck out messily under a tattered little hat. Ice beads hung from his eyebrows and short whiskers. As soon as he entered, he grabbed a bench and sat down, struggling to say: "Brew a pot."  

This tea house had always been a gathering place for monthly contract pullers. A puller like this old man would never have come in on an ordinary day. Everyone looked at him, as if feeling something deeper than what they had been talking about just now. No one wanted to speak. On usual days, one or two clueless young men would crack a few jokes at the expense of customers like this, but today not a sound was heard.  

Before the tea was brewed, the old puller's head slowly lowered, lower and lower, until his whole body slumped down. Everyone immediately stood up: "What's wrong? What's wrong?" As they spoke, they all moved forward.  

"Don't move!" The tea house owner, experienced, stopped them. He went over alone, unfastened the old man's collar, helped him up from the floor, propped a chair behind his back, pressed his shoulders, and shouted: "Sugar water, quick!" After saying this, he listened near the old man's neck and muttered to himself: "It's not phlegm!"  

No one moved, but no one sat down either. They all blinked amid the smoke filling the room, staring toward the door. Everyone seemed to think silently: "This is our fate! When our hair turns white, any one of us might take a fall to our deaths!"  

Just as the sugar water reached the old puller's lips, he groaned twice. Still with his eyes closed, he raised his right hand—black and shiny, as if lacquered—and wiped his mouth with the back of it.  

"Drink some water!" the owner said into his ear.  

"Huh?" The old puller opened his eyes. Seeing himself sitting on the floor, he curled his legs and tried to stand up.  

"Drink some water first, no rush," the owner said, letting go. Almost everyone rushed over.  

"Ah! Ah!" The old puller looked around, held the tea bowl with both hands, and slowly sipped the sugar water.  

After finishing the sugar water, he looked around again: "Ah, thank you all!" His voice was incredibly gentle and warm, nothing like what you'd expect from that stubbly mouth. Saying this, he tried to stand again. Three or four people hurried over to help him up. A faint smile appeared on his face, and he said just as gently: "It's okay, it's okay, I'm fine! I was just cold and hungry, got a little dizzy! No problem!" Though his face was thick with dirt, that smile made everyone seem to see a gentle, fair-skinned face beneath it.  

Everyone seemed moved. The middle-aged man who had been holding a bowl of liquor had already finished it, his eyes red and now a little teary: "Come, come, two more ounces!" When the liquor arrived, the old puller was already sitting on a chair by the wall. A little tipsy, the middle-aged man respectfully placed the liquor before the old puller: "My treat, you drink! I'm over forty myself, to be honest, pulling a monthly contract is just getting by, one year at a time. The legs know! In another two or three years, I'll be just like you! You must be—" nearing sixty, right?"  

"Younger, fifty-five!" The old puller took a sip of liquor. "It's cold, nobody's hiring. Me, ah, empty stomach; I spent my last few cents on liquor to warm up a bit! Walking here, I just couldn't hold on anymore, thought I'd come in to warm up. The room was too hot, and I hadn't eaten, so I guess I passed out. It's nothing, nothing! Thank you all, brothers!"  

At that moment, the old man's straw-like gray hair, his mud-caked face, his charcoal-like hands, and his tattered hat and cotton jacket all seemed to emit a pure light, like a statue in a broken temple—though worn down, still dignified. Everyone looked at him, as if afraid he would leave. Xiangzi had remained silent the whole time, standing there dazed. When he heard the old puller say his stomach was empty, he suddenly dashed out, flew back just as fast, holding ten lamb-stuffed buns on a piece of cabbage leaf. He brought them right up to the old man and said: "Eat!" Then he sat back down in his seat, lowered his head, as if exhausted. "Ah!" The old man seemed both happy and about to cry. He nodded at everyone. "That's what it means to be brothers! When you pull a fare, you put in all your strength, and then asking for one more cent is so hard!" Saying this, he stood up to leave.  

"Eat!" Everyone shouted almost in unison.  

"I'll go call Little Ma, my little grandson, he's outside watching the rickshaw!" "I'll go, you sit!" said the middle-aged puller. "The rickshaw won't get lost here, don't worry, the police booth is right across the street." He opened the door a crack: "Little Ma! Little Ma! Your grandpa is calling you! Bring the rickshaw over here!"  

The old man touched the buns several times but never picked them up. As soon as Little Ma entered the door, the old man picked up one: "Little Ma, good boy, here you go!" Little Ma was around twelve or thirteen, thin-faced but wearing a round, padded coat, his nose red from the cold, with two streaks of white snot hanging from it.He wore a pair of tattered ear flaps on his head. Standing beside the old man, he took a stuffed bun with his right hand, while his left hand automatically grabbed another, taking a bite from each one.  

"Hey! Slowly!" The old man rested one hand on his grandson’s head, picked up a bun, and brought it slowly to his mouth. "Grandpa will have two, that’s enough; the rest are all yours! When you’re done, we’ll pack up the rickshaw and go home. No more runs. If it isn’t this cold tomorrow, we’ll set out earlier. Right, Little Horse?"  

Little Horse nodded at the bun, sniffled, and said, "Grandpa, have three, and the rest are mine. I’ll pull you home later!" "No need!" The old man grinned proudly at everyone. "We’ll walk back later; it’s cold sitting in the rickshaw."  

After finishing his share, the old man drained the wine from his cup and waited for Little Horse to polish off the buns. He pulled out a ragged cloth, wiped his mouth, and nodded to everyone again. "My son joined the army and never came back; his wife—"  

"Don’t say that!" Little Horse’s cheeks bulged like two small peaches as he interrupted his grandfather between bites and words.  

"It’s fine to talk! They’re not strangers!" Then he lowered his voice to everyone: "The kid’s sensitive, you can’t imagine how determined he is! His wife left too. We two live off this rickshaw. It’s broken, but it’s ours, and at least we don’t have to worry about the daily rental every day. However much we earn, we scrape by, the two of us. There’s no way around it! No way!"  

"Grandpa," Little Horse said, nearly done with the buns, tugging at the old man’s sleeve, "we’ve got to make one more run. There’s no money for coal tomorrow morning! It’s all your fault—just now, when we could have taken that fare to the back gate for twenty cents, you insisted on skipping it! If there’s no coal tomorrow morning, see what you’ll do!"  

"I’ve got a plan. Grandpa can go and get five pounds of coal briquettes on credit."  

"Need some kindling too?"  

"Right! Good boy, eat up; after you’re done, we’d better start moving!" Saying this, the old man stood up and addressed everyone in a circle: "Thanks for your trouble, folks!" He reached out to take Little Horse’s hand, and the boy stuffed the last whole bun into his mouth. Some of the people stayed seated, while others followed outside. Xiangzi was the first to follow; he wanted to see that rickshaw.  

An extremely battered rickshaw, the paint on the boards cracked and the handles worn down to the wood grain. A broken lantern rattled noisily, and the poles of the canopy were tied together with hemp rope. Little Horse fished a match out of his ear flaps, struck it on the sole of his shoe, cupped it in his two small black hands, and lit the lantern. The old man spat into his palm, grunted, grabbed the shafts, and said, "See you tomorrow, folks!"  

Xiangzi stood dumbfounded outside the door, watching the old man, the boy, and the broken rickshaw. The old man talked as he walked, his voice rising and falling; the streetlights and shadows alternated between light and dark. Xiangzi listened and watched, feeling a kind of distress he had never known before. In Little Horse, he seemed to see his own past; in the old man, his own future! He had never let go of a single coin easily, but now he felt a strange satisfaction in having bought ten buns for this pair. Only when they had disappeared from sight did he go back inside. The others were laughing and chatting again, but he felt restless. He paid for his tea and stepped out again, pulling the rickshaw to the entrance of the movie theater to wait for Mr. Cao.  

It was bitterly cold. Dust and sand floated in the air; the wind seemed to race overhead, and the stars were barely visible—only a few big ones trembled in the sky. There was no wind on the ground, but a chill emanated from all around. Long frozen cracks had formed in the ruts, the earth a pale gray, as cold and hard as ice. Xiangzi stood outside the theater for a while and already felt the cold, but he didn’t want to go back to the teahouse. He wanted to think quietly alone. That old man and boy seemed to have shattered his greatest hope—the old man’s rickshaw was his own! Ever since his first day pulling a rickshaw, he had resolved to buy his own, and now he still toiled all day for that goal. With his own rickshaw, he believed, he would have everything. Hah, look at that old man!  

If he didn’t want to take Huniu, wasn’t it because of his wish to buy a rickshaw? Buy the rickshaw, save money, and then marry a clean wife; hah, look at Little Horse! If he had a son, wouldn’t he turn out just like that?  

Thinking this way, he felt no need to resist Huniu’s threats. After all, he couldn’t escape the trap—why not take any woman? Besides, she might even bring a few rickshaws with her. Why not enjoy some easy days for a while! Seeing through himself, there was no need to look down on others. Huniu was Huniu, and that was that!  

When the movie ended, he quickly set up the small water barrel and lit the lantern. He even took off his padded jacket, leaving only a thin shirt, wanting to run like mad, to run until he forgot everything, not caring if he fell dead!  

[1]  

Mofan means to stall for time.  

[2]  

Hengshi means "probably" or "most likely."  

Chapter Eleven  

Whenever he thought of that old man and Little Horse, Xiangzi wanted to give up all his hopes and just enjoy one day at a time. Why grit his teeth and make things hard for himself every day? The fate of the poor, as he saw it, was like a date pit—sharp at both ends: as a child, not starving to death was sheer luck; in old age, not starving was difficult. Only the middle stretch, when one was young and strong, not fearing hunger or hard work, could one live like a proper person. In that stretch, if he didn’t dare to enjoy himself when he could, he was a complete fool—once this village was passed, there’d be no such shop! With this thought, he didn’t even worry about Huniu anymore.  

But when he saw the little gourd jar, his mind turned again. No, he couldn’t be careless. He was only a few dozen dollars short of buying a rickshaw; he couldn’t throw away all he’d done. At least he couldn’t squander the savings in that jar, so hard to save! He still had to stick to the right path—definitely! But what about Huniu? Still no solution; he still had to worry about that hateful twenty-seventh.  

Worrying to the point of helplessness, he hugged the clay jar and muttered to himself: Let it be whatever it will—this money is mine, anyway! No one can take it! With this money, Xiangzi isn’t afraid of anything! If I get desperate, I can stamp my feet and run; with money, my legs will move!  

The streets grew livelier; sugar melons for the Kitchen God festival lined the stalls, and everywhere he went, he heard the cry, "Sugar candies for pulling, sugar candies!" Xiangzi had once looked forward to the New Year, but now he felt no excitement at all. The more chaotic the streets, the tighter his heart grew; that terrible twenty-seventh was right before his eyes! His eyes sank, and even the scar on his face became dull. Pulling his rickshaw through the chaos and the slippery ground, he had to be extra careful. Pressed by both his worries and his caution, he felt his mind wasn’t up to the task; thinking of one thing made him forget another, and he often started suddenly, his skin prickling like a child with heat rash in summer.  

On the afternoon of the Kitchen God festival, a steady east wind brought black clouds all day. The weather suddenly warmed a bit. By the time lamps were lit, the wind had died down, and sparse snowflakes began to fall. The sugar melon sellers were frantic—warmth and snow made them sprinkle white dust on the sweets, afraid they’d all stick together. The snowflakes didn’t last long, turning to small hailstones that rustled softly as they whitened the ground. After seven o’clock, shops and homes started the Kitchen God ceremony. Amid the incense and firecracker flashes, dense tiny snow fell, adding a grim air to the bustle. People on the street looked anxious; pedestrians and riders alike hurried home to worship the gods, but the wet, slippery ground kept them from moving fast. The candy vendors, desperate to sell their holiday goods before it was too late, shouted breathlessly, their voices jarring.  

Around nine o’clock, Xiangzi was pulling Mr. Cao home from the west part of town. Past the busy Xidan Archway, they turned east into Chang’an Street, where people and horses thinned out. A thin layer of snow covered the smooth asphalt road, dazzling under the streetlights. Occasionally a car passed, its headlights shining far; the tiny hailstones glowed yellow in the beams like ten thousand grains of golden sand. Near Xinhua Gate, the road was naturally wide, and the thin snow made it seem even more open and refreshing, as if everything had taken on a graver air. The "Chang’an Archway," the gate of Xinhua, the red walls of Nanhai Lake—all wore white crowns, paired with crimson pillars and walls, quietly displaying the capital’s dignity under the lights. At this time and place, it felt as if there were no residents in Beijing, only a jade palace in the clouds, with a few old pines silently catching the snow. Xiangzi had no time to admire this beauty; facing the "jade road" before him, he only wanted to run home in one step. That straight, white, silent avenue seemed to let his heart and eyes see all the way to his doorstep. But he couldn’t run fast; the snow on the ground wasn’t thick, but it stuck to his feet, and soon the soles of his shoes were caked with a thick layer. Stomp it off, and it would stick again. The hailstones were tiny but heavy, both clinging and blinding him, so he couldn’t dash. The hail didn’t melt easily when it hit him, and a thin layer had gathered on his shoulders; though not much, the dampness made him uncomfortable. There were few shops here, but distant firecrackers still exploded continuously, occasionally sending up double-bang or "five ghosts" firecrackers into the dark sky. Sparks scattered, making the darkness deeper, almost terrifying. He heard the explosions, saw the sparks and the black sky, and wanted to reach home instantly. But he couldn’t let loose; it was awkward!  

What made him even more uneasy was that, starting from the west part of town, he felt a bicycle following him. On West Chang’an Street, where it was quieter, he sensed it even more—the tires crunching the thin snow, though not loud, were unmistakable. Xiangzi, like any other rickshaw puller, hated bicycles. Cars were annoying, but at least they made noise, so you could dodge them from afar. Bicycles slipped through every gap, wobbling left and right, dizzying to look at. And if there was an accident, the rickshaw puller was always at fault. In the cops’ minds, a rickshaw puller was always easier to deal with than a cyclist, so they blamed the puller first. Several times, Xiangzi had wanted to slam on his brakes and send the guy behind him tumbling. But he didn’t dare; a rickshaw puller had to swallow his anger. Whenever he needed to stamp his shoes, he had to shout, "Stop!" At the front gate of Nanhai, the street was so wide, and the bicycle was still close behind. Xiangzi got angrier. He deliberately stopped, brushed the snow off himself. As he stood, the bicycle brushed past. The rider even turned to look back. Xiangzi purposely stalled until the bike was far away, then grabbed the shafts and cursed, "Annoying!" Mr. Cao’s "humanitarianism" meant he wouldn’t let Xiangzi install the windproof cotton canopy; even the canvas cover wasn’t allowed up except in heavy rain, to save the puller some effort. This light snow, he thought, didn’t warrant the canopy, and he also wanted to enjoy the night snowscape. He’d noticed the bicycle too, and after Xiangzi cursed, he said quietly, "If he keeps following, don’t stop at home. Go to Mr. Zuo’s place on Huanghua Gate. Don’t panic!"  

Xiangzi was a bit flustered. He only knew bikes were annoying, not that they could be dangerous. Since even Mr. Cao dared not go home, this guy must be serious! He ran a few dozen steps and caught up to the man, who was deliberately waiting for him and Mr. Cao. The bike let Xiangzi pass, and he got a look at the rider. In one glance, he recognized him: Plainclothes detective squad. He often saw those guys in teahouses. He’d never spoken to them, but he knew their look and gear. This one’s outfit seemed familiar: a black overcoat, felt hat worn low.  

At the entrance to South Long Street, Xiangzi took the chance of turning to glance back; the man was still following. He almost forgot the snow on the ground, putting effort into his steps. The long, white, straight road, lit only by cold lamps, with a detective tailing him! Xiangzi had never experienced this; he broke into a sweat. At the back gate of the park, he looked back—still following! At the house door, he dared not stop, yet hated to leave. Mr. Cao was silent, so he had no choice but to keep running north. All the way to the north exit, the bike was still there! He entered a small alley, still followed! Exited the alley, still followed! Going to Huanghua Gate, he shouldn’t have gone into that alley; he only realized it when he reached the north end. He admitted to himself he was a bit lost, and that made him angrier. Running to the back of Jingshan Hill, the bike turned north toward the back gate. Xiangzi wiped off his sweat. The hail had lessened, but a few snowflakes mixed in. Xiangzi seemed to love the snowflakes, floating boldly in the air, unlike the irritating hailstones. He looked back and asked, "Where to, sir?"  

"Still to Mr. Zuo’s house. If anyone asks you about me, say you don’t know me!"  

"Got it!" Xiangzi’s heart pounded, but he didn’t press for details.  

Arriving at the Zuos’, Mr. Cao told Xiangzi to pull the rickshaw inside and quickly close the gate. Mr. Cao was still calm, but his expression wasn’t good. After instructing Xiangzi, he went inside. Xiangzi had just pulled the rickshaw into the entryway and set it down when Mr. Cao came out again with Mr. Zuo; Xiangzi knew him and knew he was Mr. Cao’s good friend. "Xiangzi," Mr. Cao said quickly, "you take a car back. Tell my wife I’m here. Have them come too, by car—another one, don’t take that one.""There's no need to teach you to wait for the car that's coming for you. Got it? Good! Tell Madam to bring the necessary things and those few paintings in the study. Understood? I'll call Madam now, just to remind you again, afraid she might forget my instructions in her haste, so you can jog her memory."

"Shall I go?" Mr. Zuo asked.

"No need! That man just now might not necessarily be a detective, but I have that matter on my mind and can't be too careful. First, call a car, will you?" Mr. Zuo went to phone for a car. Mr. Cao instructed Xiangzi again: "When the car arrives, I'll have paid for it. Tell Madam to pack quickly; nothing else matters, just make sure to bring the child's things and those few paintings in the study—those paintings! When Madam is ready, have Gao Ma call for a car and come here. Got all that? After they leave, lock the main gate and move to the study to sleep—there's a phone there. Do you know how to use the phone?"

"I can't make outgoing calls, but I can answer." In truth, Xiangzi didn't even like answering the phone, but not wanting to upset Mr. Cao, he had to agree.

"That'll do!" Mr. Cao continued, still speaking very quickly: "If anything happens, don't open the door! Once we're all gone, leaving you alone, they won't let you off! If things look bad, turn off the light and jump over the back wall to Wang's place. Do you know the Wangs? Yes! Hide there for a while before leaving. Don't worry about my things or your own—just jump the wall and go, so you don't get caught! If you lose anything, I'll compensate you later. Here's five yuan for now. Good, I'll call Madam now, and later you repeat it to her. Don't mention the arrest; that cyclist might be a detective, or might not be. Don't panic just yet!"

Xiangzi's mind was in turmoil, as if he had many questions to ask, but eager to remember everything Mr. Cao had instructed, he dared not ask further.

The car arrived, and Xiangzi climbed in, dazed and confused. Snow was falling moderately, making it hard to see clearly outside. He sat upright, his head almost touching the roof of the car. He wanted to think things through, but his eyes were fixed on the red arrow ahead, so fresh and lovely. The little brush in front of the driver swayed automatically, wiping the fog from the glass, which was quite amusing. Just as he seemed to tire of it, the car reached the gate, and he got out feeling uneasy.

Just as he was about to press the bell at the street gate, a person seemed to emerge from the wall, grabbing his wrist. Instinctively, Xiangzi tried to pull his hand free, but recognizing the man, he stopped moving—it was the cyclist detective from earlier.

"Xiangzi, don't you recognize me?" The detective smiled and let go.

Xiangzi swallowed hard, not knowing what to say.

"Don't you remember when you were taken by us to the Western Hills? I was that company commander Sun. Remember now?"

"Ah, Company Commander Sun!" Xiangzi couldn't recall. When he was taken into the mountains by the soldiers, he hadn't had time to notice who was the company commander or the battalion commander.

"You don't remember me, but I remember you; that scar on your face is a good marker. I followed you for a while just now and at first wasn't sure, but looking left and right—that scar couldn't be wrong!"

"Something you need?" Xiangzi moved to press the bell again.

"Of course there's something, and something important! Shall we go inside and talk?" Company Commander Sun—now a detective—reached out and pressed the bell.

"I'm busy!" Xiangzi suddenly broke out in a sweat, thinking fiercely: "If I can't even avoid him, how can I invite him in?"

"Don't worry, I'm here for your own good!" The detective showed a hint of sly cunning. When Gao Ma opened the door, he stepped inside: "Much obliged, much obliged!" Without giving Xiangzi or Gao Ma a chance to speak, he dragged him inside and pointed to the gatehouse: "You live here?" Once in the room, he looked around: "Not a bad little room! Things aren't so bad for you!"

"Something you need? I'm in a hurry!" Xiangzi couldn't take any more of this idle chatter. "Didn't I tell you? It's urgent!" Detective Sun was still smiling, but his tone was very stern. "To put it plainly, that Cao is a rebel—catch him and he'll be shot; he won't get away! We've met before, you served me in the barracks; besides, we're both men of the street, so I'm taking a big risk to tip you off! If you're even a step late, they'll nab everyone in the nest, and no one will escape. We earn our living by hard work—why get caught up in their lawsuits? Right?"

"That wouldn't be fair to others!" Xiangzi was still thinking about what Mr. Cao had told him. "Unfair to who?" Detective Sun's mouth smiled, but his eyes were sharp. "They brought this on themselves—how are you being unfair to anyone? They dare to act and bear the consequences; it's not worth us suffering with them! Never mind the rest—lock you up for three months, and you, used to being free as a wild bird, stuck in a dark cell—could you take it? Besides, if they go to jail, they have money to grease palms and won't suffer; you, my good brother, with no backing, will be tied next to the piss bucket! That's the least of it—if they spend money to pull strings, they might get a few years' sentence; if the authorities can't close the case, they'll use you as a scapegoat. We don't provoke anyone or cause trouble, and in the end, go to Tianqiao for a bullet—wouldn't that be unjust? You're a smart man; a smart man doesn't take losses right in front of him. Being fair to others, huh! Let me tell you, good brother, there's never been any fairness for us poor guys!"

Xiangzi was frightened. Recalling the misery of being taken by the soldiers, he could imagine what prison would be like. "So I have to go and leave them behind?"

"If you look after them, who looks after you?"

Xiangzi had no answer. After a moment's pause, even his conscience nodded: "Fine, I'll go!"

"Just like that?" Detective Sun gave a cold laugh.

Xiangzi was confused again.

"Xiangzi, my good fellow! You're too naive! As a detective, do you think I'd just let you go?"

"Well—" Xiangzi was so anxious he didn't know what to say.

"Don't play dumb!" Detective Sun's eyes fixed on Xiangzi: "You probably have some savings—hand them over to buy your life! I don't even earn as much as you in a month, I have to eat, dress, and support a family, relying on a little extra income—I'm telling you this honestly! Think about it—can I just let you off with a wave of my hand? Bro's friendship is friendship; without it, would I have come to advise you? But business is business—if I don't get something out of it, am I supposed to let my family starve? A man of the world doesn't need to waste words—just tell me the truth!"

"How much?" Xiangzi sat down on the bed.

"Whatever you have—no fixed price!"

"Then I'll just wait to go to jail!"

"Is that what you're saying? Don't regret it?" Detective Sun's hand reached into his padded coat: "See this, Xiangzi! I can take you in right now—if you resist, I'll shoot! I'll haul you away immediately; forget the money—even your clothes will be stripped off the moment you enter the prison gate. You're a smart man—figure it out for yourself!" "If you have time to squeeze me, why not squeeze Mr. Cao?" Xiangzi finally stammered out after a long pause.

"He's the main culprit—catch him and there's a reward; miss him and you're in trouble. You, you, my silly brother—letting you go is like letting off a fart; killing you is like squashing a bedbug! Hand over the money and you're free; don't, and it's Tianqiao for you! Stop the nonsense, come clean—you're a grown man! Besides, I won't pocket this money all for myself; the boys need a share—who knows how much I'll get. You won't take such a cheap way out to save your life? Then I've got no choice! How much do you have?"

Xiangzi stood up, his temples pulsing, fists clenched.

"Don't even think about fighting—I'm warning you, there's a whole gang outside! Hurry up, hand it over! I'm doing you a favor—don't be ungrateful!" Detective Sun's expression turned very ugly.

"Who have I provoked or offended?!" Xiangzi's voice cracked with tears as he sat back down on the edge of the bed.

"You haven't provoked anyone—you just got caught in the crossfire! People have to be born into wealth; we're both at the bottom. Say no more!" Detective Sun shook his head, as if filled with infinite regret. "Alright, consider this me doing you wrong—no more dawdling!"

Xiangzi thought for a moment longer. There was no way out. His hands trembling, he pulled the gourd-shaped jar out from under the bedding.

"Let me see!" Detective Sun laughed, snatched the earthenware jar, and smashed it against the wall.

Xiangzi watched the money scatter on the ground, his heart about to crack.

"Is that all?"

Xiangzi said nothing, just kept trembling.

"Forget it! I won't be merciless; friends are friends. But you should know, buying your life for this money is a bargain!"

Xiangzi still said nothing, trembling as he began to bundle up the bedding.

"Don't touch that!"

"It's so cold…" Xiangzi's eyes blazed with fury.

"I told you not to move—so don't! Get lost!"

Xiangzi swallowed hard, bit his lip, and pushed the door open to leave.

Snow had fallen over an inch thick. Xiangzi walked with his head down. Everything was white, save for the large dark footprints left behind him.

**Twelve**

Xiangzi wanted to find a place to sit and think through everything from start to finish, even if it meant just crying afterward—at least to know what he was crying about. Things had changed too fast; his mind couldn't keep up. There was nowhere to sit—everywhere was snow. The small teahouses were already closed; it was past ten o'clock. Even if they were open, he wouldn't go in. He wanted a quiet place, knowing the tears welling in his eyes could fall at any moment. With nowhere to sit, he had to walk slowly. But where would he go? This silver-white world had no place for him to sit, no destination for him. A vast, white expanse, with only the hungry little birds and the desperate man knowing what it meant to sigh.

Where to go? That became the question, never mind the rest! Stay at a cheap inn? No! With his clothes, he could lose something in the middle of the night, not to mention how terrifying the lice would be. A better inn? Too expensive—he only had five yuan, his entire fortune. A bathhouse? Closed at midnight, no overnight stay. Nowhere to go.

Because there was nowhere to go, he felt his predicament all the more. After all these years in the city, he had only a set of clothes and five yuan—even his bedding was gone! From this, he thought of tomorrow. What would he do tomorrow? Pull a rickshaw? Go back to pulling? Hah—all pulling got him was no place to stay and the little money he had stolen! Start a small business? With only five yuan in capital, and having to buy even the carrying pole and baskets, and with no guarantee that any trade would earn him a living? Pulling a rickshaw could earn him thirty or forty cents out of thin air; a small business required capital and offered no certainty of three meals. If he ate up the capital, then went back to pulling, wouldn't that be like taking off his pants to fart—wasting five yuan for nothing? He couldn't let go of a single cent of that five yuan; it was his last hope! Become a servant? He wasn't cut out for it—couldn't wait on people, couldn't wash clothes or cook! He was good at nothing and knew nothing—just a big, dark, clumsy good-for-nothing!

Without realizing it, he arrived at Zhongnanhai. On the bridge, the area was empty; as far as the eye could see, it was all snowflakes. Only then did he seem to realize the snow hadn't stopped. He touched his head—his woolen hat was already very wet. No one was on the bridge; even the traffic policeman had disappeared somewhere. A few electric lights, battered by the snowflakes, seemed to blink endlessly. Xiangzi looked at the snow all around, his mind blank.

He stood on the bridge for a long time. The world seemed dead—not a sound, not a movement. The gray-white snowflakes seemed to seize the opportunity, falling in a flustered, light hurry, silently burying the world. In this stillness, Xiangzi heard the faint whisper of his conscience. Forget himself for now—he should go back and check on the Cao family. Only Mrs. Cao and Gao Ma were left, not a single man! Hadn't that last five yuan been given by Mr. Cao? He dared not think further; he turned and rushed back, very fast.

There were footprints at the door and two fresh tire tracks on the road. Had Mrs. Cao already left? Why hadn't that Sun fellow taken them?

He dared not push the door open, afraid of being caught again. He looked left and right—no one. His heart began to race. Might as well try; he had no home to go to anyway—if he was caught, so be it. He gently pushed the door; it was open. Following the wall for a few steps, he saw the light in his own room—his own room! He felt like crying. He bent low and went to the window, listening. Inside, someone coughed—Gao Ma's voice! He pulled open the door.

"Who? Oh, it's you! You scared me to death!" Gao Ma clutched her chest, steadied herself, and sat on the bed. "Xiangzi, what happened?"

Xiangzi couldn't answer; he felt as if he hadn't seen her in years, a lump of heat choking his chest.

"What's going on?" Gao Ma asked, on the verge of tears. "You hadn't come back, and the master called, telling us to go to Zuo's house, saying you'd be right over. When you came, wasn't it me who opened the door? As soon as I saw you with a stranger, I didn't say a word and hurried in to help the mistress pack. You never came in. In the pitch dark, the mistress and I fumbled around blindly; the young master was sound asleep, and we had to pull him out of his warm bed. We tied up the bundles."He went to the study to pick up paintings again, but you never showed your face. What’s wrong with you? I’m asking you! I roughly tidied up and came out to look for you, well, you were nowhere to be seen! The mistress was so angry—half from worry too—she was trembling. I had to call for a car. But we couldn’t just leave the place empty, like an abandoned city, with everyone gone. Well, I stood firm with the mistress, I told her to go ahead, I’d stay and watch. If Xiangzi comes back, I’ll rush straight to Mr. Zuo’s house; if he doesn’t, I’ll accept my fate! What’s this all about? What’s going on with you? Tell me!”

Xiangzi had nothing to say.

“Speak up! Standing there dumb won’t solve anything. What on earth is the matter?”

“You go!” Xiangzi finally found a word: “Go!”

“You’ll watch the house?” Gao Ma’s anger eased a bit.

“When you see the master, tell him—the detective caught me, but then, but then, didn’t catch me!”

“What kind of nonsense is that?” Gao Ma was so angry she nearly laughed.

“Listen to me!” Xiangzi took a deep breath. “Tell the master to run fast. The detective said he’s sure to nab him. Mr. Zuo’s place isn’t safe either. Run fast! Once you’re gone, I’ll jump over to the Wang family’s and sleep there for the night. I’ll lock the front gate here. Tomorrow, I’ll go find a job. Tell Mr. Cao I’m sorry!”

“The more you talk, the more confused I get!” Gao Ma sighed. “Alright, I’m off. The young master might have caught a chill, I need to check on him! When I see the master, I’ll tell him you said to run fast. Tonight, Xiangzi locks the gate, jumps over to the Wangs’ to sleep; tomorrow he’ll find work. Is that it?”

Xiangzi nodded, deeply ashamed.

After Gao Ma left, Xiangzi locked the front gate and went back inside. The broken gourd jar was still lying on the floor. He picked up a piece of the shard, looked at it, then tossed it back down. The bedding on the bed hadn’t been touched. Strange—what was really going on? Could it be that Detective Sun wasn’t a real detective? No way! If Mr. Cao hadn’t sensed some danger, why would he abandon his home and flee? I don’t get it! I don’t get it! Without realizing it, he sat down on the edge of the bed. As soon as he sat, he jumped up as if startled. He couldn’t stay here long! What if that Sun guy came back?! His mind raced: Sorry, Mr. Cao, but Gao Ma’s taking the message to tell him to run fast—that should be good enough. In good conscience, Xiangzi hadn’t meant to deceive anyone, and he himself was aggrieved. His own money was lost first; he couldn’t worry about Mr. Cao’s anymore. Muttering to himself, he rambled on while starting to pack up his bedding.

Shouldering his bedding, he turned off the lamp and headed to the back courtyard. Putting the bedding down, he grabbed the top of the wall and called softly, “Old Cheng! Old Cheng!” Old Cheng was the Wang family’s rickshaw puller. No one answered. Xiangzi made up his mind—he’d jump over first. He tossed the bedding over; it landed on the snow with hardly a sound. His heart pounded for a while. Then he climbed back up the wall and jumped over. He picked up the bedding from the snow and quietly went to find Old Cheng. He knew where Old Cheng stayed. It seemed everyone was asleep; not a sound came from the whole yard. Xiangzi suddenly felt that being a thief wasn’t all that hard. He gathered some courage, walking steadily on the packed snow, which gave off a faint crunch. Finding Old Cheng’s room, he coughed. Old Cheng sounded like he’d just lain down: “Who is it?”

“Me, Xiangzi! Open the door!” Xiangzi’s voice was very natural and gentle, as if hearing Old Cheng’s voice was like hearing reassurance from a loved one. Old Cheng turned on the light, threw on a tattered leather jacket, and opened the door: “What’s up, Xiangzi? In the dead of night!”

Xiangzi went in, put his bedding on the floor, sat on it, and fell silent again.

Old Cheng was in his thirties, with hard, knobby muscles on his face and body. On ordinary days, Xiangzi and he weren’t close, just nodding and exchanging words when they met. Sometimes, when Mrs. Wang and Mrs. Cao went out together, they’d have a chance to sit and drink tea together. Xiangzi admired Old Cheng a lot—Old Cheng ran fast, but he was always flustered and seemed unable to hold the rickshaw handles steady. In character, though Old Cheng was decent enough, this flaw kept Xiangzi from fully respecting him.

Today, Xiangzi found Old Cheng completely likable. Sitting there, he couldn’t say anything, but he felt grateful and warm inside. Just now, he’d been standing on the Zhonghai Bridge; now, he was sitting in a familiar person’s room. The sudden change left him feeling hollow inside, yet also a bit warm.

Old Cheng crawled back under the covers, pointing at the tattered leather jacket: “Xiangzi, have a smoke—there’s some in the pocket, ‘Bieye’ brand.” The “Bieye” brand cigarettes had been renamed “Bieye” by the rickshaw pullers ever since they came out.

Xiangzi didn’t usually smoke, but this time he couldn’t refuse. He took a cigarette, put it between his lips, and puffed.

“What’s up?” Old Cheng asked. “Quit your job?”

“No,” Xiangzi stayed sitting on his bedding. “There’s trouble! Mr. Cao’s whole family has fled, and I don’t dare watch the house alone!”

“What kind of trouble?” Old Cheng sat up again.

“Can’t really explain it, but it’s no small matter—even Gao Ma’s gone!”

“All the doors open wide, with no one in charge?”

“I locked the front gate!”

“Hmph!” Old Cheng thought for a long time. “Should I go tell Mr. Wang?” He started to put on his jacket.

“Leave it for tomorrow; it’s all too tangled to explain!” Xiangzi was afraid Mr. Wang would grill him.

The thing Xiangzi couldn’t explain went like this: Mr. Cao taught a few hours at a university. There was a student there named Ruan Ming, who had always been on good terms with Mr. Cao and often came to chat with him. Mr. Cao was a socialist, and Ruan Ming’s ideas were even more radical, so they got along well. However, age and status caused some friction: Mr. Cao, from a teacher’s stance, thought he should teach diligently and students should do their homework properly, not slack off on grades due to personal feelings. Ruan Ming, on the other hand, felt that in this messed-up world, a young man with ambition should focus on revolutionary work and not worry about grades. He kept up with Mr. Cao partly because they had good conversations, partly hoping emotional ties would earn him passing marks regardless of his exam results. In chaotic times, idealists often turned a bit rascally, and history had plenty of such excusable examples.

When exams came, Mr. Cao didn’t give Ruan Ming a passing grade. Ruan Ming’s performance would have been enough to get him suspended even if he’d passed. But he especially hated Mr. Cao. He thought Mr. Cao had no sense of face—face, in China, was as valuable as revolution. Eager to do something, Ruan Ming looked down on academics. And because he looked down on academics, he gradually got used to laziness, wanting admiration and affection without any effort; after all, his ideas were progressive! Mr. Cao’s refusal to pass him clearly showed he didn’t understand a young man with ambition; if so, why had they acted so chummy? To be friendly all term and then humiliate him at exams—he thought Mr. Cao was insidious. Since the grades couldn’t be fixed and the suspension couldn’t be fought, he wanted to vent his anger on Mr. Cao. If he was losing his schooling, he’d drag a teacher down with him. That way, he could make a show and prove his toughness. Ruan Ming wasn’t someone to mess with! Besides, if this incident could get him into a new group, it was better than doing nothing.

He compiled what Mr. Cao said in class and in casual chats about politics and society, and reported him to the party headquarters—claiming Mr. Cao was spreading radical ideas among youth.

Mr. Cao had heard rumors but found it amusing. He knew how half-baked his own socialism was and how his traditional love of art hindered any radical action. Ridiculous, that he’d earned the title of revolutionary mentor! Ridiculous, so he didn’t take it seriously, though students and colleagues warned him to be careful. Calmness didn’t ensure safety in chaotic times. Winter break was a good time to purge schools, and detectives were busy investigating and arresting. Mr. Cao had several times felt someone following him. The shadow behind him turned his amusement into seriousness. He had to think: this was a chance to build a reputation—a few days in jail was easier and safer than setting off a bomb, with the same value. Jail time was a qualification for being a big shot. But he wouldn’t do it. He wouldn’t stoop to create a false reputation. In good conscience, he regretted not being a fighter; in good conscience, he also refused to be a fake fighter. He went to Mr. Zuo. Mr. Zuo had ideas: “When necessary, move in here—they won’t search my place!” Mr. Zuo knew people; people were more powerful than the law. “Stay here a few days to lie low. We’ll act like we’re scared of them. Then we’ll smooth things over, maybe shell out some cash. Save face, spend money, and you can go home without trouble.”

Detective Sun knew Mr. Cao often went to Mr. Zuo’s and knew he’d go there if pressured. They didn’t dare offend Mr. Zuo, but they could scare Mr. Cao. By driving him to Mr. Zuo’s, they hoped to get money and save face. Shaking down Xiangzi wasn’t in the detectives’ plans, but since they saw him, why not grab a quick ten or twenty?

Right, Xiangzi was just in the wrong place at the wrong time—tough luck. Everyone had a way out, every place had a crack, but Xiangzi couldn’t escape because he was a rickshaw puller. A rickshaw puller ate coarse grain and coughed up blood; he gave his utmost strength for the lowest pay; he stood at the bottom of society, waiting for every person, every law, every hardship to strike him.

After finishing a cigarette, Xiangzi still couldn’t figure it out. He was like a chicken in a cook’s hand, only able to hope for a breath of relief, with no other plan. He wanted to talk with Old Cheng but had nothing to say; his words couldn’t express his thoughts. He had endured all the suffering, but his mouth wouldn’t open, like a mute. He bought a rickshaw—lost it; saved money—lost it; all his efforts were just for others to bully him! He didn’t dare provoke anyone, even avoided stray dogs, and in the end, he was still bullied until he couldn’t breathe! Forget the past—what about tomorrow? He couldn’t go back to Mr. Cao’s place—where to go? “Can I sleep here tonight?” he asked, like a stray dog finding a windbreak corner, just to endure a little longer; but even this had to be clear, to see if it bothered anyone else.

“Stay here—where else would you go in this freezing weather? Is the floor okay? You could squeeze up here if you want!”

Xiangzi didn’t want to squeeze up; the floor was fine.

Old Cheng fell asleep, but Xiangzi tossed and turned, unable to sleep. The cold from the floor soon turned the bedding as hard as iron. He curled his legs, his calves threatening to cramp. The wind through the door crack stabbed his head like tiny needles. He squeezed his eyes shut, pulled the covers over his head, but couldn’t sleep. Listening to Old Cheng’s snores, he grew agitated, wanting to get up and hit Old Cheng to feel better. It got colder and colder, making his throat itch, but he was afraid of coughing and waking Old Cheng.

Unable to sleep, he thought about sneaking up and going back to Mr. Cao’s place to check. Since everything was over and the yard was empty, why not grab a few things? The money he’d saved with such difficulty was stolen because of Mr. Cao’s affair—why couldn’t he steal something back? He lost the money for Mr. Cao’s sake; having Mr. Cao pay it back would be fair, wouldn’t it? With that thought, his eyes lit up, and he forgot the cold. Go! Money so hard to get, lost, and now so easy to get back—go!

He sat up, then quickly lay back down, as if Old Cheng were watching him! His heart raced. No, he couldn’t be a thief, no! Just now, to clear himself, he hadn’t done what Mr. Cao told him—that was already wrong; how could he steal from him? He couldn’t go! Starve to death, but don’t steal!

But how did he know others wouldn’t steal? Who would know if that Sun guy took some things? He sat up again. A dog barked in the distance. He lay back down. Still, he couldn’t go. If others stole, let them—his conscience was clear. He was this poor, but he couldn’t add another black mark on his heart!

Besides, Gao Ma knew he’d come to the Wangs’. If something went missing tonight, it would be blamed on him whether he did it or not!
