He not only refused to steal, but also feared someone else might break in. If something was lost tonight, he couldn’t wash himself clean even in the Yellow River! He wasn’t cold anymore; his palms were even sweating a little. What to do? Jump back to watch the house? He didn’t dare. His life was bought with money; he couldn’t walk into a trap. If he didn’t go, and something was stolen?

He couldn’t figure it out. He sat up again, legs bent, head nearly touching his knees. His head was heavy, his eyes wanted to close, but he dared not sleep. The night was so long, yet there wasn’t a moment for Xiangzi to shut his eyes.

He sat for who knows how long, changing his mind countless times. Suddenly, a light dawned on him. He reached out and pushed Old Cheng: “Old Cheng! Old Cheng! Wake up!”

“What for?” Old Cheng was very reluctant to open his eyes. “To pee? The chamber pot’s under the bed.”

“Wake up!”"Turn on the light!"  

"What, a thief?" Old Cheng mumbled as he sat up drowsily.  

"Are you fully awake now?"  

"Yeah!"  

"Old Cheng, take a look! This is my bedding, these are my clothes, and here's the five dollars Mr. Cao gave me; that's all, right?"  

"That's it, what's the matter?" Old Cheng yawned.  

"Are you sure you're awake now? My belongings are just these—I didn't take a single blade of grass from the Cao family?"  

"No! We brothers have worked long in mansions; how could we have sticky fingers? If the job's worth doing, we do it; if not, we don't. We don't take other people's things! Is that what this is about?"  

"Are you certain?"  

Old Cheng chuckled: "Absolutely! Say, aren't you cold?"  

"Alright!"  

[1]  

*This refers to a vow made by crossing the bridge of the nose, i.e., a guarantee.*

Thirteen

Because of the snow's glow, it seemed to dawn a bit earlier. As the year-end approached, many families bought and fed chickens, and their crowing was several times more frequent than usual. Cocks crowed everywhere, giving a strong sense of a prosperous year and timely snow. Xiangzi, however, hadn't slept well all night. In the small hours, he dozed off a few times, groggy and half-asleep, rising and falling like floating on water, restless in his heart. The colder he got as he slept, hearing the roosters crow all around, he simply couldn't hold out. Unwilling to disturb Old Cheng, he curled up his legs, used his quilt to cover his mouth while coughing, and still didn't dare get up. He endured, waited, feeling extremely anxious. Finally, he waited until daybreak, when the sounds of cart wheels and drivers' shouts filled the street. He sat up. Sitting was also cold, so he stood, buttoned his clothes, opened the door a crack, and looked outside. The snow wasn't very thick; it must have stopped falling around midnight. The sky seemed clear, but it was hazy and hard to see clearly, even the snow seemed to have a faint gray shadow. In one glance, he saw the large footprints he had left last night, though covered again by snow, the pits were still quite distinct.

Partly to have something to do, and partly to erase the traces, he silently felt for a broom in the corner and went to sweep the snow. The snow was heavy and not easy to sweep, and he couldn't find a large bamboo broom right away. He bent very low, scraping hard; the top layer was swept away, but some snow grains clung to the ground, as if they had gripped the earth. Straightening his back twice, he swept the entire outer courtyard, piling all the snow under two small willow trees. He broke a slight sweat, felt warmer, and a bit lighter. Stamping his feet, he let out a long, long, white breath.

Entering the room, he put the broom back in its place and thought about packing his bedding. Old Cheng woke up, yawned, and before his mouth was even shut, he spoke, “Isn’t it late?” His tone was very complex. After speaking, he wiped his eyes, and casually fished a cigarette out of his leather jacket pocket. After a couple of puffs, he was fully awake. “Xiangzi, don’t leave just yet! Wait for me to fetch some hot water, and we’ll brew a pot of tea to warm ourselves. You must have had a rough night!”

“Should I go?” Xiangzi offered politely. But as soon as he said it, he remembered last night’s terror, and his heart suddenly clenched into a knot.

“No; I’ll go! I still owe you a treat!” With that, Old Cheng quickly put on his clothes, not bothering to button a single one, just tying a sash around his tattered leather jacket, and ran out with a cigarette dangling from his mouth: “Whoa! You’ve swept the whole yard? You’re something! I’ll treat you!” Xiangzi felt a little relieved.

After a while, Old Cheng returned, carrying two large bowls of sweet rice porridge and countless horse-hoof-shaped baked cakes and small fried dough sticks. “Didn’t brew tea, have some porridge first. Come on, eat up; if it’s not enough, I’ll buy more; no money, we can put it on credit; doing hard labor, just don’t pout, come on!”

It was fully daylight now, and the room was cold and bright. The two of them held their bowls and drank loudly, sweetly. Neither spoke, finishing all the cakes and dough sticks in one go. “What do you think?” Old Cheng picked a sesame seed from his teeth.

“Time to go!” Xiangzi looked at the bedroll on the floor.

“Tell me, I still don’t get what happened!” Old Cheng handed Xiangzi a cigarette, but Xiangzi shook his head.

Thinking it over, Xiangzi felt embarrassed not to tell Old Cheng everything. Stammering, he recounted last night’s events. Though it was an effort, he told it quite completely.

Old Cheng pursed his lips for a long time, as if savoring the idea. “The way I see it, you should still go find Mr. Cao. This matter can’t just be dropped, and the money can’t just be lost! Didn’t you just say Mr. Cao told you to run if things looked bad? So, when you got out of the car and were cornered by a detective, who’s to blame? It’s not that you were disloyal; it’s that things turned too sinister, and you had no choice but to save your own skin! As I see it, there’s nothing to be ashamed of. Go, find Mr. Cao, and tell him the whole story from beginning to end. I’m sure he won’t blame you; he might even make up for your lost money! Go on, leave your bedding here, and find him early. The days are short; once the sun’s out, it’ll be eight o’clock. Hurry up!”

Xiangzi’s heart wavered; he still felt sorry for Mr. Cao, but what Old Cheng said made sense—when a detective has a gun to you, how can you worry about the Cao family’s affairs? “Go on!” Old Cheng urged again. “I think you were a bit confused last night; in an emergency, anyone can lose their head. The plan I’ve come up with now is bound to be good. I’m a bit older than you, and I’ve been through more. Go on, isn’t the sun out now?”

The first rays of the morning sun, with the help of the snow, had already lit up the whole city. Blue sky, white snow, light on the sky, light on the snow, a golden shimmer flashing between the blue and white, making it so dazzling one couldn’t open one’s eyes! Just as Xiangzi was about to leave, someone knocked at the door. Old Cheng went out to see and called from the doorway: “Xiangzi! Someone’s looking for you!”

Wang Er from the Zuo residence, his nose dripping from the cold, was stamping the snow off his feet in the doorway. Seeing Xiangzi come out, Old Cheng said, “Come inside, all of you!” The three of them entered the room.

“Well,” Wang Er said, rubbing his hands, “I’ve come to check on the house, but how do I get in? The gate’s locked. Well, it’s cold after the snow, really cold! Well, Mr. Cao and Mrs. Cao left early this morning; to Tianjin, maybe Shanghai, I’m not sure. Mr. Zuo asked me to check the house. Well, it’s really cold!”

Xiangzi suddenly felt like crying! Just as he was about to follow Old Cheng’s advice and go find Mr. Cao, Mr. Cao had already left. He stood stunned for a long time, then asked, “Did Mr. Cao say anything about me?”

“Well, no. They got up before dawn, barely had time to talk. The train, well, leaves at 7:40! Well, how do I get over to that yard?” Wang Er was eager to go over.

“Jump over it!” Xiangzi glanced at Old Cheng, as if handing Wang Er over to him, and picked up his own bedroll.

“Where are you going?” Old Cheng asked.

“Renhe Shed. There’s nowhere else to go!” This one sentence said it all: Xiangzi’s grievance, shame, and helplessness. He had no other choice but to surrender! All roads were blocked; he could only go on the snowy ground to find that black tower-like Tigress. He cared about face, wanted to be strong, was loyal, and righteous; none of it was any use, because he had a “dog’s” life!

Old Cheng took over: “You go on your way. Here, in front of Wang Er, you didn’t touch a single thing at the Cao’s! Go on. When you’re on this street, drop by for a chat. Maybe I’ll find out some good news and recommend you something. After you leave, I’ll send Wang Er over there. Is there any coal?”

“Coal and firewood are in the back courtyard shed.” Xiangzi shouldered his bedroll.

The snow on the streets wasn’t so white anymore; on the road, it was pressed down by cart wheels, revealing ice. On the dirt paths, it was trampled by horses into patches of black and white, a pity. Xiangzi didn’t think about anything, just carrying his bedroll and walking forward. He went straight to Renhe Shed. He didn’t dare stop, knowing that if he did, he wouldn’t have the courage to go in. He walked straight in, his face burning hot. He had prepared a sentence to say to Tigress: “I’m here. Do what you will! Whatever you do is fine, I’m out of options!” But when he saw her, he turned the words over in his mind several times, but couldn’t get them out; his mouth wasn’t that quick.

Tigress had just gotten up, her hair messy, her eyelids puffy, her dark face covered with a layer of fine goosebumps, like a plucked frozen chicken.

“Oh! You’re back!” She was very affectionate, her eyes shining with laughter. “Rent me a rickshaw!” Xiangzi said, looking down at the unmelted snow on his shoe tips.

“Go talk to the old man,” she said softly, then nodded toward the eastern room.

Fourth Master Liu was inside drinking tea, a large white stove in front of him, flames leaping half a foot high. Seeing Xiangzi come in, he said, half annoyed, half laughing, “You’re still alive, kid?! Forgotten me, have you? Let’s see, how many days have you been gone? How’s it going? Bought a rickshaw?”

Xiangzi shook his head, his heart aching as if stabbed. “I still need a rickshaw to pull, Fourth Master!”

“Hmph, another plan falls through! Fine, go pick one yourself!” Fourth Master Liu poured a bowl of tea. “Here, have a bowl first.”

Xiangzi picked up the bowl, stood in front of the stove, and drank in big gulps. The tea was very hot, the fire very warm, and he felt a bit drowsy. He put the bowl down and was about to leave when Fourth Master Liu called him back.

“Wait, where’s the rush? Let me tell you: you came just in time. The twenty-seventh is my birthday, and I’m going to put up an awning and host a party. You can help out for a few days; no need to pull the rickshaw for now. Those fellows,” Fourth Master Liu pointed toward the yard, “are unreliable. I won’t have them loafing around and messing things up. You help out. Do whatever needs to be done, no need to wait for me to tell you. Go sweep the snow first. At noon, I’ll treat you to hot pot.” “Yes, Fourth Master!” Xiangzi had made up his mind. Since he was back here, he’d leave everything to the Liu father and daughter; they could arrange him however they liked, he’d accept his fate! “See?” Tigress came in right on cue. “It’s still Xiangzi. The others just don’t measure up.”

Fourth Master Liu laughed. Xiangzi lowered his head even more.

“Come on, Xiangzi!” Tigress called him outside. “Here’s some money, go buy a broom first, a bamboo one, good for sweeping snow. We need to hurry; the awning builders are coming today.” Once in her room, she counted out the money for him and said softly, “Look sharp! Win the old man over! Our prospects are looking up!”

Xiangzi said nothing, nor did he get angry. He seemed to have given up, not thinking about anything, just muddling through day by day. He ate when there was food, drank when there was drink, worked when there was work, keeping his hands and feet busy; a few turns and the day was done. He thought it best to be like a mill donkey: know nothing when asked, just keep pulling the mill.

But he could also feel that, no matter what, he couldn’t be happy. Though he refused to think, talk, or lose his temper, something always weighed on his heart. He forgot it while working, but as soon as he had a moment’s rest, he’d feel it—soft, but always the same size; no distinct taste, but choking, like a sponge. With this lump in his heart, he forced himself to work, trying to tire himself out completely so he could sleep in a daze. He left the night’s affairs to his dreams, the day’s affairs to his hands and feet; he was like a corpse that could still work. He swept snow, bought things, ordered gas lamps, washed the rickshaw, moved tables and chairs, ate Fourth Master Liu’s reward meals, slept—he knew nothing, said nothing, thought nothing, only faintly felt that sponge-like thing!

The snow on the ground was swept clean, the snow on the roofs gradually melted, and the awning builders, shouting out their heights, climbed up to set up the frame. It was agreed to be a heated awning covering the entire yard, with eaves on three sides, railings on three sides, and glass windows on three sides. Inside the awning were glass partitions, hanging screens, and every piece of wood was wrapped in red cloth. The main and side doors were hung with colored silk, and the kitchen was set up in the back courtyard. Fourth Master Liu, celebrating his sixty-ninth birthday, wanted to do it up grandly, so first and foremost, he wanted a stylish awning. With the short days, the builders only managed to set up the awning frame, install the railings and cloth; the intricate decorations inside and the colored silk at the doors had to be hung the next morning. Fourth Master Liu flew into a rage over this, his face red with anger. Because of this, he sent Xiangzi to hurry the gas lamps and the cook, making sure nothing was delayed. Actually, neither of these things would be delayed, but the old man was uneasy. Xiangzi had just come back from that errand when Fourth Master Liu sent him to borrow mahjong sets, three or four of them, so they could gamble heartily on the day. After borrowing the mahjong, he was sent off again to borrow a gramophone; for a birthday, there had to be some noise. Xiangzi’s legs didn’t stop for a moment, running until eleven o’clock at night. Used to pulling a rickshaw, walking empty-handed was even more tiring than running; on his last trip back, even he could barely lift his feet. “Good lad! You’re something! If I had a son like you, I’d be happy to live a few years less! Go rest, there’s still work tomorrow!”

Beside him, Tigress winked at Xiangzi.

The next morning, the awning builders came back to finish the work. Colored screens were hung, depicting battle scenes from the “Three Kingdoms”—the Three Battles with Lü Bu, the Battle of Changban Slope, the Fire at the Allied Camp—painted characters with painted faces, both major and minor, all mounted on horses brandishing swords and spears. Old Man Liu looked up and surveyed the work, feeling very satisfied. Then, the furniture shop came to deliver the furnishings: eight seats were set up in the awning, with aprons, cushions, and seat covers all of bright red embroidered silk. A birthday altar was set up in the main hall, with incense burners and candle holders all made of cloisonné, and four red felt rugs were placed before the table. Old Man Liu immediately sent Xiangzi to order a display of apples, while Tigress secretly pressed two dollars into his hand, telling him to order birthday peaches and noodles, with a set of the Eight Immortals on the peaches, as if they were a gift from Xiangzi. The apples arrived and were arranged at once; shortly after, the birthday peaches and noodles came, placed behind the apples. The large birthday peaches had red tips and were adorned with the Eight Immortals, looking very grand.

“Xiangzi’s gift, see how thoughtful he is!” Tigress whispered into her father’s ear, and Fourth Master Liu smiled at Xiangzi.

The center of the birthday altar was still missing a large character for “Longevity,” which customarily was given by friends, not prepared oneself. Since no one had sent one yet, Fourth Master Liu grew impatient and began to lose his temper: “I go all out for everyone else’s happy or sad occasions, but when it’s my turn, they leave me high and dry!”"Go to hell!"

"Tomorrow's the twenty-sixth, the feast's not till then, so what's the rush?" Tigress yelled to comfort him. "I'd rather have everything set out all at once; all this piecemeal stuff is maddening! I say, Xiangzi, the gas lamps have to be installed today, and if they're not here by four, I'll skin them alive!"

"Xiangzi, go and give them another push!" Tigress deliberately relied on him, always calling Xiangzi to do things in front of her father. Xiangzi said nothing, listened, understood, and left.

"Not that I'm saying it, Old Man," she pursed her lips and said, "if you had a son, he'd either be like me or like Xiangzi! Too bad I was born wrong. But there's no helping that. Actually, having a foster son like Xiangzi wouldn't be bad! Look at him, doesn't let out a single fart all day, but gets everything done!"

Fourth Master Liu didn't reply. He thought for a moment: "What about the phonograph? Let's have some music!"

The broken-down phonograph, borrowed from who knows where, screeched every note like stepping on a cat's tail! Fourth Master Liu didn't mind, as long as there was some noise.

By afternoon, everything was ready, just waiting for the cook to come the next day to set things up. Fourth Master Liu made a round of inspection everywhere was decorated in red and green, and he nodded to himself. That evening, he went to invite Mr. Feng, the accountant from Tianshun Coal Shop, to handle the books. Mr. Feng was a Shanxi native and most meticulous with accounts. Mr. Feng came over immediately, looked things over, and told Xiangzi to buy two red account books and a sheet of red paper. He cut the red paper and wrote some characters for 'longevity', pasting them up everywhere. Fourth Master Liu thought Mr. Feng was really meticulous and wanted to invite him for a few rounds of mahjong. But Mr. Feng knew Fourth Master Liu's prowess and didn't take the bait. The mahjong game didn't happen, which put Fourth Master Liu in a bit of a mood. He rounded up a few rickshaw pullers: "Open a gambling game, do you have the guts?"

Everyone wanted to, but none dared to gamble with Fourth Master Liu. Who didn't know he used to run a gambling house!

"You bunch of good-for-nothings, how did you even survive!" Fourth Master flew into a rage. "When I was your age, I'd dare to do it with not a copper in my pocket. I'd think about losing later. Come on!"

"Betting coppers?" one puller asked tentatively.

"Keep your coppers, Liu doesn't play games with children!" The old man downed a cup of tea in one gulp and rubbed his bald head. "Forget it, I wouldn't come even if you begged me! I say, you go tell everyone: the feast is tomorrow. Relatives and friends will start arriving in the afternoon. Everyone must return their rickshaws by four o'clock. No shoving in and out with the carts! Your rental fees for today are waived. Return the carts by four. Let you pull for a whole day free, just remember to wish me some good luck in your hearts, don't be ungrateful! The main day is the day after tomorrow. Nobody is allowed to pull. At eight-thirty in the morning, I'll feed you first: six big bowls, two seven-inch dishes, four small plates, one hotpot – that's fair to you! Everyone wear a long gown; anyone who comes in short clothes gets kicked out! After you eat, get the hell out so I can entertain my relatives and friends. They'll have three seafood bowls, six cold dishes, six stir-fried dishes, four big bowls, and one hotpot. I'm telling you now, so don't get jealous. Relatives and friends are relatives and friends; I don't want anything from you. Those with a conscience can give me a gift of ten coppers, I won't think it's too little; if you give me nothing but kowtow three times, I'll accept that too. But you must behave yourselves. Got it? If you want to eat here again in the evening, come back after six o'clock. Everything left over is yours. But don't come back early! Understand?"

"Some of us pull late shifts tomorrow, Fourth Master," a middle-aged puller asked. "Why do we have to return the carts by four?"

"Those on late shifts can come back after eleven! Just don't come shoving around when people are in the tent! You pull rickshaws, I'm not in the same trade as you, understand?"

No one had anything more to say, but they couldn't find a way to leave. Standing there was awkward. Fourth Master Liu's words left everyone with a knot of resentment in their hearts. Although having a day's rental fee waived was a bargain, who would eat for free? At the very least, they had to give a gift of forty coppers. Besides, Fourth Master's words were so unpleasant, as if for his birthday party, they had to scurry away like mice. Moreover, forbidding them from pulling on the main day, the twenty-seventh, right at the end of the year when business was good, was a problem. Fourth Master Liu could afford to lose a day's income, but they couldn't afford to loaf around for a day! They stood there, daring to be angry but not to speak, and certainly weren't wishing Fourth Master Liu good luck in their hearts.

Tigress tugged at Xiangzi, and he followed her out.

Everyone's anger seemed to suddenly find an outlet. They all glared at Xiangzi's back. For the past couple of days, everyone felt that Xiangzi was the Liu family's lackey, desperately currying favor, taking on all the menial tasks without complaint. Xiangzi was completely unaware of this. Helping the Liu family was his way of distracting himself from his own troubles. He had nothing to say to them in the evenings because he genuinely had nothing to talk about. They didn't know his grievances but thought he had latched onto Fourth Master Liu and therefore disdained to talk to them. Tigress's attentiveness to Xiangzi particularly stung them. Thinking about the current situation, Fourth Master Liu wouldn't let them near the festive tent, but Xiangzi would certainly be able to eat well all day. Why were there different classes among rickshaw pullers? Look, Miss Liu was calling Xiangzi out again! Their eyes followed Xiangzi, and their feet itched to move. They all shuffled out, making excuses. Miss Liu was talking to Xiangzi under the gaslight. They nodded knowingly at each other.

[1] "Nazhe shihou" means estimating an appropriate time.

[2] "Shuiyue deng" means gas lamp.

[3] "Pao" means to dawdle or kill time, a deliberate action.

[4] "Suicui" means a servant who does odd jobs.

14

The Liu family's event was very lively. Fourth Master Liu was very pleased that so many people came to kowtow and wish him a happy birthday. What made him even prouder was that many old friends had also hurried over to offer their congratulations. Through these old friends, he could see that his event was not only lively but also "reformed." The clothes and accessories of those old friends were already outdated, while Fourth Master's fur robe and mandarin jacket were newly made. In terms of occupation, several friends were wealthier than him back in the day, but now—after the changes of these past twenty or thirty years—they had fallen lower and lower, some even struggling to get enough to eat. Looking at them, then looking at his own festive tent, birthday hall, the hanging screen depicting the Battle of Changban Slope, and the three-seafood-bowl feast, he felt he was indeed a cut above them. He had "reformed." Even for gambling, he had prepared mahjong tiles, which seemed much more refined than guessing the dice. Yet, amidst this lively scene, he also felt a touch of desolation and sadness. Accustomed to a solitary life, he had originally thought that only shopkeepers and accountants, and old friends from the underworld would come for his birthday. He hadn't expected some female guests as well. Although Tigress could entertain them on his behalf, he suddenly felt his own loneliness. He had no wife, only a daughter, and she looked like a man. If Tigress had been a man, she would have long since married and had children. Even if he were an old widower, perhaps he wouldn't feel so utterly alone. Yes, he lacked nothing but a son. The older he got, the slimmer the hope of having a son. A birthday celebration was supposed to be a happy event, yet it felt like it should be a time for tears. No matter how much he reformed, without someone to carry on his business, wasn't it all for nothing?

In the first half of the day, he was very happy. Everyone offered him birthday greetings, and he accepted them generously, as if feeling he was the preeminent old hero among them. In the afternoon, his spirits dampened a little. Seeing the children brought by the female guests, he felt both envy and jealousy. He didn't dare get close to them, and not getting close made him feel awkward. He wanted to throw a tantrum but didn't want to lose face in front of his relatives and friends. He knew he was a man of the world and couldn't make a scene. He wished the day would end quickly so he wouldn't have to suffer any longer.

There was another blemish on the day. During the morning meal for the rickshaw pullers, Xiangzi almost got into a fight.

The meal was served after eight o'clock, and the pullers were a bit unhappy. Although their rental fee had been waived the day before, none of them had come empty-handed to eat. Whether it was a dime or forty coppers, everyone had brought a gift of some size. Usually, they were poor laborers, and Liu was the yard owner. But today, in their view, they were guests and shouldn't be treated this way.

Besides, they had to leave right after eating and weren't even allowed to take their rickshaws out, and at the end of the year at that!

Xiangzi knew for sure that he wasn't among those who had to leave right after eating, but he still wanted to eat with the others. First, finishing early would let him get on with his work; second, it would show goodwill. He sat down with the group, and they transferred all their dissatisfaction with Liu onto him. As soon as he sat down, someone said, "Hey, you're an honored guest here, why are you sitting with us?" Xiangzi gave a foolish grin, not catching the sarcasm in the words. For days, he hadn't spoken a casual word himself, and his brain seemed to have stopped working properly. Not daring to vent at Liu, the pullers could only eat a bit more to get back at him. There was no refill on the dishes, but there was no limit on the alcohol—it was wedding feast wine! They tacitly decided to drown their anger in drink. Some drank in silence; others started playing drinking games. Old Liu couldn't stop them from playing games. Seeing everyone drinking, Xiangzi didn't want to seem too aloof, so he downed a couple of cups too. As they drank, their eyes turned red, and their mouths ran loose. One said, "Xiangzi, Camel, you've got a sweet gig! Eat all day, wait on the master and miss! Tomorrow you won't need to pull a rickshaw anymore, you'd better become a personal attendant!" Xiangzi sensed a bit of malice, but it didn't really sink in. Ever since he entered Harmony Yard, he had decided not to play the tough guy anymore, to let fate take its course. Whoever wanted to say anything could say it. He held his temper. Another chimed in, "Xiangzi here is taking a different path. We earn our money by brute strength, but Xiangzi uses inner energy!" Everyone burst out laughing. Xiangzi realized they were "biting" at him, but having endured so much grievance already, why bother about a few idle words? He stayed silent. A man at a nearby table saw an opening and craned his neck to shout, "Xiangzi, when you become the yard owner tomorrow, don't forget your buddies!" Xiangzi still said nothing. Then someone at his own table spoke up: "Say something, Camel!"

Xiangzi's face reddened. He muttered, "How could I become the yard owner?!" "Hah! How couldn't you? Looks like it's almost time for the wedding drum!" Xiangzi didn't catch the literal meaning of "dong dong cang" [wedding music], but intuitively guessed it referred to his relationship with Tigress. His face slowly turned from red to white. All the grievances he had suffered came flooding back, clogging his heart. Days of patient silence seemed about to break, like pent-up water finding an outlet, ready to burst forth. Just then, another puller pointed at his face and said, "Xiangzi, I'm talking to you. You're the one who's really 'the mute eating dumplings—knowing the count in his heart.' Isn't that right? Speak up, Xiangzi? Xiangzi?"

Xiangzi shot up, his face ashen. He faced the man and asked, "Say it outside, do you dare?"

Everyone was stunned. They had indeed meant to "bite" at him and make rude jokes, but they hadn't planned on a fight.

Suddenly, it was quiet, like forest birds suddenly spotting a hawk. Xiangzi stood alone, much taller than the others. He felt his own isolation. But with anger in his heart, he was also deeply convinced that even if they all jumped him, he could take them. He added, "Is there anyone who dares to step outside?"

They suddenly came to their senses and said almost in unison, "Enough, Xiangzi, we're just joking with you!"

Fourth Master Liu saw it: "Sit down, Xiangzi!" Then to the others, "Don't bully someone just because he's honest. If I get angry, I'll kick you all out! Eat up!" Xiangzi left the table. The others glanced sideways at Old Liu and picked up their food. Soon, they were whispering and chattering again, like forest birds after a danger has passed, softly chirping once more.

Xiangzi squatted by the door for a long time, waiting for them. If any of them dared to say another idle word, he'd beat them up! He had nothing left anyway, so he might as well go all out! But they came out in groups of three or five and didn't bother him. Although no fight broke out, he had let off some steam. Then he thought further: his outburst today had probably offended many people. He hadn't had any close friends to begin with, which was why he had nowhere to vent his bitterness. How could he afford to make more enemies? He felt a twinge of regret. The little food he had just eaten sat heavily in his stomach, causing a dull ache. He stood up. To hell with it! The guys who fought and caused trouble every few days seemed to live interesting lives, didn't they? Did being honest and well-behaved always pay off? Thinking this way, he mentally drew a new path for himself. The Xiangzi on this path was completely different from the one he had dreamed of before. This was a person who would befriend everyone, take advantage wherever possible, drink other people's tea, smoke other people's cigarettes, borrow money without repaying it, refuse to dodge cars, piss wherever he pleased, play tricks on the police all day long, and consider being locked up in the police station for a couple of days nothing serious. Yes, this kind of rickshaw puller also lived, and lived happily, at least happier than Xiangzi. Alright then, since being honest, well-behaved, and ambitious was useless, turning into this kind of scoundrel wasn't bad. Not only wasn't it bad, Xiangzi thought, it even had a heroic, fearless spirit about it, absolutely refusing to swallow one's grievances in silence. Right! That's what he should do! A bad egg was created from a good person. On the contrary, he now regretted that the fight hadn't happened. Never mind, there was no hurry. From now on, he would never bow his head to anyone again.Fourth Master Liu wouldn’t tolerate any nonsense. Piecing together everything he’d heard and seen, he had a pretty clear picture in his mind. These last few days, his daughter had been unusually obedient—hah, all because Xiangzi was back! The way her eyes kept following him around. The old man kept this to himself, and it only made him feel more bitter and desolate. Think about it—no son to begin with, couldn’t build a lively family; and now if his daughter ran off with someone, his whole life’s efforts would be wasted! Xiangzi wasn’t bad, sure, but as a son-in-law, he fell far short—just a filthy rickshaw puller! He’d spent his whole life struggling, brawling in gang fights, kneeling on iron chains, and now in the end, some country bumpkin was going to take both his daughter and his property? No such luck! Even if it were possible, Liu wouldn’t let it happen! Fourth Master Liu had been a troublemaker since childhood!

Around three or four in the afternoon, a few guests came to offer birthday greetings, but the old man already felt bored. The more they praised his health and fortune, the less meaning it held for him.

By the time lamps were lit, the guests had gradually left, leaving only a dozen or so close friends and neighbors nearby. Looking at the empty awning in the yard, lit bluish by the limelight, and the tables stripped of their cloths, the old man felt lonely and pointless, as if envisioning his own death—just like this, except the festive awning would be replaced by a white one, with no sons or grandsons in mourning before the coffin, just a bunch of irrelevant people playing mahjong through the night! He felt like driving out the remaining guests; while he still had breath, he ought to show some authority! But in the end, he couldn’t bring himself to take it out on his friends. His anger swerved, and the more he looked at his daughter, the more she irked him. Xiangzi sat under the awning, looking smug, the scar on his face glowing like a piece of jade in the lamplight. The more the old man watched this pair, the more uncomfortable he felt!

Tiger Girl had always been wild and unrestrained. Today, she was all decked out from head to toe, pretending to be polite to the guests—partly to win their praise and partly to show off in front of Xiangzi. In the morning, she found it kind of amusing, but by afternoon, tiredness set in, and she grew annoyed, wanting to pick a fight with someone. By evening, she had no patience left at all, her eyebrows knitting together stubbornly.

By seven o’clock, Fourth Master Liu felt drowsy but refused to admit his age and go to bed. Everyone invited him to join a few rounds of cards, but he claimed he wasn’t in the mood, saying card games weren’t satisfying and that he preferred betting or dominoes. No one wanted to switch games, so he just sat to the side. To perk himself up, he decided to have a few more drinks, insisting he hadn’t eaten enough and complaining the cook had overcharged him—the food wasn’t plentiful. Starting from this, he overturned all the satisfaction he’d felt during the day: the awning, the furniture, the cook, and everything else weren’t worth that much money—they’d all cheated him, taken him for a fool!

Accountant Feng had already tallied the gifts: twenty-five birthday scrolls, three sets of birthday peaches and noodles, one jar of birthday wine, two pairs of birthday candles, and about twenty dollars in cash gifts. The numbers were decent, but most people had given forty coppers or a dime.

Hearing this report, Fourth Master Liu got even angrier. If he’d known, he would have prepared “noodle stir-fry”! Eating a full three-bowl feast and only giving a dime? This was just treating the old man like a fool! He’d never host another event—couldn’t afford to lose money like this! No doubt, all these so-called friends and relatives just wanted to freeload off him; at sixty-nine, he’d been shrewd all his life but lost his head for a moment, letting a bunch of scoundrels take advantage of him! The more he thought, the angrier he got, even counting his earlier satisfaction as foolishness. He muttered his thoughts aloud, laced with curses no longer common on the streets.

The friends hadn’t all left yet. Tiger Girl, wanting to save face for everyone, tried to stop her father’s outburst. But seeing everyone focused on their cards, seemingly ignoring his rambling, she didn’t speak up, afraid of making things worse. Let him mutter—everyone would just let it slide.

Who’d have thought that as he grumbled, he’d turn on her. She decided she wouldn’t stand for it! He’d thrown this birthday party, and she’d been busy for days, only to get no thanks—she couldn’t put up with it! Sixty-nine or seventy-nine, it didn’t matter—he had to be reasonable! She shot back immediately:

“You chose to spend money on this party—what’s it got to do with me?”

The old man perked up at the counterattack. “What’s it got to do with you? It’s all about you! You think I don’t see what’s going on?”

“What do you see? I’ve been exhausted all day, and now you take it out on me? Hold on! Tell me, what did you see?” Tiger Girl’s fatigue vanished, her tongue sharp.

“Don’t look at me like you’re jealous! See? I’ve seen it all for a while now, huh!”

“Why would I be jealous?!” She shook her head. “What exactly did you see?”

“There!” Fourth Master pointed under the awning—Xiangzi was bending over sweeping the floor. “Him?” Tiger Girl’s heart skipped a beat; she hadn’t expected her father’s eyes to be so sharp. “Huh! What about him?”

“Stop playing dumb when you know perfectly well!” The old man stood up. “Make your choice—him or me. I’m telling you straight. I’m your father! I have the right to control this!”

Tiger Girl hadn’t expected things to come to a head so fast; her plan was barely half done, and the old man had already called it out! What now? Her face flushed—a mix of black, red, half-worn powder, and the bright lamplight, like over-boiled liver, ugly and complicated. She was tired; this provocation stirred her temper, leaving her mind scattered with no plan. She couldn’t back down. Even flustered, she needed an idea fast. A half-baked plan was better than none—she’d never yielded to anyone! Fine, let’s cut to the chase, sink or swim on this one throw! “Let’s clear the air today, treat it like a tab. What’s your move? I want to hear it! This is your own doing, don’t say I’m trying to rile you!”

The card players seemed to hear their quarrel but couldn’t spare attention; to drown out the noise, they slapped their cards harder, shouting “red” and “pong”... Xiangzi had caught on but kept his head down, sweeping. He felt steady—if it came to a fight, he’d throw down.

“Are you trying to enrage me?!” The old man’s eyes were wide as saucers. “Kill me off so you can throw yourself away on him? Don’t count on it—I’ve got years left!” “Enough nonsense, what’s your decision?” Tiger Girl’s heart pounded, but her words were tough. “My decision? Like I said, him or me! I won’t let some filthy rickshaw puller have it all!”

Xiangzi threw down the broom, straightened up, locked eyes with Liu, and asked, “Who are you talking about?” Liu burst into harsh laughter: “Haha, you little rebel, trying to start something? You, that’s who! Get out of here right now! I thought you were decent, so I showed you respect, and you dare to cross me? Do you know who I am? Go find out! Scram! Never let me see you again, looking for a free ride here, huh?”

The old man’s voice was so loud it drew a few rickshaw pullers to watch. The card players thought Liu was arguing with another puller and still didn’t look up.

Xiangzi wasn’t quick with words; he had plenty to say but nothing reached his tongue. He stood there dumbly, swallowing hard. “Get out! Now! Looking for a deal here? I was pulling tricks before you were even born, huh!” The old man was mostly just trying to scare Xiangzi; his hatred for him wasn’t as deep as for his daughter, and even angry, he felt Xiangzi was an honest guy.

“Fine, I’m leaving!” Xiangzi had nothing to say, so he just left quickly—there was no way he could out-argue them.

The rickshaw pullers had come to watch, and seeing Liu curse Xiangzi, they remembered the morning scrap and felt amused. But when the old man drove Xiangzi out, they sided with him—Xiangzi had worked so hard, and now Liu was burning the bridge, turning his back. They felt sorry for him. Some came over to ask, “What’s up, Xiangzi?” He shook his head.

“Xiangzi, wait!” Tiger Girl’s mind flashed like lightning: her plan was useless now; she had to grab Xiangzi fast before everything fell apart! “We’re in this together, like two grasshoppers tied on one string—neither of us is getting away! Wait, let me make this clear!” She turned to her father: “I’ll say it straight—I’m pregnant, with Xiangzi’s kid! Where he goes, I go! Are you giving me to him, or kicking us both out? Your call!”

Tiger Girl hadn’t expected things to move so fast, using her last resort this early. Fourth Master Liu was even more stunned. But since it had come to this, he couldn’t back down, especially in front of everyone. “You’ve got the nerve to say that—my old face burns for you!” He slapped his own cheek. “Bah! Shameless!”

The card players stopped their hands, sensing something off, but confused, they didn’t know what was happening and couldn’t jump in. Some stood up, others stared blankly at their cards.

Now that it was all out, Tiger Girl felt relieved: “Shameless me? Don’t make me bring up your dirt—you’ve done everything! This is my first time, and it’s all your fault: men should marry, women should wed. You’re sixty-nine and still clueless! Right here in front of everyone,” she pointed around, “let’s get this straight, clear as day! Use this festive awning and throw another party!”

“Me?” Liu’s face went from red to white, unleashing his old tough-guy attitude: “I’d burn this awning down before letting you use it!” “Fine!” Tiger Girl’s lips trembled, her voice harsh, “I’ll pack up and leave—how much will you give me?”

“My money’s mine—I give it to whoever I want!” The old man felt a pang hearing she’d leave, but pride hardened his heart.

“Your money? I’ve helped you for years; without me, your money would’ve gone to loose women—let’s be fair!” Her eyes found Xiangzi: “You say something!”

Xiangzi stood rigid, saying nothing.No gold-inlaid lanterns, no bridal attendants. Everything was settled - she had made herself a red satin wedding gown and finished it before the New Year, so she wouldn't have to touch a needle before the fifth day of the first lunar month. The wedding day was set for the sixth day of the first month, which was both an auspicious day and one that didn't require avoiding the doorstep. She had handled all of this herself, telling Xiangzi that he had to buy everything new from head to toe: "It only happens once in a lifetime!"

Xiangzi only had five dollars on him!

Tigress glanced at him again: "What? What about the thirty-some dollars I gave you?"

Xiangzi had no choice but to tell the truth, revealing everything about what happened at Mr. Cao's house. She blinked, half believing, half doubting: "Fine, I don't have time to argue with you. Let's each follow our own conscience! Here, take this fifteen dollars! If you're not dressed up like a proper bridegroom by the big day, you'd better watch out!"

On the sixth day of the first month, Tigress mounted the bridal sedan chair. She didn't exchange a single word with her father, had no brothers to escort her, and received no congratulations from friends or relatives. Only the gongs and drums made a lively racket on the streets after the New Year as the bridal sedan slowly passed through Xi'an Gate and Xisi Pailou, catching the attention of people in new clothes - especially shop assistants - stirring both envy and emotion.

Xiangzi, wearing new clothes bought from Tianqiao, his face flushed red, donned a satin cap that cost three dimes. He seemed to have forgotten himself, foolishly watching and listening to everything, as if he no longer recognized himself. He moved from a coal shop into a newly papered room that was dazzling white, unsure of what was happening: the past was like the coal yard, piles and piles of black; now he was dazedly entering the new room, so white it hurt his eyes, pasted with several blood-red double happiness characters. He felt a kind of mockery, something white, vague, and suffocating. In the room were Tigress's original tables, chairs, and bed; the stove and kitchen counter were new; in the corner stood a duster made of five-colored chicken feathers. He recognized the furniture, but the stove, counter, and chicken feather duster felt unfamiliar. The mixture of old and new objects reminded him of the past and made him worry about the future. Everything was arranged by others; he himself was like something old, yet something new - some kind of ornament, some strange thing; he no longer recognized himself. He couldn't think of crying, couldn't think of laughing; his big hands and feet moved about in the small, warm room like a large rabbit in a small wooden cage, its red eyes looking inside and out, having legs that could run fast but nowhere to run! Tigress, wearing a red jacket, her face powdered and rouged, her eyes sliding over him. He dared not look her directly in the eye. She too was some strange thing, both old and new - a maiden, yet a woman; like a female, yet like a male; like a person, yet like some ferocious beast! This beast, in its red jacket, had caught him and was preparing to deal with him thoroughly. Anyone could handle him, but this beast was particularly fierce, intending to stay with him every moment, glaring at him, laughing at him, and able to hold him tightly, draining all his strength. He had no way to escape. He took off the satin cap, stared blankly at the red knot on top until his eyes blurred - when he turned his head, the wall was covered in red dots, spinning and dancing, with one larger red dot in the middle - Tigress's face, wearing an ugly smile! On their wedding night, Xiangzi finally understood: Tigress wasn't pregnant at all. Like a magician, she explained to him: "If I hadn't tricked you like this, how could I have gotten you to nod so wholeheartedly! I stuffed a pillow in my pants! Haha, haha!" She laughed until tears came: "You fool! Forget it, I've treated you well enough; what kind of person are you, and what kind of person am I? I dared to quarrel with Dad to follow you - shouldn't you be thanking heaven and earth?"

The next day, Xiangzi went out very early. Most shops had already opened for business, but some were still closed. The Spring Festival couplets on the doors were still bright red, but some of the yellow hanging money had been torn by the wind. The streets were very quiet, but there were plenty of rickshaws, and the pullers seemed a bit more spirited than usual, almost all wearing new shoes, some even with red paper pasted on the back of their rickshaws. Xiangzi envied these rickshaw pullers, feeling that they at least had something of the New Year spirit, while he had been stifled in a gourd for days; they all got by contentedly, while he had no livelihood, just wandering aimlessly on the main street. He wasn't comfortable with idleness, but thinking about tomorrow meant he had to discuss it with Tigress - his wife; he was living off his wife - such a wife! - for food. All that height and all that strength he had were useless. First and foremost, he had to serve his wife, that thing in the red jacket with the tiger teeth; something that drained a person's vitality; he was no longer a person, just a piece of meat. He had lost himself, struggling only between her teeth, like a mouse caught by a cat. He didn't want to discuss things with her; he had to leave. He thought of a plan: to leave without saying goodbye. There was nothing wrong with that; she was a female demon who could use a pillow to trick him! He was sick at heart; he not only wanted to tear up his new clothes but also wash himself from the inside out in clean water, feeling that his whole body was covered with something unclean and disgusting that filled him with loathing. He wished he could never see her face again!

Where could he go? He had no destination. On normal days when he pulled his rickshaw, his legs followed other people's directions. Today, his legs were free, but his heart was at a loss. Following Xisi Pailou straight south, he went out through Xuanwu Gate: the road was so straight, but his mind couldn't turn a corner. Outside the city gate, still heading south, he saw a bathhouse. He decided to take a bath.

Stripping completely naked and looking at his own body, he felt extremely ashamed. Going down into the pool, the hot water numbed his whole body. He closed his eyes, his skin tingling as if releasing accumulated filth. He almost didn't dare to touch himself, his mind empty, big beads of sweat rolling down his head. Only when his breathing became a bit labored did he lazily climb out, his whole body red, like a newborn baby. He almost didn't dare to walk out like that; wrapping himself in a large towel, he still felt ugly; although sweat was dripping down, he still felt unclean - that dirt in his heart seemed impossible to wash away forever. In the eyes of Fourth Master Liu and everyone who knew him, he would always be a man who stole women!

Before the sweat had completely dried, he hurriedly put on his clothes and ran out. He was afraid of everyone seeing him naked! Once out of the bathhouse, cooled by the wind, he felt a lightness in his body. The streets were much livelier than before. The bright, clear sky gave everyone's face a bit of radiance. But Xiangzi's heart was still twisted, not knowing where to go. South, then east, then south again, he headed for Tianqiao. After the New Year, around nine or ten in the morning, the apprentices from the shops had already finished breakfast and come here. Various stalls and performance grounds were set up and occupied early. When Xiangzi arrived, crowds were already gathered in circles, with gongs and drums playing inside. He had no heart to watch any entertainment; he had already forgotten how to laugh.

On ordinary days, the comedians, bear tamers, magicians, street singers, yangge dancers, storytellers, and martial artists here could all provide him with genuine joy, making him laugh heartily. He couldn't bear to leave Beiping, and Tianqiao was half the reason. Whenever he looked at the mat sheds of Tianqiao and the circles of people, he would think of many funny and lovely things. Now he couldn't be bothered to push through the crowd; there was no place for him in Tianqiao's laughter anymore. He avoided the crowds and walked toward quieter places, but then felt reluctant to leave! No, he couldn't leave this lively, lovely place, couldn't leave Tianqiao, couldn't leave Beiping. Leave? There was nowhere to go! He still had to go back to her - to her! - to discuss things. He couldn't leave, and he couldn't stay idle; he had to think conservatively, just as everyone had to when things got hopeless. He had endured so many grievances already, why be stubborn about this one thing? He couldn't correct everything in the past, so he might as well go with the flow.

He stood still, listening to the chaotic noise of the crowd and the sound of gongs and drums, watching the people and carriages coming and going, when suddenly he thought of those two small rooms. The sounds in his ears seemed to disappear, the figures before his eyes seemed to vanish, leaving only those two small, white, warm rooms with red double happiness characters, standing square and upright before him. Although he had only stayed there one night, they felt very familiar and intimate; even that woman in the red jacket didn't seem like something he could casually abandon. Standing at Tianqiao, he had nothing, was nothing; in those two small rooms, he had everything. Going back was the only way to find a solution. Everything for tomorrow was in those small rooms. Shame, fear, sadness - none of it mattered; if he wanted to live, he had to find a place where there was a solution.

He strode back all the way and entered the house, probably just around eleven o'clock. Tigress had already prepared lunch: steamed buns, boiled cabbage with meatballs, a plate of jellied meat, and a plate of pickled radish. Everything else was already set out, only the cabbage was still simmering on the stove, giving off a wonderful aroma. She had taken off her red jacket and put on her usual cotton-padded trousers and jacket, but on her head, she wore a small red velvet flower with a little gold paper ingot on it. Xiangzi glanced at her; she didn't look like a new bride. Her every movement was like that of an experienced wife - quick, deft, and with a hint of self-satisfaction. Although she didn't look like a new bride, it still made him feel something new; she cooked, she cleaned the room; the fragrance and warmth in the room were things he had never experienced before. No matter what she was like, he felt that he had a home. A home always had its lovable side. He didn't know what to do.

"Where did you go?" she asked as she went to dish up the cabbage. "Took a bath," he said, taking off his gown.

"Oh! Next time you go out, let me know! Don't just walk off so casually!" He didn't say anything.

"Can you grunt or not? If not, I'll teach you!"

He grunted, having no choice! He knew he had married a she-devil, but this she-devil could cook, clean the house, scold him, and help him - it all made him feel wrong! He started eating the buns. The food was indeed tastier and warmer than usual, but it didn't taste good; as he chewed, his heart didn't feel the usual wolfing-down pleasure; he didn't break a sweat from eating.

After eating, he lay down on the kang, his head resting on his hands, staring at the ceiling. "Hey! Help me wash the dishes! I'm not your maid!" she called from the outer room.

Reluctantly, he stood up, glanced at her, and went over to help. He was usually very diligent, but now he was doing things with a stifled anger. At the rickshaw yard, he used to often help her, but now the more he looked at her, the more he hated her; he had never hated anyone as fiercely as he hated her, and he couldn't say why. He was angry but refused to let it out, holding it all in his heart; since he couldn't make a clean break with her, arguing was pointless. Pacing around in the small room, he felt his entire life was a story of grievance.

After finishing the cleaning, she glanced around the room and sighed. Then she immediately smiled. "Well?"

"What?" Xiangzi squatted by the stove, warming his hands; his hands weren't cold, but having nowhere to put them, he just warmed them anyway. These two small rooms did feel like a home, but he didn't know where to put his hands or feet.

"Take me out for some fun? To the White Cloud Temple? No, it's too late; how about strolling on the streets?" She wanted to fully enjoy the happiness of being newlyweds. Although the marriage wasn't one for show, being so free and easy was actually fine; it was good to spend more time with her husband and have a few days of pure fun. At her parents' home, she hadn't lacked food, clothing, or pocket money; she just hadn't had an understanding man. Now she wanted to make up for that lack, to swagger through the streets and temple fairs with Xiangzi.

Xiangzi didn't want to go. First, he thought it was shameful to be seen everywhere with his wife; second, he thought that a wife obtained this way should only be kept at home - this wasn't something honorable, and the less people saw of her, the better. Also, if they went out, how could they avoid running into acquaintances? Every rickshaw puller in the western half of the city knew about Tigress and Xiangzi; he couldn't go and invite people to whisper behind his back.

"How about we talk it over?" He was still squatting there.

"What's there to talk about?" She came over and stood by the stove. He took his hands down, placed them on his knees, and stared blankly at the flames. After a long silence, he said, "I can't just stay idle like this!"

"Born to suffer!" She laughed. "Can't go a day without pulling your rickshaw, huh? Look at the old man; he played his whole life, and in his old age, he still owns a rickshaw yard. He doesn't pull rickshaws, doesn't use brute force; he lives by his wits. You should learn something from that. What's the point of pulling a rickshaw your whole life? Let's play for a few days first; things aren't so urgent that we have to rush. What's the hurry? I don't plan to argue with you these next few days, but don't you go out of your way to provoke me!"

"Let's talk it over first!" Xiangzi was determined not to give in. Since he couldn't just stamp his foot and leave, he had to find something to do. He had to take a stand; he couldn't just swing back and forth.

"Fine, you talk!" She moved a stool over and sat by the stove. "How much money do you have?" he asked.

"See? I knew you were going to ask that! You're not marrying a wife; you're marrying the money, right?"

Xiangzi felt like he had been choked by a gust of wind, swallowing hard several times. Old Man Liu and the pullers at Harmony Yard all thought he was after money when he got involved with Tigress; now,She said it herself! Her own car, her own money, thrown away for no reason, and now crushed under his wife’s few dollars—he had to swallow even his meals sideways! He wished he could grab her by the throat with both hands, squeeze! Squeeze! Squeeze! Until her eyes rolled white! Kill everything, and then slit his own throat. They weren’t human, so they had to die; he wasn’t human either, so he’d die too—no one should think about living!

Xiangzi stood up, wanting to go out for another walk; he shouldn’t have come back in the first place. Seeing his grim expression, she softened a bit: “Alright, I’ll tell you. I have about five hundred bucks in total. Including the sedan chair, renting the house—three installments[1], papering the ceiling, making clothes, buying stuff, and giving some to you—all together it cost a little under a hundred, leaving over four hundred. I’m telling you, don’t worry. Let’s just enjoy ourselves while we can. You, sweating all year long pulling a rickshaw, deserve a few days of looking sharp and having fun; me, an old maid all these years, I deserve a few days of pleasure too. When the money’s almost gone, we’ll go beg the old man again. Me, if I hadn’t fallen out with him that day, I’d never have gotten out. Now that my anger’s cooled down, a father’s still a father. He’s got only me, his daughter, and you’re someone he likes. If we humble ourselves and apologize to him, there probably won’t be anything we can’t get past. How convenient is that! He’s got money, and we can rightfully inherit it—there’s nothing unreasonable about it. It’s way better than you being a beast of burden for others! In a couple of days, you go see him first; he might not see you. If he doesn’t the first time, go a second time—give him all the face, and he’ll have no choice but to change his mind. Then I’ll go, say a few nice words to him, and who knows, maybe we can all move back. Once we move back, I guarantee we’ll hold our heads high, and no one will dare look at us sideways. If we keep putting up with it here, we’ll always be a pair of nobodies, don’t you think?”

Xiangzi had never thought of this. Ever since Tigress came to find him at Cao’s place, he thought he’d marry her, use her money to buy a rickshaw, and go back to pulling it himself. Though using his wife’s money wasn’t exactly dignified, their relationship was something unspeakable, so he had no choice. He hadn’t expected Tigress to have this move. If he dropped his long face and went along with it, it was indeed a plan, but Xiangzi wasn’t that kind of man. Thinking it over from start to finish, he seemed to understand a bit: when you have your own money, others can just take it for nothing, and you have nowhere to complain. But when someone gives you money, you have no choice but to accept it. Once you accept it, you can’t see yourself as a person anymore. You may have ambition and strength, but you have to become their slave—a plaything for your own wife, a servant for your father-in-law. A person is nothing at all, just a bird that, in foraging for food, flies straight into a net. Eating other people’s grain means staying quietly in the cage, singing for them, and being sold off at any moment!

He refused to go see Fourth Master Liu. With Tigress, it was a flesh-in-flesh relationship; with Fourth Master Liu, there was no connection. He’d already been taken advantage of by her; he couldn’t go begging her father! “I don’t want to be idle!” was all he said, to save the trouble of talking and quarreling.

“Born to suffer, huh!” she said sarcastically. “If you don’t like being idle, go run a business.”

“I don’t know how! I can’t make money! I know how to pull a rickshaw, and I love pulling it!” The veins on Xiangzi’s forehead bulged.

“Let me tell you, I won’t let you pull a rickshaw! I won’t let you climb into my bed all stinking with sweat! You’ve got your ideas, I’ve got mine. Let’s see who outlasts who! You married a wife, but I paid for it—you didn’t shell out a single cent. Think about it, who should listen to who?”

Xiangzi was speechless again.

[1] Three installments: renting a house requires paying three months' rent upfront for the first month.

Sixteen  

Xiangzi couldn't hold out any longer until the Lantern Festival.  

Tigress was delighted. She bustled about cooking yuanxiao and making dumplings, visiting temples by day and watching lanterns by night. She wouldn't let Xiangzi have any say in anything, but she never let him go hungry, constantly finding ways to buy or cook something fresh and tasty for him to eat. There were seven or eight families in the compound, most of them crammed into a single room; some rooms housed as many as seven or eight people, old and young. Some of these folks pulled rickshaws, some peddled small goods, some were policemen, some were servants. Each had their own business, no one had any free time, even the children carried little baskets, going to fetch porridge in the morning and picking coal cinders in the afternoon. Only the very smallest kids, with their bottoms frozen bright red, played or fought in the yard. Furnace ash, dust, and dirty water were all dumped in the yard, nobody bothered to clean up, and the middle of the courtyard was frozen over with ice. The older kids, returning with their coal cinders, used it as an ice rink, shouting and playing at sliding on the ice. The worst off were the old folks and the women. The elderly, without clothes or food, lay on the cold brick kang, waiting for the young to earn a little money so they could have a bowl of porridge. The young men doing hard labor might earn some money, or might come back empty-handed, and when they returned, they'd lose their tempers, picking fights over nothing. The old folks had to swallow their tears like water on empty stomachs. The women had to look after both the old and the young, and also placate the young men who earned the money. Even pregnant, they had to keep working, eating nothing but cornbread and sweet potato porridge; no, not only did they have to work as usual, they also had to go fetch porridge and scrounge up some odd jobs—only once the old and young were fed and lying down could they take up a little kerosene lamp to wash, mend, and sew for others. The rooms were so small, the walls so broken, the cold wind seeped through cracks in one side and went straight out the other, taking whatever little warmth there was with it. They wore nothing but rags, their bellies held a bowl or half a bowl of porridge, or maybe a fetus six or seven months along. They had to work, had to make sure the old and young ate first. Their bodies were riddled with illness, losing their hair before thirty, but they couldn't rest for a moment, moving from sickness to death; when they died, the coffin had to be begged for from "charitable" folks. The girls, sixteen or seventeen, had no pants, just wrapped some rag around themselves inside the house—a natural prison—helping their mothers with chores and work. To go to the outhouse, they had to make sure no one was in the yard before sneaking out like thieves; all winter, they never saw the sun or the clear sky. The ugly ones would inherit everything from their mothers; the ones with any looks knew, even themselves, that sooner or later their parents would sell them off, "to enjoy a life of comfort"!  

In just such a compound, Tigress felt very pleased with herself. She was the only one with enough to eat and wear, no need to worry, and free to stroll around. She held her head high, coming and going, feeling both her own superiority and fearing others might cling to her, so she ignored these miserable people. The small peddlers who came here sold almost nothing but the cheapest things—scrap meat off bones, frozen cabbage, raw bean juice, horse and donkey meat—all looking for customers here. Ever since Tigress moved in, vendors of sheep head meat, smoked fish, hard flour cakes, and fried tofu in brine would also hawk their wares at the door. Holding a bowl, head high, she carried these snacks inside, and the children would stick their fingers, like iron bars, in their mouths, staring at her as if she were some princess. She had come to enjoy herself; she couldn't, wouldn't, and didn't want to see others' hardships.  

Xiangzi disliked her behavior the most. He came from poverty himself and knew what hardship meant. He didn't want to eat those odds and ends; it was a waste of money. Second, and more humiliating, he sensed something: she wouldn't let him pull a rickshaw but kept him well-fed with good food every day, just like fattening up a cow to milk it! He had completely become her plaything. He had seen it on the street: an old, skinny female dog, when in heat, would also choose a strong, fat male dog. Thinking of this, he not only loathed this life but also worried about himself. He knew that a man who lived by his strength should take care of his body; the body was everything. If he went on like this, he would one day become a skeleton, still the same size, but hollow inside. He shuddered. To stay alive, he had to go pull a rickshaw immediately, run all day, come back and fall straight asleep, oblivious to everything; not eating her good food, and not playing along with her. He decided to do it, no more concessions; if she was willing to buy a rickshaw, fine; if not, he'd rent one. Without a word, he made up his mind and went to rent a rickshaw. On the seventeenth, he started pulling again, renting one for the "whole day." After two long hauls, he noticed something he'd never had before—tightness in his calves and soreness in his hip joints. He knew where the problem came from, but to comfort himself, he thought it might be because he hadn't pulled for over twenty days, his legs had gotten stiff; after a few runs, they'd loosen up, and he'd be fine.  

He took another fare, this time a group job, four rickshaws going together. Lifting the shafts, everyone let a tall, forty-some-year-old man lead the way. The tall man smiled and went along, knowing the other three rickshaws were better than his own. But he still gave it his all, though he knew he couldn't outrun the three young guys behind him. After running a li or so, someone behind praised him: "Hey, you're putting in some effort! Not bad!" Panting, he replied: "Pulling with you boys, how could I go slow?!" He was indeed running fast; even Xiangzi had to use seven or eight-tenths of his strength to keep up. But his running style was ugly: tall as he was, he couldn't bend his back; his waist and back seemed like a single board, so his whole body had to lunge forward. Leaning forward, his hands seemed to lag behind. It wasn't like running, more like he was dragging something and drilling forward. His waist was stiff, so his hips had to move a lot; his feet almost scraped the ground, twisting forward hard. He twisted pretty fast, but you could tell it took a huge effort. At corners and turns, he'd twist his whole body hard, and everyone sweated for him; he seemed to just care about pushing forward, not whether the rickshaw could make it.  

After the haul, sweat dripped steadily from the tip of his nose and his earlobes. Setting down the rickshaw, he quickly straightened his back and grimaced. When taking the money, his hands trembled so much he could barely hold it.  

Having run one trip together made them friends, so the four of them parked their rickshaws together. Xiangzi and the others wiped their sweat and went back to chatting and laughing. The tall man wandered around for a while, coughed dryly for a long time, spat out a lot of white foam, and then seemed to recover enough to start talking to them: "I'm done! What's the point anymore? My back and legs just won't cooperate! No matter how I try to lift my back, I can't raise my legs! I'm so frustrated!"  

"Those last few steps weren't slow, you think they were?" a short, twenty-something young man chimed in. "No lie, the three of us are pretty good, but didn't any of us sweat?" The tall man was a bit proud but also seemed ashamed, sighing.  

"With that running style of yours, you'd really leave most people in the dust, believe it or not?" said another young man. "It's age, not something to joke about." The tall man smiled and shook his head: "It's not all about age, boys! Let me tell you something true: in this line of work, don't get married, really!" Seeing everyone lean in to listen, he lowered his voice: "Once you marry, you're busy day and night—finished! Look at my back, stiff, not a bit of flexibility! Can't run too hard; if I grit my teeth, I start coughing, and my chest burns! Forget it, in this line of work, we've got to stay bachelors forever! Even the damned sparrows go in pairs, but they won't let us marry! And another thing, after marriage, a kid every year—I've got five now! All with mouths open, waiting to be fed! The rickshaw rent is high, food is expensive, the business is tough, what can you do! Better to stay a bachelor forever; when the urge hits, go to a whorehouse, catch a case of syphilis, and accept your fate! A single man, dead is dead! Once you're married, with young and old, several mouths to feed, you can't even close your eyes when you die! Isn't that right?" he asked Xiangzi.  

Xiangzi nodded but said nothing.  

Just then, a customer came. The short guy named the price first, but gave way, calling to the tall man: "Big brother, you take it! The guy's got five kids at home!"  

The tall man smiled: "Alright, I'll make another run! This isn't how it's supposed to be done! Fine, I'll bring back a few more flatbreads! See you later, boys!" Watching the tall man walk away, the short guy muttered to himself: "Struggling all my life, can't even get a wife! Those rich bastards, each one hugs four or five women!"  

"Don't talk about them," another young man cut in. "You see this business, you've got to be careful—the tall guy wasn't wrong. Let's put it this way—why get married? Just to have something to look at? No! That's where the trouble starts! Eating cornbread every day, squeezed from both ends, even the strongest guy will break!" Hearing this, Xiangzi pulled his rickshaw up and said casually: "Let's head south; there's no business here."  

"See you!" the two young men said together.  

Xiangzi seemed not to hear. As he walked, he kicked his legs; his hip joints were indeed still a bit sore! He thought about packing up, but had no courage to go home. The thing at home wasn't a wife, but a bloodsucking demon!  

The days were getting longer; he wandered around for two or three more trips, and it was only just past five o'clock. He turned in his rickshaw and lingered at the teahouse for a while. After two pots of tea, he felt hungry and decided to eat out before going home. He ate twelve ounces of meat pies and a bowl of red bean millet porridge, then walked home slowly, burping loudly. He knew a thunderstorm was waiting for him at home, but he was calm; he had made up his mind: not to argue, not to fight, just fall asleep, and tomorrow go out and pull rickshaw again, she could do whatever she wanted!  

As soon as he stepped inside, Tigress was sitting in the outer room. She glanced at him, her face so grim it seemed ready to drip water. Xiangzi thought about smoothing things over, pulling a long face and greeting her. But he wasn't used to that; he hung his head and went into the inner room. She didn't say a word; the little room was as quiet as a deep mountain cave. The neighbor's coughing, talking, and children crying in the courtyard were all clearly audible, yet also seemed very far away, like hearing distant sounds from a mountain.  

Neither wanted to speak first; they kept their mouths shut and lay down one after another, like a pair of silent giant turtles. After a nap, Tigress spoke, her tone half-annoyed, half-joking: "Where did you go? Gone all day!"  

"Went rickshaw pulling!" he said sleepily, his voice seeming a bit choked.  

"Oh! Can't stand not breaking a sweat, itching all over, you ungrateful wretch! I cooked you food, but you don't come home to eat, just gobble garbage all over town! Don't push me too far; my father was a bachelor, and I'm capable of anything! If you dare go out again tomorrow, I'll hang myself in front of you—I say it, and I'll do it!"  

"I can't just sit idle!"  

"Why don't you go see the old man?"  

"No!"  

"Stubborn!"  

Xiangzi was really angry. He couldn't hold back anymore: "I want to pull a rickshaw, buy my own. Whoever stops me, I'll leave and never come back!"  

"Hmm—" She let out a long, winding sound through her nose. In that sound, she showed both pride and contempt for Xiangzi, but inside, she was scheming. She knew Xiangzi was a tough guy—though very honest. Tough guys don't say things lightly. She had finally caught him; she couldn't let him go easily. He was ideal: honest, hardworking, strong; with her looks and age, it wasn't easy to find another treasure like him. Softness and hardness together was real skill; she needed to coax him: "I know you want to be strong, but you also have to know I really care for you. If you won't go see the old man, then I'll go. After all, I'm his daughter; losing some face doesn't matter."  

"Even if the old man takes us in, I still have to pull a rickshaw!" Xiangzi laid it all out.  

Tigress was silent for a long time. She hadn't expected Xiangzi to be this sharp. His words were simple, but clearly stated he wouldn't fall into her trap—he wasn't a dumb donkey. For that reason, she found it more interesting; she'd have to use some real skill to keep this grown man, or this big creature, in line when he could kick back. She couldn't push him too hard; finding such a big thing wasn't easy. She had to loosen and tighten her grip, so he'd never escape her hand. "Alright, if you love pulling rickshaws, I can't help it. But you have to swear you won't take a private hire job, and come home every day; look, if I don't see you for a day, my heart gets uneasy! Promise me you'll be back early every evening!"  

Xiangzi thought of the tall man's words during the day! Staring at the darkness with open eyes, he saw a group of rickshaw pullers, small peddlers, manual laborers, all with hunched backs and dragging legs. He would end up like that too. But he didn't want to argue with her anymore; as long as he could pull a rickshaw, he had already won a victory. "I'll only pull random fares!"He agreed.

Though she said that, she wasn't really keen on going to find Fourth Master Liu. Father and daughter had often bickered on ordinary days, but things were different now; it couldn't just blow over with a few words, because she was no longer considered part of Liu's family. A married woman always drifts apart a bit from her parents. She didn't dare to go straight back and confront him. What if the old man really turned his back on her? She could make a scene, but if he refused to let go of his property, she'd have no way out. Even if someone mediated, in the end they'd only persuade her to come back, since she had her own home now.

Xiangzi went on pulling his rickshaw as usual, while she paced back and forth alone in the room. Time and again she thought about dressing up and going to find her father, but her mind was willing while her hands were lazy. She was in a bind. For her own comfort and happiness, she had to go back; for her own dignity, it was better not to go. If the old man had cooled down, she could just drag Xiangzi to Harmony Yard, and naturally he'd find work there, no need to pull a rickshaw anymore, and she'd smoothly take over her father's business. Her heart brightened. But what if the old man hardened all the way? She'd lose face, no, not just lose face, she'd have to resign herself to being a rickshaw puller's wife; she, hmph! She'd be no different from those women in the mixed compound. Her heart suddenly went dark. She almost regretted marrying Xiangzi. No matter how strong-willed he was, if her father didn't nod, he'd be a rickshaw puller for life. Thinking this, she even considered going back to her parents' home alone and cutting ties with Xiangzi, unwilling to lose everything for him. But then she thought again, the happiness of being with Xiangzi was beyond words. She sat on the edge of the kang, dazed and lost, recalling the joy after marriage; her whole body felt like a big red flower, fragrant and warm, blooming in the sun. No, she couldn't bear to leave Xiangzi. Even if he went on pulling a rickshaw or begging, she'd stick with him forever. Look at those women in the compound; if they could bear it, so could she. Forget it, she wouldn't go to the Liu family.

Xiangzi, since leaving Harmony Yard, refused to walk down Xi'anmen Avenue again. These past two days, whenever he went out to pull his rickshaw, he'd head straight for the eastern city to avoid the western city, where Harmony Yard's rickshaws were everywhere, and meeting them would be awkward. But today, after finishing work, he deliberately passed by the yard's gate, for no other reason than to take a look. Tigress's words were still echoing in his mind, as if he wanted to test whether he had the courage to return to the yard, in case Tigress could talk things over with the old man; before going back to the yard, he'd first see if he dared to walk this street. Pulling his hat down low, he glanced from afar toward the yard, afraid of being seen by acquaintances. Seeing the light at the gate from a distance, he felt inexplicably sad. He recalled his first days there, Tigress's temptation, and that scene on her birthday night. These memories were crystal clear, floating before his eyes like pictures. Amid these pictures, there were others, sharp and brief, wedged in between: the Western Hills, the camels, the Cao residence, the detective... all distinct and terrifying, merging into one.
