These images were so clear that he felt a bit bewildered, almost as if he were just looking at some paintings, forgetting he was part of them. When he thought of his connection to them, his mind became chaotic; they suddenly spun around, messy and hazy, and he couldn't figure out why he had to endure all this torment and injustice. These scenes seemed to take a very long time, yet also very short; he couldn't tell how old he was. He only felt that, compared to when he first arrived at Harmony Yard, he had aged so much. Back then, his heart was full of hope; now, it was brimming with worry. He didn't understand why, but those images wouldn't deceive him.

There was Harmony Yard before him. He stood on the other side of the street, staring blankly at that extremely bright electric light. As he stared, he was suddenly startled. The four gold characters under the light—"Ren He Che Chang"—had changed! He couldn't read, but he remembered what the first character looked like: two sticks joined together, neither a fork nor a triangle, a simple yet strange character. Judging by the sound, it must be "ren." This "ren" had changed into something else—"ren" (benevolence)—an even stranger character than "ren." He couldn't figure out why. He looked at the east and west rooms—the two rooms he'd never forget—and there were no lights on.

After standing there until he grew impatient, he lowered his head and headed home. As he walked, he wondered: had Harmony Yard been sold off? He'd have to slowly find out, without saying anything to his wife for now. Back home, Tigress was in the room, cracking melon seeds to pass the time. "So late again!" Her face showed no goodwill. "Let me tell you, I can't stand this anymore! You're out all day, and I can't even move an inch. The whole yard's full of poor folks, I'm afraid of something getting stolen. I don't even have a place to talk all day long. No, I'm not made of wood. Figure something out, this won't do!" Xiangzi didn't say a word.

"Say something! Are you trying to provoke me on purpose? Do you have a mouth or not? Do you?" Her words came faster and sharper, like a string of firecrackers popping off. Xiangzi still had nothing to say.

"Fine, let's do this," she said, truly agitated, but with a hint of helplessness toward him. Her face showed neither crying nor laughing, just extreme irritation without being able to fully let it out. "We'll buy two rickshaws and rent them out. You stay home and live off the rental fees, okay or not?" "Two rickshaws bringing in three dimes a day isn't enough to eat! Rent one out, and I'll pull the other—that'll do!" Xiangzi spoke slowly, but naturally; hearing about buying a rickshaw, he forgot everything else.

"Isn't that the same? You'd still never be home!"

"This works too," Xiangzi's ideas all seemed tied to the rickshaw issue. "Rent one out for a full day's fee. For the other, I'll pull it half the day and rent it out the other half. If I pull during the day, I'll leave early and come back by three; if I pull at night, I'll leave at three and come back in the night. That's fine!" She nodded. "Let me think it over. If there's no better idea, we'll do it that way."

Xiangzi was very happy. If this plan worked out, he'd be pulling his own rickshaw again. Even though his wife bought it, he could slowly save up and buy another himself. Until now, he realized Tigress had some good points too; he even smiled at her, a genuine, heartfelt smile, as if all past hardships were wiped clean, and he could laugh and swap into a new world as easily as changing clothes!

[1]

  Jue, to lose in a contest. To be defeated.

Take a break if you're tired!

Public account: Good Morning Li

Study resource sharing seventeen

Xiangzi slowly found out what happened to Harmony Yard: Fourth Master Liu had sold off part of the rickshaws and pawned the rest off to a well-known owner in the western city. Xiangzi could guess that the old man, having reached his age and without his daughter's help, couldn't run the business anymore, so he simply closed it down and took the money to enjoy life. But where he went, Xiangzi couldn't find out.

About this news, he didn't know whether to be happy or not. From his own ambition and pride, with Fourth Master Liu determined to abandon his daughter, Tigress's plan had completely failed; he could honestly pull a rickshaw to earn his bread, without relying on anyone. From the perspective of Fourth Master Liu's property, it was a pity; who knew how the old man would spend the money, leaving him and Tigress without a single copper coin.

But since things had come to this, he didn't dwell on it much, let alone get emotional. He thought: So long as his own strength was his, and he was willing to sell it to earn money, eating wasn't a problem. He told Tigress simply, without any feeling. She, however, was shaken. Hearing this, she immediately saw her future clearly—done for! Everything was finished! She'd have to be a rickshaw puller's wife for life! She could never escape this big compound! She had imagined her father might remarry, but never thought he'd just up and leave like this. If the old man had really taken a young wife, Tigress would fight for the property, maybe even ally with the stepmother to get some benefit... There were plenty of ideas, as long as the old man kept the rickshaw yard. She never expected him to be so resolute, so ruthless—turning all his property into cash and secretly stashing it away! When she'd quarreled with him before, she thought it was just a tactic and they'd soon make up; she knew Harmony Yard couldn't do without her. Who could have guessed the old man would just abandon the yard?!

Spring had arrived, with red buds on the branches. But in this big compound, spring didn't come to the branches first; there wasn't a single flower or tree. Here, the spring wind first blew little pockmarks into the ice in the yard, stirring up a stench from the dirt, blowing chicken feathers, garlic skins, and scraps of paper into corners, forming small whirlwinds. The people in the compound suffered all year round. Only now did the elderly dare come out to bask in the sun; young girls finally wiped a bit of coal dust off the tips of their noses, revealing some red-yellow skin; women dared to chase their kids into the yard to play without too much shame; children dared to drag a torn piece of paper as a kite, running freely in the yard without their little black hands cracking open. But the porridge kitchen had stopped boiling, charity had ceased giving rice, and the kind had stopped handing out money; it was as if the poor had been handed over to the spring wind and light! It was when spring wheat was just turning green like grass and old grain was scarce that grain prices rose as usual. The days grew longer, so even the old couldn't lie down early and use dreams to fool their empty bellies. Spring had come to the world, but in this big compound, it only brought more hardship. The aging lice—especially fierce—sometimes crawled out to the cotton wadding of the elderly or children, briefly enjoying the spring light!

Tigress looked at the melting ice in the yard and the tattered clothes, smelled the complex, slightly warm odor, heard the old people's laments and children's cries, and her heart sank. In winter, everyone hid indoors, and filth froze on the ice; now, people came out, things showed their true form, even the brick walls crumbled, as if preparing to collapse in the rain. The yard was a mess, blooming with wretched, colorful flowers, several times uglier than in winter. Hmph, at this moment, she felt she'd live here forever; her money would run out someday, and Xiangzi was just a rickshaw puller!

She told Xiangzi to watch the house while she went to Nanyuan to visit her aunt and ask about the old man. The aunt said that Fourth Master had indeed come to her house once, probably on the twelfth of the first month. One, to thank her; two, to tell her he planned to go to Tianjin or Shanghai for a trip. He said: Having lived all his life without leaving Beijing, he couldn't be considered a hero; while he still had breath, he wanted to see the world. Besides, he felt too ashamed to stay in the city, since his own daughter had shamed him. That was all the aunt's report; her judgment was even simpler: The old man might have really gone away, or might just be saying so, hiding somewhere quiet; who knew!

Back home, she threw herself onto the kang, crying quietly, not a hint of pretense, sobbing for a long time until her eyelids swelled.

After crying, she wiped her tears and said to Xiangzi, "Fine, you're tough! Everything goes your way! I bet wrong on this. Marry a rooster and follow the rooster—nothing more to say. Here's a hundred yuan, go buy a rickshaw and pull it!"

Here, she kept a trick: Originally, she'd planned to buy two rickshaws, one for Xiangzi to pull, one to rent out. Now she changed her mind, buying only one for Xiangzi to pull; the rest of the money she kept in her hands. With money in her hands, she'd have power; she wasn't willing to give it all out. What if Xiangzi—after the money was spent on a rickshaw—changed his mind? She had to guard against that! Besides, Fourth Master Liu's departure made her feel nothing was reliable; who could know tomorrow's events? Better to enjoy life while it lasted, keep some money on hand, eat what she liked—she'd always been used to snacking. Living on what Xiangzi earned—he was top-notch rickshaw puller—and supplementing her own pocket money for small expenses, she'd enjoy the present. Money would run out someday, but people wouldn't live forever! Marrying a rickshaw puller—though forced—was already a compromise; she couldn't keep asking him for money every day with empty pockets. This decision cheered her up a bit, even though she knew the future was bleak; at least for now, she wouldn't be upside down. It was like walking at sunset; the distance was already dim, but there was still some light ahead, so she'd take a few more steps while it lasted.

Xiangzi didn't argue with her. One rickshaw was fine; as long as it was his own, he could earn at least six or seven dimes a day.He could manage to earn a living. Not only did he not argue, he even felt somewhat pleased. All the hardships he had endured in the past were merely to buy a rickshaw. Now that he could buy one again, what more was there to say? Naturally, one rickshaw supporting two people wouldn't leave any money left over; there would come a time when this rickshaw wore out, and without any provision for buying a new one, that was dangerous! But since buying a rickshaw was so difficult, being able to buy one now should be satisfying enough—why think so far ahead!

Er Qiangzi in the compound was just about to sell his rickshaw. Last summer, Er Qiangzi had sold his daughter, Xiaofuzi—nineteen years old—to a soldier for two hundred yuan. After Xiaofuzi left, Er Qiangzi lived it up for a while, redeeming everything from the pawnshop and even making a few new clothes, so the whole family was quite neatly dressed. Er Qiangzi's wife was the shortest and ugliest woman in the whole compound, with a protruding forehead, large cheeks, almost no hair on her head, teeth always showing, and her face covered in freckles, quite disgusting to look at. She also had red-rimmed eyes, crying over her daughter while putting on a new blue gown. Er Qiangzi had always had a hot temper, and after selling his daughter, he often drank a few shots; with tears in his eyes after drinking, he was especially quick to find fault. Although Er Qiangzi's wife wore the new gown and had enough to eat, her joy didn't outweigh her suffering, and the number of beatings she received nearly doubled. Er Qiangzi was over forty and planned to stop pulling a rickshaw. So he bought a pair of baskets, set up a grocery stall with melons, fruits, peaches, pears, peanuts, cigarettes—a full stock. After two months of business, a rough tally showed not only a loss but a heavy one. Having been used to pulling a rickshaw, he didn't know how to handle trade; rickshaw pulling was a hit-or-miss affair—if it worked, fine; if not, forget it—but running a small business required hard bargaining, which he couldn't manage. Rickshaw pullers knew how to buy on credit, so he couldn't be rude enough to refuse regulars who owed money; once they owed, it was hard to collect. Thus, he couldn't attract good customers, and those he dealt with were all keen on running up debts without paying, so he couldn't help but lose money. Losing money made him miserable; the more miserable he felt, the more he drank. Drunk, he often bickered with the police on the streets and took out his anger on his wife and kids at home. Offending the police and beating his wife—all because of drink. When he sobered up, he was deeply regretful and pained. Thinking again that this money came from selling his daughter, and it was being squandered like this, along with drinking and hitting people, he felt he wasn't human. At such times, he could lie around all day sleeping, entrusting his misery to dreams.

He decided to give up the business and go back to pulling a rickshaw, unwilling to let that money all go to waste. He bought a rickshaw. When he was drunk, he was completely unreasonable. When he was sober, he cared most about appearances. Because of that, he often put on airs of poverty, having a set way for everything. With a new rickshaw and neatly dressed, he felt he was a top-tier puller, so he had to drink good tea and pull respectable fares. He could stand at the rickshaw stand, showing off his vehicle and his white trousers and shirt, chatting with everyone, always too proud to hustle for passengers. He'd swat his rickshaw with a new blue duster, stamp his new white-soled double-layered shoes, or stand by the rickshaw smiling, staring at the tip of his nose, waiting for someone to praise his rickshaw, and then start a conversation that would go on endlessly. He could waste a whole day or two like that. But when he finally got a good fare, his legs wouldn't do justice to his rickshaw and clothes—he couldn't run fast! That upset him greatly. Upset, he'd think of his daughter and inevitably turn to drink. So his money all seeped away, leaving only the rickshaw.

Around the beginning of winter, he got drunk again. As soon as he entered the door, his two sons—one thirteen, one eleven—tried to hide. That annoyed him, and he gave each a kick. Er Qiangzi's wife said something, and he stormed over, kicking her in the lower belly. She lay on the ground, unable to speak for a long time. The two boys panicked; one grabbed a coal shovel, the other a rolling pin, and they fought their father desperately. The three tumbled together, trampling Er Qiangzi's wife a few more times in the chaos. The neighbors came and finally managed to pin Er Qiangzi down on the kang, while the two boys hugged their mother and cried. Er Qiangzi's wife came to, but she never got back on her feet. By the third day of the twelfth lunar month, her breathing stopped, and she was buried in the blue gown she had worn when her daughter was sold. Er Qiangzi's in-laws refused to let it go and insisted on suing. Only after much persuasion from friends did they relent, but Er Qiangzi had to promise a proper burial and give her family fifteen yuan. He pawned the rickshaw for sixty yuan. When the new year came, he wanted to sell the rickshaw, having no hope of redeeming it himself. When drunk, he even thought of selling one of his sons, but no one would take them. He had also tried to find Xiaofuzi's husband, but the man flatly refused to acknowledge him as a father-in-law, so there was nothing more to say.

Xiangzi knew the history of this rickshaw and wasn't keen on buying it. There were plenty of rickshaws around; why pick this one—this unlucky vehicle, bought with a daughter and sold after killing the wife! Tigress didn't see it that way. She wanted to buy it for just over eighty yuan—a bargain! The rickshaw had only been used for half a year; the leather straps hadn't even changed color, and it was genuinely made by Decheng, a famous factory in the western district. A seven-out-of-ten condition rickshaw would still cost fifty or sixty yuan, wouldn't it? She couldn't pass up such a deal. She also knew that just after the new year, money was tight everywhere, and Er Qiangzi wouldn't get a high price and was in a hurry for cash. She went to see the rickshaw herself, negotiated the price with Er Qiangzi herself, and handed over the money. Xiangzi had no choice but to wait and pull it; he said nothing and had no reason to speak, since the money wasn't his. After buying it, he examined it closely—it was indeed sturdy and strong. But he still felt uneasy. What bothered him most was the black lacquered body with white brass fittings; when Er Qiangzi had made this rickshaw, he intended the black-and-white contrast to look sharp, but Xiangzi always felt it was unlucky, like wearing mourning clothes. He wanted to change the fittings to earthy yellow or moon white, which might lessen the starkness. But he didn't discuss it with Tigress, to avoid provoking another round of nagging.

When he took this rickshaw out, everyone paid special attention; some even called it the "Little Widow." Xiangzi was unhappy. He tried not to think about it, but the rickshaw was with him day and night, and he was always on edge, as if something might go wrong at any moment. Sometimes he'd suddenly think of Er Qiangzi and his fate, and it felt as if he wasn't pulling a rickshaw but a coffin. On this rickshaw, he seemed to see ghostly figures all the time.

Yet, since he started pulling this rickshaw, nothing had gone wrong, though he was secretly restless. The weather was getting warmer; once the padded clothes were off, he could almost wear just thin trousers and a shirt without need for a lined jacket—Beijing had hardly any spring. The days stretched so long that everyone felt drowsy. Xiangzi went out early and wandered until four or five in the afternoon, already feeling he'd put in enough effort. But the sun was still high. He didn't want to run anymore, yet he wouldn't stop, hesitating with long, lazy yawns.

If the days were this long and Xiangzi felt weary and bored, Tigress was even more lonely at home. In winter, she could warm herself by the stove and listen to the wind outside, which, though dreary, brought some comfort in thinking "It's better not to go out." Now the stove had been moved under the eaves, and there was nothing to do inside. The courtyard was dirty and stinking, without even a blade of grass. Going out into the street, she worried about the neighbors; even a simple errand meant going straight there and back, not daring to linger. She was like a bee trapped in a room, watching the sunlight outside but unable to fly out. She couldn't connect with the women in the compound. They talked about household trivialities, while she was used to being wild and unfettered; she neither liked to say nor hear such things. Their grievances came from the hardships of life, and every little thing could bring tears; her grievances were discontent with life itself—she had no tears to shed but wanted to curse someone to vent her pent-up frustration. They couldn't understand each other, so it was best to mind their own business and not talk[1].

It wasn't until mid-April that she found a companion. Xiaofuzi, Er Qiangzi's daughter, had returned. Xiaofuzi's "man"[2] was an officer. He set up a simple household wherever he went, spending a hundred or two to get a young girl, buying a large bed board and two chairs, and then he could have a good time for a while. When the troops moved elsewhere, he'd abandon her, leaving the girl and the bed board behind. Spending such a hundred or two for a year or so wasn't a loss; just for washing, mending clothes, cooking, and such small tasks, hiring a servant would cost eight or ten yuan a month, including food and wages. By "marrying" a girl this way, she was both a servant and a bedmate, and bound to be clean and disease-free. If he was happy, he'd buy her a printed cotton gown for a few yuan. If not, he'd leave her to stay home stark naked, and she had no recourse. When he transferred away, he didn't regret the bed board and the chairs at all, because he'd leave behind two months' rent for her to figure out, and selling the bed board might not even cover that.

Xiaofuzi came back home after selling the bed board to pay the rent, wearing only a printed cotton gown and a pair of silver earrings.

After selling the rickshaw, Er Qiangzi had about twenty yuan left after clearing the pawn and interest. Sometimes he felt pitiful, a middle-aged widower; since no one else pitied him, he'd drink a cup, eat something nice, and console himself. At such times, he seemed to hold a grudge against money, spending recklessly. At other times, he thought he ought to work harder at pulling the rickshaw, raise his two boys properly, and have some hope for the future. When he thought about his sons, he'd buy a pile of odds and ends and feed them. Watching them wolf down the food, he'd have tears in his eyes and mutter to himself, "Motherless children! Hard-luck kids! Dad toils for the kids! I don't cheat my conscience—whether I'm full or not doesn't matter; the kids must eat enough! Eat up! Just don't forget me when you grow up!" At such times, he spent a lot of money too. Slowly, the twenty yuan all trickled away.

With no money left, and if he'd been drinking and falling into a rage, he'd go a day or two without caring what the kids ate. The kids had no choice but to think of ways to scrounge a few coppers to buy food. They'd run errands at weddings and funerals, follow dump carts to pick up scraps of metal and paper, sometimes afford a few sesame cakes, sometimes only a pound of sweet potatoes that they'd devour whole, peel and all, and sometimes they'd only have one big copper between them, so they'd buy peanuts or hard broad beans, which, though not filling, could be chewed longer.

When Xiaofuzi returned, they saw their kin again. Each clung to one of her legs, speechless, just smiling at her through tears. With no mother, their sister was their mother!

Er Qiangzi showed no reaction to his daughter's return. Her coming meant one more mouth to feed. But watching his two sons' joy, he couldn't deny the household needed a woman to cook and wash for everyone. He didn't feel like saying anything—let things take their course.

Xiaofuzi wasn't bad-looking. Though she had been thin and small before, after being with the officer, she had filled out and grown a bit taller. With a round face and well-proportioned features, she had nothing outstanding, but she was solid and not ugly. Her upper lip was short, so whether she was about to get angry or laugh, she'd part her lips first, revealing very white, even teeth. That officer had especially loved those teeth. When she showed them, she looked a bit simple and indecisive, yet also somewhat charmingly naive. This manner made her—like any poor but not unattractive girl—like a flower or weed that, with just a hint of fragrance or color, gets plucked and taken to market to be sold.

Tigress, who usually ignored the people in the compound, took Xiaofuzi as a friend. First, because Xiaofuzi had some looks; second, because she had a printed cotton gown; and third, because Tigress thought that having been married to an officer, she must have seen something of the world, so she was willing to associate with her. Women don't easily make friends, but when they do, it happens fast; within a few days, they were close friends. Tigress loved snacks, and whenever she had some melon seeds or the like, she'd call Xiaofuzi over, chatting and laughing while they ate. Amid their talk, Xiaofuzi would naively show her white teeth and tell Tigress many things she'd never heard. With the officer, she hadn't been spoiled, but when he was in a good mood, he'd take her to a restaurant or a show, so she had plenty to say, making Tigress envious. She also had many things she couldn't speak of: for her, they were humiliations; for Tigress, they were pleasures. Tigress begged her to tell, and though she was embarrassed, she couldn't refuse. She had seen pornographic pictures, which Tigress never had. Things like that, Tigress would listen to once and then want to hear again. She saw Xiaofuzi as the most lovely, most enviable, and even a bit jealous creature. After hearing it all, looking at her own looks, age, and husband, she felt her life had been too unfair. She had no youth, and no hope for the future. For now,Xiangzi was such a stubborn block through and through! The more dissatisfied she was with Xiangzi, the more she adored Little Fuzi. Though Little Fuzi was so poor and pitiful, in her eyes she was someone who had enjoyed happiness and seen the world; even if she died that instant, she'd have no regrets. In her view, Little Fuzi embodied all the pleasures a woman deserved.

Tiger Cub seemed not to notice Little Fuzi's hardships. Little Fuzi brought nothing back, yet she had to—no matter how worthless her father was—look after her two brothers. Where could she find money to prepare meals for them?

Er Qiangzi, drunk, had an idea: "If you truly care for your brothers, you can find a way to earn money to support them! You're relying on me to be their beast of burden all day, but I need to eat first; can I run on an empty stomach? Would you find it amusing if I tripped and fell dead? You're idle anyway, and there's something ready—why not sell it?"

Looking at her drunkard of a father, at herself, at her two brothers as hungry as mice, Little Fuzi could only cry. Tears couldn't move her father, tears couldn't fill her brothers' bellies—she had to come up with something more concrete. To feed her brothers, she had to sell her own flesh. Hugging her little brother, her tears fell on his hair as he said, "Sister, I'm hungry!" Sister! Sister was a piece of meat to feed her brother!

Not only did Tiger Cub fail to comfort Little Fuzi, she was even willing to help her out: Tiger Cub was ready to put up some capital to dress her up nicely so she could earn money and pay her back. Tiger Cub was willing to lend her a place, because her own room was too dirty, while Tiger Cub's at least had Xiangzi, and besides, there were two rooms so everyone had space to turn around. Since Xiangzi wouldn't be back during the day anyway, Tiger Cub was happy to help a friend, and could also see more and understand more of what she lacked—things she wanted to do but couldn't. Every time Little Fuzi used the room, Tiger Cub set a condition: she had to give her twenty cents. Friends were friends, business was business; for Little Fuzi's sake, she had to keep the room tidy, which required labor and extra expense. Didn't brooms and dustpans cost money? Twenty cents was hardly much, and because they were friends, she could show such consideration.

Little Fuzi bared her teeth slightly, her tears falling into her belly.

Xiangzi knew nothing of this, but he couldn't sleep well either. Tiger Cub had "made" Little Fuzi, and she also wanted to reclaim her lost youth through Xiangzi.

[1]

  Overhearing, i.e., conversation.

[2]

  Person, here meaning a man. This term is limited to informal male-female relationships.

Eighteen

By June, the compound was almost silent during the day. The children got up early, carrying broken baskets to scavenge whatever they could find; by nine o'clock, the scorching sun was about to crack their thin backs, so they had to bring back their findings and eat whatever food the adults could give them. Then, the older ones, if they could scrape together the smallest capital in the world, would buy and scavenge ice cores to sell. If they couldn't find this capital, they'd band together and go outside the city to bathe in the moat, snatching a few chunks of coal at the station on the way, or catching dragonflies and cicadas to sell to the children of wealthy families. The younger ones, afraid to wander far, would stay near the trees outside the gate, picking locust worms or digging for "jin gang" [1]  to play with. The children were all out, the men were all out, and the women, bare-backed indoors, refused to come out; not out of shame, but because the ground in the yard was so hot it burned their feet.

It wasn't until nearly sunset that the men and children began to return one by one. By then, the yard had some shade and a cool breeze, while the rooms, trapped with the day's heat, were like ovens; everyone sat in the yard, waiting for the women to cook. At this hour, the yard was very lively, like a market with no goods. Everyone had endured the day's heat, red-eyed and bad-tempered; hungry too, they all had pale, anxious faces. At the slightest disagreement, some would hit their children, others would beat their wives; even if no blows were exchanged, they'd curse to their hearts' content. This uproar continued until everyone had eaten. Some children fell asleep right in the yard, others went into the streets to play wildly [2]. After the adults were full, their tempers calmed down a bit, and the talkative ones gathered in small groups to discuss the day's hardships. Those who couldn't afford food found nothing left to pawn or sell—even if they had anything to pawn or sell—because night had fallen. The men, regardless of how hot the room was, would flop onto the kang without a word, or start yelling loudly. The women, with tears in their eyes, would go around borrowing, enduring countless rejections before scraping together a tattered twenty-cent note. Clutching this precious note, they'd go out to get some mixed flour to cook a pot of porridge for everyone.

Tiger Cub and Little Fuzi were outside this order of life. Tiger Cub was pregnant—this time for real. Xiangzi went out early, and she wouldn't get up until eight or nine; the belief that pregnant women shouldn't exercise much was a traditional fallacy. Tiger Cub believed it, and moreover, wanted to use it to assert some status: everyone had to get up early to work, but she alone could lie around leisurely whenever she pleased. In the evening, she'd take a small stool to sit outside the gate where there was a breeze, not coming in until nearly everyone in the yard was asleep; she disdained to chat with them.

Little Fuzi also got up late, but for a different reason. She was afraid of the men in the yard leering at her, so she waited until they were all gone before daring to leave her room. During the day, she either came to Tiger Cub or went out for walks, because her advertisement was herself. At night, to avoid the stares of the yard's residents, she'd wander the streets again, only sneaking back in when she guessed everyone was asleep.

Among the men, Xiangzi and Er Qiangzi were exceptions. Xiangzi dreaded entering this compound, and even more so going into the room. The talk of the yard's poor made him uneasy; he wanted a quiet place to sit alone. As for the room, he increasingly felt Tiger Cub was like a tigress. The little room was so hot and stifling, and with that tigress added, he felt he couldn't breathe as soon as he entered. A few days ago, he had no choice but to come back early to avoid Tiger Cub's nagging and quarreling. Lately, with Little Fuzi as her companion, she wasn't so strict with him, so he came back later.

As for Er Qiangzi, he'd hardly been coming home at all lately. He knew about his daughter's business and felt too ashamed to enter the gate. But he couldn't stop her, knowing he lacked the strength to support his children. He simply stopped coming back, reasoning that out of sight was out of mind. Sometimes he hated his daughter; if Little Fuzi had been a boy, he'd surely never have to suffer such disgrace. Since she was born female, why did she have to land with him! Sometimes he pitied his daughter; she was selling her body to support her two brothers! Whether he hated or pitied her, he was helpless. When he got drunk and had no money, he stopped hating and pitying; he'd come back and demand money from her. At such times, he saw his daughter as a money-making thing, and as her father, demanding money from her was perfectly justified. Then too, he'd think of face: weren't people despising Little Fuzi? Her father hadn't let her off either; he pushed her for money, cursing and swearing, as if to announce to everyone—Er Qiangzi was blameless; Little Fuzi was born shameless.

He ranted, and Little Fuzi didn't dare make a sound. It was Tiger Cub, half scolding and half coaxing, who managed to get rid of him, though naturally he had to take some money with him. That money was only good for him to drink again, because if he saw it sober, he'd go jump in the river or hang himself.

On the fifteenth of June, the heat went mad. As soon as the sun came out, the ground seemed on fire. A gray haze, neither cloud nor mist, hung low in the air, making people gasp for breath. Not a breath of wind. Xiangzi looked at the gray-red sky in the yard, planning to pull late shifts—going out after four in the afternoon; if he couldn't earn money, he could pull until dawn: the night was always more bearable than the day.

Tiger Cub urged him out, afraid he'd get in the way at home, in case Little Fuzi brought a customer. "You think it's any better staying home? By noon, even the walls are hot!"

Without a word, he drank a gourd of cold water and went out.

The willows on the street seemed sick, their leaves coated with dust and curling on the branches; the branches hung listlessly, too lazy to stir. The road was bone dry, glaring white. Dust on the sidewalk flew high, merging with the gray sky into a vicious cloud of grit stinging pedestrians' faces. Everything was dry, hot to the touch, stifling; the whole old city was like a fired brick kiln, suffocating. Dogs lay panting with red tongues, mules' nostrils flared wide, vendors dared not shout, the asphalt melted; even the brass signs in front of shops seemed ready to liquefy. The streets were unusually quiet, except for the irritating, monotonous clanging from the copper and iron shops. The pullers, knowing that without moving they'd have no food, still couldn't be bothered to hustle for business: some parked their rickshaws in shady spots, propped up the hoods, and dozed; others ducked into small teahouses for tea; some hadn't even brought out their rickshaws but came to the street to see if there was any chance of work. Those who did get a fare, even the handsomest young men, were willing to lose face, no longer daring to run, only shuffling slowly with their heads down. Every well became their salvation; no matter how few steps they'd taken, they'd dash for a well; if they missed the freshly drawn water, they'd drink their fill from the trough alongside the donkeys and horses. Some, struck by heatstroke or sunstroke, would suddenly collapse on the road, never to rise again.

Even Xiangzi was a bit scared! Pulling an empty rickshaw a few steps, he felt the heat wrapping around him from head to toe, sweat running even on the backs of his hands. But when he spotted a fare, he still wanted to pull, thinking maybe running would bring a breeze. He took a job, lifted the shafts, and realized the weather had reached a point where it wouldn't allow anyone to work. Running, he couldn't catch his breath, and his lips burned; though he knew he wasn't thirsty, he craved water at the sight of it. Not running, the blazing sun would crack his hands and back. Barely making it to the destination, his trousers and shirt were plastered to his body. He fanned himself with a palm-leaf fan, but the air was hot. He'd already drunk several gulps of cold water, but he ran to a teahouse. After downing two pots of hot tea, he calmed down a bit. The tea went in through his mouth, and sweat immediately poured from his body, as if he were hollow inside, unable to retain any moisture. He dared not move anymore.

After sitting a long time, he grew bored. Not daring to go out and with nothing to do, he felt the weather was deliberately tormenting him. No, he wouldn't give in. He'd been pulling rickshaws for more than a day, and this wasn't his first summer; he couldn't just "waste" a day like this. He wanted to go out, but his legs were too lazy to move, his body limp, like after an unsatisfying bath—though he'd sweated plenty, he still felt lousy. He sat a while longer, unable to bear it; he'd sweat anyway, so he might as well go out and try.

As soon as he stepped out, he realized his mistake. The gray haze in the sky had dissipated, making it less stifling, but the sunlight was far fiercer: no one dared look up to find the sun; everywhere was blinding, the sky, rooftops, walls, ground all glaring white, tinged with red; from top to bottom, it was like a huge burning mirror, each beam of light like its focal point, baking everything until it seemed ready to ignite. In this white glare, every color was harsh, every sound grating, every smell mixed with the stench rising from the earth. The streets seemed empty, the road suddenly wider, vast yet without a trace of coolness, a terrifying whiteness. Xiangzi didn't know what to do; head down, pulling the rickshaw, he moved forward extremely slowly, aimless, mindless, in a daze, covered in sticky sweat reeking of sourness. After a while, the soles of his feet stuck to his socks and shoes, like stepping on wet mud, terribly uncomfortable. He hadn't meant to drink more water, but at the sight of a well, he couldn't help gulping again, not to quench his thirst, but almost to savor the coolness of the well water—from mouth to stomach, a sudden chill, his pores contracting violently, a shiver, extremely pleasant. After drinking, he kept belching, and the water threatened to come back up!

He walked a bit, sat a bit, always too lazy to hustle for business. Come noon, he still didn't feel hungry. When he thought to eat something out of habit, the sight of food made him nauseous. His stomach was nearly full of various waters, sometimes gurgling softly, like the sound a mule makes after drinking.

Comparing winter and summer, Xiangzi always thought winter was more frightening. He'd never imagined summer could be so unbearable. He'd spent more than one summer in the city, but he didn't remember it ever being this hot. Was the weather hotter than previous years, or was his body weaker? At this thought, he suddenly felt less dazed, a chill in his heart. His body—yes, his body was failing him! He was scared, but there was no way out. He couldn't drive Tiger Cub away; he would become Er Qiangzi, become that tall man he'd met, become Little Horse's grandfather. Xiangzi was finished!

Just after one in the afternoon, he picked up another fare. This was the hottest part of the day.It happened to be the hottest day of that summer, but he decided to go out on a run. He didn't care how hot it was under the sun: if he finished a run and felt fine, it would prove his body wasn't worn out yet; but if he couldn't pull off this fare, then there was nothing more to say, falling dead on the scorching ground would be just as well.

He had only taken a few steps when he felt a slight cool breeze, like a wisp of cool air slipping in through a crack in a door in an extremely hot room. He couldn't believe it himself; looking at the willow branches by the roadside, they indeed swayed faintly twice. Suddenly, more people appeared on the street, and those in the shops rushed out, all clutching palm-leaf fans to shield their heads, looking around and calling out: "There's a cool breeze! There's a cool breeze! The cool wind is coming!" They almost jumped up and shouted. The willows by the roadside suddenly seemed like angels, delivering a message from the heavens: "The willow twigs are moving! Heaven, grant us a bit more cool wind!"

It was still hot, but his mind was much calmer. The cool breeze, even just a little, gave people a lot of hope. After a few gusts of cool wind, the sunlight grew less intense, brightening and then dimming slightly, as if some flying sand were drifting overhead. The wind suddenly picked up, and the willow branches that had been motionless for half a day, as if delighted by some good news, began to sway and dance gracefully, their tips seeming to have grown longer. After a gust of wind passed, the sky darkened, and dust flew into the air. Some dust settled, and on the northern horizon appeared ink-black storm clouds. Xiangzi was no longer sweating. He glanced northward, stopped the rickshaw, and put on the rain cover, knowing that summer rain came without warning, giving no time to spare.

Just as he finished putting on the rain cover, another gust of wind came, and black clouds rolled in, darkening half the sky. The heat from the ground mixed with the cool wind, carrying the smell of dry, dusty earth—both cool and hot. The southern half of the sky was clear and bright, while the northern half was covered in pitch-black clouds, as if some great disaster was imminent, throwing everything into panic. Rickshaw pullers hurried to put on rain covers, shopkeepers rushed to take down their shop signs, hawkers fumbled to pack up their stalls, and pedestrians quickened their pace. Another gust of wind came. After it passed, the shop signs, stalls, and people on the street seemed to have been swept away by the wind, disappearing entirely, leaving only the willow branches wildly dancing in the gale.

The clouds had not yet fully covered the sky, but the ground was already very dark, as if the bright, scorching noon had suddenly turned into night. The wind brought raindrops, darting around randomly, as if searching for something on the ground. A red flash of lightning appeared in the distant north, seeming to tear open a piece of the black clouds, revealing a patch like blood. The wind died down, but it was sharp and strong, making people shiver. After such a gust passed, everything seemed at a loss, even the willows waiting uneasily for something. Another flash of lightning struck directly overhead, and bright, white raindrops followed immediately, hitting the ground hard, raising a lot of dust that was faintly moistened by the rain. A few large raindrops hit Xiangzi's back, and he shivered twice. The raindrops stopped, and the black clouds spread evenly across the sky. Another gust of wind, stronger than before, sent willow branches flying sideways, dust scattering in all directions, and rain pouring down in sheets; wind, dust, and rain mingled together into one, creating a grey, chilly blur in every direction, enveloping everything, making it impossible to tell tree from ground from cloud. All around was chaos, noise, and confusion. The wind passed, leaving only straight sheets of rain, falling from sky to earth, no longer visible as individual streaks, just a vast, continuous mass, shooting up countless arrows from the ground and forming thousands of waterfalls from the rooftops. Within minutes, sky and earth were indistinguishable. A river fell from the sky, and rivers flowed across the ground, creating a dim, yellow-brown, sometimes bright white, watery world.

Xiangzi's clothes were already soaked through, not a dry spot left on his body; even under his straw hat, his hair was completely wet. The water on the ground was above his insteps, making it hard to walk; the rain above pounded directly on his head and back, lashed across his face, and soaked his crotch. He couldn't raise his head, couldn't open his eyes, couldn't breathe, couldn't take a step. He felt as if he were standing still in water, unaware of the road, unable to tell front from back or left from right, only feeling the bone-chilling cold water pouring over every part of his body. He was no longer aware of anything, only a vague warmth in his heart and the sound of rain in his ears. He wanted to put down the rickshaw, but didn't know where to put it. He thought of running, but the water trapped his legs. So, half-dead, he lowered his head and dragged himself forward step by step. The passenger seemed dead in the rickshaw, silent, letting the puller struggle for his life in the water.

The rain lightened a bit, and Xiangzi straightened his back slightly, letting out a breath: "Sir, how about we take shelter and then go?"

"Hurry up! What's the point of leaving me here?" the passenger shouted, stomping his feet.

Xiangzi really wanted to put the rickshaw down and find a place to take shelter. But looking at himself, with water streaming down, he knew that if he stopped, he would shiver into a ball. He gritted his teeth and started running through the water, regardless of its depth. He hadn't gone far when the sky darkened for a moment, then brightened, and the rain blurred his vision again.

They arrived at their destination, and the passenger didn't give a single extra copper coin. Xiangzi said nothing; he could barely take care of himself.

The rain paused for a moment, then came down again, much lighter than before. Xiangzi ran all the way home. Huddled by the fire, he dried himself for a while, shivering like a leaf in the wind and rain. Tigress brewed him a bowl of ginger and brown sugar water, and he, like an idiot, held the bowl and drank it all in one go. After drinking, he crawled into bed, unaware of anything, half-asleep, with the sound of rain rustling in his ears.

By around four o'clock, the black clouds began to show signs of fatigue, weakly flashing with faint red lightning. After a while, the clouds in the west split open, the black peaks edged with golden borders, and some white vapor racing beneath them; all the lightning moved to the south, dragging along some not-so-loud thunder. After a little longer, sunlight shone through the gaps in the western clouds, turning the rain-soaked leaves into a golden green. In the eastern sky hung a double rainbow, its ends plunging into the black clouds, its arch supporting a patch of blue sky. The rainbow soon faded, leaving no black clouds in the sky. The washed blue sky and everything else seemed reborn from darkness into a new, cool, beautiful world. Even over the puddles in the compound, a few colorful dragonflies appeared.

But aside from the barefoot children chasing those dragonflies, the people in the compound had no time to appreciate this clear sky after the rain. A section of the back wall of Joy's room had collapsed, and the three sisters were busy lifting the brick bed mat to plug the hole. The courtyard wall had collapsed in several places, but no one had time to attend to it, busy as they were cleaning their own rooms; some had steps too low, and water had already entered the house, so everyone frantically used dustpans and broken bowls to scoop out the water. Some saw their gable walls fall and tried to fill and block the gaps. Others had roofs leaking like watering cans, soaking everything, and they hurried to move their belongings out to dry by the stove or on the windowsill. During the downpour, they hid in those houses that could collapse at any moment and bury them alive, leaving their fate to the heavens; after the rain, they calculated and dealt with their losses. Although after a heavy rain, a pound of grain might cost half a copper less, their losses could not be compensated by that. They paid rent, but no one ever came to repair the houses; unless they were so dilapidated as to be uninhabitable, one or two masons would come and loosely patch things up with some plain mud and broken bricks—ready to collapse again. If rent wasn't paid, the whole family would be evicted, and their belongings confiscated. The houses were broken, they could kill people by collapsing, but no one cared. With the little money they had, they could only rent such places; broken, dangerous—they deserved it!

The biggest loss was getting sick from the rain. They, both children and adults, spent all day on the streets looking for work, and the summer rainstorms could pour down on their heads at any time. They all earned money by physical labor, always sweating from the heat, and the northern rainstorms were so sudden, so cold, sometimes mixed with hailstones the size of walnuts; the icy raindrops, hitting their open pores, would at least leave them lying on the brick bed, feverish for a day or two. If a child got sick, there was no money for medicine; a rainstorm could make the corn and sorghum in the fields grow tall, but it could also drown many poor city children. If adults fell ill, it was even worse; after the rain, poets would recite verses about lotus pearls and double rainbows, but in poor families, if an adult fell ill, the whole family went hungry. A rainstorm might add a few more prostitutes or petty thieves, send a few more people to prison; if an adult was sick, the children would rather steal or become prostitutes than starve! The rain fell on the rich and the poor alike; it fell on the righteous and the unrighteous. In fact, the rain was not fair, because it fell on a world without justice.

Xiangzi fell ill. He was not the only one in the compound who was sick.

[1] Jingang, meaning the pupa of the locust tree caterpillar.

[2] Sahuan, originally referring to animals frolicking and running wildly, also used to describe this kind of behavior in children.

十九  

Xiangzi lay in a daze for two days and nights, throwing Tigress into a panic. She went to the Goddess Temple and begged for a divine prescription: besides some incense ash, there were two or three herbal medicines. She forced them down his throat. He did open his eyes and look around, but after a while he fell asleep again, mumbling unintelligibly. Only then did Tigress think to call a doctor. After two acupuncture needles and a dose of medicine, he came to. As soon as he opened his eyes, he asked, "Is it still raining?"

When the second dose was ready, he refused to take it. Worried about money and hating himself for being so weak that a rainstorm could knock him out, he wouldn't drink that bitter brew. To prove he didn't need medicine, he wanted to put on his clothes and get up right away. But as soon as he sat up, his head felt like a heavy stone was weighing it down, his neck went limp, stars flashed before his eyes, and he collapsed again. Without another word, he took the bowl and gulped down the medicine.

He lay in bed for ten days. The longer he lay, the more anxious he became. Sometimes he would bury his face in the pillow and cry silently. He knew he couldn't earn money, so all expenses would have to be covered by Tigress out of her savings. Once her money ran out, everything would depend on his rickshaw. With Tigress's love for spending and eating, he couldn't afford to support her, especially now that she was pregnant! The more he couldn't get up, the more his mind wandered, and the more he worried, the harder it was to recover. As soon as he was out of danger, he asked Tigress, "Where's the rickshaw?"

"Don't worry, I've rented it out to Ding Si!"

"Ah!" He didn't trust his rickshaw, fearing that Ding Si or anyone else would damage it. But since he couldn't get up, he had to rent it out—what else could he do? He calculated: if he pulled it himself, he could make at least five or six dimes a day, even with a slack pull. Rent, coal, rice, firewood, lamp oil, tea—not even counting new clothes—would barely cover the two of them, and they'd have to scrape by, unlike Tigress who didn't care. Now, with only a little over a dime in daily rickshaw rent, he'd lose four or five dimes, not to mention the cost of medicine. If his illness never got better, what would he do? Yes, no wonder Er Qiangzi drank, no wonder those poor friends acted recklessly—pulling a rickshaw was a dead end! No matter how hard you worked or how determined you were, you couldn't start a family, get sick, or have any mishap. Hmph! He thought back to his first rickshaw, the little money he had saved—what had he done to deserve losing it? It wasn't because of illness or starting a family; it was just gone without reason! Good or bad, this path only led to death, and you never knew when it would come. With that thought, his worry turned to despair. Forget it, if he couldn't get up, he'd just lie there—that's how it was! He stopped thinking and lay still. But soon he couldn't stand it anymore and wanted to get up, to struggle on. The road was dead, but the heart was alive; until you were in your coffin, you always hoped. Yet he couldn't stand. Helplessly and pitifully, he wanted to say something to Tigress:

"I told you that rickshaw was unlucky, truly unlucky!"

"Just focus on recovering! Always talking about rickshaws, you rickshaw nut!"

He said nothing more. Yes, he was a rickshaw nut! Ever since he started pulling, he believed the rickshaw was everything, but... When he was a bit better, he got out of bed. Looking in the mirror, he didn't recognize the person there: a face full of stubble, sunken temples and cheeks, eyes like deep pits, and the scar covered in wrinkles! The room was stiflingly hot, and he didn't dare go into the yard—partly because his legs were as weak as if they had no bones, partly because he was afraid of being seen. Not just in this compound, but at every rickshaw stand east and west of the city, who didn't know Xiangzi was a top-notch tough guy? Xiangzi couldn't be this sickly wreck! He refused to go out. Inside, he felt suffocated. He wished he could get strong all at once and go out pulling. But illness destroys a person, coming and going as it pleases.

After resting for a month, whether he was fully recovered or not, he got on the rickshaw. He pulled his hat down low so no one would recognize him, and he could run at a moderate pace. "Xiangzi" and "speed" were inseparable; he couldn't just dawdle along and let people look down on him.

His body wasn't fully healed, but he greedily took on more fares to make up for the losses during his illness. After a few days, the illness returned, this time with dysentery added. He was so anxious he slapped himself, but it was useless. His belly seemed to touch his waist, and he kept having diarrhea. When the dysentery finally stopped, his legs could barely manage squatting and standing, let alone running.

He rested another month! He knew Tigress's money was probably almost gone! By the Mid-Autumn Festival, he decided to go out with the rickshaw. If he got sick again, he swore he'd jump into the river!

During his first illness, Joy often came by to check on him. Xiangzi was never good at arguing with Tigress, and feeling so pent up, he would occasionally chat with Joy. This angered Tigress. When Xiangzi wasn't home, Joy was a good friend; when Xiangzi was home, Joy was, in Tigress's mind, "flirting! Shameless!" She then pressured Joy to pay back the money she owed, saying, "From now on, don't come in again!"

Joy lost her place to entertain guests, and her own room was so dilapidated—the kang mat blocked the back wall—she had no choice but to sign up with the "Transport Company." But the "Transport Company" didn't need goods like her. They dealt with "female students" and "young ladies from good families," with high connections and big money; they didn’t want someone ordinary like her. She was stuck. She thought about going to a brothel, but without capital, she couldn't run her own business and would have to be pawned to a house. But that would mean losing all freedom—who would look after her two younger brothers? Dying was the simplest and easiest thing; living was already hell. She wasn't afraid of death, but she didn't want to die because she wanted to do something braver and greater than dying. She wanted to see her two brothers earn money, then she could die in peace. She would die sooner or later, but she had to die to save two! After thinking it over, she had only one path: sell herself cheap. Those willing to enter her little room naturally wouldn't pay much; fine, whoever came, just give a little money. This saved on clothes and makeup; those who came didn't expect her to dress up nicely—they paid for pleasure based on the money. She was young, which was already a bargain.

Tigress's body was no longer convenient; even going to the market to buy things felt risky. With Xiangzi gone all day and Joy unwilling to come over, she was as lonely as a dog tied up in the house. The lonelier she got, the more she resented, believing Joy's cheap sales were deliberately to annoy her. She wouldn't take such a loss: sitting in the outer room with the door open, she waited. Whenever someone headed toward Joy's room, she would shout gossip, embarrassing them and putting Joy on edge. Joy had fewer guests, and Tigress was pleased. Joy knew that if this continued, everyone in the compound would side with Tigress and drive her out. She was only afraid, not angry; someone in her situation knew to put facts before anger and tears. She brought her little brother over and knelt before Tigress without saying a word, but her expression showed: if this kneeling didn't work, she wasn't afraid to die, and no one else would live either! The greatest sacrifice was enduring humiliation, and the greatest endurance was preparing to resist.

Tigress was at a loss. No matter how she thought about it, it didn’t sit right, but with her big belly, she didn't dare fight. Since she couldn't use force, she had to give herself an out: she was just teasing Joy, never thinking it would turn serious. Joy took things too literally. With this explanation, they became friends again, and Tigress continued to support Joy.

Since starting work after the Mid-Autumn Festival, Xiangzi had been cautious everywhere. Two illnesses taught him he wasn't made of iron. His ambition to earn more money wasn't completely forgotten, but repeated blows made him realize how weak an individual's strength was. A good man had to grit his teeth when the time came, but gritting them could also make you spit blood! Though the dysentery was gone, his stomach still ached from time to time. Sometimes, when his legs were finally limbered up and he wanted to pick up speed, his stomach would twist like a rope, and he'd slow down or even suddenly stop, head down, belly sucked in, enduring it. When he had a single fare, it was manageable, but when he was in a group, stopping abruptly confused everyone and embarrassed him. He was only in his twenties and already making a fool of himself; what would it be like when he was thirty or forty? With that thought, he broke out in a cold sweat!

For his health, he wanted to go back to a regular contract job. At least then he could rest between shifts; you ran fast, but the breaks were longer, making it easier than picking up random fares. But he knew Tigress would never let go of him. Once married, you lost freedom, and Tigress was especially fierce. He accepted his bad luck. Over the half-year from autumn to winter, he half-managed, half-struggled, not daring to be careless or lazy, with a heavy heart, head down, slogging along. He no longer acted recklessly like before, caring about nothing. When it came to earning money, he still made more than the average rickshaw puller. Unless his stomach was cramping, he never let a fare slip away. He took whatever came his way and never picked up bad habits. He didn't learn to hold out for a higher price, switch fares mid-trip, or wait for the best customers. This way, he worked harder but earned money every day. He didn't cut corners, so he wasn't in danger.

But the money came in too little to save. In one hand, out the other, each day left them clean. He didn't even dare think about saving. He knew how to be frugal, but Tigress knew how to spend. Tigress's confinement was due in early February of the lunar new year. Since winter started, her belly had shown, and she liked to stick it out deliberately to show her importance. Looking at her belly, she hardly wanted to get off the kang. Cooking and meals were all left to Joy, which naturally meant the leftovers and scraps went to Joy's brothers. That cost a lot. Besides meals, she had to have snacks; the more her belly showed, the more she felt she needed good food—she couldn't starve her mouth. Not only did she buy odds and ends at any time, but she also told Xiangzi to bring something back every day. Whatever he earned, she spent, and her demands rose and fell with his earnings. Xiangzi couldn't say anything. When he was sick, he spent her money, so tit for tat, he had to spend on her. If Xiangzi pinched pennies, she would immediately fall ill, saying, "Pregnancy is over nine months of sickness, what do you know?" She wasn't lying. By the New Year, she had even more ideas. Unable to move herself, she sent Joy back and forth to buy things. She hated not being able to go out, yet she cherished herself and wouldn't go, but staying in made her bored, so she bought more things to feel better. She insisted she wasn't buying for herself but for Xiangzi: "You've worked hard all year, shouldn't you eat a bit? You haven't been strong since your illness; if you don't eat at year's end, you'll starve like a shriveled bedbug!" Xiangzi couldn't argue and wouldn't; when the food was ready, she'd eat two or three big bowls. After eating, with no exercise, she'd feel bloated and insist she had pregnancy complications!

After the New Year, she absolutely forbade Xiangzi from going out at night. She could give birth anytime, and she was scared. Only then did she remember her real age, though she wouldn't say it outright, and no longer told him, "I'm only 'a little' older than you." With all this fuss, Xiangzi was bewildered. The continuation of life was just having children, and Xiangzi couldn't help but feel a little pleased. Even if he didn't want a child at all, the thought of becoming a "father"—the simplest yet most mysterious word—made even a hard-hearted person pause and think. No matter how he thought about it, the word moved him. Xiangzi, clumsy as he was, couldn't see any good in himself or anything to be proud of; but thinking of this wondrous word, he suddenly felt his own worth, as if nothing else mattered, as long as there was a child, life wouldn't be empty. At the same time, he wanted to provide for and serve Tigress as best he could. She was no longer alone; even if she was annoying, in this matter she had all the credit. But no matter how great her merit, her fussing was unbearable. She changed her mind every minute, making a racket over nothing, while Xiangzi had to go out to earn money, needing rest. Even if money could be spent thoughtlessly, he needed a peaceful night's sleep to struggle again the next day. She wouldn't let him out at night and wouldn't let him sleep well. He had no idea what to do, dazed all day, not knowing where to turn. Sometimes happy, sometimes anxious, sometimes annoyed, sometimes ashamed of his happiness, sometimes consoling himself in his anxiety, sometimes happy in his annoyance—emotions whirled in his mind, confusing the simplest man until he didn't know east from west. Once, he even pulled a fare past the destination, forgetting where they had hired him to go!

Around the Lantern Festival, Tigress decided to call for the midwife.She could hold out no longer. The midwife came and told her it was not yet time, describing some of the signs of labor. She endured two more days, then the pain started up again. The midwife was summoned once more, but still it was not time. She cried and screamed, wanting to kill herself, unable to bear the torment any longer. Xiangzi was completely at a loss; to show he was doing his best, he had to go along with her demands and temporarily stop pulling his rickshaw.

The torment continued until the end of the month, and even Xiangzi could see that the time had truly come. She no longer looked human. The midwife came again and gave Xiangzi a hint; she feared it might be a difficult labor. Huniu's age, the fact that this was her first child, her usual lack of exercise, and the baby's large size — due to her craving for greasy food during pregnancy — all combined to make a smooth delivery hopeless. Moreover, she had never been examined by a doctor, and the baby's position had never been corrected. The midwife didn't have the skill for that, but she could say: "I'm afraid it might be a transverse or breech delivery!"

In this mixed compound, the birth of a child and the death of its mother were commonly spoken of together. But Huniu faced more dangers than others. Other women worked and moved about right up until the day of delivery, and they didn't eat enough, so the baby wasn't large, making birth easier. Their danger lay in postpartum complications, but Huniu was the exact opposite. Her advantages were precisely her downfall.

Xiangzi, Joy, and the midwife stayed by her side for three days and three nights. She called out to every god and Buddha, and made countless vows, but none of it worked. Finally, her voice was hoarse, and she could only whisper, "Mama! Mama!" The midwife was helpless; everyone was helpless. In the end, she came up with the idea herself, telling Xiangzi to go outside Desheng Gate to invite Granny Chen the Second — who was possessed by a Toad Immortal. Granny Chen wouldn't come without five dollars. Huniu took out her last seven or eight dollars: "Dear Xiangzi, hurry and go! Don't worry about the money! When I'm better, I'll be good and live with you! Go quickly!"

Granny Chen the Second arrived with her "acolyte" — a sallow-faced, forty-something-year-old man — just before lamplighting time. She was about fifty, dressed in a blue silk jacket, wearing a red pomegranate flower in her hair and a full set of gilded jewelry. Her eyes were glassy. Upon entering, she first washed her hands, then lit incense; she kowtowed herself, then sat behind the incense table, staring dully at the glowing incense tips. Suddenly, her whole body shook, she gave a violent shudder, dropped her head, closed her eyes, and remained still for a long time. The room was so quiet you could hear a pin drop; even Huniu clenched her teeth and dared not make a sound. Slowly, Granny Chen raised her head, nodding as she looked around; the "acolyte" tugged at Xiangzi, telling him to kowtow quickly. Xiangzi didn't know if he believed in gods or not, but he felt kowtowing couldn't be wrong. In a daze, he didn't know how many times he bowed. Standing up, he looked at those glassy "divine" eyes and the burning red incense tips, smelling the incense smoke, vaguely hoping that this ceremony might bring some benefit. Dazed, cold sweat formed on his palms.

The Toad Immortal spoke in an old, gruff voice, with a bit of a stutter: "D-d-don't worry! I'll draw a p-p-prompt delivery charm!"

The "acolyte" quickly handed over a piece of yellow cotton paper. The immortal grabbed at the incense tips a few times, then moistened the paper with spit and drew on it.

After drawing the charm, she stammered a few more sentences: the general idea was that Huniu owed a debt to this child from a past life, so she had to suffer some torment. Xiangzi, muddle-headed, didn't understand much, but he felt a bit scared.

Granny Chen gave a long, drawn-out yawn, closed her eyes and was still for a moment, then opened them as if just waking from a deep sleep. The "acolyte" quickly reported the immortal's words. She seemed very pleased: "The immortal is happy today, talkative!" Then she instructed Xiangzi on how to make Huniu drink the divine charm, and gave her a pill to take with it.

Granny Chen the Second eagerly waited to see the charm's effect, so Xiangzi had to prepare some food for her. Xiangzi entrusted this to Joy. Joy bought hot sesame seed cakes and spiced pork shoulder; Granny Chen even complained that there was no wine to drink.

After Huniu took the charm, and Granny Chen and her "acolyte" had eaten, Huniu still tossed and turned in pain. This went on for over an hour; her eyes gradually began to roll back. Granny Chen had another idea; unhurriedly, she told Xiangzi to kneel before a tall stick of incense. Xiangzi's faith in Granny Chen was already running thin. But since he had already spent five dollars, he might as well try all her methods; not wanting to beat her, he might as well follow her advice — just in case it worked!

Kneeling stiffly before the tall incense, he didn't know which god he was praying to, but he wanted to be devout. Watching the incense flame flicker, he pretended to see some shapes in the fire and prayed silently. The incense burned lower, black streaks appearing in the flame. He lowered his head, hands on the ground, feeling drowsy and sleepy — he hadn't slept well in two or three days. Suddenly, his neck went limp, startling him. He looked again; the incense had burned down to almost nothing. Not caring whether it was time to stand up, he slowly pushed himself up from the ground, his legs numb.

Granny Chen and the "acolyte" had already slipped away.

Xiangzi didn't bother to resent her; he hurried over to see Huniu, knowing things had reached a desperate point. Huniu was only gasping for air, no longer able to make a sound. The midwife told him to try getting her to a hospital; her methods were exhausted.

Xiangzi felt as if his heart had suddenly cracked; he opened his mouth wide and wept. Joy also shed tears, but being in a position to help, she kept a clearer head. "Brother Xiang! Don't cry yet! Let me go to the hospital and ask?"

Whether Xiangzi heard her or not, she wiped her tears and ran out.

She was gone for about an hour. When she ran back, she was too breathless to speak. Leaning on the table, she coughed dryly for a long time before she could get the words out: Ten dollars for a doctor to come, just to look, not to deliver. Twenty dollars for delivery. If it was a difficult labor, she'd have to go to the hospital, which would cost several dozen. "Brother Xiang! What should we do?!" Xiangzi had no way out; he could only wait for whoever was meant to die to die!

Stupidity and cruelty were phenomena here; but the reasons for the stupidity and cruelty lay elsewhere.

At midnight, Huniu breathed her last, with a dead child.

[1]

 背拉，即平均。

[2]

 头顶头的，即第一等的。

Twenty

Xiangzi's rickshaw was sold!

Money flowed like water; he couldn't hold it back. The dead had to be carried out, and even getting a funeral notice cost money.

Xiangzi stood like an idiot, watching everyone bustle about; he just kept handing out money. His eyes were frighteningly red, with yellow-white mucus gathered in the corners; his ears were deaf; he wandered around dazedly with the crowd, not knowing what he was doing.

Following Huniu's coffin out of the city, he became a bit clearer, but his mind still couldn't focus on anything. No one followed the funeral procession except Xiangzi and Joy's two younger brothers, each holding a thin stack of paper money, scattering it along the way for the ghosts blocking the road.

Dazedly, Xiangzi watched the bearers lower the coffin into the grave; he didn't cry. His mind felt like a blazing fire, burning away all his tears; he wanted to cry but couldn't. He stared blankly, hardly knowing what was happening. Only when the foreman came to give instructions did he remember to go home.

The room had already been tidied up by Joy. When he got back, he collapsed onto the kang, too exhausted to move. His dry eyes couldn't close; he stared blankly at the ceiling with its rain stains. Unable to sleep, he sat up. He glanced around the room but dared not look again. He didn't know what to do. He went out and bought a pack of "Yellow Lion" cigarettes. Sitting on the edge of the kang, he lit one; he didn't really want to smoke. Staring blankly at the blue smoke rising from the tip, suddenly tears streamed down his face. He didn't just think of Huniu, but of everything. In the years since he came to the city, this was the result of his hard work — that's how it was, that's how it was! He couldn't even sob out loud! The rickshaw, the rickshaw — it was his livelihood. He bought one, lost it; bought another, sold it — three rises and three falls, like a ghostly shadow, never able to hold onto it, enduring all that hardship and grievance for nothing. Gone, everything was gone, even his wife! Huniu was fierce, but without her, how could he have a home? Looking at the things in the room, they were all hers, but she herself was buried outside the city! The more he thought, the angrier he got; tears were cut short by fury. He sucked hard on the cigarette; the more he disliked it, the more he insisted on smoking it. When he finished the cigarette, he held his head in his hands. His mouth and heart were both burning; he wanted to scream wildly, to spit out all the blood in his heart, just to feel some relief.

He didn't know when, but Joy had come in. Standing in front the kitchen counter in the outer room, she stared blankly at him.

He looked up suddenly and saw her; tears came again, very quickly. At that moment, even if he saw a dog, he would have cried. His heart was full of grievance; he had to vent it when he saw a living thing. He wanted to talk to her, to get some sympathy. But there was too much to say; his mouth couldn't open.

"Brother Xiang!" She moved closer. "I've put everything away."

He nodded, unable to thank her. Politeness in grief is hypocrisy. "What do you plan to do?"

"Huh?" He seemed not to understand, but then he caught on and shook his head — he couldn't think about anything.

She took another two steps forward, her face suddenly reddening, showing a few white teeth, but she couldn't speak. Her life had forced her to forget shame, but when faced with serious matters, she was still a woman with a true heart: a woman's heart relies heavily on shame. "I think..." That was all she managed to say. She had so many thoughts in her heart; but once she blushed, they all scattered and she couldn't remember them.

True words between people are rare; a woman's blush is worth more than a long speech. Even Xiangzi understood her meaning. In his eyes, she was the most beautiful woman, beautiful to the bone — even if her whole body were covered in sores and her skin rotted away, in his heart she would still be beautiful. She was beautiful, she was young, she was strong-willed, she was thrifty. If Xiangzi wanted to remarry, she was the ideal person. He didn't want to remarry right away; he couldn't think about anything. But since she was willing, and because the pressure of life forced her to bring it up immediately, he seemed to have no way to refuse. She herself was so good, and she had helped him so much; he could only nod. He truly wanted to go over and hug her, cry his heart out, wash away all his grievances, and then struggle on together with her, of one mind. In her, he saw the comfort a man could and should get from a woman. He wasn't much of a talker, but when he saw her, he was willing to chat casually; with her listening, his words wouldn't be wasted. Her nod or her smile was the most perfect reply, making him feel like he truly had a "home."

Just then, Joy's second younger brother came in: "Sister! Dad's here!"

She frowned. As soon as she pushed open the door, Er Qiangzi was already in the yard. "What were you doing in Xiangzi's room?" Er Qiangzi's eyes were wide, his feet stumbling, swaying as he lurched forward: "Haven't you sold yourself enough? Now you're letting Xiangzi have you for free? You shameless thing!"

Hearing his name, Xiangzi hurried out, standing behind Joy. "I say, Xiangzi," Er Qiangzi wobbled, trying to puff out his chest, but couldn't even stand steady, "I say, Xiangzi, are you even a man? You can take advantage of anyone, but why her? What's the matter with you?"

Xiangzi didn't want to bully a drunk, but the pent-up frustration in his heart made him unable to control his anger. He took a step forward. Four red eyes met, as if they might spark and ignite in the air. Xiangzi grabbed Er Qiangzi by the shoulder and, as if lifting a child, flung him far away.

The stirring of conscience, fueled by alcohol, turned into violence: Er Qiangzi's drunkenness was somewhat feigned. The fall sobered him up halfway. He wanted to fight back, but he knew he was no match for Xiangzi. To just walk out meekly felt deeply unsatisfying. He sat on the ground, unwilling to get up, but also not wanting to stay there. His mind was in chaos, so he just blurted out: "I'm disciplining my own child, what business is it of yours? Hitting me? Like I care! Who do you think you are?"

Xiangzi didn't want to answer back; he just waited quietly for him to attack.

Joy, with tears in her eyes, didn't know what to do. Pleading with her father was useless, and watching Xiangzi hit him made her uneasy. She searched all her pockets and scraped together a dozen or so copper coins, handing them to her brother. Her younger brother usually never dared to get near his father, but today, seeing him knocked to the ground, he gathered some courage. "Here, take this and go!"

Er Qiangzi squinted, took the money, and as he stood up, muttered: "Damn it, just you wait, you little bastards! If you piss off your old man, I'll get a knife and kill every last one of you!" As he reached the gate, he shouted, "Xiangzi! Remember this! We'll settle it outside!"

After Er Qiangzi left, Xiangzi and Joy went into the room together.

"I have no choice!" she said to herself, and this sentence summed up all her difficulties, while also holding infinite hope — if Xiangzi was willing to marry her, then she would have a way out.

Xiangzi, after this ordeal, saw many shadows in her.He still loved her, but he couldn't bear the responsibility of supporting her two younger brothers and her drunken father! He dared not imagine that with Tigress's death, he would be free; Tigress had her good points too, at least she had helped him a lot financially. He dared not think about Joy taking a bite out of his livelihood, but it was also absolutely true that none of her family could earn a living. Love or not, the poor had to decide based on money; "love children" were only born in wealthy households.

He began to pack his things.

"You're moving out?" Joy's lips went completely white.

"Moving out!" He hardened his heart. In a world without justice, the poor relied on ruthlessness to maintain their personal freedom, that very small, very tiny bit of freedom.

She glanced at him, then lowered her head and walked out. She didn't hate, didn't get angry, just felt despair.

Tigress's jewelry and nicer clothes were all taken to the grave. What remained were just some shabby old clothes, a few pieces of furniture, and some bowls, pans, and pots. Xiangzi picked out a few of the better clothes from the pile and set them aside; the rest, clothes and utensils alike, he sold. He called over a ragman and sold the lot for a lump sum of over ten dollars. He was eager to move, eager to get rid of these things, so he had no mind to find more people to haggle slowly over the price. The ragman gathered the things and left. Only his bedding and those few selected clothes remained in the house, placed on the brick bed which had no mat. The room was completely empty, and he felt somewhat relieved, as if he had shaken off many entanglements and could now fly far away. But soon after, he thought of those things again.
