The table had been moved out, but the marks from its legs remained—little piles of fine dust, forming small squares against the base of the wall. Looking at these traces, he remembered the things, remembered the people, all vanished like a dream. Good or bad things, good or bad people, without them, there was no place for his heart to rest. He sat on the edge of the brick bed and pulled out another "Yellow Lion" cigarette.

Along with the cigarette, he pulled out a torn banknote. Half-heartedly, he took out all his money; for the past two days, he hadn't bothered to count it. He pulled out a heap: silver dollars, banknotes, copper coin tickets, copper coins, everything. The pile wasn't small, but when he counted, it was less than twenty dollars. Adding the ten-plus dollars from selling things, his entire property was only thirty-some dollars.

He laid the money on the bricks of the bed and stared at it, not knowing whether to laugh or cry. The room had no people, no things, only himself and this pile of old, moldy money. What was the point of this?

He let out a long sigh, helplessly stuffed the money into his chest pocket, then picked up his bedding and those few clothes to go find Joy.

"Keep these clothes for yourself! Leave my bedding here for a while, I'll go find a good rickshaw yard first, then come back for it." Not daring to look at Joy, he kept his head down and finished saying this in one breath.

She said nothing, just acknowledged him twice.

Xiangzi found a rickshaw yard, came back for his bedding, and saw that her eyes were already swollen from crying. He didn't know what to say, but managed to force out two sentences: "Wait for me! When I've made something of myself, I'll come! I'll definitely come!"

She nodded, saying nothing.

Xiangzi only rested for one day before he went back to pulling his rickshaw as usual. He wasn't as eager to hustle for fares as before, but he didn't deliberately slack off either; he just drifted through the days, bland but not bored. After drifting like this for over a month, he felt very calm inside. His face filled out a bit, but wasn't as rosy as before; his complexion was yellow, not showing much strength, but not looking weak either. His eyes were bright, but had no expression, always shiny as if full of spirit, yet as if they saw nothing. His demeanor was like a tree after a storm, standing quietly in the sunlight, not daring to move at all. He had never liked talking, and now he was even less inclined to speak. The weather was already warm, and the willow branches were covered with tender leaves. Sometimes he would park his rickshaw facing the sun, muttering to himself with his head down and lips moving slightly; sometimes he would lie back and bask in the sun, taking a short nap; except when he had to speak, he hardly talked to anyone at all.

But he had become addicted to cigarettes. As soon as he sat in his rickshaw, his big hand would reach under the chest cushion. Lighting a cigarette, he would inhale and exhale very slowly, his eyes following the smoke rings upward, staring blankly, then nodding, as if he had figured something out.

When he pulled the rickshaw, he was still faster than most rickshaw pullers, but he no longer ran desperately. He was especially careful at corners, turns, and on uphill and downhill slopes. Almost overly careful. If someone tried to race him, no matter how much they teased or provoked him, he kept his head down, said nothing, and ran on at the same steady pace. He seemed to have seen through what pulling a rickshaw was all about, and no longer wanted any glory or praise from it.

At the rickshaw yard, though, he made friends. Even though he didn't talk much, a silent wild goose still likes to fly in a flock. If he didn't make friends anymore, he was afraid his loneliness would become unbearable. Whenever he took out his cigarette case, he would pass it around to everyone. Sometimes, seeing there was only one left in his case, people would hesitate to reach out, and he would simply say, "I'll buy more!" When people were gambling, he no longer hid in a corner like before; he would come over and watch, and sometimes place a bet, not caring whether he won or lost, just to show he was sociable and understood that everyone deserved some fun after a few days of hard work. When they drank, he joined in; he didn't drink much, but he would buy some food and drinks for everyone to share. Things he used to look down on now seemed to have some meaning to him—since his own path was blocked, he had to admit others might be doing the right thing. If someone among his friends had a wedding or funeral, he used to not understand social obligations, but now he would contribute forty copper coins or chip in for a "public gift"[2]

. Not only did he pay money, but he also went personally to mourn or celebrate, because he realized these events weren't just about wasting money, but involved human relationships that had to be fulfilled. Here, people cried or laughed for real, not just as a joke.

He didn't dare touch that thirty-some dollars. He got a piece of white cloth and clumsily sewed the money inside with a big needle, always keeping it against his skin. He didn't want to spend it, nor did he want to buy another rickshaw; he just kept it with him as a precaution—who knew what disasters lay ahead! Sickness, unexpected accidents, could come to him at any time; he had to be prepared. Man is not made of iron, he finally understood.

Just before the Beginning of Autumn, he took on a monthly contract job again. This time, the work was lighter than any previous household job; if it hadn't been, he wouldn't have taken it. He now knew how to choose his work; he would only take a monthly contract if it suited him; otherwise, he was fine pulling casual fares, no longer eagerly chasing after household jobs like before. He realized he should take care of his body; a rickshaw puller who tried to push himself to the limit—like he used to—would only die without gaining anything. Experience taught a person to be a bit slick, because you only have one life!

This time, his workplace was near the Lama Temple. His employer's surname was Xia, over fifty years old, educated and refined; he had a wife and twelve children at home. Recently, he had taken a concubine, but didn't dare let his family know, so he deliberately chose a quiet place to set up a separate small household. This small household near the Lama Temple consisted only of Mr. Xia and his new concubine; besides them, there was a maid and a rickshaw puller—Xiangzi. Xiangzi liked this job. Take the courtyard for starters: there were only six rooms in all. Mr. Xia lived in three, the kitchen took up one, and the remaining two were servants' quarters. The courtyard was very small, and against the south wall stood a half-grown jujube tree, with a dozen or so half-red jujubes hanging from its tip. When Xiangzi swept the yard, he could almost go from one end to the other with just two or three sweeps of the broom—very easy. There were no flowers or plants to water, and he felt like tidying up the jujube tree, but he knew how willful a jujube tree could be, all twisted and resistant to pruning, so he didn't bother.

Other tasks were few. Mr. Xia went to his office at the government office in the morning and didn't return until five in the afternoon, so Xiangzi only had to take him there and pick him up. Once home, Mr. Xia didn't go out again, as if he were hiding. Mrs. Xia went out often, but always returned around four o'clock, so Xiangzi could go pick up Mr. Xia—once he brought him back, Xiangzi's work for the day was done. Besides, the places Mrs. Xia went were just Dong'an Market and Zhongshan Park; after dropping her off, he had plenty of rest time. This bit of work, Xiangzi did as if playing around.

Mr. Xia was very tight-fisted, reluctant to part with a single coin; when he came and went, he never looked sideways, as if there were no people or things on the street. But the mistress was generous, going out to buy things every few days; if it was food and it tasted bad, she gave it to the servants; if it was something useful, when she wanted to buy something new, she would first give the old one to the servants, then negotiate with Mr. Xia for money. Mr. Xia's life mission seemed to be to dedicate all his energy and money to his concubine; besides that, he had no life or enjoyment. His money only left his hands through the concubine's; he couldn't spend it himself, let alone give it to others—it was said that his legitimate wife and twelve children in Baoding sometimes went four or five months without receiving a single coin from him.

Xiangzi detested this Mr. Xia: all day long he bent his back, shrank his neck, crept in and out like a thief, always staring at his toes, never making a sound, never spending money, never laughing, even sitting in the rickshaw he looked like a skinny monkey; but occasionally when he did say a word or two, they were extremely unpleasant, as if everyone was a bastard, and only he was the refined gentleman. Xiangzi didn't like people like this. But he treated "work" as "work"; as long as he got paid each month, why care about anything else?! Besides, the mistress was quite open-minded, and he often got some food and supplies; forget it, might as well be pulling an inhuman monkey. As for the mistress, Xiangzi just saw her as a woman who might give him some pocket money, and didn't particularly like her. She was much prettier than Joy, and soaked in perfumes and powders, wrapped in silks and satins, something Joy could never match. However, even though she was beautiful and dressed stylishly, he didn't know why, but whenever he saw her, he thought of Tigress; there was always something about her that reminded him of Tigress—not the clothes, not her appearance, but some attitude or aura, for which Xiangzi couldn't find the right words. He just felt that she and Tigress were, in the words he could think of, cut from the same cloth. She was very young, at most twenty-two or twenty-three, but she had a very mature air, nothing like a newlywed, just as Tigress had never had a young girl's shyness or gentleness. She had permed hair, wore high heels, and her clothes were cut just right to accentuate her curves. Even Xiangzi could tell that, although she dressed so fashionably, she didn't have the bearing of the typical lady. But she didn't seem like she came from a brothel either. Xiangzi couldn't figure her out. He just felt she was somewhat frightening, as frightening as Tigress. However, Tigress wasn't as young or as beautiful as her; so Xiangzi was even more afraid of her, as if she carried all the sharpness and venom of women that he had experienced. He hardly dared to look her straight in the eye.

After a few days there, he became even more afraid of her. When he took Mr. Xia out, Xiangzi never saw him spend any money; but sometimes Mr. Xia did go shopping—to the pharmacy to buy medicine. Xiangzi didn't know what kind of medicine it was; but whenever they bought it, the couple seemed particularly happy, and even the quiet Mr. Xia seemed unusually lively. After being lively for two or three days, Mr. Xia would become breathless again, and his back would bend even deeper, much like a live fish bought from the street, splashing around in water for a while before soon settling down. Whenever Xiangzi saw Mr. Xia sitting in the rickshaw looking like a corpse, he knew it was time to go to the pharmacy again. He didn't like Mr. Xia, but every time they went to the pharmacy, he couldn't help feeling sorry for this old skinny monkey. When Mr. Xia came back carrying the medicine package, Xiangzi would think of Tigress, and feel an indescribable discomfort. He didn't want to hold a grudge against the dead, but looking at himself and looking at Mr. Xia, he couldn't help but resent her; no matter how you looked at it, his body wasn't as strong as before, and Tigress bore most of the responsibility.

He seriously considered quitting. But quitting over such a trivial matter didn't seem right; smoking his "Yellow Lion," he muttered to himself, "Why mind other people's business?!"

[1]

 搁着这个碴儿, meaning to leave it unresolved for now and deal with it later.

[2]

 公议儿, a collectively agreed-upon gift.

**21**

When the chrysanthemums went off the market, Mrs. Xia bought four pots of flowers, and the maid Yang Ma accidentally broke one, causing a quarrel between them. Yang Ma came from the countryside and fundamentally thought flowers and plants were nothing important; but since she had broken someone else's thing, no matter how unimportant, it was still her carelessness, so she didn't dare say a word. But Mrs. Xia kept on endlessly, cursing with vulgar and nasty insults, and Yang Ma's temper finally got the better of her, so she talked back. When a country person gets angry, they don't hold back; she poured out the coarsest, most vile language. Mrs. Xia jumped up and down, cursing for a while,Tell Yang Ma to pack up and get the hell out right now.  

Xiangzi never came over to mediate; his tongue wasn't built for squabbling, let alone refereeing a fight between two women. When he heard Yang Ma cursing Xia Tai-tai as a whore, a slut for a thousand men and ten thousand to grope, he knew Yang Ma's gig was up. At the same time, he realized that if Yang Ma was finished, so was he; Xia Tai-tai probably wouldn't keep a servant who knew her history. After Yang Ma left, he waited to be fired, figuring the new maid's arrival would be his cue to pack up. But he didn't fret over it; experience had made him cool about starting and quitting jobs, no point in getting sentimental.  

However, after Yang Ma left, Xia Tai-tai was surprisingly polite to Xiangzi. Without a maid, she had to cook herself. She gave Xiangzi money to buy groceries. When he came back, she told him what to peel and what to wash. While he peeled and washed, she cut meat and cooked, chatting with him as she worked. She wore a pink undershirt with black pants and white satin embroidered slippers on her feet. Xiangzi kept his head down, clumsy at the work, not daring to look at her but wanting to. Her perfume kept wafting strongly into his nose, as if telling him he had to look, like fragrant flowers luring bees and butterflies.  

Xiangzi knew the ferocity of women, and also their charms; one Tigress was enough to make anyone fear women yet still be reluctant to let them go. And Xia Tai-tai was far beyond comparison with Tigress. Xiangzi couldn't help stealing two glances at her—if she was as terrifying as Tigress, she also had much more to admire and desire.  

Two years ago, Xiangzi wouldn't have dared to look at her like that. Now, he didn't care as much: first, having been seduced by women before, he could no longer restrain himself. Second, he had gradually fallen into the rickshaw puller's rut: what most pullers thought was right, he now agreed with; since his own hard work and self-denial had failed, the crowd's behavior must make sense. He had to be a rickshaw puller, like it or not; standing out wouldn't work. So, grabbing a bargain was considered proper by the suffering masses. Why shouldn't Xiangzi take one? He eyed this woman—yes, she was just a woman! If she was willing, Xiangzi couldn't refuse. He didn't dare believe she'd stoop so low, but what if? If she made no move, he wouldn't either; if she gave the slightest hint, he'd be at a loss. Had she already hinted? Otherwise, why fire Yang Ma without hiring someone else right away, and make Xiangzi help with the cooking instead? Why put on so much perfume just to go into the kitchen? Xiangzi dared not decide anything, dared not hope for anything, yet inside he vaguely wanted to decide something, to have some hope. He felt like he was having a wonderful dream that wasn't real, knowing it was a dream but wanting to keep dreaming. Life's heat forced him to admit he was worthless, and in that worthlessness lay the greatest joy—or perhaps the greatest misery, who cared!  

A glimmer of hope stirred up some courage; a little courage sparked great heat; a fire burned in his heart. There was nothing base about it—neither he nor she was base; desire was equal for both!  

A flash of fear awakened reason; a bit of reason doused the fire in his heart; he almost wanted to run away. There was only misery here; anyone who took this path would end up a joke!  

Now hope, now fear, his heart was like a fever. It was harder than when he'd met Tigress; back then, he knew nothing, like a young bee landing on a spider web; now, he knew how to be careful and how to be bold, yet he was inexplicably slipping down, while clearly fearing the fall!  

He didn't look down on this concubine, this prostitute, this beauty—she was everything, yet nothing at all. If he could find any excuse for himself, he thought, it was that old shriveled monkey, Mr. Xia, who was despicable and deserved his comeuppance. With a husband like that, nothing she did was wrong. With a master like that, he—Xiangzi—could do anything too. He grew bold.  

But she didn't seem to notice whether he looked at her or not. After cooking, she ate alone in the kitchen; finished, she called out, "Xiangzi, you eat. After you're done, wash the utensils. When you pick up the master this afternoon, buy the evening groceries on the way to save another trip. Tomorrow's Sunday, the master will be home, and I'll go find a maid. Do you know anyone to recommend? Maids are really hard to come by! Alright, go eat first, don't let it get cold!"  

She spoke very naturally, with great composure. That pink undershirt suddenly—in Xiangzi's eyes—seemed much more modest. He was a bit disappointed instead, and from disappointment felt ashamed, seeing clearly that he was no longer a strong-willed man, not just not strong-willed, but a bad man! He wolfed down two bowls of rice in a daze, feeling deeply bored. He washed the dishes, went to his room, and sat down, chain-smoking who knew how many "Yellow Lions" cigarettes.  

When he went to pick up Mr. Xia in the afternoon, for some reason he hated that old shriveled monkey fiercely. He really wanted to pull the rickshaw fast, then let go and throw the old man half to death. Only then did he understand: before, when he'd pulled for a certain household, the master's third concubine and eldest son had been involved, and when the master found out, the eldest son nearly poisoned the master to death; he'd thought the eldest son was just young and foolish, but now he saw how that master deserved to die. Yet he didn't want to kill anyone; he just found Mr. Xia annoying and despicable, with no way to punish him. He deliberately jounced the rickshaw shafts to shake the old monkey a few times. The old monkey said nothing, which actually made Xiangzi uncomfortable. He'd never done such a thing before, and even when he had a reason, he couldn't forgive himself. Regret made him indifferent to everything—why deliberately stir up trouble? No matter what, he was a rickshaw puller; just do the job well and that was it. What was the use of thinking about other things?  

He calmed down and forgot this pointless affair; when he occasionally recalled it, he found it laughable.  

The next day, Xia Tai-tai went out to find a maid. She came back soon with a trial worker. Xiangzi gave up hope, but no matter how he thought about it, it didn't feel right.  

After lunch on Monday, Xia Tai-tai dismissed the trial maid, finding her too dirty. Then she told Xiangzi to buy a catty of chestnuts.  

He bought a catty of cooked chestnuts and called out from outside her door.  

"Bring them in," she said from inside.  

Xiangzi entered; she was powdering her face in front of the mirror, still wearing that pink undershirt but with light green pants now. Seeing Xiangzi in the mirror, she quickly turned and smiled at him. In that smile, Xiangzi suddenly saw Tigress—a young, beautiful Tigress. He stood frozen. His courage, hope, fear, caution—all gone, leaving only a hot breath, expandable or contractible, holding his whole body together. This breath could push him forward or pull him back; he had no will left.  

The next evening, he carried his bedroll back to the rickshaw shed.  

What he usually feared and found most shameful, he now laughed off and blurted out to everyone—he couldn't pee anymore!  

Everyone rushed to tell him what medicine to buy or which doctor to see. No one found it shameful; they all sympathetically offered advice, blushing a bit while proudly recounting their own experiences. Several young men had bought the disease with money, several middle-aged men had caught it for free, and those who had done monthly contracts all had experiences of varying severity but the same nature. Some who had done monthly contracts hadn't personally experienced it but had stories about their masters worth telling. Xiangzi's ailment opened their hearts, and they shared intimate words. He forgot his shame, but didn't take pride in it either, just calmly enduring the sickness, hardly different from catching a chill or heatstroke. When it hurt, he regretted it a little; when comfortable, he recalled the sweetness again. Either way, he didn't panic; life's experiences taught him to take life lightly—what use was panic?  

A little of this medicine, a folk remedy here and there, cost him over ten dollars, but the disease wasn't eradicated. He'd half-heartedly stop taking medicine when he thought he was better. When it rained or the seasons changed, his joints would ache, and he'd take some medicine temporarily or tough it out, not taking it seriously. Since fate was bitter to the core, what was the body? Thinking this way, even a fly finds joy on a dung heap, let alone a grown man like him.  

After the sickness passed, he became almost another person. He was still tall, but his righteousness was gone; he deliberately hunched his shoulders forward, drooped his mouth, and clamped a cigarette between his lips. Sometimes he'd tuck half a cigarette behind his ear, not for convenience but just to show off. He still wasn't talkative, but when he did speak, he'd force a little wit, even if it wasn't smooth—at least it had that flair. With his mind slack, his posture and demeanor became sloppy.  

Still, compared to ordinary rickshaw pullers, he wasn't all that bad. When he sat alone and remembered his former self, he still wanted to be strong, unwilling to just slide downhill. Though being strong was useless, destroying himself wasn't any better either. At such times, he'd think of buying a rickshaw again. He'd had over thirty dollars saved up, but treating his illness had cost over ten—wasted money! But with about twenty dollars as a base, he still had more hope than those who started from nothing. Thinking this, he felt like throwing away the half-empty pack of "Yellow Lions," quitting smoking and drinking from then on, gritting his teeth to save money. From saving money he thought of buying a rickshaw, and from buying a rickshaw he thought of Joy. He felt a bit guilty toward her—ever since leaving the compound, he'd never gone to see her, and instead of improving, he'd gotten himself a filthy disease!  

But when he met his friends, he'd still smoke and drink when the chance came, forgetting Joy completely. With his buddies, he didn't start anything, but if someone wanted to do something, he couldn't refuse to join in. The day's hardships and pent-up grievances could only be forgotten by chatting and fooling around with them. Immediate comfort drove away lofty ambitions; he wanted a moment of happiness, then a deep, dark sleep. Who wouldn't like that, since life was so boring, painful, and hopeless? The sores of life could only be numbed for a while by the poisons of tobacco, alcohol, and women—fighting poison with poison, until one day the poison hits the heart. Who didn't know that? But who had a better idea to replace it?!  

The less he tried, the more he pitied himself. Before, he feared nothing; now, he sought ease: when it rained or blew, he wouldn't go out; if he ached a little, he'd rest for two or three days. Self-pity made him selfish; he wouldn't lend a cent of his money, saving it just to fall back on during bad weather. He could share tobacco and alcohol, but not money—he was more precious and pitiable than anyone. The lazier he got, the more idle he became, and with nothing to do he felt bored, so he constantly needed entertainment or a good meal. When he thought about wasting time and money like this, he always had a ready saying, forged by countless experiences: "Back then, I tried to be strong. Did I get a single bit of benefit?" No one could refute that, no one could explain it away. So who could stop Xiangzi from sinking lower?!  

Laziness made him short-tempered. Xiangzi now knew how to glare at people. At passengers, at cops, at anyone, he decided he wouldn't just go through the motions politely anymore. When he'd worked hard and sweated, he'd never gotten fairness. Now, he knew how precious his sweat was, sparing every drop he could; if anyone tried to take advantage, forget it. He'd park his rickshaw carelessly, too lazy to move, whether it was a legal spot or not. When a cop came to interfere, he'd use his mouth, not his body, stalling as long as he could. When he saw he absolutely had to move the rickshaw, his mouth wouldn't shut up—he'd curse. If the cop didn't want to take the abuse, then a fight wouldn't hurt; luckily, Xiangzi knew his own strength—beat the cop up first, then go to jail, no loss. Fighting made him feel his strength and skill again, smashing his power into another's flesh; he saw light, the sun seemed especially bright. Saving his strength for a fight—he'd never even thought of that before, but now it was real, and it was something that could cheer him up for a while. Thinking about it, how funny!  

Don't even mention an unarmed cop; he wasn't afraid of the cars rampaging through the streets, either. When a car came head-on, kicking up all the dust on the road, Xiangzi wouldn't dodge, no matter how loud the horn or how anxious the passengers. The car had no choice but to slow down. When it slowed, Xiangzi would step aside, eating much less dust. If a car came from behind, he'd use the same trick. He figured it out—since cars wouldn't dare run someone over, why get out of the way early?To teach it to raise the dust, wouldn’t it? Traffic police are specifically meant to clear the way for cars, afraid they won’t go fast enough and won’t kick up enough dust. Xiangzi isn’t a traffic cop, so he won’t allow cars to run wild. In the eyes of the police, Xiangzi is a top-notch “troublemaker,” but they don’t dare provoke a troublemaker either. A poor man’s laziness is the natural result of working hard and getting nothing in return; a poor man’s defiance carries a bit of justice.

With his passengers, he was absolutely unyielding. Where they said to go, there he’d go, not one step farther. If they said “at” the alley entrance but meant him to pull “into” the alley, no way! If a passenger glared, Xiangzi’s glare was even bigger. He knew how much those gentlemen in Western suits feared getting their clothes dirty, and he also knew how domineering and stingy most of those gentlemen were. Fine, he was ready for that; if words failed, he’d reach out and grab the sleeve of their fifty-or-sixty-dollar suit and at least leave a big black handprint! Give them that handprint, and they’d still have to pay up—they knew how strong that big hand was, that one grab had already squeezed their thin arms sore.

He wasn’t slow, but he wouldn’t speed up for nothing. If a passenger urged him on, his big feet would drag on the ground: “Hurry? How much extra?” No politeness; he was selling his sweat and blood. He no longer hoped for a few extra tips out of their generosity—you get what you pay for, and if they wanted more effort, they had to settle the price first.

As for the rickshaw, he no longer cherished it. Since his desire to buy his own had cooled, he was indifferent to others’ rickshaws. A rickshaw was just a rickshaw; pulling it could earn him his meals and the daily rent, that was all. If he wasn’t pulling it, he didn’t have to pay rent, so as long as he had enough money for a day’s food, he didn’t need to take it out. The relationship between man and rickshaw was nothing more than that. Naturally, he still wouldn’t deliberately damage someone else’s rickshaw, but he didn’t bother to take extra care of it either. Sometimes, if another puller accidentally bumped it and left a scratch, he wouldn’t fly into a rage and quarrel; instead, he’d calmly pull it back to the yard. If it warranted a fifty-cent compensation, he’d offer twenty cents and call it done. If the yard owner refused, that was easy—the final solution was always a fight; if the owner wanted to fight, Xiangzi was ready!

Experience is the fertilizer of life; whatever experience you have shapes the person you become—you can’t grow peonies in a desert. Xiangzi had completely fallen into the groove. He wasn’t better than other rickshaw pullers, nor worse—just a rickshaw puller among rickshaw pullers. This way, he felt more comfortable than before, and others found him easier to get along with; crows are all black, and he didn’t expect to be the one white-feathered one.

Winter came again, and the yellow wind from the desert could freeze many people to death in a single night. Listening to the wind, Xiangzi buried his head in the quilt, not daring to get up. Not until the wind stopped its wolf-like howling and ghostly wailing did he reluctantly rise, unsure whether to go out or take the day off. He couldn’t bear to grasp the icy rickshaw handles, afraid of the choking, sickening wind. The fierce wind yields to sunset; it wasn’t until past four o’clock that the wind completely died down, and the dusky yellow sky showed a faint hint of sunset red. He forced himself to muster some spirit and pulled the rickshaw out. With his hands tucked in his sleeves, he leaned his chest against the rickshaw handles, swaying slowly and listlessly, a half-smoked cigarette dangling from his lips. Soon, it grew dark, and he thought he’d hurry and get a couple of fares so he could knock off early. Too lazy to light the lamps, he only did so after the patrolmen along the way urged him four or five times.

In front of the Drum Tower, under the lamplight, he grabbed a fare heading to the eastern city. Without even taking off his heavy cotton robe, he jogged along in a daze. He knew it looked shabby, but so what? Who would pay him extra for looking proper? This wasn’t pulling a rickshaw—it was just getting by. When sweat broke out on his forehead, he still wouldn’t take off his long coat; he’d make do as best he could. Entering a small alley, a dog, apparently displeased at seeing a rickshaw puller in a long coat, ran after him barking. He stopped the rickshaw, grabbed his duster backward, and furiously chased the dog, swinging at it. He kept driving the dog until it was out of sight, then waited a while to see if it dared come back. The dog didn’t return, and Xiangzi felt a little better: “Damn it! Think I’m scared of you?” “What kind of rickshaw puller are you, anyway? I’m asking you!” the passenger said impatiently.

Xiangzi’s heart stirred—the voice sounded familiar. The alley was pitch black; the rickshaw lamp was bright, but the light shone downward, and he couldn’t see who was inside. The passenger wore a large wind帽, with both mouth and nose wrapped inside a thick scarf, only two eyes visible. Xiangzi was still wondering when the passenger spoke again: “Aren’t you Xiangzi?”

Xiangzi understood—it was Fourth Master Liu! A wave of heat surged through him, and he didn’t know what to do.

“Where’s my daughter?”

“Dead!” Xiangzi stood there blankly, not knowing if it was himself or someone else who had uttered those two words.

“What? Dead?”

“Dead!”

“Falling into your goddamn hands, how could she not die?!”

Xiangzi suddenly found himself: “Get down! Get down! You’re too old to take a beating from me—get down!”

Fourth Master Liu got down, his hands trembling. “Where is she buried? I’m asking you!”

“None of your business!” Xiangzi picked up the rickshaw and walked away.

After going a long way, he looked back. The old man—like a big dark shadow—was still standing there.

[1] Show off a little, act clever.

CHAPTER 22

Xiangzi forgot which way he was going. He walked with his head high, both hands gripping the handles firmly, a gleam in his eyes, taking great strides forward; intent only on walking, regardless of direction or destination. His heart was light, his body felt relaxed, as if he had vented all the bad luck he'd suffered since marrying Tigress on Fourth Master Liu. Forgetting the cold, forgetting to look for fares, he only wanted to walk on, as if somewhere along the way he could recover his former self, that unburdened, pure, ambitious Xiangzi who strove in everything. Thinking of that dark figure standing in the lane, that old man, it seemed nothing more needed to be said; defeating Fourth Master Liu was defeating everything. Although he hadn't landed a single punch or a single kick on the old fellow, the old man had lost his only relative, while Xiangzi was free and at ease; who could say this wasn't retribution! Even if the old man didn't die of rage, he'd be close to it! Old Man Liu had everything, Xiangzi had nothing; yet now Xiangzi could still happily pull his rickshaw, while the old man couldn't even find his daughter's grave! Well, so what if you, old man, have piles of silver dollars and a terrible temper; you can't control this poor wretch who scrapes together two meals a day!

The more he thought, the happier he got. He really wanted to sing out loud, let the whole world hear this song of triumph — Xiangzi was alive again, Xiangzi was victorious! The cold evening air stung his face, but he didn't feel cold, only exhilarated. The streetlights gave off a chill glow, yet Xiangzi felt a warm comfort inside; light was everywhere, illuminating his future. He hadn't smoked for ages and didn't want to now. From now on, no more tobacco or liquor. Xiangzi would start over, strive to be strong again as before. Today he had defeated Fourth Master Liu, and he would defeat him forever; Fourth Master Liu's curses would only make Xiangzi more successful, more hopeful. Having spat out that foul breath, from now on Xiangzi would always breathe fresh air. Looking at his own hands and feet, wasn't Xiangzi still young? Xiangzi would be young forever. Let Tigress die, let Fourth Master Liu die, but Xiangzi would live, happy, ambitious, alive — evil people all get their just deserts, all die. Those soldiers who stole his rickshaw, Mrs. Yang who wouldn't feed her servants, Tigress who cheated and bullied him, Fourth Master Liu who looked down on him, Detective Sun who swindled him, Second Grandma Chen who fooled him, Mrs. Xia who seduced him... all would die, only loyal Xiangzi would live, live forever! "But Xiangzi, you have to work hard from now on!" he admonished himself. "Why wouldn't I work hard? I've got ambition, I've got strength, I'm young!" He answered back: "With a cheerful heart, who can stop Xiangzi from settling down and making his way? Put what happened these past days on anyone else, who could be happy, who wouldn't slide downhill? That's all over; tomorrow you'll see a new Xiangzi, better than before, much better!"

Muttering to himself, he put even more force into his steps, as if to bear witness to his own words — not just empty talk, he really did have a strong body. Though he'd been sick, had suffered shameful ailments, what did that matter? With a change of heart, his body immediately grew stronger, no problem! He was drenched in sweat, his mouth felt parched. He wanted a drink of water, and only then realized he had reached the Rear Gate. Not bothering to go to a teahouse, he parked his rickshaw at the "stand" west of the gate, called over the tea-selling boy carrying a big earthen pot and yellow earthenware bowls, and drank two bowls of tea that tasted like dishwater. It was terribly bitter, but he told himself, from now on he'd have to drink this kind, no more wasting money on fine tea and good food. Having made this decision, he might as well eat something — something hard to swallow — as the start of a new life of hard work and endurance. He bought ten fried dumplings, filled mostly with cabbage stalks, the skins tough and gritty with sand. No matter how hard to swallow, he choked them all down. After eating, he wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. Where should he go now?

In his heart, there were only two people he could turn to, could rely on. If he wanted to strive and be strong, he would have to find them — Joy and Mr. Cao. Mr. Cao was a "saint," he would surely understand and help him, give him good advice. Following Mr. Cao's plan, with Joy's help besides; he'd handle the outside, she'd manage the home, they'd surely succeed, they'd surely succeed, there was no doubt about it!

Who knew whether Mr. Cao had returned or not? Never mind, tomorrow he'd go inquire on North Long Street; if he couldn't find out there, he'd go ask at the Zuo residence. As long as he found Mr. Cao, everything would be fine. Well, for today, he'd pull for one evening; tomorrow he'd find Mr. Cao, and after finding him, he'd go see Joy and tell her the good news: Xiangzi hadn't made good, but he was determined to, and they'd work together and forge ahead!

Having planned this, his eyes were as sharp as an eagle's. Glancing around, he spotted a fare and dashed over like the wind, stripping off his padded jacket before they'd even settled on a price. When he ran, his legs weren't what they used to be, but a surge of heat supported his whole body; he ran for all he was worth! Xiangzi was still Xiangzi; when he ran desperately, no one else could compete. Seeing one rickshaw ahead, he overtook it, as if possessed. Sweat poured out freely. After completing a run, he felt much lighter, his legs had their spring back, and he wanted to run again, like a thoroughbred that hasn't run its fill and still kicks its hooves after stopping. He kept running until one in the morning before he put his rickshaw away. Back at the shed, after deducting the rental fee, he had over nine dimes left.

He slept until dawn; turned over, opened his eyes, and the sun was already high. Rest after exhaustion was the sweetest enjoyment. He got up and stretched, his joints cracking crisply. His stomach felt completely empty; he was extremely hungry for something to eat. After eating a bit, he smiled and told the shed owner: "Taking a day off, got things to do." He calculated in his mind: take a day off, get everything settled, and start a new life tomorrow. He headed straight for North Long Street, to try his luck, just in case Mr. Cao had returned. As he walked, he prayed silently: Mr. Cao, for heaven's sake, please be back, don't let me make a wasted trip! If the first thing goes wrong, everything will go wrong! Xiangzi has reformed, won't the Lord of Heaven bless him?

When he reached the Cao residence, his hand trembled as he rang the bell. Waiting for someone to open the door, his heart felt like it would leap out. Facing this familiar gate, he didn't dwell on the past; he only hoped the door would open and he'd see a familiar face. He waited, wondering if maybe no one was in the courtyard; why else would it be so quiet, so quiet it was almost frightening. Suddenly there was a noise inside, and he jumped. The door opened, and the sound of the door was mixed with a most precious, most cordial "Oh!" It was Gao Ma! "Xiangzi? Well, this is a rare sight! How thin you've gotten!" Gao Ma, however, had put on some weight. "Is the gentleman home?" Xiangzi couldn't spare words for anything else. "He's home. That's just like you, only knowing about the gentleman, as if we don't even know each other! Not even a greeting! You really are something, always the 'bashful carpenter — makes one cut'! Come in! You've been doing well for yourself, huh?" she asked as she walked inside. "Hmph! Not well!" Xiangzi laughed. "Say, sir," Gao Ma called from outside the study, "Xiangzi is here!" Mr. Cao was inside the room, moving the narcissus plant to follow the sunlight. "Come in!" "Hey, go on in; we'll talk later; I'll go tell the mistress; we're always talking about you! A simpleton has simple luck, you see!" Gao Ma muttered as she went inside.

Xiangzi entered the study. "Sir, I'm here!" He wanted to greet him, but the words wouldn't come out. "Ah, Xiangzi!" Mr. Cao was standing in the study, dressed in a short jacket, with a pleasant, clean smile on his face. "Sit down! That —" he thought for a moment. "We came back quite a while ago. Old Cheng told me you were at — right, the Harmony Shed. Gao Ma even went looking for you once, but didn't find you. Sit down! How are you? Things going well?" Xiangzi's tears threatened to fall. He couldn't confide in others, because his words were forged from blood, buried deep in his heart. After calming himself for a long while, he wanted to let those blood-formed, simple words pour out. Everything was in his memory; the moment he thought of it, it all came back. He would have to arrange and organize it slowly. He was about to narrate a living history. Though he didn't fully grasp its meaning, the string of grievances was real and clear.

Mr. Cao saw that he was thinking, gently sat down, and waited for him to speak. Xiangzi hung his head and was silent for a long time. Suddenly he looked up at Mr. Cao, as if feeling that if he couldn't find someone to listen, it might be better not to speak at all. "Go on!" Mr. Cao nodded. Xiangzi began to recount the past, starting with how he came from the countryside to the city. He hadn't wanted to talk about these useless things initially, but without them, his heart couldn't find relief, and the story wouldn't be complete. His memory was built of sweat, blood, and suffering; he couldn't speak of it lightly, and once he started, he didn't want to cut off the head or tail. Every drop of sweat, every drop of blood had flowed from his life, so every incident was worth telling. How he had come to the city, worked as a coolie, then switched to pulling a rickshaw. How he had saved to buy his own rickshaw, how he had lost it... he went on until he reached his current situation. Even he found it strange how he could speak at such length and so fluently. One incident after another, all seemed to want to jump out of his heart. The events themselves seemed to find the right words, sentence after sentence, each one real, lovable, and pitiful. His heart couldn't stop the stories from coming out, and his words couldn't stop. Without hesitation or confusion, he seemed to want to pour out his whole heart in one breath. The more he talked, the more relieved he felt, forgetting himself, because he was wrapped up in those words; every sentence contained that ambitious, wronged, suffering, degenerate Xiangzi. When he finished, he was sweating, and his heart felt empty, empty in a comfortable way, like the comfortable, hollow feeling after fainting and breaking into a cool sweat. "So you want me to advise you now?" Mr. Cao asked. Xiangzi nodded; he had finished speaking and seemed unwilling to open his mouth again. "You still have to pull a rickshaw?" Xiangzi nodded again. He couldn't do anything else. "Since you still have to pull a rickshaw," Mr. Cao said slowly, "that leaves only two paths. One is to scrape together the money to buy a rickshaw; the other is to rent one for the time being, right? Since you have no savings, borrowing money to buy a rickshaw means paying interest, isn't that the same? Better to just rent one for now. It's still better to be a live-in puller; the job is steady, and you get room and board. I think you might as well come work for me again; I sold my rickshaw to Mr. Zuo. If you want to come, you'll have to rent one, okay?" "That would be wonderful!" Xiangzi stood up. "Sir, you don't hold that matter against me?" "Which matter?" "That time, when you and the mistress ran off to the Zuo residence!" "Oh!" Mr. Cao laughed. "Who remembers that! I was a bit too panicked that time. The mistress and I went to Shanghai for a few months, actually quite unnecessary. Mr. Zuo had already settled things. That Ruan Ming is an official now and treats me quite well. But, you probably didn't know that; anyway, I don't remember a thing about it. Let's talk about us: what about that Joy you mentioned? What will happen to her?" "I don't know what to do!" "Let me think for you: if you marry her and rent a room outside, it's still not worth it; rent, coal, lamp, charcoal, all cost money, and it won't be enough. If she goes to work with you, but where would you find such a coincidence — you pull a rickshaw, she works as a maid — not easy to arrange! This is hard!" Mr. Cao shook his head. "Don't misunderstand me, but is she reliable?" Xiangzi's face reddened; he stammered for a long time before finally speaking: "She had no choice but to do that. I'd stake my life on it, she's a good woman! She..." His mind was in turmoil; many different feelings congealed into one mass, then suddenly seemed to split, all wanting to burst out; he was speechless. "If that's the case," Mr. Cao said hesitantly, "unless I can accommodate you two here. If you were alone, you could take one room; if you two are together, you can also have one room. Living quarters wouldn't be a problem. I don't know if she can wash and sew and do housework. If she can do some work, she could help Gao Ma. The mistress will be having a baby soon, and Gao Ma is a bit too busy alone. She'd eat my food for free, but I wouldn't pay her wages. What do you think?" "That would be wonderful!" Xiangzi smiled innocently. "But I can't decide this entirely on my own; I need to discuss it with the mistress!" "Of course! If the mistress has any doubts, I'll bring her here and let the mistress see her!" "That's fine too," Mr. Cao laughed, not expecting Xiangzi to have such a clever idea. "Well then, let me first mention it to the mistress. Some other day, you bring her over; once the mistress nods, we're all set!" "Then, sir, I'll be off?" Xiangzi was eager to find Joy and tell her this good news he hadn't even dared to hope for.

Xiangzi left the Cao residence. It was probably around eleven o'clock, the loveliest time of a winter's day. The day was exceptionally clear and beautiful, not a cloud in the blue sky. Sunlight streamed through the dry, cold air, giving people a pleasantly warm feeling. Chicken crows, dog barks, and the cries of peddlers could be heard from far away; sharp, clear sounds carried across the streets, like the call of cranes descending from the sky. All the rickshaws had their canvas hoods up, the brass fittings gleaming yellow. On the side of the road, camels walked slowly and steadily.In the heart of the streets, cars and trams raced by, people and horses moved about on the ground, and white doves flew in the sky. The entire old city was full of motion within stillness, chaotic yet exhilarating, still yet exhilarating—a cacophony of sounds, ten thousand forms of life, all spread beneath the clear, crisp blue sky, with trees standing quietly everywhere.

Xiangzi's heart felt like it was about to leap out, soaring straight into the air to circle with the doves! He had everything now: work, wages, and Little Fuzi—it had all been neatly resolved in just a few sentences, something he'd never even imagined! Look at this sky, so clear and dry, just like the straight-talking, forthright people of the north. When a person meets with good fortune, even the weather seems better; he felt he'd never seen such a lovely winter's day. To show his happiness more concretely, he bought a frozen persimmon. One bite, and his mouth was full of icy slush! The cold stabbed at the roots of his teeth, slowly spreading from his mouth down to his chest, making his whole body shudder. He finished it off in a few bites, his tongue a bit numb, but his heart content. He strode off with big steps to find Little Fuzi. In his mind, he could already see that rundown compound, that small room, and the one he loved; it was as if only a pair of wings was needed to carry him there instantly. As long as he saw her, everything from before could be wiped clean, and from then on, a whole new world would open up. His urgency now surpassed even the time he went to see Mr. Cao. Mr. Cao's relationship with him was one of friend and master-servant, a mutual exchange of goodwill. But she was more than a friend—she would entrust her whole life to him. Two people from hell would wipe away their tears and walk forward hand in hand, smiling. Mr. Cao's words could move him, but Little Fuzi could move him without saying a word. He had spoken truthfully to Mr. Cao; now he would speak even more intimately to Little Fuzi, things he could tell no one else but her. She, right now, was his very life; without her, nothing mattered at all. He couldn't just work for his own food and drink; he had to rescue her from that small room, and then live together with her in a clean, warm place, happy, dignified, and affectionate, like a pair of little birds! She could ignore Er Qiangzi, and she could ignore her two younger brothers—she had to come and help Xiangzi. Er Qiangzi could fend for himself, and the two brothers could manage by sharing a rickshaw or doing something else; but Xiangzi, without her, was lost. His body, his spirit, his affairs—there wasn't a single part of him that didn't need her. And she needed a man like him too.

The more he thought about it, the more eager and joyful he became. There were plenty of women in the world, but not one was as good or as suitable as Little Fuzi! He had been married, had stolen love; he had touched both beauty and ugliness, the old and the young—but none of them had ever lodged in his heart. They were just women, not companions. Sure, she wasn't the pure, spotless girl he had once imagined, but precisely because of that, she was more pitiable and could help him more. That foolish village girl might be very innocent, but she would never have Little Fuzi's capability and insight. Besides, what about himself? He had plenty of dark spots in his own heart too! So then, the two of them were a perfect match—neither above nor below the other, like a pair of jars, both with cracks, yet both able to hold water, fitting perfectly together on the shelf.

No matter how he thought about it, this was the most fitting thing. After mulling this over, he began to think practically: first, he'd ask Mr. Cao for an advance on a month's wages, buy her a cotton gown, tidy up her shoes and feet, and then take her to meet Mrs. Cao. Dressed in a new, plain, long cotton gown, with her head and feet clean and neat, with her looks, age, and poise, she would certainly be presentable and surely win Mrs. Cao's favor. No doubt about it!

When he reached the place, he was drenched in sweat. Seeing that broken-down gate, it was like glimpsing a home he hadn't returned to in years: the broken gate, the broken walls, the few withered yellow weeds on the gatehouse—all of it seemed lovely. He entered the gate and headed straight for Little Fuzi's room. Without bothering to knock or call out, he yanked the door open. The moment he opened it, he instinctively stepped back. On the kang sat a middle-aged woman; since there was no fire in the room, she was wrapped in a tattered quilt. Xiangzi stood stunned outside the door. A voice came from inside: "What's the matter? Announcing a death or something? Why do you barge into someone's house without a word?! Who are you looking for?" Xiangzi didn't want to speak. All the sweat on his body suddenly dried up. Leaning against the broken door, he couldn't bear to abandon all hope: "I'm looking for Little Fuzi!" "No idea! Next time you look for someone, ask first before yanking the door open! Little Fuzi, big Fuzi—who knows!"

Sitting at the main gate, he was dazed for a long time. His mind was empty; he forgot what he was doing. Slowly, he began to recall a little—a thought no bigger than Little Fuzi herself. Little Fuzi walked back and forth in his mind, like a paper figure on a revolving lantern, endlessly pacing to and fro, completely useless. It seemed he had forgotten his relationship with her. Gradually, Little Fuzi's image shrank a bit, and more activity stirred in his heart. Only then did he feel the pain.

When uncertain whether things will turn out good or bad, people always think the best first. Xiangzi guessed that maybe Little Fuzi had moved, nothing more drastic than that. It was his own fault—why hadn't he come to see her more often? Shame drove him to action, to make up for his mistakes. Best to start by asking around. He went back into the compound and found an old neighbor to inquire. He got no definite news. Still not daring to lose hope, not even bothering to eat, he thought of looking for Er Qiangzi; or even finding her two younger brothers would do. Those three men were always out on the streets, so it shouldn't be hard to track them down.

He asked everyone he met—at the rickshaw stands, in the teahouses, in the rundown compounds—using his legs to the fullest, walking and asking all day long. No news.

That evening, he returned to the rickshaw shed, utterly exhausted, but still couldn't let this matter go. After a day of disappointment, he didn't dare hope for anything more. The suffering die easily, and when they die, they are easily forgotten. Could it be that Little Fuzi was no longer alive? Even if she hadn't died, it was possible that Er Qiangzi had sold her off to some faraway place—which would be worse than death!

Tobacco and drink became his friends again. How could he think without smoking? How could he stop thinking without getting drunk?

[1]

 Skin, not scorched.

[2]

 Gritty, impure flour that feels like biting into sand when eaten.

Twenty-three

Xiangzi wandered through the streets like a lost soul and ran into Little Ma's grandfather. The old man no longer pulled a rickshaw. His clothes were thinner and more tattered than before. He carried a willow staff, with a large clay teapot hanging from the front and a broken treasure-basket hanging from the back, containing some sesame cakes, fried dough sticks, and a large brick. He still recognized Xiangzi.

As they talked, Xiangzi learned that Little Ma had been dead for over half a year. The old man had sold his broken rickshaw and now spent his days selling tea and pastries at the rickshaw stands. He was still as kind and friendly as ever, but his back was much more stooped, his eyes watered in the wind, and his eyelids were perpetually red, as if he had just been crying.

Xiangzi drank a bowl of his tea and briefly poured out some of the grievances in his heart. "You think a man can make it on his own?" the old man judged Xiangzi's words. "Who doesn't think that way? But who actually makes it? Back in the day, I had a strong body and a good heart, and I've kept on like this all the way to now—look at what I've become! A strong body? Even a man of iron can't escape this net that heaven and earth have cast. A good heart? What use is it? 'Good is rewarded with good, evil with evil'—there's no such thing! When I was young, I was truly warm-hearted; I treated others' problems as my own. Did it do any good? None at all! I even saved lives—people jumping into rivers, hanging themselves—I saved them all. Was there any reward? No! I'll tell you, I don't know which day I'll freeze to death. I've figured it out: for a man who does hard labor to try to make it alone is harder than reaching the sky. What can one person do? Have you ever seen a grasshopper? All alone, it can jump pretty far, but if a little kid catches it and ties it with a string, it can't even fly. But when they gather in a swarm, in a formation, well, they'll strip an entire field of crops clean in one go, and nobody can stop them! Isn't that right? As for me, with my good heart, I couldn't even keep my own grandson. He got sick, and I had no money to buy him good medicine. I watched him die in my arms! Enough said, enough said!—Tea! Who wants a hot bowl?"

Xiangzi truly understood: Liu the Fourth, Mrs. Yang, Detective Sun—none of them would suffer retribution just because he cursed them; and he himself wouldn't gain any advantage just because he was determined. Relying entirely on himself, just as the old man had said, he was like a grasshopper tied with a string by a child—what use were wings even then?

He no longer had any desire to go to Mr. Cao's place. Going there meant he had to be strong, but what was the use of being strong? He might as well just muddle along carelessly: if he had no food, he'd take the rickshaw out; if he had enough for one day, he'd rest for a day, and leave tomorrow for tomorrow to worry about. This wasn't just a solution—it was the only solution. Saving money, buying a rickshaw—it would all be grabbed from him by others anyway. Why bother? Why not enjoy life while he could?

Besides, if he did find Little Fuzi, he should still make an effort—not for himself, but for her, right? Since he couldn't find her, just as the old man had lost his grandson, who was he struggling for? He had also told the old man about Little Fuzi, treating him as a true friend.

"Who wants a hot bowl?" the old man called out first, then started thinking for Xiangzi: "From what I can guess, there are only two paths: either Er Qiangzi sold her to someone as a concubine, or she's been pawned off to a whorehouse. Hmph, most likely she's ended up in a whorehouse! How can I say that? Since Little Fuzi, as you just told me, has been married before, it's not easy for her to be wanted again; people buying concubines want 'whole goods.' So, eight times out of ten, she's in a whorehouse. I'm almost sixty, and I've seen plenty: if a strong young rickshaw puller doesn't show up at the street corner for a day or two, go look for him—if he's not on a monthly contract, he's sure to be crawling around in a whorehouse. And if a rickshaw puller's daughter or wife suddenly disappears, seven or eight times out of ten, she's gone there too. We sell our sweat, and our women sell their bodies—I understand, I know! You go have a look there. I hope she's not really there, but—Tea! Who wants a hot bowl?!" Xiangzi ran straight to the West Straight Gate.

As soon as he left the suburb, he immediately felt the emptiness. The trees stood skinny by the roadside, not a single bird on their branches. Gray trees, gray earth, gray houses—all stood silently under a gray-yellow sky. Beyond this stretch of gray, he could see the desolate, cold Western Hills. North of the railway tracks lay a grove of trees, beyond which were a few low houses. Xiangzi figured this must be the whorehouse. He looked at the grove—not a sound, not a movement. Looking farther north, he could see some watery ground outside the zoo, with only a few remnant reeds and withered rushes scattered unevenly. There was no one outside the low houses, no activity. Everything was so still, near and far, that he wondered if this really was the famous whorehouse. He boldly walked toward the houses. The doorways were hung with new straw curtains, yellow and slightly glossy. He had heard people talk about how the women here, in summer, sat outside with their backs bare, calling out to passersby. Their customers would sing brothel tunes from a distance to show they weren't novices. Why was it so quiet now? Did they do no business here in winter?

As he was wondering this, the straw curtain of the nearest room stirred, and a woman's head poked out. Xiangzi was startled. At first glance, that head looked very much like Tigress's. He thought to himself, "I came looking for Little Fuzi, and if I find Tigress instead, that would really be seeing a ghost!"

"Come on in, silly sweetie!" the head said, but the voice didn't sound like Tigress's. The throat was hoarse, much like the old man selling dubious medicines whom he often heard at Tianqiao—raspy and urgent.

The room contained nothing except the woman and a small kang. The kang had no mat, but a small fire was burning inside it, giving off a foul, stinking smell that was unbearable. On the kang lay an old quilt, its edges and the bricks on the kang both shiny with grease. The woman was about forty, with disheveled hair, and hadn't washed her face. She wore lined pants below and a small black cotton jacket above, unbuttoned. Xiangzi had to bend his head low just to squeeze in, and the moment he entered, she grabbed him. Her small jacket was unbuttoned, revealing an extremely long and large pair of breasts.

Xiangzi sat down on the edge of the kang, because standing up, he couldn't straighten his neck. He was actually pleased to have run into her—he had often heard people say that the whorehouse had a "White Flour Sack," and this must be her. The nickname came from those two big breasts of hers. Xiangzi got straight to the point, asking if she had seen a Little Fuzi. She didn't know. He described what Little Fuzi looked like, and she remembered: "Yes, there was someone like that! Not very old, liked to show a few white teeth. Right, we all called her Tender Little Meat."

"Which room is she in?" Xiangzi's eyes suddenly widened with a murderous glint. "Her? She's long gone!" "White Flour Sack" pointed outside. "Hanged herself in the woods!"

"What happened?"

"When Tender Little Meat first came here, she was very popular. But she couldn't take it—she was quite frail. One day, at lamp-lighting time, I remember it clearly, because I was sitting at the door with two or three other women. Ah, just around this time, a customer came in and headed straight for her room; she didn't like sitting at the door with us, and when she first arrived, she got beaten for that—"Later she became famous, and everyone let her stay in her room alone, fortunately, those who came to visit her never sought out others. After about the time it takes to eat a meal, the guest left, heading straight for that woods. We didn’t notice anything, and no one went into her room to check on her. By the time the old pimp went to collect the bill from her, only then did he see a man lying in the room, stark naked, sleeping soundly. He was actually drunk. Little Tender Meat had stripped off the guest’s clothes, put them on herself, and escaped. She was really clever. If it hadn’t been dark, she never would have gotten away. It was dark, and she disguised herself as a man, fooling everyone. The old pimp immediately sent people to search everywhere, and lo and behold, as soon as they entered the woods, there she was, hanging. When they took her down, she had already stopped breathing, but her tongue wasn’t sticking out much, and her face wasn’t ugly; even in death, she was still likable! It’s been several months now, and nothing happens in the woods at night; she doesn’t come out to scare people, how kind-hearted!…” Before she finished speaking, Xiangzi wobbled out. He arrived at a cemetery, square and lined with pines, with over a dozen graves among the trees. The sunlight was already weak, and even dimmer in the pine grove. He sat on the ground, on some dry grass and pine blossoms. There was no sound at all, except for a few magpies in the trees mournfully crying out in long notes. This couldn’t be Joy’s grave, he knew, but his tears fell in streams. Nothing was left, even Joy had been buried! He was determined, and she was determined too; all he had left were useless tears, and she had become a ghost who hanged herself! A straw mat, buried in a potter’s field—this was the outcome of a lifetime of hard work!

Back at the rickshaw yard, he slept gloomily for two days. He no longer thought of going to Mr. Cao’s place, not even sending a message; Mr. Cao couldn’t save Xiangzi’s life. After two days of sleep, he took the rickshaw out, his mind completely blank, no longer thinking about anything, no longer hoping for anything; he only went out to suffer for the sake of his stomach, and when his stomach was full, he slept. What was there to think about, what was there to hope for? Watching a skinny, angular dog waiting beside a sweet potato stall for some skins and roots to eat, he understood that he was just like that dog, spending his whole day’s efforts just to pick up some sweet potato skins and roots to eat. Getting by in life was everything; there was nothing to think about.

Humans lifted themselves out of the beasts, yet now they drive their own kind back into the beasts. Xiangzi was still in that city of culture, but he had become a walking beast. It was none of his fault. He stopped thinking, so even if he were to kill someone, he wouldn’t be responsible. He no longer had hope; he just drifted downward in a daze, falling into that bottomless pit. He ate, he drank, he whored, he gambled, he was lazy, he was cunning, because he had no heart left—it had been taken from him. All that remained was that tall frame of flesh, waiting to rot, ready for the potter’s field.

Winter passed, and the spring sunshine was nature’s clothing for everyone. He bundled up his cotton clothes and sold them all. He wanted to eat well, drink well; there was no need to keep winter clothes, and he didn’t plan to see another winter at all. Live one happy day today, and die tomorrow! Who cared about winter or not! If, unluckily, he was still alive come winter, then he’d deal with it then. Before, whenever he thought, he’d consider a whole lifetime of things; now, he only focused on the present. Experience had taught him that tomorrow was just a continuation of today, and tomorrow would inherit today’s grievances. After selling his cotton clothes, he felt incredibly relieved; what couldn’t he do with cash in hand? Why keep them for that winter that could choke a person to death with a gust of wind?

Slowly, it wasn’t just clothes; he wanted to sell anything he could, anything not immediately needed was pawned off. He liked seeing his possessions turn into money that he could spend; once spent, they wouldn’t fall into someone else’s hands—that was the safest. He’d sell things, and buy them again when needed; if he had no money to buy, he simply went without. Not washing his face, not brushing his teeth—none of it really mattered; it saved money and effort. Who was he trying to look good for? Wearing ragged clothes but having flatbread with sauce meat in his stomach—that was real! If you had good stuff in your belly, even if you died, there’d be some grease, unlike a starved rat. Xiangzi, the once so respectable Xiangzi, had turned into a skinny, dirty low-class rickshaw puller. He didn’t wash his face, body, or clothes; sometimes he wouldn’t get a haircut for over a month.

His rickshaw was no longer well-maintained either; new or old, as long as the rental fee was low, it was fine. When he got a fare, if there was even a slight profit, he’d pawn it off halfway. If the passenger objected, he’d glare; if it came to a fight, spending a couple of days at the police station was no big deal! When pulling alone, he moved very slowly, cherishing his sweat. But if he joined a group of rickshaws, and he was in the mood, he’d still run for a bit, just to leave others behind. At such times, he was very good at being sneaky—cutting off other rickshaws, taking sharp turns deliberately, messing with the ones behind, or suddenly shoving the one in front. He knew all those tricks. Before, he thought pulling a rickshaw meant holding a person’s life in his hands, with the risk of killing them if careless. Now, he deliberately caused trouble; killing someone didn’t matter much—everyone deserved to die! He returned to his silent, taciturn self. Without a word, he ate, he drank, he played tricks.

Speech is how people exchange ideas and express feelings, but he had no ideas, no hope—why speak? Except for haggling over prices, he kept his mouth shut all day; his mouth seemed reserved only for eating, drinking, and smoking. Even when drunk, he wouldn’t say a word; he’d sit in a quiet place and cry. Almost every time he got drunk, he’d go to the woods where Joy had hanged herself to shed tears; after crying, he’d stay in a whorehouse. When he sobered up, his money was gone, and he’d caught a disease. He didn’t regret it; if he ever had regrets, it was why he had been so determined, so cautious, so honest in the past. All the things worth regretting were over; now there was nothing to regret.

Now, he did whatever he could to gain an advantage. Smoking an extra cigarette from someone, using a counterfeit coin to buy something, eating a few extra pickles with his soybean milk, pulling the rickshaw with less effort but earning a couple more coppers—all of it satisfied him. If he gained an advantage, others lost out; yes, it was a kind of revenge! Gradually expanding on this, he also learned to borrow money from friends, borrowing without intending to repay; if pushed too hard, he could play dirty. At first, no one suspected him; everyone knew he was a respectable, trustworthy person, so the moment he opened his mouth, he could borrow money. He used this remnant of his personality to borrow everywhere; borrowing was like getting something for nothing, and once he had it, he spent it freely. When people asked for repayment, he’d put on a pitiful act and beg for an extension; if that didn’t work, he’d borrow two more dimes to pay back one and a half, keeping the remaining five cents for a drink. Over time, he couldn’t borrow a single copper anymore, so he started swindling money. He visited all the mansions he used to know, whether the master or the servants; he’d make up lies on the spot to get a few coins; if there was no money, he’d beg for old clothes, which he’d immediately turn into cash, which then became tobacco and liquor. He’d hang his head and think up schemes; a good scheme could bring in more money than a day of pulling rickshaws, saving effort and earning cash—he found it very worthwhile. He even went to see Mrs. Gao from Mr. Cao’s house. He waited in the distance for her to come out shopping, and as soon as he saw her, he nearly rushed over in one step and called out in a moving voice, “Sister Gao!” “Oh! You scared me to death! Who did I think it was? Xiangzi! What happened to you?” Mrs. Gao’s eyes went wide, like she was seeing a monster. “Don’t even ask!” Xiangzi hung his head. “Didn’t you and the master have it all settled? Why did you disappear? I even asked Old Cheng about you; he said he hadn’t seen you. Where did you go? The master and mistress were worried!” “I was really sick, nearly died! You tell the master, help me out a little, and I’ll come back to work when I’m fully recovered!” Xiangzi said, reciting words he’d prepared long ago, simple and moving. “The master’s not home; do you want to come in and see the mistress?” “No need! Look at me! You just tell them for me!” Mrs. Gao took out two dollars for him: “The mistress sent this, told you to hurry and get some medicine!” “Got it! Thanks to the mistress!” Xiangzi took the money, already scheming in his mind where to spend it. As soon as Mrs. Gao turned away, he headed straight for the Tianshui Bridge market and played all day long.

Slowly, after visiting all the mansions, he made a second round, but this time it wasn’t very effective. He realized this path wouldn’t last and needed another idea, something easier than pulling a rickshaw. Before, his only hope was pulling a rickshaw; now, he hated it. Of course, he couldn’t completely cut ties with the rickshaw at once, but as long as he could get by for three meals, he wouldn’t touch the handles. His body was lazy, but his ears were sharp; as soon as there was news, he’d rush over. Whatever it was—Citizen Groups, Petition Groups—as long as there was money to be made, he’d do it. Three dimes, two dimes—he was happy to carry a flag for a day, wandering with the crowd. He felt this was better than pulling a rickshaw anyway; you didn’t earn much, but you didn’t have to use strength. Carrying a little flag, his head down, a cigarette in his mouth, he’d follow the crowd with a half-smile, silent. When he had to shout, he’d open his mouth wide but make no sound; he treasured his voice. He didn’t want to put effort into anything, because in the past, he’d used effort and gotten nothing in return. In these flag-carrying, shouting situations, if there was danger, he’d be the first to run away, and fast. His life could be destroyed by his own hands, but he would never sacrifice anything for anyone again. Those who strive for themselves also know how to destroy themselves; these are the two extremes of individualism.

Beng'er means skill, prospects.

[2]

Yao melodies are tunes popular in brothels.

Public Account: Good Morning Ning
