Learning resource sharing  

It was again the season for pilgrimages to the mountain, and the weather turned scorching hot.

Sellers of paper fans seemed to pop up all at once from somewhere, carrying boxes, the bells on the boxes jingling to attract attention. On the roadside, green apricots were being sold by the heap, cherries were dazzlingly red, golden bees clustered on bowls of rose-date treats, glass noodles in large porcelain bowls gave off a milky sheen, and the stalls for mung-bean jelly and cold noodles were neatly arranged with colorful condiments. People had also changed into light, bright-patterned single-layer clothes, suddenly adding a lot of color to the streets, like countless rainbows scattered on earth. Street cleaners worked harder, constantly splashing water on the roads, but light dust still rose, irritatingly. Yet in the dust, there were long willow branches and nimble, lively swallows, making people feel refreshed. It was a weather that left you unsure how to feel; everyone yawned lazily, tired yet cheerful.

Yangge dances, lions, processions, Five Tiger Stick routines, and other various festivals all headed toward the mountain in succession. Beating gongs and drums, carrying boxes and baskets, hoisting apricot-yellow flags, troop after troop stirred the whole city with unusual excitement, gave people vague yet familiar feelings, and left sounds and dust in the air. Those joining the festivals and those watching them all felt some passion, piety, and thrill. The excitement of troubled times comes from superstition; the consolation of fools lies only in self-deception. These colors, these sounds, the clear clouds in the sky, the dust on the streets—they gave people spirit and things to do: up the mountain they went, to the temple they visited, to the flowers they looked at… at the very least, they could stand by the roadside and watch the excitement, reciting a few Buddhas.

With this heat, it seemed to wake up the spring dreams of the old capital; everywhere was open for sightseeing, everyone thought of doing something, and the temperature urged flowers, grass, fruit trees, and human pleasures to grow together. The green willows and new rushes around the South and North Seas attracted youths playing harmonicas; men and women launched little boats under the willow shade or drifted among the tender lotus leaves, whistling love songs, flirting with their eyes. The peonies and herbaceous peonies in the park drew cultured gentlemen and refined ladies, strolling slowly, waving precious paper fans; when tired, they’d pause before red walls, under green pines, sipping clear tea that could evoke idle melancholy, stealing glances at the noble ladies and famous beauties passing by. Even the usually quiet places were brought visitors by the gentle wind and sunny day, just as butterflies were brought. The peonies at Chongxiao Temple, the green reeds at Taoran Pavilion, the mulberry groves and rice paddies at the Natural History Museum—all drew crowds of people and umbrellas; even the Temple of Heaven, the Confucius Temple, and the Yonghe Temple had a bit of bustle amid their solemnity. Travel enthusiasts and students went to the Western Hills, to the hot springs, to the Summer Palace, to travel, to run around, to collect things, to scribble on rocks. Even the poor had places to go: Huguo Temple, Longfu Temple, White Pagoda Temple, Earth God Temple, and Flower Market were all livelier than usual; all kinds of fresh flowers were brightly displayed by the roadside, and for just a copper or two, you could bring “beauty” home. At the soybean milk stalls, the pickles were gorgeous like big flowers, with bright red chili peppers on top.Eggs are cheap, and the golden-brown, tender fried egg dumplings make one’s mouth water. The Heavenly Bridge is even livelier; newly built mat sheds for tea houses sit side by side, with spotless white tablecloths and flashy singing girls, gazing from afar at the old pines atop the Temple of Heaven’s walls. The sound of gongs and drums extends for seven or eight hours, the crisp, dry weather making them especially clear, stirring people’s hearts. Prostitutes find it easy to dress up; a single cotton print dress can be worn out in style, clearly revealing the curves of their bodies. Those who prefer quiet also have places to go: in front of Jishui Pond, outside Wanshou Temple, the kiln pits in the eastern suburbs, and White Stone Bridge in the west, where they can fish, with small fish sometimes gently brushing against the tender reeds, making them quiver. After fishing, the pig’s head meat, boiled tofu, white liquor, and salted beans at the rustic teahouse are enough to make one drunk and full; then, carrying the fishing rod and small fish, they stroll along the willowy bank, treading on the sunset, and leisurely enter the old city gates.  

Everywhere is fun, everywhere is bustling, everywhere is full of sound and color. The sudden heat of early summer is like a divine charm, casting a spell over this old city. It pays no heed to death, disaster, or hardship; when its time comes, it exerts its power, lulling the hearts of millions into a dreamlike state, singing its hymn of praise. It is filthy, it is beautiful, it is aged, it is lively, it is chaotic, it is peaceful, it is lovely—it is the great early summer of Beiping.  

It is precisely at this time that people long for news that can relieve their boredom—news that can be read two or three times without growing tired, news that can be seen in person after finishing the paper. The days are so long and clear!  

Such news has arrived! As soon as the tram leaves the depot, the newspaper boy, with his shrill voice, chases after people shouting, “Ruan Ming to be shot! The news of his parade through the streets at nine o’clock!” One copper, another copper, and yet another copper, all snatched up by little black hands. On the tram, in the shops, in the hands of passersby, every sheet talks only of Ruan Ming: Ruan Ming’s photo, Ruan Ming’s history, an interview with Ruan Ming, in large and small print, with illustrations and captions, entire pages devoted to Ruan Ming. Ruan Ming is on the tram, in the eyes of pedestrians, in the conversations of talkers—the old city seems to have no one else, only Ruan Ming; Ruan Ming will parade through the streets today, be shot today! Valuable news, ideal news—not only do they speak of Ruan Ming, but in a little while, they can see him. Women rush to dress up; old folks go out early, afraid of being slow and falling behind; even schoolchildren want to skip half a day to witness it. By 8:30, the streets are packed with people—excited, expectant, crowded, noisy—all waiting to see this living news. Rickshaw pullers forget to solicit fares, shops break their routines, peddlers are too lazy to hawk their wares, all waiting for the prison cart and Ruan Ming. In history, there were Huang Chao, Zhang Xianzhong, and the Taiping Heavenly Kingdom—a people who can both kill and love to watch killing. Shooting seems too simple; they prefer hearing about dismemberment, beheading, flaying, and live burial, savoring it like ice cream, thrilling and shivering slightly. But this time, aside from the shooting, there’s a parade through the streets; they almost want to thank the one who came up with this idea, letting them see a half-dead man tied to a cart, feasting their eyes; even if they aren’t the execution overseer, it’s close enough. These people have no sense of good or bad, no understanding of good and evil, no ability to distinguish right from wrong; they cling to some moral codes, wanting to be called civilized, yet they love watching their own kind sliced to pieces, with the same cruelty and thrill as a child butchering a puppy. Once they have power in their hands, any one of them would massacre a city, cutting off women’s breasts and feet to pile into small hills—this is their great feat. Lacking this authority, they might as well watch pigs, sheep, and people being killed to get their fix. If they can’t even see that, they’ll curse a child with a thousand cuts and ten thousand slashes to vent their pent-up malice.  

Under a clear blue sky, with a red sun high in the east and a few gentle eastern breezes, the willow branches by the roadside sway slightly. On the eastern side path, a large shadow is packed with people: young and old, male and female, ugly and pretty, fat and thin—some dressed fashionably, others just in undershirts—all chatting and laughing, hoping and waiting, occasionally craning their necks south or north. When one person cranes their neck, everyone follows, their hearts beating a little faster. Little by little, they push forward, the crowd gradually squeezing to the edge of the road, forming a wall of flesh, with only uneven heads bobbing about. Patrolmen come out in squads to maintain order; they block, they shout, and sometimes they grab a child like a clod of dirt and give it a couple of punches, drawing hearty laughter from the crowd. They wait, patiently wait, their legs growing sore, but unwilling to leave empty-handed; those at the front won’t budge, and newcomers from behind push forward, sparking arguments—fists and feet still, relying only on tongues, cursing each other, while the crowd cheers. Children grow impatient and are slapped by adults; pickpockets succeed, and those who lost their belongings break into loud curses. Noise, clamor, all blending into a din; no one is willing to move. The more people gather, the less they want to move, showing their unanimous delight in watching the half-dead prisoner.  

Suddenly, everyone falls silent. In the distance, a squad of armed police arrives. “Here they come!” someone shouts. Immediately, the crowd erupts into chaos, the whole mass like a machine, surging forward an inch, then another inch. They’re coming! They’re coming! Eyes all light up, mouths all muttering something, a sea of voices, the whole street stinking of sweat—the people of a land of ritual passionately love watching executions.  

Ruan Ming is a short, small man, his hands tied behind his back, sitting in the cart like a sick little monkey; he hangs his head, with a white placard over two feet long stuck behind him. The noise of the crowd surges like a tide, each wave urging the next; everyone curls their lips in criticism, all somewhat disappointed: Just a little monkey like that! So limp and unimpressive! Head down, face deathly pale, silent as can be! Some get an idea, wanting to tease him: “Brothers, give him a cheer!” Immediately, from all directions come cheers of “Bravo!”—like applauding a female star on stage, contemptuous, malicious, annoying. Ruan Ming still doesn’t make a sound, not even lifting his head. Some grow truly angry, can’t stand such a weak prisoner; they squeeze to the roadside and spit on him with a “pfft pfft.” Ruan Ming doesn’t move, shows no reaction. The more they watch, the more boring it gets, yet the less willing they are to leave; what if he suddenly says, “In twenty years, I’ll be a hero again”? What if he asks for two pots of white liquor and a plate of braised pork from a tavern? No one budges, waiting to see what he’ll do. The cart passes by, and they still follow; he shows nothing now, who knows if he’ll revive at the Single Plank Archway and belt out a few lines from “Silang Visits His Mother”? Follow! Some follow all the way to the Heavenly Bridge; although he never does anything praiseworthy or satisfying, seeing him take the bullet with their own eyes makes it not a wasted trip.  

In such a lively time, Xiangzi alone walks slowly with his head down at the base of Deshengmen City Wall. Reaching Jishui Pond, he looks around. No one is there. Slowly, tiptoeing, he makes his way to the lakeshore. By the lake, he finds an old tree, leans his back against the trunk, and stands for a while. Hearing no human voices around, he sits down gently. A slight rustle of reed leaves, or a sudden bird call, makes him spring up anxiously, sweat beading on his forehead. He listens, he looks—no movement around—then slowly sits down again. This happens several times before he grows accustomed to the rustle of reed leaves and the birdsong, deciding not to panic anymore. Staring blankly at the ditch outside the lake, some small fish, with eyes bright as pearls, gather and scatter, come and go; sometimes they nudge a patch of tender duckweed, sometimes they blow bubbles. By the ditch, some tadpoles that have grown legs straighten their bodies, wagging their big black heads. The water suddenly flows faster, sweeping away the fish and tadpoles, their tails askew as they drift downstream, but soon another group follows, struggling to stop. A water scorpion scampers past quickly. The current gradually steadies, the small fish regroup, opening tiny mouths to nibble at a floating green leaf or a bit of grass. Larger fish hide in the depths, occasionally showing a back before swiftly diving down, leaving a swirl and faint ripples on the surface. A kingfisher darts across the water like an arrow, and both big and small fish vanish, leaving only duckweed on the surface. Xiangzi stares blankly at all this, seeming to see and not see, absently picking up a small stone and tossing it into the water, splashing up some droplets and scattering many duckweeds; he jolts, startled again, about to stand up.  

He sits for a long time, then stealthily feels his waist with his big, black hand. Nodding, he stops his hand there; after a while, he pulls out a stack of banknotes, counts them, and carefully tucks them back in place.  

His heart is wholly occupied with that money: how to spend it, how to keep it from others, how to enjoy it while staying safe. He is no longer thinking for himself; he has become an appendage of the money, everything subject to its command.  

The source of this money has already determined its destination. Such money cannot be spent openly and honorably. This money, and the person holding it, dare not face the light. Everyone is on the street watching Ruan Ming, while Xiangzi hides by the quiet city wall, seeking an even quieter, darker place. He dares not walk the streets again, because he sold out Ruan Ming. Even alone facing the still water, leaning against the deserted city wall, he dares not lift his head, as if a ghostly shadow is always following him. Ruan Ming, lying in a pool of blood at the Heavenly Bridge, lives on in Xiangzi’s heart, lives on in the banknotes at his waist. He does not regret it, only fears—fears that ghost that follows him everywhere, always.  

After Ruan Ming became an official, he indulged in many things he had previously thought should be overthrown. Money leads people into depraved society, casting aside noble ideals and willingly descending into hell. He wore fancy foreign clothes, went whoring, gambled, and even took up opium smoking. When his conscience stirred, he thought it was this wicked society that had trapped him, not entirely his own fault; he admitted his actions were wrong, but blamed society’s overwhelming temptation, which he couldn’t resist. Soon enough, his money ran short, and he recalled his radical ideas, but not to act on them with vigor; he wanted to use ideas to get money. Turning ideas into money was just like, in school, trying to get passing grades through currying favor with teachers. A lazy person’s ideas cannot stand alongside their integrity; anything that can be exchanged for money will sooner or later be sold. He accepted a subsidy. Organizations eager to promote revolution couldn’t afford to be too picky about their recruits; anyone willing to join was considered a comrade. But those who took subsidies had to show some results, no matter the means; the organizations needed reports. Ruan Ming couldn’t just take money without doing anything. He joined the work of organizing rickshaw pullers. As for Xiangzi, he was already an old hand at cheerleading; thus, Ruan Ming got to know Xiangzi.  

Ruan Ming sold his ideas for money; Xiangzi accepted ideas for money. Ruan Ming knew that, if necessary, he could sacrifice Xiangzi. Xiangzi never planned to do such a thing, but when the time came, he did it—selling out Ruan Ming. Those who work for money fear encountering more money; loyalty cannot be founded on money. Ruan Ming believed in his own ideas, using their radicalism to excuse all his bad behavior. Xiangzi found everything Ruan Ming said very reasonable, but he also envied Ruan Ming’s pleasures—“If I had more money, I could have a few days of joy too! Just like that Ruan!” Money diminished Ruan Ming’s character, money dazzled Xiangzi’s eyes. He sold Ruan Ming for sixty yuan. Ruan Ming wanted the power of the masses; Xiangzi wanted more of the pleasures Ruan Ming enjoyed. Ruan Ming’s blood was spilled over the subsidy; Xiangzi stuffed the banknotes into his waistband.  

He sat until the sun tilted west, when the reeds and willows by the lake were tinged with golden-red glimmers, then stood up and walked west along the city wall. Cheating for money was something he was used to; selling someone’s life was a first. Especially since he still found Ruan Ming’s words quite reasonable! The emptiness of the city wall and the towering height of the wall made him more afraid with each step. Occasionally spotting some crows on a trash heap, he would go out of his way to avoid them, fearful of startling them into ominous caws. Reaching the western city wall, he quickened his pace, slinking out of Xizhimen like a dog that had stolen food. Having someone to keep him company at night, to numb him and make him unafraid—that was the ideal place; the white house was just such a place.  

By autumn, Xiangzi’s illness no longer allowed him to pull a rickshaw, and his credit had been so ruined he couldn’t rent one. He became a regular at a small inn. At night, for two coppers, he could lie down in the inn. During the day, he did odd jobs that earned him only enough for a bowl of gruel. He couldn’t beg on the streets—so big a man, no one would take pity on him. He couldn’t paint sores on himself to beg at temple fairs, not having been taught how to dress up his boils as pitiful misfortunes. As for stealing, he lacked the skill; even thieves have their gangs and connections. Only he could earn his own food, with no other support or help. He worked for himself, and he completed his own death. He waited for his last breath; he was a dead man still breathing, with individualism as his soul.This soul would rot together with his body into the soil.  

  

Since Beiping was designated as the former capital, its pomp, craftsmanship, food, speech, and police have gradually trickled outward, seeking to lend prestige to places and people with the same majesty as the Son of Heaven. The Westernized Qingdao now has Beiping's instant-boiled mutton; the bustling Tianjin can hear the mournful cry of "Hard-faced—buns" in the dead of night; in Shanghai, in Hankou, in Nanjing, there are now police and runners speaking the Beijing dialect, eating sesame seed cakes; jasmine tea travels from south to north, gets double-smoked in Beiping, and then heads back south; even the funeral bearers sometimes take the train to Tianjin or Nanjing to carry the coffins of high officials and nobles.  

  

Beiping itself, however, is gradually losing its former grandeur. Past the ninth day of the ninth lunar month, pastry shops still sell flower cakes; lantern-rice dumpling vendors might appear in autumn; centuries-old shops suddenly think of celebrating their anniversaries, using the occasion to distribute clearance sale flyers... Economic pressure forces pomp to seek new outlets, for dignity cannot be eaten. Yet, in broad terms, red and white affairs—weddings and funerals—still preserve old rituals and airs, as if they deserve attention and require some display. The processions, music, bridal sedan chairs, and official canopies for weddings and funerals remain unmatched by any other city. The paper cranes and lions, the paper figures, horses, and carriages for funerals, the full regalia and twenty-four musicians for weddings still parade through the streets with official pomp, evoking the prosperity and grace of peacetime years.  

  

Xiangzi's livelihood largely depends on these lingering rituals and customs. If there is a wedding, he carries flags and umbrellas for others; if there is a funeral, he holds wreaths and elegiac couplets. He does not rejoice, nor does he weep; he merely accompanies others on the parade for a dozen or so coppers. Donning the green or blue robes provided by the funeral parlor or bridal sedan shop, wearing an ill-fitting black hat, he can temporarily cover his tattered clothes and appear somewhat respectable. When a wealthy household holds an event, requiring everyone to get a haircut and wear boots, he gets a chance to look clean and tidy from head to toe. His filthy ailments make it hard for him to take large steps, so it suits him to hold a flag or two elegiac couplets and shuffle slowly along the roadside.  

  

Yet, even for this, he is not skilled. His golden days are over; he neither started a family nor built a career from his rickshaw. Everything, following his hopes, has become "just that." Despite his large frame, he insists on fighting for a flying tiger flag or a pair of short, narrow elegiac couplets, refusing to touch heavier items like the red umbrella or the "silence" placards. He would even compete with an old man, a child, or a woman. He refuses to suffer even the slightest loss.  

  

Carrying such a small item, he shuffles along slowly, head down, back bent, a cigarette butt picked up from the road dangling from his mouth, listlessly dragging his feet. When everyone stops, he might keep walking; when everyone moves on, he might stand a while longer. He seems not to hear the gong signaling commands. He never bothers to check whether the distances before and after are even or whether the left and right ranks are aligned. He walks his own way, head lowered, as if in a dream or pondering some profound truth. The gong-beater in red and the processional attendant holding silk banners hurl every curse imaginable at him: "Hey, grandson! You there, camel! Watch your alignment, dammit!" He still seems not to hear. The gong-beater comes over and hits him with the gong mallet; he rolls his eyes and glances around dazedly, ignoring whatever was said, instead focusing on the ground, searching for any cigarette butt worth picking up. Dignified, ambitious, dreamy, self-serving, individualistic, robust, great Xiangzi—who knows how many funerals he has attended for others? Who knows when and where he himself will be buried, burying this fallen, selfish, unfortunate offspring of society's ills, this ghost of individualism's dead end!
